Aleksandr Dugin

The Metaphysics of the Good News

(Orthodox Esotericism)

Moscow: Arktogeia, 1996 · Volume II of the “Absolute Homeland” cycle

Translated from the Russian · Reader edition, assembled 14 August 2026

A literary translation with a critical apparatus. Dugin’s note markers (n) are preserved in the text; the notes’ contents are not reproduced in this edition. The apparatus beside each chapter group records translator decisions and the author’s factual errors, which are rendered as written and flagged rather than silently corrected. Two instalments (Chapters XIV–XVII and XXVI–XXVII) are pending and marked in place.

Introduction

Part I · The Metaphysics of the Orthodox Dogmas

Introduction · Chapter I

Christian Metaphysics:
The Essence of the Problem

Христианская метафизика: сущность проблемы

Christianity is the tradition whose metaphysical dimension has been studied least of all. This is rather paradoxical, since one would think that the deep investigation of Christianity — the religion of the West — should attract everyone who takes an interest in metaphysics and who, in the wake of Guénon, seeks to grasp the most profound aspects of Tradition. Yet all the debates around Christianity in Traditionalist circles tend, as a rule, to confine themselves to fairly secondary, applied questions: the virtual initiation conferred by the Church’s sacraments, the absence of any idea of cyclical time, and so forth. Behind all this one can make out a tacit agreement among Traditionalists that Christianity is no more than a reduced, incomplete tradition whose esotericism has been practically lost, and whose metaphysical content is impossible to extract from beneath the dense veil of exoteric, scholastic theology and the vague, subjective intuitions of its mystics. Every attempt to establish correspondences between Christianity’s fundamental principles and the conceptual categories of other, more metaphysically developed traditions — above all Hinduism — has yielded quite pitiful results, resting on forced interpretations and a tendentious desire to arrive, at whatever cost, at conclusions coinciding with Guénon’s conception. (This is most strikingly evident in Abbé Henri Stéphane’s Introduction to Christian Esotericism.)1

The explanation, however, is fairly simple. The Guénonian approach gained currency only in the narrow circles of the Western intellectual elite, where “Christianity” is traditionally understood to mean, at best, Catholicism. But the specificity of Catholicism is such that from the very moment of the Western Church’s separation from the Eastern, it built its dogmatic and intellectual edifice precisely upon a deliberate rejection of Christianity’s metaphysical content. All its scholastic constructions were, at bottom, an effort to elaborate a coherent theological doctrine while entirely ignoring the ontological and metaphysical elements that had in fact not only been present in the Christian tradition before the schism but had survived in it afterward as well. They survived, however, exclusively in the Eastern Church — that is, in the bosom of Orthodoxy. But of this the Catholics, even the most penetrating among them, appear to have had no suspicion.

Orthodoxy, for its part, while preserving its ontological and metaphysical wholeness, found itself unable, from a certain moment onward, to articulate its metaphysical content — that is, Christian metaphysics properly speaking — in clear categories. Soon after the close of the Palamite controversies, when Orthodox esotericism achieved its last, dazzling ascent in history, this line was somewhat marginalized and frozen; priority passed instead to the more exoteric dimensions of the Church. In the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, a number of Russian theologians — and even secular philosophers — intuitively divining the special metaphysical nature of Orthodoxy, undertook to formulate certain of its principles, to revive the forgotten dimension of the tradition. More often than not, however, these efforts came to nothing, since not one of them was acquainted with the works of Guénon, from which alone, in our view, it is presently possible to draw adequate knowledge of the essential proportions governing the structure of a complete metaphysics.

One might put it this way: Western Traditionalists possessed the intellectual instruments forged by Guénon, but lacked any adequate object to which they might be applied, since Catholicism does not, in principle, permit the passage from the exoteric to the esoteric and metaphysical levels — indeed, it erects insuperable barriers along that path. The Orthodox, meanwhile, possessed and still possess the complete object: the ecclesiastical Christian Orthodox Tradition and a full, unreduced body of dogma. What they lacked was the adequate metaphysical instrument. And so, for two diametrically opposed reasons, in both the West and the East the most widespread, most familiar, most intimate of traditions — Christianity — remained the most unknown, the most enigmatic, the most closed, even as Traditionalists proved quite capable of mastering Islamic metaphysics, Buddhism, Hinduism, Taoism, and even a number of archaic cults. The distant and exotic became, by a strange paradox, closer to modern investigators who nominally belong to Christian civilization than what is “their own,” habitual and near.

Be that as it may, the first encounter of Russians with the ideas of Guénon2 opens the possibility of charting a way out of this impasse: of attempting to compare the general metaphysical picture with the dogmas of Orthodox Christianity. One ought not to deceive oneself about the simplicity of such an undertaking. The virtually total absence of any reference to Orthodoxy among Traditionalist authorities makes the task extraordinarily difficult and fraught with risk. All the same — without claiming any final truth in the matter, and leaving the way open for alternative inquiries — we shall attempt in this work to understand the metaphysical nature of Orthodoxy and, consequently, to draw nearer to a formulation and awareness of the essence of Christian metaphysics.

Part I · Chapter II

Three Aspects of the Metaphysical Absolute

Три аспекта метафизического абсолюта

The highest category of a complete metaphysics is the “absolute.” In his book The Multiple States of Being, René Guénon gives an exhaustive description of this reality. The sense of Guénon’s investigation of the absolute reduces to the following.3 The absolute coincides with infinity, or with the metaphysical All. “Infinity,” in the conceptual sense, is a negative category, since its definition rests on the negation of limit, of end — and consequently, strictly speaking, it cannot be defined at all. But this negative formulation, according to Guénon, must not be taken literally, for the negation is merely a consequence of the ontological and cosmological position of beings who belong to relative reality.

In other words, the negativity of infinity flows from viewing it from the standpoint of finitude, whereas in itself it is, on the contrary, identical with the highest metaphysical affirmativeness. It is important to stress at once that infinity (= the absolute) does not coincide with pure being, and accordingly that metaphysics (the doctrine of the highest principles) is not a synonym of ontology (the doctrine of being). More on this below.4

Within infinity Guénon distinguishes two aspects: one conditionally “active,” the other conditionally “passive” — although, as he emphasizes, the designations “activity” and “passivity” exist only for an “external” gaze, and within the absolute itself they may be characterized in some other way entirely. The “active” aspect of the absolute Guénon defines as infinity proper, or as the formula “infinity = infinity.” The “passive” aspect is universal possibility, or all-possibility, and its formula is “infinity = all-possibility.” “Passivity” here means that infinity is turned toward the finite by the side of itself that is identical with possibility; “activity” means that infinity is turned toward itself by the side identical with infinity. All-possibility, as the “passive” aspect of the absolute, is called in the terms of the Far Eastern tradition “non-being,” and in the Vedantic tradition asat (likewise “non-being,” a-sat). One may point at once to the analogy with the conception of the Orthodox mystical theologian St. Dionysius the Areopagite, who defined God as the apophatic (in principle unknowable) Origin, exceeding and thereby negating every determination — including the determination of “being-ness” (thus the Areopagite speaks of the “super-essence” of the Godhead, that is, of its superiority to everything comprehended in the concept of being5).

Since we are, by the nature of the case, situated within the bounds of the finite (even when the question concerns the highest ontological principles), it makes sense to consider almost exclusively the “passive” aspect of the absolute, taken as all-possibility. This all-possibility likewise has two aspects. One aspect of all-possibility is designated the possibility of non-manifestation. This is the “active” aspect of all-possibility, while the “passive” aspect of all-possibility is the possibility of manifestation. The possibility of non-manifestation is reckoned the “active” principle and the possibility of manifestation the “passive” one, because the first presupposes no determination or limitation of all-possibility whatsoever, while the second implies both. It is evident that the possibility of non-manifestation corresponds more closely to the “nature” of all-possibility than does the possibility of manifestation — although when the whole absolute is considered in its entirety (that is, as pure all-possibility “together with” pure infinity), this correspondence is not so unambiguous.6

The possibility of manifestation coincides with pure being, or with its essential, “active” side. Pure being carries within itself everything that constitutes the content of manifestation, yet does not itself belong to the sphere of manifestation: it remains within all-possibility as a particular possibility, contrasting with all the other possibilities precisely in this — that it alone presupposes manifestation, while all the rest (collectively named the possibility of non-manifestation) do not.

Guénon indicates that the symbol of all-possibility, embracing both the possibility of manifestation and the possibility of non-manifestation, is the metaphysical zero. Pure being (= the possibility of manifestation) is the metaphysical one, which is, however, nothing other than the “affirmed metaphysical zero.” In other words, pure being is not something other than general all-possibility; it is merely a particular, partial (and rather paradoxical) mode of its metaphysical affirmation.

Thus, without even stepping beyond the bounds of the absolute, without entering the sphere of manifestation — where the absolute is genuinely estranged from its essential nature — we have obtained three foundational metaphysical categories which, being first and metaphysically prior, cannot fail to shape the further logic of manifestation, constituting its essential paradigm.

The first and highest category is infinity: the “active” aspect of the absolute, its absolute identity with itself. The second category is the possibility of non-manifestation, which belongs to the “passive” aspect of the absolute while at the same time embodying the “active” aspect of all-possibility. And the third category is the possibility of manifestation — the most “passive” instance within the whole of infinity, and yet the highest principle for all of manifestation.

All three categories belong to the sphere of non-being. They may also be defined otherwise: the possibility of non-manifestation (pure non-being), the possibility of manifestation (pure being), and infinity or the absolute (the totality of pure being and pure non-being).

For rigor of exposition, a digression is needed to clarify the metaphysical terms we are using. The possibility of manifestation is the designation of a certain metaphysical instance viewed from the sphere of the manifested. And looking from that sphere, there is no other way to name the aspect of all-possibility that is the immediate cause of manifestation.

The same holds for the possibility of non-manifestation, which, for the gaze from within the manifested, embraces everything that, while metaphysically possible, does not “participate” in the principial securing of manifestation. Since we are by definition located in the manifested, it is most precise to speak of the aspects of all-possibility by relating them to manifestation. But abstracting from the sphere of manifestation, other terms may also be used.

These are pure being and pure non-being. The addition of the qualifier “pure” here signifies the absolutization of the qualities — the being-ness of being and the non-being-ness of non-being. Such a definition does not reflect the relation of these aspects of all-possibility to the manifested; it describes them without reference to anything whatsoever. It is very important to understand that pure being itself, being in fact a principle, is not — but lies at the foundation of everything that is. Pure non-being likewise is not — but does not lie at the foundation of everything that is. Thus pure being belongs to non-being, yet is distinct from pure non-being. Pure non-being, in its turn, not only is not, but cannot be (whereas pure being can). And finally, one may consider the relation of these two metaphysical principles to all-possibility itself — that is, not from outside, from the manifested, but from within, from the sphere of the purely possible. In that case the possibility of manifestation will be defined as the possibility of being, and the possibility of non-manifestation as the possibility of not-being. The term “possibility of not-being” (with “not-being” hyphenated) must be written precisely so, for the following reason: what is at issue here is not that non-being is possible — within all-possibility it is, on the contrary, actual — but the metaphysical affirmation of a possibility radically distinct from the possibility of being (and, correspondingly, from the possibility of manifestation), one defined solely through the negation of a concrete possibility: the possibility of being.

Now all the terms can be arranged hierarchically. The gaze from outside, from the manifested, distinguishes two aspects of all-possibility: the possibility of manifestation and the possibility of non-manifestation. Such designations constitute, as it were, the “lowest” level of the absolute, correlated with something external to it, non-absolute. In themselves, and without reference to realities more inward or more outward, these aspects may be called, respectively, “pure being” and “pure non-being.” And finally, in the highest and most inward dimension, they are defined as “the possibility of being” and “the possibility of not-being.”

In essence, for everything manifested these terms are synonyms, and therefore in using them we point to the same principles: possibility of manifestation = pure being = possibility of being; and possibility of non-manifestation = pure non-being = possibility of not-being.

But since we naturally consider the sphere of all-possibility from outside, it is more convenient to use precisely the terms “possibility of manifestation” and “possibility of non-manifestation,” implying in each the presence of deeper and more inward dimensions (without stressing this specially, so as not to overburden the exposition).

Let us now note one crucial point: Guénon always maintains that in relation to pure being and pure non-being, the pure absolute7 figures precisely as a “totality,” as a kind of mathematical sum. From our point of view, however, what is at issue is a more complex correlation of one “active” and two “passive” instances within infinity. To this, however, we shall return further on.

Be that as it may, in the metaphysical absolute there is plainly present a Trinity — and it is astonishing that its quality can best be characterized by the formula “undivided and unconfused,” and likewise “consubstantial.” This Trinity consists of the unaffirmed metaphysical zero (the possibility of non-manifestation, pure non-being), the affirmed metaphysical zero (the possibility of manifestation, pure being), and the integrating, primordial instance of the metaphysical zero at once affirmed and unaffirmed.

It is difficult to avoid the identification that suggests itself: this metaphysical Trinity, belonging to non-being, with the apophatic aspect of the Triune God in St. Dionysius the Areopagite.8 It is astonishing to what degree, precisely in this case, one may fittingly speak of a metaphysical “consubstantiality, unconfusedness, and undividedness,” since a full conception of the inner structure of the absolute makes it necessary to distinguish in it precisely three aspects — no more and no fewer. Any subtraction or addition here would distort the entire metaphysical picture: it would lose its fullness or acquire a superfluous, inessential determination.

Part I · Chapter III

The Apophatics of the Three Persons

Апофатика трех лиц

If the metaphysical understanding of triplicity as the essential character of the absolute reveals itself through such determinations as “consubstantiality, undividedness, and unconfusedness” — and here one may indeed speak of a certain correspondence between the highest reaches of Christian mystical contemplation and universal metaphysical doctrine9 — then with the clarification of the metaphysical distribution of the persons within this apophatic, transcendent Trinity, matters stand far more difficult. Let us note at once that the Catholic Credo, with its dogma of the Filioque and the “subordinationism” that follows from it — that is, the strict hierarchical subordination of one person to another10 — permits no correspondences whatsoever to be traced between the aspects of the absolute and the persons of the Trinity, for we immediately collide with insurmountable contradictions between the dogmatic characteristics of the divine persons11 and the metaphysical essence of the three inner aspects of infinity. Since for the Catholics the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and from the Son, and not from the Father alone — being hierarchically “third” and “lowest” — then in passing from theology to metaphysics He could correspond only to the most “passive” side of the absolute, that is, to the possibility of manifestation.12 But this produces a contradiction between the qualitative metaphysical characterization of the possibility of non-manifestation as the “hidden,” strictly “non-luminous” side of all-possibility, and the dogmatic attributes of the second person of the Most Holy Trinity. In short, the total divergence of theological and metaphysical determinations hardly permits us to speak seriously of the correlation of Trinity and absolute outlined above, if the formulation of the Catholic Credo is accepted.

One may suppose that it was the collision with just this contradiction that restrained Catholic Traditionalists from any attempt at a metaphysical interpretation of the Trinity. And they could not adopt the dogmatic position of Orthodoxy, owing to an entirely understandable adherence to the norms of the Western Church and to a scholastic inertia which even Guénon never seriously criticized.

Be that as it may, within the channel of Orthodoxy the transfer of theological characteristics onto the metaphysical absolute, though no simple matter, does not entail irresolvable contradictions as it does in the case of Catholic dogmatics. Both the affirmation that the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father alone, and the emphasis that He is sent forth by the Son (rather than proceeding jointly from Son and Father), sharply change the whole picture.

Let us note that we are speaking here of a particular apophatic vision of the Trinity, which St. Dionysius the Areopagite calls “super-essential.” St. Dionysius speaks of hyperousia, “super-essence” — of the most transcendent and purely metaphysical understanding of the Godhead, distinct from its ontological (being-related) understanding. This division into the essential (ontological) and super-essential (super-ontological, metaphysical, absolute) aspects of the Trinitarian Godhead in no way doubles the Trinity or splits it into halves. It is simply that a manifested (and all the more a created) being cannot grasp both these aspects of infinity simultaneously as the single thing they in fact are. One may say with Guénon that this division is the result of our gaze, and not an inner quality of God himself. Yet the triplicity (more precisely, the triunity) of the absolute must, in the Christian perspective, be understood on the contrary precisely as an intra-divine quality, and not merely as the product of the limitation of man’s knowing perspective. The doubling of the Trinity — grasping it separately as ontological and as super-ontological — is the result of an “optical” illusion of contemplation on the part of manifested beings. Triplicity, however, belongs to the Godhead as an inner, foundational characteristic. One may say that the division of the Godhead into ontological and super-ontological aspects ceases at the moment manifestation is annulled, folded up. The Triunity of God remains even “after” this, being wholly independent of manifestation as fact.13

Having made this last clarification, we may propose a distribution of the metaphysical aspects of the absolute among the three persons of the Most Holy Trinity. God the Father is unconditionally correlated with the “active” side of the absolute: He is infinity itself, the absolute itself in its most perfect and complete self-identity. Clearly, self-identical infinity embraces its “passive” aspect as well — it is primary in relation to all-possibility in all its shades. The greatest difficulties arise in establishing the correspondence between the two remaining persons of the Trinity and the aspects of the absolute. Evidently each person must be correlated with one of the two most general kinds of possibility — the possibility of manifestation and the possibility of non-manifestation — of which all-possibility properly consists.

Symbolic correspondences compel us to identify God the Son with the possibility of manifestation,14 and God the Holy Spirit with the possibility of non-manifestation.15 Here the first difficulty arises. From the purely metaphysical point of view, the possibility of manifestation is the more “passive” side of all-possibility compared to the possibility of non-manifestation; yet in Christian contemplation — even given the Orthodox affirmation of the equality of the three divine persons and the Eastern Church’s explicit rejection of “subordinationism” — the stress falls on the special closeness between Father and Son. But if we take this last observation into account, we shall be forced back to the Catholic perspective: to identifying the possibility of non-manifestation with the Son and the possibility of manifestation with the Holy Spirit — which, as noted, contradicts the dogmatic symbolism of the personal attributes ascribed, respectively, to Son and Spirit.

To break the deadlock, we should nonetheless accept the first identification (possibility of manifestation = Son; possibility of non-manifestation = Holy Spirit) and try to discover the reason for a certain disturbance of the metaphysical hierarchy within the absolute in Christianity. First of all, the very conditional division of all-possibility into possibility of manifestation and possibility of non-manifestation arises only thanks to the fixing of the possibility of manifestation, which introduces into the homogeneous super-ontological “darkness”16 of the absolute the first “alien” element. And only “after” this introduction — as the transcendent negation17 of this possibility of manifestation — does the specific status of the possibility of non-manifestation become clear, corresponding to the newly affirmed self-identity of an all-possibility touched by the perspective (however nominal) of a “departure” from that self-identity. Thus the metaphysically prior “active” aspect of all-possibility, the possibility of non-manifestation, discovers its essential identity only in answering the challenge of the “passive” aspect. Here one may recall the Gospel formula: “And the light shineth in darkness, and the darkness comprehended it not.”18 The invisible light of the possibility of manifestation began to shine in the darkness of all-possibility, and that which did not comprehend it was disclosed as a new and particular darkness, distinct from the invisible light — the darkness of the possibility of non-manifestation.

In Christian terms, the accentuation of the possibility of manifestation may be likened to the pre-eternal “begetting of the Son” from the Father without a mother (the arising of the invisible light). In that case, the thickening of the transcendent darkness of the possibility of non-manifestation is logically correlated with the “procession” from the Father of the third person of the Most Holy Trinity. And it thereby becomes clear why the Holy Spirit is sent forth precisely by the Son. The kindled invisible light “provokes” the transcendent darkness of the possibility of non-manifestation into affirming its essential self-identification in the face of the disclosure, within all-possibility, of that paradoxical possibility which, while possible, is opposed to (or “incompossible” with, in Leibniz’s terms) the remaining possibilities — henceforth defined collectively as the general possibility of non-manifestation. The Christian tradition stresses that the second person of the Trinity, the Son, is “open” and “evident,” while the third person, the Holy Spirit, is on the contrary “closed” and “secret.” One further crucial point. The Christian tradition regards cosmogony, sacred history, and eschatology as a process of triune divine kenosis (literally, “emptying”) — the sacrificial self-diminution of the Godhead. This self-diminution, God’s love for that which He essentially is not, is seen by Christians as the chief, the most intimate divine quality. This sacrificial, “kenotic” character of the Christianly understood absolute largely determines the metaphysical hierarchy of infinity itself, in which the first of the two remaining places — after the affirmation of the complete and perfect supremacy of infinity’s identity with itself in its “active” aspect — is taken precisely by the Son (the possibility of manifestation), as the aspect most marked by the perspective of “kenosis,” “self-diminution,” “emptying,” “self-evacuation,” “sacrifice.” And here it suddenly becomes clear why the Eastern Church so insisted on rejecting the Filioque and “subordinationism” and on preserving the strict Orthodox formulation. In this case the “ethical” priority of the “begotten” Son at the level of the absolute did not contradict the independent and even metaphysically more important position of the “proceeding” Holy Spirit. Between the second and third persons of the Most Holy Trinity a relation was affirmed which neither openly denied the metaphysical role of the possibility of non-manifestation nor distorted the specificity of the peculiarly Christian value of divine “kenosis.” More than that: the value of “kenosis” extended to the absolute itself in its highest understanding — which means that the sacrificial character of the Son half-opened the mysterious benevolence of the Father himself toward the sacrificial orientation as such.

This last point shows that infinity in its essential aspect is something more (contrary to Guénon) than merely the “totality” of pure being (the Son) and pure non-being (the Holy Spirit). In the perspective of Christian metaphysics, the essence of infinity mysteriously shows through in the absolute’s own benevolence toward “kenosis” — and this endows manifestation, beginning from the level of the possibility of manifestation, with an additional, hidden meaning entirely absent from Vedanta, from Taoism, from Islamic metaphysics.

The Trinitarian dogma singles out the first person of the Trinity, God the Father, as an independent Person precisely because it sees here the source of a great metaphysical enigma — rooted not simply in the “objective” structure of all-possibility, but in a certain paradoxical will of the highest absolute, in the secret and inscrutable inclination of infinity itself. One may even say that on the far side of the “totality” of the affirmed metaphysical zero and the unaffirmed metaphysical zero (which, according to Guénon and Vedanta, should exhaustively describe the absolute), Christian metaphysics descries a special “supplementary,” metaphysically non-evident mystery — paradoxically hinting at a quality of infinity which supra-rationally passes beyond the “limits” of the limitless metaphysical zero itself.

And if our intuition is correct, then Christian metaphysics is in its essence not simply a variation of the one traditional metaphysics, but a particular and exceptional species of it.

Part I · Chapter IV

The World of the Divine Energies
(The Eternal Eve of Creation)

Мир божественных энергий (вечный канун творения)

Let us stress, to avoid ambiguity, that all the considerations set out above concerned exclusively the “inner” aspects of the absolute, radically prior to manifestation and, accordingly, to creation — creation being a more concrete and specific variation of manifestation (more on this in the chapters that follow). The apophatic Trinity (the super-ontological Trinity) lies far beyond the reach of the properly religious gaze, and therefore only the most general, the deepest determinations of the Trinitarian mystery may be applied to it — not everything that dogmatically enters into the characterization of the Trinity19 in ordinary theology, soteriology, and cosmology. This absolute Trinity belongs essentially to the sphere of non-being, to the field of metaphysical possibilities, and is in no way touched by all the subsequent stages of concretization in manifestation, up to and including creation and providential sacred history. This metaphysical Trinity is uncontemplatable and unthinkable; it lies in the region of the purely transcendent, and may therefore be called the “super-essential” and even the “super-divine” Trinity. But being the secret structure of the absolute, it abides at the foundation of all the other, lower ontological levels of reality, predetermining the order of the universe, its logic, its bearings, its character. From the standpoint of the symbolism of metaphysical numbers, this Trinity pertains to the metaphysical zero and radically precedes not only the metaphysical first pair but the metaphysical one. It is not built up by the addition or the tripling of the one. On the contrary: it is the impulse toward the arising of the one, which in its turn precedes all manifestation.

Metaphysics holds that the process of manifestation begins with the splitting of the possibility of manifestation (pure being) into two metaphysical components — the “active” principle of manifestation and the “passive” principle of manifestation (in Hinduism, purusha and prakriti). The “active” principle is the possibility of manifestation in its self-identity; the “passive” is its ontological antipode. (Let us recall that from this point on we are located not only in the sphere of metaphysics but in the sphere of ontology, since the matter concerns modifications within being.) This ontological antipode bears different names in different traditions. In Hinduism it is mulaprakriti (“root prakriti”); in Taoism, the feminine principle yin; in scholastic terminology, natura,20 “nature,” and so forth. If the possibility of manifestation is the metaphysical “limit” of the kenosis of the absolute itself, then natura is the ontological “limit” of the kenosis of pure being, of the possibility of manifestation. In the Aristotelian tradition this principle is called materia prima,21 “first matter.”

The first matter of manifestation is an ontological principle, the substantial foundation of the universe, and therefore it remains itself outside manifestation, figuring in manifestation only indirectly, through its derivatives. Hence the actualization of the passive principle of manifestation does not yet inaugurate manifestation as such, but precedes it, forming the substantial precondition that complements the essential precondition embodied in the possibility of manifestation (pure being). First matter is sometimes regarded as the metaphysical number two — although, strictly speaking, neither the metaphysical one nor the metaphysical two belongs to manifestation: they are modifications of “potential numbers,” the strict division between which is not actual or clearly marked. These are special metaphysical numbers having no analogies whatever in manifested reality. Neither one (the affirmed metaphysical zero) nor two (the substance of manifestation) figures in the manifested as a fixable category. They acquire true ontological scope in the manifested only after the appearance of the immanent third.

All varieties of metaphysical doctrine know of this special instance lying between the absolute in itself — between the purely transcendent realities, including the possibility of being — and what is properly the manifested world. This intermediate, mediating instance is most often symbolized by the feminine principle. The conception is developed in greatest detail in the Hindu doctrine of prakriti and in the Kabbalistic conception of the Shekhinah, the “feminine” hypostasis of the Godhead. At first glance the Christian doctrine lacks this link, and the process of manifestation preceding the creation of the world proper is nowhere considered in it. The most diverse mystical and Gnostic currents attempted to overcome this insufficiency, elaborating theories of “Sophia,”22 the feminine aspect of the Godhead serving as the intermediate element. But with astonishing constancy all such views were rejected by the Church and pronounced heretical.

The only theological conception accepted as orthodox Orthodox ecclesiastical teaching was the hesychast theory developed by the venerated Orthodox saint Gregory Palamas. It received the name of the “doctrine of the divine energies.”23 According to Palamas, “in God there are three distinct notions: essence, energies, and the Divine Hypostases of the Trinity.”24 The essence pertains to the apophatic, absolutely transcendent side of the Godhead — to the sphere of the Super-Godhead of the Areopagitica. The Hypostases are present both within the very nature of the Godhead and, in relation to creation, in the distinction of the economic functions. This corresponds to the Orthodox doctrine formulated back in the epoch of the Ecumenical Councils. But the greatest significance in Palamas’s mystical theology belongs to the “energies.” It is precisely they that describe the ontological interval that interests us here: the interval between God in himself and his creation.

Palamas writes:

In God there exist not only hypostatic distinctions but certain others as well; and Dionysius calls this other distinction, in contrast to the hypostatic, divine, for the distinction according to Hypostases is not a division of the Godhead. According to these divine manifestations and energies, he says, God is multiplied and increased; he calls these same manifestations also goings-forth (literally, “ecstasies”).25

Elsewhere: “energy is something other than essence, distinct from it, yet inseparable.”26 “God has also that which is not essence. But this does not mean that what is not essence is an accident. It is not an accident, for it is wholly unchangeable; yet it is not essence, for it is not self-subsistent being.”27 And again: “God has, therefore, both that which is essence and that which is not essence, though it is not called an accident — namely, the divine will and energy.”28

Will, energy, providence, the counsel of the three persons, going-forth, participations — all this, according to the theological teaching of Palamas incorporated into the Synodal Tomos of 1351, forms a special sphere of divine being turned toward the creature: not, however, according to the classical creationist scheme of the relation of Creator to creature through law, but through the gracious, luminous, sacrificial power of divine Love.

Palamas emphasizes that all the energies forming the fabric of the luminous manifestation are not manifestations of any single separate hypostasis of the Godhead and are not differentiated according to the hypostases. They are common to the entire Trinity and express its unity outwardly. This is unmultiplied multiplicity, non-quantitative fullness, the gracious increase of the Godhead pouring out its abundance.

In metaphysical terms this may be called the pre-creational manifestation of being, its self-disclosure.

This outpouring of divine Love or will, this supra-creational going-forth of the Godhead, is the Christian analogue of those metaphysical realities which other traditions posit between the absolute and the manifested world.

It would be forced to identify this luminous reality — which the hesychasts identified with the light of the Transfiguration on Mount Tabor — with the feminine aspect of the deity, or with a passive receptive principle strictly analogous to prakriti or the Shekhinah. Yet at the same time it is precisely this level that is the threshold of the super-ontological Trinity’s passage into being: its sacrificial ontologization, its self-manifestation and self-disclosure. And although what is at issue is the single action of all three persons, one cannot fail to recognize in it the features of a certain triplicity, inherited from the very transcendent structure of the absolute — not as a reproduction of the hypostases, still less as the addition of a new, “fourth” hypostasis, but as the good news of the Triunity.

This is the descent of the super-ontological absolute into being: of the Super-Godhead into the Godhead.

Apparatus · Chapters I–IV

Apparatus

Part I · Chapter V

Between Manifestation and Creation

Между проявлением и творением

After the ontologization of the triadic absolute — that is, after the first step toward manifestation — there follows the remaining logical chain of economic descents. In Christianity this chain of descents is identified with “creation.”

Here it must be noted at once that the theory of “creation” — “creationism” — is a specifically Judaic idea, absent from all the non-Abrahamic traditions. Christianity and Islam drew it precisely from Judaism, and this became the distinguishing feature of what are customarily called “religions” proper, or “monotheistic traditions.” This does not mean that the other traditions fail to consider the further processes of manifestation once the ontological principles are affirmed (such a supposition would sound absurd on its face). What they lack, rather, is the strictly fixed barrier between the world of ontological principles and the manifested itself (that is, the universe) — the barrier that divides them in the creationist religions.

The conception of creation strictly divides being into two unequal, asymmetrical parts: the uncreated world, which is the Godhead proper (the metaphysical and ontological principles), and the created world, the product of the Godhead’s radical estrangement from itself (the manifested). More than this, creationism maintains that between these two categories there is no common measure whatsoever. Even the best of the creature — heaven, paradise, the angels, and so on — is wholly incommensurable with God and not consubstantial with him. God figures in such a situation as something absolutely external to creation: a closed, unknowable, and utterly other object.

In its most finished variant (Judaism, Islam) the creationist perspective excludes any concretization whatsoever of the structure of the Godhead (still more of the absolute), and insists on a radical, impenetrable, strict monotheism. Christianity is, without question, an atypical monotheism, since it affirms the triadic structure of the Godhead. Yet at the same time the Orthodox tradition unreservedly accepts the theory of creation borrowed from Judaism, and builds many of its dogmas precisely on the creationist understanding of reality.

Since it is Christian metaphysics that interests us, we are obliged to consider the process of manifestation in the creationist perspective, drawing when necessary, for clarity’s sake, on the metaphysical theories of the non-creationist kind.

Creationism excludes “creation ex deo” — the essential passage of the principles into the fabric of the manifested universe — and insists on “creation ex nihilo”: the bringing of “nothing” into existence by means of the divine act of will, the fiat, “let there be.” It is in the “nihilistic” (ex nihilo) nature of creation that the sharp boundary lies which separates the universe from its Maker. The “nothing” which all creation essentially is can under no conditions pass qualitatively into “something”; accordingly, the strictly creationist model holds that the creature receives existence from outside, from God, “on loan,” and sooner or later will be compelled to give it back — becoming “nothing” once more. The fullest expression of this “pessimistic” approach to the creature is Judaic theology, where from the formula given in the second book of Maccabees — “Look upon heaven and earth, and seeing all that is in them, know that God created all of it out of nothing”29 — the most radical conclusion has been drawn concerning the creature’s prospect. Strict creationist doctrine, in contrast to all other metaphysical teachings, holds that the substantial foundation of creation is pure “nothing” (ouk on), radically distinct both from all the ontological principles and from transcendent non-being — which in Greek even bears another name, me on — whereas the “nothing” of the Bible on which creation rests is designated by the formula ouk on (shema, in Hebrew).

The Christian tradition, in the cosmogonic sense, accepted the creationist Judaic perspective and thereby canonized the “nihilistic,” “nugatory” foundation of the whole creature. And yet the process of creation, described in Christian terms, differs essentially from the Judaic optic, since Christians regard this event (creation) as the “economic design of the Most Holy Trinity” and, more concretely, as the “kenosis of the Father,” the “economic kenosis of the Father.”30 Here once again the teleological essence of the Orthodox tradition discloses itself — the tradition that invests the notion of the “voluntary self-diminution of the Godhead,” the notion of “sacrifice,” with a particular metaphysical charge. In Christianity, creation ceases to be a simple act of Divine Omnipotence (as Judaism sees it) and becomes a further step in a providential, transcendent mystery rooted already in the pre-ontological absolute. For this reason the patristic tradition inclines toward a concretized description of the participation of the persons of the Most Holy Trinity in creation — thereby departing as far as possible from strict Judaism and approaching as nearly as possible the non-Abrahamic traditions, while remaining all the while within the bounds of creationism. In the final reckoning, Christian creationism has an entirely different meaning from the Judaic doctrine, since what it affirms is not the contingency but the saving necessity of creation ex nihilo, for the accomplishment of a secret plan conceived by the absolute.

The personified participation of the three persons in creation is not defined with dogmatic strictness. It is held that the principal role belongs to God the Father, whose kenotic sacrifice is the whole of creation. Metaphysically this is understandable, since in creation (manifestation) there is realized at the concrete level only what is contained in the First Principle. God the Father is the general “cause of things created” (Basil the Great).

God the Son — the Word, the Logos — answers for the spiritual-luminous order of creation: for the affirmation within it of the hierarchical structure that binds the worlds to one another and points them toward the supremacy of the supra-creational energies, giving to every creature, being, and thing its significance, its meaning, and its spiritual life. God the Word is the measure of things and the axis of the universe (“by whom all things were made”). He is at the same time “the fashioning cause” (Basil the Great). The kenosis of God the Word in creation consists in this: that He exteriorizes his qualities, turning them not toward his own center, not toward the Father, but away from Him — sacrificing himself for the creature’s sake. Hence the Son is often designated in the Christian tradition by the name “the Lamb slain before all ages.”

God the Holy Spirit figures as the “completer,” “the perfecting cause” (Basil the Great). His participation in creation is enigmatic, like everything connected with this person. He is present everywhere and in everything, being the most immanent aspect of the Godhead. In contrast to the singleness (and only-begottenness) of the Son, He is capable of being multiple while preserving the essential unity of his nature and of his person. Guénon (on another occasion, it is true) wrote of a special spiritual “integrating multiplicity” which, “addressing itself to the various beings and things individually, unites them, drawing them toward the luminous center.”31

In a similar manner the “economy” of the Holy Spirit is described by the Fathers of the Church when they treat of Pentecost, the division of tongues, and the personalization of the Holy Spirit in Christian baptism.32

Had Orthodoxy not affirmed a strict creationism, and thereby not erected an insurmountable barrier between the Divine and the created, one might have discerned in the Holy Spirit’s role with respect to creation the idea of his transfiguration into the very substance of the creature — into natura, or even into matter. And in that case his “kenosis” would have been the final element of the trinitarian sacrifice: creating out of the Divine fullness of the ontological principles the structure of the manifested, concretizing at the last frontier of the external the fullness of the divine goings-forth. But in that case we should have to speak of a “manifestationist” perspective, not a creationist one. In creationism, what serves as the base of creation is precisely “nothing” — shema, ouk on — and notwithstanding the likeness of metaphysical and ontological characteristics between the third person of the Trinity in Christianity and the substance of creation in other traditions, their identity cannot be affirmed without stepping outside the bounds of Orthodoxy. One may point only to this: that the Holy Spirit in creation economically and kenotically “patronizes” the substantial aspects, secures the continuity of the created planes, serves as conductor between earth and heaven, herald of the highest supra-creational decrees of the Godhead. It is no accident that he “spake by the prophets,” as the Orthodox Symbol of Faith defines it.

Be that as it may, the Christian trinitarian view affirms a clear correlation of the second and third persons of the Most Holy Trinity in the matter of creation. God the Word “answers” for its principial, highest, archetypal, essential aspects; God the Holy Spirit for the concrete, lower, substantial ones.33

It is also worth remarking that those Christian thinkers who inclined most of all toward the “manifestationist” perspective (insofar as that was compatible with the acknowledgment of Orthodox creationism) often tended to present creation as the “concretization” of the divine uncreated energies “flowing forth” from the Trinity without touching its self-identity. One may cite here the first passage of Dionysius the Areopagite’s treatise On the Celestial Hierarchy, where this idea is expressed with utmost clarity:

“Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, and cometh down from the Father of lights, with whom is no variableness, neither shadow of turning” (James I:17). Let us add that when, at the Father’s bidding, the Light goes forth from itself in order to spread abroad and to illumine us all with its precious gifts, it is precisely this Light, as a unifying power, that restores us to our primordial condition, raising us upward; it is precisely this Light that transforms us into the One and into the deifying simplicity of the Father who gathers — for, according to Holy Scripture, “of him, and through him, and in him are all things” (Rom. XI:36).34

Attention should be paid to the expressions “transforms us into the One,” “restores us to our primordial condition,” and the like — expressions wholly unthinkable in a strictly monotheistic creationist approach, since no transformation of the “creature” into the One (God) is possible there, and “return to the primordial condition” could only mean return to “nothing.”

The theories of Dionysius the Areopagite stand at the very edge of strict creationism and hint at the possibility of an essential drawing-together of the created aspects with the energies of the Trinity.35 Wherever such an approach is practiced, we are dealing with a Christian (more exactly, a “Helleno-Christian”) manifestationism, close to the esotericism of other traditions and corresponding to the theories of manifestation in the non-Abrahamic versions of metaphysics (in Vedanta, for instance, or Neoplatonism). It may be observed that in Judaism itself, and in Islam, there exists an esoteric line that likewise overcomes the creationist approach. In Judaism this is the Kabbalah, where the four levels of reality, or four levels of manifestation — atzilut, beriah, yetzirah, and assiyah — are treated as stages of manifestation; even beriah, literally “the world of creation,” is taken entirely apart from the creationist, strictly Judaic optic of ex nihilo.36

So too in Islam, where at the esoteric level, in Sufism, the doctrine of creation is in effect overcome. The most vivid examples of such esoteric Islam are the teaching of Ibn ʿArabi and the “theosophy” of Suhrawardi.

The Christian tradition’s recourse to the theory of creation cannot be assessed merely as the adaptation of an originally initiatic doctrine to the level of mass consciousness — incapable, by definition, of forming any notion of God except as an object external to the creature and having no common measure with it. There is in this something more mysterious and more profound, which cannot be grasped within the frame of Christian manifestationism. The creationist theory was introduced into the dogmatic context of Christianity not for pragmatic reasons alone. Christian metaphysics has its own understanding of creation, construing it in a particular teleological key proper to this tradition and to no other.

We shall return to this theme in greater detail when we come to consider the metaphysics of the Incarnation.

Part I · Chapter VI

“Bereshit bara Elohim”

«Берешит бара Элохим»

Let us pass directly to the mechanics of creation as Christianity understands them. In the non-creationist (and correspondingly non-Christian) perspective, creation is identified with manifestation in its actual — not its principial (as with the three ontological principles) — reality. In this version of metaphysics, the division of the principle of manifestation (pure being) into two components (active and passive, masculine and feminine, purusha and prakriti) is followed by the many-planed organization of the various levels of the manifested.37

From the correlation — the mutual contact — of the active principle of manifestation with the passive there appears a third instance: the first-intellect. In the Neoplatonic tradition, and likewise in Islamic, Hindu, and Mazdean esotericism, it is most often named the “logos,” or by some analogue of that term. Let us note at once that this manifestationist “logos” is, in the Orthodox optic, in no case identical with God the Word, who is a strictly transcendent person of the super-divine Trinity. The manifested celestial logos may be regarded only as an expression of the Son’s economy — as his function with respect to creation, his action, his hypostatic energy — but not as the Son himself, and not in any sense as a separate, self-subsistent person. In all that follows this must constantly be borne in mind, the more so as Christian creationism affirms the created nature of the spiritual heavens and of everything abiding in them, while God the Word is “begotten, not made.”

The first-intellect, manifestationistically understood, turning toward the substance or first matter “where” it appeared as “light in the darkness,” translates the “first matter” into “second matter” (the passive principle of manifestation), rendering it, too, manifested. Thus arises the manifested universe, woven of manifested substance differentially “pervaded” by the rays of manifested spirit. In this manifested universe there is an “above” and a “below,” a “heaven” and an “earth” — two poles, the first of which is the world of “formless manifestedness” (heaven), the second the world of “formed manifestedness” (earth, or water). Entering the manifested, the first-intellect becomes light, and substance becomes darkness. But this time the two notions — light and darkness — are not mere symbols of transcendent realities lying beyond being; they describe concrete spiritual realities (though ones very far removed from the light and darkness of the physical, human world). Between these poles the hierarchies of the worlds are ranged, each of which has two principal ontological dimensions — vertical and horizontal. The vertical of a concrete manifested world defines its distance from the poles, its place in the general hierarchy of the universe. The horizontal arises as the expansive development “outward” of those possibilities which are lodged in each point of the vertical axis established between “heaven” and “earth,” between light and darkness, between the first-intellect and substance. The biblical symbol of this axis may be taken to be “Jacob’s ladder,” on which myriads of beings move up and down.

The creationist doctrine of creation naturally describes the arising of the manifested universe otherwise. Heaven and earth appear here not as “ideo-variations,” the results of inner metamorphoses of the ontological principles, but as the product of a volitional extraction from nothing, ex nihilo, of the two basic aspects of the universe — which henceforth become the upper and lower limits of the creature, separating the “existing non-existent” (creation) from the “existing Existent” (God the Creator: “I am He Who Is,” Eheieh asher Eheieh, in the words of the Old Testament). Thus, in creating, God sets a boundary between Himself and what is not Himself; and that which is not He is enclosed in a “hermetic vessel,” whose top — heaven — concentrates in itself the most “luminous” and “simple” products of the divine workmanship, and whose “bottom” — earth — the most “dark” and “chaotically complex” of them. “And the earth was without form, and void; and darkness was upon the face of the deep.”38 “Without form, and void” (“invisible and unadorned”; tohu-va-bohu in the Hebrew) signifies the chaotic, shapeless character of manifested substance in its pure state. It is likewise the darkness; and the “deep” beneath it is the nothing concealed under the lower boundary of the universe. At the same time both heaven and earth in their primordial state are called “waters,” since the Creator had not yet established in them a strictly hierarchical order; and His Spirit encompassed the first-creation from without — “moved upon the face of the waters.”39 Further on, heaven in creation is concretized still more, becoming the light which God divides from the darkness. In this formula is underscored the final and strict separation of heaven and earth, of above and below. The positivity of heaven and of light is confirmed in addition by the phrase: “And God saw the light, that it was good.”40 So is described the First Day of creation — principial creation — in biblical, creationist terms. The descriptions of the cosmogonic process in the creationist and non-creationist perspectives differ essentially, though they treat common elements of the manifested, which in both cases are necessarily polarized in the form of heaven and earth. It is for just this reason that the Bible says: “In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth” (Bereshit bara Elohim at-shemaim va at-eretz).41 The beginning of manifested existence must without fail be twofold, stretched between two opposite poles. It is held that on the First Day God created also the angels and the ministering spirits (that is, the population of heaven), which in the non-creationist perspective are regarded as rays of the first-intellect — its “emanations,” its celestial aspects.

In the manifestationist optic, the manifested is not strictly sundered from, or heterogeneous in essence with, the ontological principles. On the contrary: the connection discloses itself everywhere and in everything, and things and beings themselves have at all times the possibility of passing beyond the bounds of the manifested — moving along the vertical either toward the first-intellect and through it (“active perfection,” “liberation,” moksha), or toward the substance of manifestation and through it, into the bosom of first matter (“passive perfection”). By virtue of this openness, the non-creationist approach sees the manifested as the result of a constant, uninterrupted process of manifestation, and not as a single, unrepeatable event (as with creationism). The manifestationist perspective is akin to the esoteric Sufi theory of “perpetual creation,” since what is at issue here is an understanding of the universe as an essentially open, unclosed system. Hence heaven and earth in creationism and in manifestationism are different categories. In the first case they are the once-and-for-all established boundaries of a “closed system”; in the second, the transparent modalities of a single, though many-leveled, complex directly conjoined with the absolute. This relation is expressed with particular clarity in the Taoist tradition, where the absolute is designated by the term tao, “the way” — the metaphysical reality that penetrates all levels of both the manifested and the principial worlds.

Orthodoxy, in its orthodox aspect, inherits precisely the creationist approach. For Christians the God-Trinity creates only once; consequently heaven and earth present themselves to Christians as the limiting bounds of the created universe. In the question of cosmogony the Church stands unambiguously on the point of view of the Old Testament. Why — we shall see later.

Part I · Chapter VII

The Division of the Waters

Разделение вод

The next stage in the organization of the manifested is the division of the universe into three principal planes, corresponding to the three basic states of the cosmic medium. Hinduism calls this the tribhuvana, the “three worlds.”

The first world is the world of heaven, of spirit (svar, in Sanskrit).

The second world is the world of soul, the atmosphere (bhuvas, in Sanskrit). This is the intermediate state between heaven and earth.

The third world is the world of body, of earth (bhur, in Sanskrit).42

This structure of the universe is acknowledged not only by Hinduism or the other manifestationist doctrines but by all traditions without exception. The same threefold division of creation belongs also to the Judaic and Christian cosmologies.

The Old Testament describes the beginning of the threefold crystallization of creation on the Second Day: “And God said, Let there be a firmament in the midst of the waters, and let it divide the waters from the waters.”43 The firmament is heaven in a more restricted sense than before. It signifies the boundary between the first world and the remaining two. What is at issue is the partition of manifested substance (the waters) into two unequal halves.

The first half is the world of pure light,44 where all essences — angels, archangels, seraphim, cherubim, and the rest — have no form and are therefore not strictly separate from one another. These are aspects of the celestial light which “is good.” Tradition calls this world the “celestial waters” or the “upper waters.” To this level the notion of the “upper waters” and the notion of the “celestial fire” apply equally. In accordance with traditional cosmology, “beneath the firmament” of the visible sky this world manifests itself mediately: through the light of the fiery luminaries (sun, moon, stars) and through rain. And both these symbolic aspects are joined in the image of the thunderstorm, where the fire of the lightning is accompanied by streams of celestial moisture.45

The second half unites the worlds of atmosphere-soul and of earth-body. Here all beings and things have individual form, which differs between these worlds only in degree of density: the subtle forms — souls, the beings of the world of atmosphere — are more rarefied than the dense forms, the bodies, the physical objects and things. These are the worlds of the “lower waters.”

On the boundary between the upper and lower waters (that is, on the firmament) there is a special instance from which all subtle forms proceed (some of them subsequently becoming dense forms as well). Here is accomplished the “fractioning” and individuation of beings, the “birth of souls,” after which they participate fully in the existence that runs its course according to the laws of the “lower waters.”46 Hinduism calls this instance jivaghana, the “synthesis of souls,” or hiranyagarbha, the “golden embryo.”47 It is often symbolized by an egg, of which one (the upper) half is the celestial firmament and the other (the lower) the bowl of the cosmos filled with the “lower waters.” In the firmament are concentrated all the “soul-related,” “formal” possibilities of individual existence itself, and hence the same cosmological instance is frequently depicted in the form of a circle. Moreover, since it is precisely the celestial firmament (or more exactly its rotation) that makes possible the measurement of temporal cycles, the “golden embryo” is often linked with the year, the completed cycle. Wherever in tradition we encounter the symbolism of the firmament, of the egg, or of the number 365 (the number of days in the year), it is most likely this reality that is in question.

In the biblical tradition there is an enigmatic personage — Enoch, son of Jared and father of Methuselah — of whom the Book of Genesis says: “And Enoch walked with God: and he was not; for God took him.”48 The number of Enoch’s years is exactly 365. Tradition holds that God took Enoch up to heaven, and that he did not die. The Kabbalists went so far as to identify Enoch with the highest of the angels, Metatron, “He Who Stands before the Throne of the Lord,” and with the king of the celestial world. This figure bears directly on the point of the “division of the waters,” since it is precisely through the celestial egg that individual beings, “souls,” can penetrate into the celestial reality, escaping death and the wandering of the “lower waters.” It is important that Enoch, together with the other biblical “immortal,” Elijah, is identified by Christian sacred tradition with the two witnesses of the Apocalypse,49 and consequently both bear directly on the Incarnation of the Son. The firmament, as the seat of the stars and constellations, is linked in its turn with the source of the “sidereal bodies” — that is, with the substance of which the “subtle forms” of individuals are woven. Primordial traditional astrology was founded precisely on a clear awareness of the connection between the configuration of the stars of the celestial firmament, the laws of cyclical time, and the substance of the individual soul.

The “division of the waters” bears directly also on what the Apocalypse calls the “second death.” This enigmatic expression is often left without adequately intelligible interpretation, though its meaning in cosmology is exceedingly simple. The point is that the individual soul has strict limits to its existence in the universe. It is not immortal in the “incarnationist” sense (in the perspective of the non-Abrahamic doctrines), nor is it endowed with a guaranteed, unidirectional, “ray-like” immortality understood in the absolute, metaphysical sense.50 The soul — a subtle form woven of the substance of the atmosphere (more rarefied than the dense substance of the bodily world) — outlives the body in which it passed its earthly life, and can exist independently after bodily death. This bodily death is the “first death.” But the way to the heaven of spirit — into the world of the upper waters — is impossible for the individual soul,51 since that world, by definition, has no forms and admits no beings clothed in form. On the threshold of that world — that is, at the point of the “division of the waters” — the soul undergoes a process analogous to the death of a being at the bodily level: here the spirit is divided from the soul, as earlier the soul was divided from the body. This is the “second death.” The soul can escape this “second death” only in an exceptional case, as rare as the possibility of slipping past bodily death (at least two cases of physical immortality are described, let it be noted, in the Bible itself — the same Enoch and Elijah, taken up to heaven in the body). Theoretically, what is at issue is this: that in the process of metaphysical realization a being, entirely consciously and before natural death, “draws” the body into its soul, preserving that body at the principial level — as the “body of resurrection”; then the spirit52 does the same with the soul, likewise before the natural onset of the “second death.” One may say that the “absorption of the body by the soul” is the process inverse to the formation of earthly flesh from the lower waters in the cosmogony of creation. Thus the physical body returns to its primary “liquid,” soul-state: “earth becomes water.” This is the “first death.” If it begins while a man still lives — that is, in the course of the ascetic practice of the conscious and volitional “mortification of the flesh” — then the connection between the bodily and soul states, between “earth” and “water,” remains unbroken, and at the limit the final physical dying tells on the man not so sharply after all (whence the incorruption of the relics of the saints, and other phenomena conjoined with sanctity). The “second death” is the disclosure, within the “drop” of the lower waters (that is, the individual human soul), of the presence of another watery substance — the upper waters, identical with the celestial fire. Those ascetics who succeed in translating the lower waters into the upper, or the waters of the soul into the fire of the spirit, escape the fatality of the “second death,” accomplishing it beforehand in a conscious and volitional manner. As in the first case, a lucid and unbroken connection with the preceding soul-state is preserved here, and the “syncope of death” is overcome. The archetype of such initiatic realization in the Bible is the prophet Elijah, who accomplished the whole cycle of ascetic transformations of his being “from earth to fire” — from the dust of the flesh, through the lower waters (the calling down of rain on Mount Carmel), to the fiery upper waters (the chariot of fire).

The threefold articulation of the manifested is characteristic of all traditions. Here the divergences between creationism and manifestationism are far less significant than in the question of creation itself and its relation to its cause. The three-membered understanding of the universe belongs also to the Christian doctrine, which in this question is no exception and on the whole reproduces the Judaic understanding of cosmology. One may only add that in the strictly Christian perspective the division of creation into three basic levels reflects the archetype of the life-giving Trinity itself.

One may even trace certain correspondences between the principial Trinity and the structure of the created world.

The world of heaven — of pure spirit, light, and angelic beings without form — reflects on the created plane the economy of the first person (compare the opening of the chief Christian prayer: “Our Father, who art in heaven”). At the same time, heaven is the created antechamber of the Most Holy Trinity itself, which the angels glorify with the thrice-repeated “Holy! Holy! Holy!”

The world of the atmosphere, of soul, will then be correlated with the economic function of the Son as Christ and Savior. Not for nothing is He named “the Savior of human souls.” More exactly, what corresponds to Christ is that firmament where the division passes between the upper and the lower waters. Hence the “two witnesses” — Elijah and Enoch — are linked with him in the Apocalypse.

The world of earth, of matter, where the manifested finds its final formation, is correlated with the economic function of the Holy Spirit. Dionysius the Areopagite speaks of matter as an element “necessary for the completion of the universe”53 — and it is precisely the function of completion that is the special “economic” business of the third person of the Trinity. These correspondences uncover the seal of the Trinity, left by Her upon the structure of creation. And in the Christian tradition such correspondence is still more evident and well-founded than in the Judaic cosmogony, within which the coherence of the three-membered model of the universe was not always grasped unambiguously or in full. On the whole, the singling out of three worlds in the universe and the doctrine of the “division of the waters” accord with all the varieties of traditional interpretation of the world’s origin. And yet, by virtue of the “sacrificial” orientation of Orthodox metaphysics, by virtue of its “kenotic” specificity, the Christian tradition attends with a particular and rather rare solicitude precisely to the lowest aspects of the manifested — to the worlds of the lower waters, to the individual sphere — without, of course, in any way calling into question the priorities of the cosmogonic hierarchy. The saying that “the last shall be first” may be referred also to the manifested worlds, hinting at some special, paradoxical, and non-evident structure of value hidden beneath the covering of the ordinary, traditional cosmogony accepted by Christianity itself. This paradoxicality shows itself most vividly in the Orthodox dogma of the Incarnation: in the unexpected Good News that the transcendent God himself descended into creation — and into its lower regions at that — and became incarnate at the very bottom of the lower waters, in a human body.

By this, too, is predetermined the accent placed in Christianity on the mystery of the resurrection in the flesh at the moment of the Last Judgment at the end of the world. In every case, the flesh — hierarchically the lowest thing in the whole universe — possesses here some special, exceedingly important, and far from evident meaning: a deep significance reaching far beyond the bounds of that relative and contentless cosmological instance which the world of earth, of flesh, of dust appears to be in the other traditions.

Apparatus · Chapters V–VII

Apparatus

Basil the Great. The three “causes” — originating, fashioning, perfecting — cite On the Holy Spirit XVI.38 (προκαταρκτικὴ, δημιουργικὴ, τελειωτικὴ αἰτία), which Dugin gives in Russian dogmatic wording.

Part I · Chapter VIII

The Freedom of the Creature
and the Choice of the Angels

Свобода твари и выбор ангелов

Creationist cosmogony contains one exceedingly interesting point, practically absent from the majority of manifestationist traditions. It is bound up with the “metaphysics of evil” and the problem of freedom. Since this question has great importance for the Orthodox doctrine, let us dwell on it in more detail.

The understanding of creation as the radical estrangement of the creature from the Creator, as the single, once-for-all generation of the universe out of nothing, leads to the logical conclusion that the creature is endowed with a special quality: freedom. This quality cannot fully belong to the manifested in the manifestationist perspective, since there what is at issue is merely the concealment of the principle beneath the veil of maya, “illusion” — and consequently, if a manifested being follows the principle, it does so by virtue of a vividly felt connection with the principle itself; and if it does not follow it, this means only that the connection has weakened and the being’s share of illusoriness has grown. In any case, being uncreated, every manifested being is the result and the playing-field (lila, in Sanskrit) of the ontological principles. Though such a being possesses a certain share of responsibility, that responsibility is never absolute, and rests on the purely negative fact of ignorance (avidya) concerning the true nature of reality. In manifestationist doctrine there is no problem of choice; there is a problem of knowledge. The principle is not divided from the manifested by an impassable frontier, as in creationism, and therefore a manifested being, in choosing its essential orientation, is guided not by a risk-laden act of will founded on faith, but by the degree of its own gnoseological development (and at times by the prehistory of its pre-bodily existence). Hence in manifestationism the problem of evil has never stood in earnest; and what is nonetheless accounted evil there is, in the last reckoning, simple ignorance.

Creationism presupposes in the creature a freedom limited by practically nothing, and the conditions of creation out of nothing are such that the Creator cannot directly influence the creature’s orientation. In this, as the Christian theologians said, lies the “risk of the Godhead” in accomplishing creation. The creature, for its part, is at liberty to determine its own relation to the Creator, guided by inner considerations alone. And from this evil is born — as a volitional choice of the creature contradicting the affirmation of that relation between Creator and creature which the creationists hold to be the only right one and, accordingly, good. Clearly, the “good” in such a perspective is the created being’s volitional faith in the One who created it, and the acknowledgment of its own principial otherness, secondariness, and incommensurability with the Creator. The creature, as it were, acknowledges of its own will its own “nugatory” origin, and by a reverse logic glorifies the invisible and unknown Creator as Deus Absconditus. The creature that makes the opposite choice — that does not acknowledge its “nothingness” and does not glorify the Creator as absolutely other than itself — by that very choice engenders evil as a special reality, existing solely at the expense of the free volition of the created. This is why the creationist theologians deny evil any ontological standing or independence whatsoever. The Creator, from their point of view, creates nothing evil; creation is good from the outset. But He does knowingly endow creation with freedom — and it is freedom that, in certain cases, engenders evil and brings it to existence. This is perfectly clear. It is merely curious that certain unorthodox thinkers answered this with a rather witty rejoinder: if God, in creating the creature free, thereby creates the preconditions for the arising of evil, is it not after all precisely He who is the (implicit and indirect) author of that evil, insofar as freedom itself is fraught with the wrong choice?54

Be that as it may, in creationism the problem of choice stands with extreme seriousness and predetermines the entire concrete structure of the universe, filling existence with significance and content. This problem arises from the very first moment of creation, on the First Day and at its very summit — in heaven. What is at issue is the “fall of the morning star.”

To grasp this crucial moment adequately, we must explain in more detail the relation between the celestial world of unformed essences (the angels) and the worlds of formal manifestedness, populated by individual beings possessed of form.

The relation between the formless angelic world (the region of the upper waters) and the two worlds of the lower waters (the bodily and the soul-world) consists in this: that the higher, celestial world lies on the far side not only of time but of every duration whatsoever. It is to the celestial reality that the designation “eternity” applies in its etymological sense — “that which encompasses all time, all ‘ages,’ and the whole compass of the other modes of duration.” This is why, with respect to the world of principles, Orthodoxy uses the term “pre-eternal”: that which was before the creation of the eternal, which exceeds the eternal, which is transcendent with respect to the whole of creation and to all the modes of its existence, successive or simultaneous. Creation itself is eternal, since all the kinds of duration are actual only within the frame of creation, while creation in itself surpasses them all. For the exact understanding of this relation the Greek theological language has the term aeon: the “eternity” of creation, radically distinct from the “pre-eternal” or “supra-eternal” world of the Godhead.

In the celestial, formless, angelic reality everything exists at once, synchronously. Celestial eternity is all time and all duration taken together. Events, things, and beings abide in that world in their archetypal state. Time in heaven is like space on earth — a fixed and permanent complex in which the trajectories of all beings and things and their paths are traced motionlessly and stably, paths which in the worlds of the lower waters unfold successively, one after another. To picture the angelic reality, one may liken it to a detached gaze upon the physical life of a man — with all its bodily and psychic transformations, deeds, events, and dreams, from birth (even from conception) to death — seen simultaneously, where, according to the concentration of the angelic attention, one may fix upon any segment regardless of how the man himself perceives it: as past, as future, or as present. This celestial reality is called in tradition the “Book of Life,” for in it is written the scenario of all the cycles of the bodily and soul-history of the universe together with all the beings that people it. Everything that has happened, is happening, and will happen in all the compartments of the worlds of the lower waters has already happened in the world of formless manifestedness. Or otherwise, in Guénon’s terms: the factual content of all the cycles of duration (including human time) is nothing other than the unfolding, in the form of succession, of what already exists in the form of simultaneity in heaven.

But in the creationist perspective heaven is the first thing created — and consequently it is endowed with that quality of freedom which characterizes creation in general.
