Hence the theme of the “fall of the angels,” which, according to tradition, took place already on the First Day of creation. Nor could it have been otherwise, since the First Day is the instant of the creation of the entire basic content of the universe in its archetypal state, and the remaining Days are but the concretization and detailing of the essential archetypes laid down in the creation of heaven and earth (or of the “waters not yet divided”) on the First Day. The First Day lasts eternally. It is the aeon, standing on the far side of all kinds of duration. There is nothing in creation that did not already exist in its archetypal state on the First Day, when heaven — the receptacle of all created possibilities, existing simultaneously — was made. Unhappily, this logical and natural traditional view of things is often lost sight of in theological constructions, where in place of a full ontological position we meet a purely temporal treatment of the universe — legitimate only for the narrow, physical, strictly human perception of reality (and that only after the fall).

On the First Day, in the first instant of creation, the division of the celestial archetypes into two groups takes place. This division is the result of the “freedom” given to the creature by the Creator. The whole fullness of freedom is realized precisely in heaven and precisely in that first instant. One part of the angels — the luminous, intelligent, eternal essences — affirms its “nugatory” created nature, hymning the other, uncreated God. This is the “right side”: “the myriad at Thy right hand,” as the Ninetieth Psalm says. These angels insist on the heterogeneity, the difference in essence, between their own nature (which is, in the last reckoning, “nothing”) and the transcendent nature of the Godhead, with which they can never merge under any circumstances. So, and only so, does full and finished creationism understand the good. The second part (the lesser — one third, or one tenth, in the various sources) of all the angels refuses to acknowledge its created nature and, accordingly, its “nothingness,” and affirms the consubstantiality of its own nature with the nature of God. These angels apply to themselves, in place of the term “servants of God,” the term “sons of God” (Bne Elohim, in Hebrew), and “fall” — take their stand on the left side, oshuiuiu: “A thousand shall fall at thy side, and ten thousand at thy right hand; but it shall not come nigh thee” (the Ninetieth Psalm). It is astonishing that the “rebel angels,” the “companions of the morning star, of Lucifer,” occupy in the metaphysical sense a strictly manifestationist position, and call into question the legitimacy of perceiving creation as creation. It now becomes entirely comprehensible why the creationist traditions — and Judaism first among them — reject all manifestationist teachings so unambiguously, and strictly identify the divine celestial personages figuring in those teachings with demons and evil spirits. This is the characteristic trait of every religious monotheism affirming an impassable wall between the creature and the Creator — who is called “one” precisely on the ground that he is radically other with respect to the created universe, so that no concretization of his inner nature, beyond the affirmation of his oneness, is possible from the creature’s side. Let us note in passing that Christian trinitarianism occupies a most peculiar position with respect to the strictly monotheistic tendency — and it was on just this ground that Judaic and Islamic theologians built their critique of Christianity. For the Jews the teaching of Christ was a plain refutation of the creationist doctrine, and therefore, remaining within the bounds of metaphysically consistent Judaism, they could acknowledge Christ only as a false messiah or, at best, a “prophet” (as certain Judeo-Christian sects did).

Thus, in the world of the aeon, in motionless eternity, once and for all, the division of the angels takes place — a division co-eternal with creation itself. This is the fundamental archetype of the “Book of Life,” repeated with indefinitely great variety on all the lower levels of reality. The whole freedom of the creature is concentrated precisely in the world of heaven, where the principial choice between creationist good and creationist evil has its purest and simplest structure: two camps of angels, the “Yahwists” and the “Luciferites.”55 The Kabbalah calls them the “powers of the right side and of the left.” All other beings — products of the concretization of the remaining Five Days of creation — are free “not directly” and “not purely,” but according to the measure of their participation in the angelic choice; for only that choice is posed in essential, principial terms, while all the other “either-ors” are secondary and derivative, hierarchically nearer to or farther from the instance of the eternal “first freedom.” This is precisely why the biblical account of the mechanism of the fall includes the “tempter,” the “ancient serpent”; and why man, created on the Sixth Day, is drawn into the element of freedom not immediately, but through the model of choosing one of the poles of the angelo-demonic dualism — a choice which in this case ended with Adam’s acceptance of the non-creationist approach to reality. The “serpent” opens before Eve the prospect of “deification,” hinting at the consubstantiality of creature (man) and Creator. The “serpent,” let it be noted, voices a strictly Advaita-Vedantist point of view on creation-manifestation.

The consequences are well known. The creationist, Judaic approach punishes both the “serpent” and man for the very thing for which, in Hinduism, in Taoism, in Zoroastrianism, or in Zen Buddhism, both would only have been praised. Be that as it may, it is important to understand clearly that the realization of the freedom of the whole creature — its choice — passes of necessity through the aeonic reality; and consequently freedom itself, within the frame of formal manifestation (in the worlds of the lower waters), is predetermined in advance by the celestial first-choice of the angels. This is why one may say, with Jacob Boehme, that “man stands on the knife-edge between hell and paradise” — that is, between two modalities of eternity, one of which unites the “good angels” and all the beings participating directly or indirectly in their eternal choice, the other all the “evil angels,” all who have “risen up” against the creationist dogma of radical monotheism. And this is the normal cosmological dualism of the universe, which lies at the foundation of traditional exotericism — without in any way calling into question the rightness of the monotheistic orientation. More than that: strict theological monotheism is itself the source of an equally strict cosmological dualism, born as the necessary and inevitable consequence of freedom — the essential attribute of the creature in creationist doctrine.

Here the feeling may arise that the aeonic choice of the angels, eternally in progress, by virtue of its archetypal and supreme position in the universe, predetermines the fate of all beings belonging to the sphere of the lower waters — and that, consequently, no freedom of these beings outside the celestial reality simply exists. Such a “determinist” approach is proper to certain heretical teachings within the frame of creationism, inspired in one degree or another by the Zoroastrian doctrine transferred into a creationist context. Indeed, in Zoroastrianism — and especially in Manichaeism and Bogomilism — it is clearly affirmed that men possess a deterministically dual nature (“sons of light” and “sons of darkness”), which is the consequence of the incarnation, in the human shell, of one of the two primal principles — Ormuzd and Ahriman — dispersed, in the world of formal multiplicity, into two groups of individuals. The late Zoroastrian magi affirmed such a division not only among men but among animals, plants, and even stones, part of which were held to be a manifestation of Ormuzd, part of Ahriman.56

Full creationism, however, does not fall into this dualistic extreme. It insists that the angelic choice is not, strictly speaking, the content of the individual’s fate in its concreteness as determined in advance by heaven. If the deeds and personal events of the individual are celestially predetermined, and the beings of the lower waters truly have no real freedom in that regard, there remains to them a special, vertical vector, independent of the fabric of fate, within which the full and free choice is accomplished. A being chooses paradise or hell not by deeds or by states, but by a special, “angelic,” secretly present “organ” — the “free spirit of the heart,” subject to no determinism. This is precisely why man is called in theology “equal to the angels.” The Orthodox formula of prayer speaks of this plainly: “Let faith be imputed to me in place of works, O my God, for Thou shalt find no works that could justify me.” And further: “But let that faith of mine suffice in place of all…” What is here called faith is precisely the exercise of the freedom which every being carries in its secret center.57

At the level of the archetype, in the spiritual heavens, everything has already occurred and everything has already come to pass; all beings have “been born, lived, and died.” But this does not mean that their spiritual freedom was thereby curtailed. The turn toward the vertical choice is possible always, at any stage of formal existence — before death and after it. Though, to be sure, the medium of manifestation, and each cosmic state, is always defined by whether it is easy or hard, within it, to break through to the purity of the angelic problematic. On the lowest levels of existence the very difference between hell and paradise is so far effaced that to reach this primordial spiritual problem can be exceedingly difficult (though never impossible).

The Orthodox tradition inherits from Judaism the creationist model of understanding creation, of freedom within it, of the angelic choice, of the mechanism of the fall, and so on. The introduction of the “Old Testament” into the Orthodox canon points unambiguously to Christianity’s succession to the creationist perspective as a whole. Which means that all cosmogonic problems, valuations, and correspondences are resolved in Christianity on the basis of the logic we have tried to explain, in general outline, in this chapter. Keeping in view the proportions between the celestial reality of the upper waters and the formal reality of individual existence will make it easy to explain many obscure and, at first glance, dark places of the Orthodox tradition where questions of responsibility, morality, freedom, choice, and the like are examined.

But it may be said at once that, in distinction from Judaism and Islam, Christian creationism is charged with an entirely specific quality, the close investigation of which can lead to paradoxical conclusions and sharply alter all the proportions. Inheriting from Judaism the theme of the choice of the angels, Christianity gives it, in the end, a wholly particular, “revolutionary,” and unexpected resolution — conjoined with the unique mission of the Virgin Mary.58

Part I · Chapter IX

The Adam of Paradise and the Fallen Adam

Райский Адам и падший Адам

On the Sixth Day of creation — that is, on the last Day of divine activity (which must be understood as the completion of the concretization of the created universe) — man was made: the Old Adam.59 His position in the cosmos was privileged. He was an angel60 projected onto the very bottom of creation: a mediator between heaven and earth, an axis joining the worlds of formless angelic thoughts with all the worlds of forms, down to the bodily. He was made from dust, from “red clay” (adama, in Hebrew). He was the “substitute” of the Creator within creation. The likeness to the Creator is sealed in his being made “after the image”;61 the difference, in his being made “after the likeness.”62

The Old Adam is sometimes regarded as “surpassing the angels,” on the ground that he possesses a concrete projection into the worlds of formal manifestation — being, in a certain sense, an angel who has totalized in himself all the possibilities of creation, including the lowest. This superiority, however, adds nothing whatever to him from the standpoint of the spiritual, celestial dimension proper, which simply coincides with the angelic, luminous quality embodied in the human spirit. If the angels answer for the whole of creation in the archetypal sense, the Old Adam answers for it in the sense of the concretization of all the possible levels of creation. That is why he is created on the last Day, and not on the First (like the angels).

The Old Adam is the universal all-man: the axial string stretched through the worlds ranged between heaven and earth. He serves as the prototype of the concretization of all the objects and beings that fill the universe. He is the synthesis of thinking and unthinking creatures. In a certain sense he “stretches” toward the bodily bottom of the universe that point of the “division of the waters” on the celestial firmament where the “synthesis of souls,” the “golden embryo,” resides.63 The Old Adam is the projection of that point onto the lower plane. His constitution is the completion of the celestial, angelic possibilities of the manifested (the spirit of Adam) by the soul-possibilities of the “subtle world” (the life, or soul, of Adam) and the bodily possibilities of the “dense world” (the body of Adam, of dust). In the beginning Adam is an androgyne;64 but later the “life” of Adam acquires an independent personification in the form of his wife, Eve — whose name in Hebrew means precisely “life.”

Adam in his archetypal state is co-eternal with creation, just as paradise and hell are, and heaven and earth. He is not an individual, but the synthesis of all individuals. He does not possess a separate body, but is the synthesis of all individual bodies and physical objects. The Old Adam is the actualization of angelic eternity in the worlds of forms; and therefore he himself stands, in a certain manner, on the far side of forms, being at once their source and their sufficient ground. Adam is the source of time and space, and of the other forms of duration, which receive their created existence precisely through him.

Being the source of space and time, the Old Adam both enters time and space and does not enter them. In his pure form he abides eternally in the earthly paradise, where all the possibilities of the bodily metamorphoses of the earthly world are concentrated, and where the world of dense forms touches the world of subtle forms.65

Paradise, marked by vegetal symbolism, is also the source of “embodiments” — of the passage of subtle forms, souls, into dense forms, bodies. The seeds of the plants of paradise are human souls, understood as aspects of the soul of the Old Adam; their stems and roots are bodily garments. Yet on the other hand, remaining essentially outside time, he pours his possibilities “downward,” into the concreteness of existence which he himself has prefigured — conditioned, in the bodily world, by the spatio-temporal continuum, and in the soul-world by another, analogous continual modality.

The beginning of cyclical time and of physical space is symbolized in the Bible by the “fall.” This is the moment of the Old Adam’s entry into history — though only a particular aspect of him is touched by it; essentially he remains at the center of creation. The cause of the “fall” is explained in the Bible as the result of the Old Adam’s inclusion in the problematic of the angelic choice. The “serpent” (the “leader of the rebel angels”) draws Adam, through his hypostasized soul (Eve), into the metaphysical choice concerning Creator and creature. Adam, as an integral spirit-soul-body being, is made a partaker of created freedom precisely through Eve (the soul), who in the hierarchy stands nearer to the celestial reality than the earthly world of bodies. The “serpent,” Eve, and Adam himself, in the account of the fall, are as it were three levels of one and the same principial being, partaking — in the concreteness of the Sixth Day — of the problem of the archetypal choice of the First.

At this moment the static character of the principially completed Creation acquires a dynamic character, for beginning from the highest causal reality a dualism is disclosed all the way down — a dualism incompatible with the neutrally affirmative fullness of integral manifestedness. In place of the single Tree of Life there arises the twofold Tree of Good and Evil. The splitting of the spiritual reality into two angelic hosts is projected into the world of forms, brings polarization into it, and immediately calls forth the dialectical development of all temporal and spatial possibilities. Adam himself is polarized and actualized, clothing himself in the “garments of skin” and quitting paradise.66

Thus there appear two Adams — the Adam of paradise and the fallen Adam. The first is principial and eternal; the second historical and concrete: Adam after the fall. Neither the one nor the other has any common measure with what we are accustomed to call “man” in the generally accepted sense, for what is at issue is two universal states of existence — one of which principially precedes every soul-related and spatio-temporal concretization of all the beings of the formal worlds (the Old Adam), while the second lies at the foundation of a concretization of more limited scope, proper to the dynamic process of cyclical history. The assertion of the “original sin” of all human individuals after the fall reflects the fact that the birth of any man in a determinate state of existence (in this case, in our earthly human world) is nothing other than the realization of a particular possibility predetermined by the general structure of the species; and this species is itself the precondition for the actualization of a determinate sector of the more general possibilities enclosed in the total scope of the Adamic archetype. Coming into the world after the fall, a man is by that fact the individualization not of the whole Adamic archetype but only of a particular modification of it — and a hierarchically lower one at that. This sector, the fallen Adam, is the source of all humanity from the beginning of history; consequently his quality of “fallenness” — of abiding on a lower and dialectically contradictory level of existence compared with the principial Adam of paradise — necessarily and in advance conditions all the particular manifestations of the archetype in the form of separate men. The original sin of every embodied soul consists in this: that it flows directly from an instance in advance more secondary and more limited than the primordial state of the Adam of paradise. But this “sin,” besides conditioning the inner quality of the soul, equally conditions the cosmic medium of human birth, which undergoes the same fall as all humanity. In other words, one may say that the fall of nature is the result of the fall of man, since the outer world depends on the inner far more than appears at first glance. Hence one may speak not only of two Adams — of paradise and fallen — but of two earthly worlds, a first and a second. The first earthly world is paradisal, potential, polar, and motionless. The second is non-paradisal, actual, many-vectored, and mobile.67

The fallen Adam is not eternal, unlike the Adam of paradise. He “lives” exactly as long as actual humanity in all its continuations and variations — as long as our earthly world.

Such an understanding of Adam throws light on the difference between the theories of the “circulation of souls” and of the singleness of embodiment. The first theory is characteristic of the manifestationist approach, which holds that the soul — the individual subtle form — has, before its embodiment in an earthly body (a dense form), an autonomous existence, and can be embodied and disembodied in other bodies (the orthodox formulation of this doctrine presupposes embodiments in different worlds, and excludes — contrary to “reincarnationism” — repeated manifestation in one and the same world, for example the world of earthly humanity). This point of view is held by Hinduism, by Mahayana Buddhism, by the Kabbalah, and by certain heretical versions of Christianity (Origen thought so as well). At the base of such an approach lies the derivation of the individual soul directly from the Adam of paradise — from his world-soul, taken apart from bodily form. In that case the soul, before entering a concrete body, may pass through other bodies in other worlds, since it is in the soul that the most essential aspect of individuality is enclosed. Entry into the field of the fallen Adam, and passage through his level, is for such a soul equivalent to the “forgetting” of its inner nature, its investiture in the “chain-mail of the elements” (in the figure of the Iranian esotericist Shihab al-Din Yahya Suhrawardi68) — the elements not only bodily but of the lower psyche as well. This view presupposes that the soul exists in bodiless form in two directions: in the past (before embodiment) and in the future (after disembodiment).

The second theory, proper to orthodox creationism, affirms that the soul undergoes individuation only at the moment of acquiring a body — and that it derives not from the Adam of paradise but from the fallen Adam, whose inner nature is already darkened and worsened in comparison with the paradisal. This soul, which becomes discrete only with the acquisition of a dense form, preserves its individual quality after the death of the body, but can no longer undergo embodiment, awaiting in the disembodied state the Last Judgment and the end of the world. This position corresponds precisely to strict creationism, which strives to project the fundamental rupture and irreversibility proper to creation in its primordial state onto all the subsequent levels of reality. Thus even within creation, at the very bottom of its concreteness, the processes of the severance of cause from effect acquire an irreversible (and not, as in manifestationism, a cyclical) character. The Adam of paradise, after the fall, cannot in this perspective be realized immediately, since his metamorphosis into the fallen Adam is an irreversible historical fact, annulled under no conditions.69

This difference between the two theories corresponds on the whole to the manifestationist and creationist points of view, since manifestationism treats (in this case) the anthropological problem as an open system, and creationism as a closed one. Hence, too, the duality in the understanding of the quality of time: the manifestationists insist on the cyclical conception of time, the creationists on the unidirectional and irreversible.

The creationist approach to the theme of the “two Adams” takes its most restrictive forms in Judaism, where the individual is placed in such dependence on the bodily-discrete that certain of the most consistent versions of Judaism (the Sadducees and Pharisees) deny outright the existence of the soul after the death of the body and, accordingly, the resurrection of the dead. The Adam of paradise will end together with the whole of creation, together with heaven and earth. The fallen Adam will exist within the frame of actual humanity — until the instant when it vanishes together with its specific earthly world.

The whole stretch of the historical existence of the fallen Adam, from the fall to the epoch of the mashiach, is divided into two parts.70 The first period belongs to the epoch preceding Moses’s reception of the Torah; the second to the epoch that follows that event. Of the first period — the “order of the forefathers” — tradition speaks rather obscurely, though it must be supposed that this stretch of history stands in the same relation to the period of the Torah as that period itself stands to the messianic epoch. In other words, if the Torah anticipates and prefigures the mashiach, the period of the forefathers anticipates and prefigures, in its turn, the era of the law. Yet in the quality of the uncreated Godhead’s participation in the affairs of the world, from Adam to the messiah, both these periods — before Moses and after him — are qualitatively close, and are characterized by the common denominator of the Old Testament in the broadest meaning of that concept. Taken together this is one general epoch of the law, though before Moses no “written Torah” existed.71

Let us note that the Judaic Torah — law, covenant — is a specifically creationist concept, having nothing in common with the Sanskrit counterpart of this word, dharma, which designates the inner, essential law of being, coinciding with the very substance of the divine presence — not a supernatural code given from outside by way of revelation, like the law of Moses.

Creationism understands the law as an ethical imperative established in the universe by the will and work of the “good angels” who serve the transcendent One God. Such a law is an externally affirmed line of existence, the observance of which guarantees to beings conformity with the general order of creation — harmony and adequacy. The law changes nothing in the inner nature of the individual; it does not touch his essential quality, which is from the outset acknowledged to be “nugatory” (ex nihilo). Strict creationism excludes not only the possibility of angelo-realization — the transformation of the individual soul into supra-individual, supra-formal spirit (into a person), though within the frame of creation such a transformation is not, strictly speaking, anything impossible — but denies also the possibility of the soul’s potential return to paradise, and even, at the limit, of the soul’s surviving physical death. The law is obedience to an external principle whose essence, logic, and structure remain absolutely inscrutable and unapproachable. Only at the coming of the mashiach will the quality of the existence of men and of the outer world change; but Judaism underscores that the mashiach will not simply affirm a new, less estranged dimension in the universe — he will only lay bare the whole truth and justice of that line in the historical past which was oriented toward the observance of the law.72

The fallen Adam will not thereby become the Adam of paradise again. Simply, his “right side” — observance of the Torah — will affirm its superiority over the “left side,” non-observance. “The righteous shall shine forth as the sun”: the keeping of the law will, in the messianic epoch, be disclosed and affirmed as a universally acknowledged positive. And then will come the great Sabbath, the Seventh Day of creation.73

The fallen Adam, in the capacity of law or covenant, possesses in the last reckoning that answer of the “good angels” which consisted in the acknowledgment of their “nothingness.” This Adam remains free, like all the creature; but the “good angels,” serving the Creator, tirelessly prompt him with the “right answer,” the “correct choice,” which is gradually embodied in ever more external form until it becomes retrospectively self-evident to all. Then will come the “time of the mashiach,” the “anointed one,” who will stand as “the signature beneath the collective deeds of the sons of Israel.”

Here we have come right up to the theme of the second part of this book, where it will be shown how the fact of the Incarnation of God in Christianity fundamentally transformed the whole structure of traditional metaphysics and cosmology. But in order to grasp the uniqueness of the metaphysical message of Christianity, it was necessary first to sketch in general outline the contours of the most important metaphysical and cosmological problems, both in the manifestationist perspective of the non-monotheistic traditions and in strict creationism, whose basic postulates orthodox Christianity has borrowed (though we shall convince ourselves below of the ambiguous character of that borrowing). To grasp adequately the specificity of the Orthodox doctrine and of Trinitarian theology, it is important to keep constantly in view the fundamental difference between creationism and manifestationism — for Christianity, in truth, carries within itself particular aspects of both approaches, while being in essence neither the one nor the other, but a third thing.

Apparatus · Chapters VIII–IX

Apparatus

Source notes. The superscript numerals preserve Dugin’s footnote markers (nos. 54–73 fall in these chapters); their content, stripped by the electronic transcription, will be restored from the paginated source in the complete reader edition.

The Ninetieth Psalm. Dugin cites the Psalter in the Septuagint/Slavonic numbering; Psalm 90 is Psalm 91 of the Hebrew and English Bibles. His phrase «тьма одесную Тебе» turns on a Slavonic pun the English cannot keep: t’ma means both “ten thousand / myriad” (its sense in Ps. 90:7, “ten thousand at thy right hand”) and “darkness” — so the verse can be heard as “the darkness at Thy right hand,” which is precisely how Dugin wants it heard when assigning the loyal angels to the right side. The verse is rendered here after the King James wording with “myriad” retained at first occurrence.

The prayer of faith in place of works is from the Order of Preparation for Holy Communion («Вера же вместо дел да вменится мне, Боже мой…»), rendered directly from the Slavonic. “The righteous shall shine forth as the sun” is Matthew 13:43. The Boehme sentence is given as Dugin quotes it, from the Russian.

Two factual cautions. (1) The claim that “the Sadducees and Pharisees” alike denied the soul’s survival and the resurrection is Dugin’s error: the sources (Josephus; Acts 23:8) attribute that denial to the Sadducees, while the Pharisees affirmed the resurrection. The sentence is translated as he wrote it. (2) The printed text gives the Sanskrit counterpart of Torah as «харма», an evident misprint for «дхарма» (dharma), corrected silently here; note also that Dugin’s «этимологический эквивалент» (“etymological equivalent”) overstates the case — Torah and dharma are semantic counterparts, not cognates.

Translator’s terms. «Ветхий Адам» = the Old Adam (deliberately echoing «Ветхий Завет», the Old Testament — the same adjective in Russian); «денница» = the morning star (Lucifer of Isaiah 14:12); «ошую / одесную» = on the left hand / on the right hand (Slavonic); «кольчуга элементов» = the chain-mail of the elements (Suhrawardi); «риза кожаная» = the garments of skin (Gen. 3:21); «круговращение душ» = the circulation of souls (metempsychosis).

Part II · The New Truth of the Incarnation

Part II · Chapter X

God Became Flesh
(“There Is Neither Jew Nor Greek”)

Бог плоть бысть («несть ни иудея, ни эллина»)

The Christian tradition rests upon one absolute metaphysical fact — the Incarnation of God. It is this that makes Christianity what it is, was, and will be.

It is entirely illegitimate to regard this tradition as a messianic, universalized continuation of Judaism, and for this reason the term “Judeo-Christianity” (which, incidentally, Guénon himself used) is wholly inadequate as a designation of the Orthodox Christian Church and its teaching. The concept “Judeo-Christianity” can mean either an eschatological-messianic tendency within Judaism itself (as took place in the Bar Kokhba revolt, in the seventeenth-century events surrounding Sabbatai Zevi, and with the other Jewish false messiahs), or a particular current in early Christianity that remained within the bounds of Judaism — echoes of which can be seen in the Ebionite heresy and, in part, in the religion of the Mandaeans and Sabians. It is entirely wrong to draw the Christian tradition close to a continuation of Jewish esotericism and the gnosis of the Essenes, of the Nag Hammadi community, or of the school of the Merkabah gnostics, which rested on an oral esoteric tradition going back to the prophet Ezekiel. If certain points of resemblance do in fact exist between Christianity and these forms of the Judaic tradition, then at the level of specifically Christian metaphysics — before the absolute centrality with which the fact of the Incarnation is invested within Orthodox orthodoxy — they lose their significance entirely. In the case of Christianity we are dealing with an entirely particular religion and, what is most important, with an entirely particular metaphysics, having no analogue in any of the traditions.

This metaphysics is founded on an absolutely supra-rational and a-logical assertion, refuting all the conceptual norms both of the Judaic creationist and of the manifestationist approaches.74 God became flesh. God — and not simply an angel, a prophet, a messenger; not a Hindu avatar; not an incarnation of the “awakened state,” as in Buddhism. The highest God himself — transcendent, divided from creation by an impassable abyss — united indivisibly his purely transcendent hypostasis, in the person of the Son, with the lowest created human world; and with that world, moreover, in its gravest cyclical period, near the limit of the degeneration of the fallen Adam, “in these last days.”75 The Incarnation of God the Son, God the Word, in Jesus Christ has nothing in common with the coming of the Judaic mashiach and the beginning of the great Sabbath as orthodox Judaic theologians understand that event. In crucifying Christ, the Jews did not merely commit an “error,” or momentarily fail to recognize their Savior, their Anointed. They rejected a revelation absolutely new to them, a particular and paradoxical Good News whose acceptance would have meant nothing more nor less than the end of Judaism. The holy apostle Paul declared unambiguously: “There is neither Jew nor Greek.”76 And in another place: “The shadow of the law has passed.”77

These two assertions the Jews could not receive otherwise than as absolute metaphysical blasphemy, for in them the logic of strict creationism — the foundation of the Judaic worldview — is rejected. For Judaic theology the “shadow of the law,” that is, the “epoch of the law,” can never “pass” (“come to an end”), since in the closed system of creation ex nihilo there exists no prospect of the creature’s “return” to the Creator, by virtue of their principial and irremovable difference of essence. Consequently the law, the Torah, though it has a beginning in time and in history, must have no end. Accordingly the Talmud canonically rejects the entire content of Christianity, accounting it a species of extra-Judaic “idolatry” — as is plainly visible in the aggressively anti-Christian passage about the supposed earthly “father” of Jesus, a Roman legionary named “Pantera.” In other words, for all Jews, including the Jewish esotericists, Christianity is regarded as the influence of a non-creationist, manifestationist (and therefore non-Judaic) tradition upon the Old Testament symbolic series (which is what explains the Talmudic ascription to Christ of a “Roman” origin).

God became flesh. Such an assertion is unthinkable in Judaism. And what is at issue is not simply piety or religious dogma, but the very essence of strict and consistent creationism. Yet it is astonishing that this formula is entirely unacceptable to the manifestationists as well. This point seems at first glance less clear, since the manifestationist teachings often speak of “avataric” appearances of the principle (God) in human guise. The theory is especially developed in India, where one speaks both of the great, rare avatars of Vishnu and of the more frequent and relative avatars of other gods, in particular Shiva. But if for the Jews the “scandal” begins with the concept of God — who by definition cannot become incarnate under any circumstances — for the manifestationists the problems arise at once over “flesh” and “humanity.” The manifestationists, collectively named “Greeks” by the apostle Paul, do not know that conception of created flesh which is present in Judaism. Flesh for them, like independent human status, is nothing but the veil of maya, of illusion — the result of avidya, “ignorance.” Of a “God-man” there can be no talk there, for the simple reason that in such a conjunction the “human,” the “fleshly,” would simply vanish, dwindle, dissolve like a mirage or a mist. God would swallow up the man, who would become no more than a semblance, a shadow, possessing neither its own separate nature nor its own separate individual face.

The difficulty and supra-rationality of the Christian assertion of the Incarnation in its canonical Nicene form never ceased, through the whole history of the Church, to confound the Christian consciousness, which gravitated toward the acceptance of more familiar, more “normal” models of theology. Two archetypal heresies on this question are highly instructive: the Judeo-Christian current of the Ebionites, continued in the views of Arius and later, in part, among the Nestorians (the idea that Christ was not God but a prophet); and the Helleno-Christian current of the Monophysites, the Platonists, elements of which can be met among the Gnostics (the idea that Christ was not man and flesh, but only a phantom). These two poles define not only the paradigms of all possible heresies but also two positions within ecclesiastical orthodoxy itself, where the possibility exists — while remaining faithful to the letter of the dogma — of placing the accent on the one or the other nature of the God-man. This, incidentally, sharply distinguishes Orthodoxy from Catholicism: Orthodoxy, within the strict observance of the dogmas, gravitates intuitively toward the Helleno-Christian point of view; Catholicism toward the Judeo-Christian. And yet, wherever Christianity remains itself, the dyophysite line — the presence in Christ of precisely two natures, divine and human — is affirmed strictly and unambiguously.

Thus the fact of the Incarnation joins the unjoinable: the creationist perspective of the fundamental estrangement of creation, the “nihilistic” nature of the universe, the unidirectionality and irreversibility of time, on the one hand (this explains the canonization of the “Old Testament”); and the manifestationist approach of the immanent presence of the principle at the very center of the manifested world, on the other (in this, properly, consists the “Good News,” the “Gospel,” the “New Testament”). It is very important that creationism and manifestationism are here affirmed simultaneously, without rejecting or canceling one another. Nor is there here that hierarchical subordination which characterizes the esoteric lines of the creationist religions, where the manifestationist doctrine (Sufism in Islam; Merkabah gnosis and Kabbalah in Judaism) serves as a secret teaching — higher, but destined only for the elect. Christianity acknowledges both metaphysical perspectives fully and unambiguously at once (both the Judaic and the Hellenic); yet they are not summed, not integrated, not hierarchically subordinated. Both remain, in themselves, at once true and untrue. The Truth is identical with Christ alone, and is an attribute exclusively of the Christian faith, which is universal and absolute.

The Old Testament creationist metaphysics received by Christianity is called upon to underscore the radical separateness of Creator and creation, their incommensurability. On this question the Christian theologians go so far as to place even Adam, the righteous, the patriarchs, and the prophets temporarily in hell, until the Savior’s descent there. Here there are no compromises with “Hellenism” (manifestationism). The universe is woven of “nothing” and is pure dust, brought to existence at a single stroke by the divine good pleasure. The Christian Bible opens with the stern and unambiguous words: “In the beginning God created…” As in strict creationism, the “good angels” are regarded as those who acknowledged their ontological “nothingness,” their “non-being-ness,” while the rebel companions of the morning star are unambiguously condemned. No “transmigration of souls.” Man undergoes individuation only at the moment of bodily embodiment, which is not repeated. No possibility exists of independently passing beyond the bounds of the fallen Adam, of overcoming the “gravitational field” of the fall. Before the Incarnation there is only the law, the Torah; and God addresses the creature from outside (sometimes through angels). Thus the Orthodox doctrine insists rigidly on the Judaic line, which is accepted fully and without reservation in everything that concerns the period from the expulsion of the first parents from paradise to the Nativity of Christ.

At the moment of the Nativity something unthinkable and impossible takes place, overturning not only common sense but the whole of metaphysics. The transcendent God himself, the second person of the Trinity, the Word, breaks into the estranged, “nugatory” universe — onto its very material floor, to the “fallen,” into the sector of the final degeneration of the fallen Adam. This time it is no angel, no messenger, no indirectly inspired prophet, but God the Creator himself, in his filial hypostasis. Not the Judaic mashiach, but the dread and ulterior Deus Absconditus, the distant and inscrutable “unknown God.” He does not merely come, appear: he commingles himself with the “fallen” human bodily nature, takes upon himself the “sins of the world,” plunges kenotically into the very bottom of a universe already “humbled” and “nugatory.” This is no longer even manifestationism but “supra-manifestationism,” for the investing of the Word with human flesh makes him evident to all; in a single instant it burns away all the veils of illusion, of avidya, of ignorance; it annuls the manifested as something separate from the principle (God), since the principle (God) discloses itself in its very essence and nature.

This is the turning point of Christian metaphysics — which affirmed the utmost estrangement proper to strict creationism before the Incarnation; for after the Incarnation all the ontological proportions change abruptly. At the bottom of the created world there arises a unique and supra-rational region which is henceforth joined directly not only to the highest regions of creation but to the transcendent God himself, two of whose hypostases are constantly present in this region. What is at issue is the New Testament Church, which becomes the vessel of the metaphysics of the Incarnation and which exists, after Pentecost, according to norms having nothing in common with the logic of the universe’s functioning in the era of the law. A special epoch of metaphysical grace begins, in which Christians receive the possibility of union with God in the most intimate manner — notwithstanding the persistence, outside the Church, outside the Christian world, of the same creationist proportions as before the Incarnation.

Thus the “supra-manifestationism” of the Incarnation is concentrated exclusively in the One, Catholic, Apostolic Church, which truly lives in the era of grace, bears the good tidings, and abides in the ray of direct and constant contact with the Godhead. In this respect one may say that within the bounds of the Church there henceforth reign special ontological proportions which do not acknowledge creationist logic. But around the Church, beyond its bounds, beyond the frontier of the unique Christian transfiguring ritual experience, the same estranged laws persist as before. The natural world is not actually transfigured or changed. In it the old principles remain in force, annulled only upon the voluntary entry of a man, a people, an empire into the bosom of the Church. Thus after the Incarnation there arises a paradoxical dualism between two superimposed and non-intersecting realities: the ecclesial, saved, grace-filled reality of Christianity as an island of Uncreated Light, and the old, graceless, created, actual reality of the last moments of the historical existence of the fallen Adam.

The Good News is universal. It concerns the whole complex of earthly and cosmic existence. But between the Incarnation and the Second Coming the universality of grace abides in a potential state, within the Church, resting on faith in Christ, on the dogmas, and on Christian mystical practice. Faith requires of the Christian that he affirm, by an act of will, behind the suffering and crucified Jesus, the absolute principle — the Savior-in-Strength — whose universality will be displayed in its whole scope only at the Second Coming, when the ecclesial potentiality with all its metaphysical implications becomes total universal actuality. But at that moment the unique and supra-rational ecclesial mode of being will end. The Heavenly Jerusalem will descend to earth, and the Tree of Life will be affirmed as the luminous axis freeing the whole creature from the law. At that moment the saying “the shadow of the law has passed” will become not only the possession of believing Christians but will be displayed with evidence to the entire universe.

Here the question must be posed: why does the Christian tradition — whose mystical content plainly bears a supra-manifestationist character — nonetheless have recourse to the creationist perspective in describing the ontological quality of the Old Testament period, and even of the extra-ecclesial reality of the New Testament period (for it is affirmed that those who come to the Christian Faith are creatures under the law, freed only by the mystery of the second birth, that is, Orthodox baptism)? From our point of view, this is the essential metaphysical axis of Christianity as a particular tradition, which accents, above everything else, the value of kenosis.

The kenosis of the transcendent Trinity, as we have shown, consists in the accentuation and glorification of the sacrificial nature of the second person — the Son — as the possibility of manifestation within the absolute. Thereafter, kenosis unfolds in three elements. The first is creation, when God not merely diminishes Himself in realizing the manifested, but diminishes Himself absolutely, creating that which is ontologically opposed to His essence — ex nihilo. In Old Testament creationism the kenotic orientation of the Christians discerned what is absent from manifestationism: the supra-rational sacrifice of a God who creates, and endows with freedom, a reality so paradoxical that its nothingness contrasts with the fullness of the ontological principles, with the Trinity. The second element of kenosis consists precisely in the Incarnation, when the essentially nugatory — and, moreover, fallen, sinful — reality of creation, drawn near to its lower limit, to the end of the cycle, is visited by God the Word himself, who does not simply save, improve, or refurbish it, but takes upon himself the whole fullness of the sufferings and privations that constitute the essence of created being. And finally the third element: at Pentecost is accomplished the final kenosis of the Holy Spirit, the Comforter, who, remaining one God, divides himself into the tongues of flame descending upon the apostles — and thereafter into as many parts as there are individuals receiving holy baptism in the Christian Church.

Outside the creationist model, the sacrifice of God — which is the secret metaphysical meaning of the whole of Christianity — would not be so absolute, so radical, so paradoxical. Outside this model the kenosis of the absolute could not be recognized, just as its inner Trinitarian structure would remain in shadow; in place of it there would exist, at best, an Advaitist, purely apophatic conception. The absoluteness of the kenosis half-lifted for Christians the veil over infinity; it pointed to an aspect of the transcendent that was entirely indiscernible in the other varieties of metaphysics.

The metaphysics of the Incarnation — taken, moreover, in the strictest dogmatic terms, apart from the almost manifestationist line of Helleno-Christianity (and, naturally, apart from the Judeo-Christian versions) — is the secret and profound tidings of the absolute concerning its own nature: unclarifiable and incomprehensible in principle, yet appearing, paradoxically, in the absoluteness of the sacrifice. The kenosis of God not only disclosed the Godhead to the creature; it disclosed to the whole of metaphysical reality the secret side of infinity — the side of Love.

Part II · Chapter XI

The “Evil Demiurge”
(First Excursus into the Gnostic Doctrines)

«Злой демиург» (первый экскурс в гностические доктрины)

To understand more clearly the metaphysical content of the preceding chapter, one may turn to the unorthodox material of Christian gnosis, in which certain aspects of Christian metaphysics were grasped with such piercing acuity that they gave rise to complex and paradoxical philosophical systems. The Gnostics, to be sure, stand outside dogmatic Christianity; they are anathematized, and their teaching is rejected by the Church. But this does not yet mean that their views constitute pure error. On the contrary, the excessiveness of some of their doctrines only shows more clearly the specificity of Christian metaphysics, lays bare its paradoxical essence, hints at the hidden mystery of the Christian revelation.

The Christian Gnostics divided into three categories. The first were continuers of the Jewish esoteric traditions. To them belong the Ebionites and the Essenes. Thus the Ebionites taught of an “Angel-Christ” who moves through history by a series of embodiments until he appears in the person of the messiah. A similar theory can be met among the Islamic gnostics of the Ismaili type. It is entirely clear that such Judeo-Christian Gnosticism bears a very distant relation to Christian doctrine proper. Such Gnostics (their continuation was Arianism and Nestorianism) in the overwhelming majority of cases accepted Islam and formed within it a special esoteric current of the type of the Alawites and certain Shiʿite movements in Iran, Iraq, and Syria.

The second category of Gnostics — Valentinus and the Valentinians, and many others besides — is on the whole comparable with esoteric Christianity, or Helleno-Christianity. Characteristic of this category were the manifestationist approach, Platonic motifs, and the striving to “purify” Christianity of creationism altogether. The Old Testament, where Gnostics of this type acknowledged it, was interpreted in a manifestationist key, and all its strictly creationist assertions were treated as symbols and allegories. Such Gnostics insisted on the Trinitarian understanding of the Godhead, but often inclined toward affirming the “seeming” humanity of Jesus. The borderline case is the great Christian writer and ascetic Origen, who, having received the Gnostic teaching of the Valentinians, long balanced on the edge between Orthodox orthodoxy (many aspects of which go back, properly, to his formulations) and Christian manifestationism. But these Gnostics too are not very interesting from the standpoint of clarifying the metaphysical essence of Christianity.

The third and deepest form of Gnostic philosophy is the views of the Christian dualist Gnostics — from Bardesanes, Marcion, the authors of the Pistis Sophia, and the Ophites, to the medieval Bogomils and Cathars. It was in this category of Christian Gnostics that the understanding of the incompatibility and contradictoriness of the creationist perspective of the Old Testament, of the Judaic spirit, with the metaphysical essence of the revelation of Jesus Christ was present most acutely. In principle, it is precisely among the dualist Gnostics that one can find an at times paroxysmal, yet deeply correct, understanding of the chief problem of Christianity (though their solution of that problem was unsuccessful — which is what led to their anathematization). Gnostics of this type gave themselves a clear account of the metaphysical importance both of Old Testament creationism and of the Christian message. And unlike the first two groups, they did not try to reconcile the two positions by choosing only one of the two metaphysical perspectives — the creationist (like the Judeo-Christian Gnostics) or the manifestationist (like the Helleno-Christians). Contrary to orthodoxy, they were not content to point to the supra-rationality of the purely dogmatic synthesis, but attempted to think that conjunction through. And here, perhaps, their chief error (which, properly, entailed their excommunication from the Church) was their failure to understand the “kenotic” orientation of the orthodox doctrine — the metaphysical meaning of the sacrificial nature of the absolute. Nonetheless it is precisely dualist Gnosticism that most clearly lays bare the foundations of Christian metaphysics, that formulates the problem most truly — though it proposes an unsatisfactory, too hasty and crude solution.

The dualist approach of the Gnostics affirms the presence not of one but of two divine principles — the Old Testament and the New Testament “gods.” The Old Testament God, Yahweh, is for them the demiurge, the “evil god,” creator of a closed and estranged, “nihilistic” system of the creationist type. They do not deny the reality of creationism; they insist on the evil, negative, defective nature of that reality. The Old Testament god of the Jews becomes for them a usurper, a universal jailer, drawing the luminous energies of the “good god” (the Trinity) into the dark regions of “nugatory” created being. Acknowledging the reality of creationism, they discerned in it a purely negative aspect. The law became for them a synonym of slavery and humiliation. And the dualist Gnostics held that the whole tragedy of created being consists in this: that within it are enclosed certain uncreated luminous forces, suffering and tormented under the yoke of the “nothing” out of which the usurper made his closed and exitless system.

It is not hard to discern here the motif of the “choice of the angels.” Evidently the dualist Gnostics stood on the side of those angels who preferred “revolt” against the Creator and declared their consubstantiality with the pre-creational reality.

From their point of view, the god of the New Testament — the second person of the Trinity — comes into the world of the “evil demiurge” in order to “save” precisely these luminous beings, subjected to persecution and pursuit by the Creator from the First Day of creation. Confirmation of this they saw in the words of Christ: “For the Son of man is come to seek and to save that which was lost.”78 And again: “They that are whole have no need of the physician, but they that are sick; I came not to call the righteous, but sinners to repentance.”79 In the various Gnostic myths — of Helen, of the fall of Pistis Sophia, of the “divine spark,” of the “pneumatics,” and so on — one and the same motif is repeated in different registers. The true God, the “good god,” manifests his eternal energies. But the “evil god,” the demiurge, seizes these energies and lodges them in the “nihilistic” trap, in creation. The energies, remembering their uncreated nature, refuse to acknowledge the absolute priority of the demiurge — that is, creationism and strict “monotheism” — and as a result they “fall” and are pursued by the demiurge, “driven from heaven,” then from the earthly paradise, and so on. Their sufferings last until the moment when the “good god” (the non-creator) sends his Son for the salvation of the “sparks of light” — that is, of his “children,” the “children of God,” the “sons of light.” This is Jesus, bringing to fallen humanity a prayer “revolutionary” for Judaism, in which the “good god” is called not Creator but by his true name: “Our Father.” The Gospel narrative abounds in phrases that can be understood in just this key: the indication that “I am the good shepherd, and know my sheep,”80 and the apostle Paul’s phrase that “the father of the Jews is the devil,”81 and so on. Thus, on the whole, dualist gnosis affirms at the beginning an uncreated manifestation; then a tragic and wholly negative cycle of creationism; and a concluding salvation, restoring the truth of the uncreatedness of the uncreated and opening the prospect of a “new deification.” The dualist Gnostics gave special prominence to the writings of the apostle Paul, in which they saw (not without certain grounds) confirmation of their own dualist approach, opposing the negativity of the “era of the law” to the positivity of the “era of grace.”

Curious are the most extreme conclusions of the dualist Gnostics, reached by the Ophites of the Cainite sect. Here we meet an esoteric doctrine of so radical an anti-Judaism that all the subjects of the Old Testament are submitted to total reinterpretation and ethical revaluation. Every negative personage of the Bible, without exception, here becomes positive, and the reverse. Beginning with the “serpent,” glorified as the “bearer of luminous memory,” and ending with Cain and the inhabitants of Sodom and Gomorrah, each negative Old Testament figure is regarded as a herald of the final redemption and a “son of light,” “slandered” by the demiurge and his “chosen people.” The reverse lot befalls here the Old Testament righteous, who are branded “base servants” of the demiurge. The Cainites are interesting precisely because, being extremists, they illustrate with utmost (excessive) vividness the opposition and incompatibility of the manifestationist and creationist approaches — both of which the orthodox Christian doctrine nonetheless includes within its own composition.

The views of the dualist Gnostics stand nearer than the other versions to the properly Christian, orthodox perspective, for here two incompatible approaches to the origin of the world and of man are indeed taken up simultaneously. But the Christian Church does not affirm, as the primordial pre-cosmological instance, a conflict between uncreated manifestation and creation; it accepts the ethics of creationism and does not justify the morning star and his host. Orthodoxy sees in the Incarnation a certain volitional and non-obligatory act of the Godhead, having no ontological preconditions either in creation or in manifestation. Christ affirms, within the estranged creationist reality, a paradoxical corridor of “deification,” closing the floor of reality directly with the uncreated world of principles. This theme is very close to the general disposition of the dualist Gnostics. But “deification” is offered precisely to the “creature” — and not as a reward, but as an entirely unmerited gift, as grace. And in this consists the impassable line of demarcation between gnosis and orthodoxy. Dualist gnosis strives to vindicate the pre-creational dignity of the saved and the saving. The ecclesiastical doctrine, on the contrary, accents the “kenotic” vector of the absolute itself, which wills to “lose” and not to “preserve” its quality; wills to “sacrifice itself” totally and radically — not to arrange for itself a “test” and “trial” in the created labyrinths of the demiurge (as it comes out in Gnosticism).

But how, in that case, does orthodox Christianity resolve the “angelic problem”? What choice does the Christian spirit make in the celestial dispute? How is spiritual freedom realized in the Church founded on the Incarnation — and how is the very existence of the Church inscribed in the eternal Book of Life, in the aeonic, frozen world of formless essences?

Apparatus · Chapters X–XI

Apparatus

Source notes. The superscript numerals preserve Dugin’s footnote markers (nos. 74–81 fall in these chapters); their content, stripped by the electronic transcriptions, will be restored from the paginated source in the complete reader edition.

Scriptural citations. The chapter title «Бог плоть бысть» adapts the Church Slavonic of John 1:14 («И Слово плоть бысть», “And the Word was made flesh”); “There is neither Jew nor Greek” is Galatians 3:28. «Прейде сень законная» (“The shadow of the law has passed”) is not a biblical verse but the opening of the Dogmatikon of the Second Tone from the Octoechos — a liturgical text, which is itself telling for Dugin’s method. “In these last days” renders «в последняя» (cf. Heb. 1:2, Slavonic). “The Savior-in-Strength” renders «Спас-в-силах» (Spas v silakh), the Russian iconographic type of Christ enthroned amid the powers — the image that traditionally crowns the iconostasis deesis row. Luke 19:10, Mark 2:17/Luke 5:31–32, and John 10:14 are given after the King James Version.

One factual caution. In Chapter XI Dugin attributes “the father of the Jews is the devil” to the apostle Paul. The reference is in fact John 8:44 — words placed in the mouth of Jesus in the Fourth Gospel; no such phrase occurs in Paul. (Dugin himself cites the verse correctly as Christ’s words later, in Chapter XIV.) The sentence is translated as he wrote it, with the error noted here. The Talmudic “Pantera” material Dugin invokes refers to the scattered Yeshu ben Pandera passages; their reference to Jesus of Nazareth is itself debated in the scholarship.

Translator’s terms. «сверхманифестационизм» = supra-manifestationism (Dugin’s coinage for the Incarnation as exceeding the manifestationist paradigm itself); «срастворяется» = commingles (echoing the patristic language of κρᾶσις); «экклесиастическая реальность» = ecclesial reality; «обожение» = deification (theosis); «Утешитель» = the Comforter (Paraclete).

Part II · Chapter XII

New Testament Freedom

Новозаветная Свобода

Christianity is the third path. This definition applies universally to the whole complex of its metaphysical problematic. It is neither creationism nor manifestationism. It is a third thing — independent, complete, total. Consequently, all the metaphysical answers of this tradition necessarily carry within them an unexpected, paradoxical element.

Let us return to the theme of the “angelic choice.” The creationist approach, in determining which angels are “evil” and which “good,” is opposed to the manifestationist approach. Consequently, the realization of the dimension of spiritual freedom within the manifested has a strictly dual perspective. The choice is made from only two possible relations of the creature to the Creator. A third is here excluded in advance. But Christianity gives to this question precisely a “third answer.” For Christians, in the final reckoning, neither the one party nor the other is right: neither the “fallen” nor the “unfallen.”82 And this answer is bound up with the historical fact of the Incarnation, after which it becomes actuality. But how are we to reconcile the historicity of the Incarnation — its conjunction with the concreteness of created, temporal reality — with the eternity of the two answers? It is absurd, from the metaphysical point of view, to suppose that in the concreteness of creation something can take place that is not contained, primordially and eternally, in the motionless celestial archetype. Consequently the Christian period of earthly history — from the Incarnation to the Last Judgment — is already present, synchronously and in advance, in the celestial reality. And accordingly, both what precedes the Incarnation and what follows it has already “been accomplished” in the world of the created archetypes.

Evidently the comprehension of this paradox — the “affirmation of the impossible third” — demands an extraordinary approach to the metaphysics of creation. On the one hand, the created concreteness of the Incarnation and of all that follows it is unquestionably present in the heaven of the First Day. But the creationist model is in principle incapable of encompassing and grasping the metaphysical content of these events and occurrences: heaven preserves, in its static created fullness, only the factual structure of what has eternally taken place. Even the highest angels remain in ignorance of the metaphysical mission of the Incarnation of God; consequently the whole metaphysical content of the Good News necessarily escapes them, though its factual content cannot but be known to them. The whole of Christian history is, naturally, already written in the Book of Life — and yet its essential side may (and must) remain concealed from celestial understanding.

The Church, as the vessel of the New Testament, is a profoundly twofold reality. On the one hand, in its created component, it is subject to the logic of the law. On the other, it abides wholly on the far side of that law and on the far side of creation. The angels see it, but cannot understand it. And therefore the “third choice,” the “third path” of the Christian realization of freedom, adds nothing to the angelic celestial problematic and subtracts nothing from it.

The created part of the Christian remains within the frame of the Old Testament choice, and his spiritual reality is bounded by heaven. Here, as before Christ, only two possible answers exist. But by the grace of baptism through the Holy Spirit, and by the eucharist, the Christian is made a partaker of another, uncreated perspective — of the “excluded third.” And in this paradoxical dimension, which remains metaphysically unnoticed by heaven and the angels, there arises the possibility of acquiring a new freedom, unconjoined with duality: a freedom coinciding with participation in the absolute, a freedom unconditional and transcendent, standing on the far side both of the “rebels” and of the “unrebelled.” At baptism the Christian acquires participation in an entirely new nature, not subject to the creationist, Old Testament mechanics. The Holy Spirit communicates to him the germ of a special “uncreated” flesh, a special “uncreated life,” and a special “uncreated” spirit. His being is, as it were, doubled, split in two; and in the measure of his Christian spiritual realization the accent is transferred ever more onto this second, grace-filled dimension — onto the “new man,”83 in the apostle’s phrase.

It is precisely in the Church that the “new heavens and new earth” arise, which have an entirely different nature from the old, created aspects of the First Day. These heavens are a principially different reality from the old ones, since the angels who “people” them are the uncreated luminous energies of the pre-eternal glory of the Trinity, free from the choice and from the problematic of creation. These Christian angels correspond to the apostles, whose name coincides in meaning with the name “angel”: both words signify a “herald,” a “messenger.” It is characteristic of the metaphysically complete Christian doctrine that the angelic functions are replaced by apostolic ones, since it is precisely the apostles who are the true bearers of the uncreated Holy Spirit, while the created angels remain, in the last reckoning, only the bearers of their own particular spiritual opinion concerning the nature of the Creator and of creation.

Having put on Christ, man potentially parts with his created nature — which, however, does not vanish, but is transfigured.

This is a very important point, distinguishing Christianity proper from the manifestationist traditions. Receiving the prospect of “deification” by grace, the Christian preserves his created nature exactly as the Christ risen from the dead preserves his. In fact, in this process — unlike the Gnostic and manifestationist approaches — there takes place the integration by God of that “nothing” which lay at the foundation of the created “kenosis.” Nothing remains outside the divine totality; and in the present case this phrase must be understood literally.

The third path of the Church takes into itself both manifestationism and creationism, without mixing them and without choosing between them. Christian manifestationism — the uncreated “new heavens and new earth,” the “new man” — takes into itself, as witness, as “proof,” even that which is most incompatible with the notion of divine fullness: the element of “poverty,” of “deprivation,” of “suffering,” of “sin.”

Hence the specifically Christian ethic: the striving to integrate into the world of uncreated fullness all the “outcast” — “they that mourn,” “they that suffer,” “the poor in spirit,” and the remaining ranks of the Beatitudes. One may say that the third path in metaphysics takes something from both groups of angels, without deciding the eternal “dispute” in favor of either. Christ conquers death. This is something other than a proof of the victory of the “humble” celestial essences; but at the same time it is no confirmation of the Gnostic rightness of the “rebels.” It is, rather, the affirmation of another truth, another logic, another reality, inaccessible and transcendent to all the contradictions within the creature. The victory of Christ is the victory of the organizer of creation over the concreteness of creation. It is a victory over heaven; it is the taking of the “kingdom of heaven” by force, and its transfiguration into the “Kingdom of God.”

Here it is important to dwell on the difference between the expressions “kingdom of heaven” and “Kingdom of God,” which are often treated as synonyms. In fact, the “Kingdom of God” is the uncreated reality which is disclosed “within” the creature only with the coming of Christ: “for, behold, the Kingdom of God is within you.”84 This is not merely the statement of something that already had place, but the grace-filled affirmation of something that did not yet exist up to the blessed instant of the Nativity. The “kingdom of heaven,” for its part, existed always, and always within the creature85 — only the way to it was, before Christ, closed, and guarded by a dread archangel with a flaming sword.

Here we approach the problem of the two paths of the realization of New Testament freedom, which may be called the “path of salvation” and the “path of deification.” These are things as different as the “kingdom of heaven” and the “Kingdom of God.”

Part II · Chapter XIII

Salvation and/or Deification

Спасение и/или обожение

In the Nicene Symbol of Faith there is a phrase concerning the purpose of the Incarnation. It is said there that Christ came down from the heavens “for us men, and for our salvation.” This is neither tautology nor pleonasm when the purpose is divided in two: (1) “for us men” and (2) “for our salvation.” Full Orthodox theology understands these two statements differently. “For us men” means that “God became man, that man might become God” (in the words of St. Irenaeus of Lyons and St. Athanasius the Great). What is at issue here is the prospect of “deification” — the complete realization of the premises laid down in the Good News; the radical transfiguration of the created individuality in the rays of the uncreated light of Tabor; the possibility of becoming a “new man,” wholly “clothed in Christ” and identified with him. This possibility is given through God the Word’s assumption of human flesh and human nature. “Deification” is something radically distinct from the salvation of the soul, since in this case one cannot speak of the “preservation” either of the individual subtle form (the soul) or even of the supra-formal (yet created) celestial spirit which causally brought the soul into being. To this refers the enigmatic saying of Christ: “Whosoever shall seek to save his soul shall lose it; and whosoever shall lose it shall quicken it.”86

Deification takes place through commingling with the uncreated divine reality, wherein all the elements of the created nature are exchanged for the uncreated elements corresponding to them. Yet, by the Orthodox teaching, even in this process no complete fusion of the natures — created (human) and uncreated (divine) — occurs. The deified man becomes “a god by participation,”87 partaking of the Trinitarian pre-eternity, clothing himself in the uncreated energies, yet still preserving his “nugatory” essence. In the deified man there is preserved neither soul, nor body, nor spirit separately, but an infinitely small element of “nothing,” unfixed and undifferentiated. This is not even a human component, but the general, universal quality of createdness. Be that as it may, deification bears the most direct relation to the essential metaphysics of the Incarnation, and this prospect leads the being once and for all beyond the bounds of the three worlds of creation, into the supra-celestial, “hyperuranian” region of the pre-eternal principles. The deified creature does not become consubstantial with the Creator, but becomes an element of the Creator’s design for the world. Salvation (as the salvation of the soul) is something distinct from deification. It is the liberation of the soul from under the pressure of the archetype of the fallen Adam, from under the dictate of the law, from under the burden of original sin. One may say that it is the opening of the way of return to the earthly paradise, to the Old Adam, to the polar position of the soul at the center of the lower waters — the ocean of individual subtle and dense forms. If deification is the essential prospect brought into the world by the Incarnation, salvation is an indirect consequence of that Incarnation. In this case the soul remains within the frame of creation — even within the frame of the two lower, formal worlds — but quits their periphery, returning to the center. Such an operation, indirectly effected by Christ, has nothing in common with the main line of the Christian tradition, and indeed in a certain manner falls outside the context of Christian metaphysics, which is oriented, on the whole, entirely transcendently. Salvation, of course, opens within creation a higher prospect as well — the prospect of sanctity, attained by the being’s passage beyond the bounds of the lower waters and its penetration into the “kingdom of heaven,” the world of the formless angelic archetypes. Without the possibility of the soul’s salvation this way was closed, since the world of the lower waters and the world of the upper waters touch one another only by means of the axis passing through the center of the world of formal manifestation — that is, through the earthly paradise. But even this opened way of sanctity cannot be the chief purpose of the Incarnation, for the whole creature taken together is incommensurable with the uncreated reality of the Trinitarian Unity. And strictly speaking, in comparison with the possibility of “deification,” both the salvation of the soul and the prospect of sanctity are not so very different from the condition of the “fallen man,” the “sinner.” Christ’s sympathies for the publicans, the poor, the lepers, the outcast, and even the harlot (Mary Magdalene) symbolically underscore this indifference of the uncreated principle to all intra-created hierarchies.

It must be observed that the two “purposes” of the Incarnation divide the Christian world, and the Christian Church itself, into two halves: into the Church of deification and the Church of salvation. The Church is, of course, in essence one and universal, “catholic”; but its action upon the flock divides into two principal “rays.” One of them falls upon all the “called” (they are the majority, and salvation — given the observance of Christian norms, rituals, and ethical prescriptions — is in a certain sense guaranteed them); the other upon the “chosen” (who are drawn into the full and perfect realization of the metaphysical premises of the Gospel). And these rays divide not because of any dual nature of their own, but by virtue of the quality of the individuals who receive them: some polarize the ray of grace into salvation, others into deification.

It is astonishing that the historical schism of the Churches in the eleventh century gave this mystical aspect of intra-ecclesial actuality a historico-geographical definiteness. The Western Church — the Vatican88 — unambiguously identified its doctrine with the doctrine of salvation, forgetting the line of deification or pronouncing it an “Eastern heresy.” The Eastern Church — Greek, and subsequently Russian — insisted, on the contrary, on the fullness of Christian metaphysics, and defended the prospect of deification as the essential and principal side of the Christian teaching. This division reached its metaphysical culmination in the fourteenth century with St. Gregory Palamas, who developed in detail the conception of the “divine energies,” of the uncreated Light of Tabor, and thunderously affirmed the prospect of deification as the central line of Christianity.

Catholicism by that time had already dogmatically elaborated its scholastic postulates on the foundation of the theses of Tertullian, of St. Augustine, and especially of Thomas Aquinas, in which there was no place for any direct irruption of the uncreated into the created (or the reverse), and the mission of Christ was reduced exclusively to an indirect influence upon the potential quality of the human soul. Were it not for the idea of the Trinity and of the God-man, one could say that Catholicism had drawn close up against Judeo-Christianity. Naturally, the reaction to Palamas in the Western Church (and among theologians subject to the Catholic influence of scholastic thought within Orthodoxy itself) was one of indignation. The Catholic doctrine affirmed that every action of God upon creation (with the single exception of the fact of the Incarnation itself) can only be indirect, mediated by the celestial (created) world of causes, which Catholicism calls “supernatural” in distinction from the “natural” world. What is at issue is the distinction between the spiritual world of formless manifestations and the two lower worlds of formal manifestation. The uncreated reality of the Son, from the Catholic point of view, established in created reality no special paradoxical dimension that would open itself metaphysically to Christians as potential union with the uncreated world of the “divine glory.” Divine reality acted, and continues to act, in the world — even after the Resurrection — only mediately, through the “celestial,” “supernatural” world.

In essence, this Catholic doctrine denies the foundation of Christian metaphysics, turning the whole Christian tradition into a soteriological teaching — into an ordinary exoteric monotheistic religion, almost exhausted by creationism. A difference from Judaism does remain, of course, and even a very considerable one, since the Catholic teaching admits, thanks to the Incarnation, broad possibilities of individual realization far exceeding even the extreme messianic intuitions of the Jews. But from the purely metaphysical point of view, the Western Church, having rejected Orthodoxy, has not gone far from Judaism.

Orthodoxy, in affirming deification, does not deny salvation. But the general disposition of Orthodox spirituality accents far more precisely the hesychast, metaphysical, contemplative approach. Not for nothing does this Church so venerate St. Gregory Palamas and his Greek and Russian followers. The mystical soul of the Orthodox flock — Greek, Russian, Serbian, Bulgarian, Romanian, and so on — as the foundation of the Church, draws to itself precisely the ray of deification, while the Romano-Germanic peoples, who received Catholicism, gravitate to the opposite pole — to the salvation of the soul. It was in the West, in consequence, that there arose the contradictory theory of the “immortality of the soul,” which ignores both the “second death” plainly marked in the Apocalypse and the finitude of creation itself.
