This narrowing of perspective touched certain rituals and dogmas of Catholicism as well; but more often, all the genuinely metaphysical content of the Christian dogmas remained potential and “unclaimed” in the West.

Thus the fact of the Incarnation definitively fixes the structure of Orthodox metaphysics. Its picture is as follows. The Uncreated absolute has a kenotic nature; that is, it gravitates toward “movement” outward. This determines a series of transcendent metaphysical operations, sealed in the Trinitarian structure of the Godhead and lying at the source of creation.

Creation, understood strictly creationistically, is the lower limit of the divine “kenosis” and the ontological outer boundary of the absolute’s voluntary, sacrificial estrangement from itself.

Within creation, on the basis of the law of progressive decay (universal for the whole sphere of estrangement), the “fall” proceeds to the critical point where the distance between the condition of the creature and the Creator attains its maximum. (Such unidirectional and irreversible decay flows precisely from the creationist perspective.)

At this critical moment there bursts into the lowest (spatially) and final (historically) point of creation the transcendent Presence of God — the second person of the Trinity, the Uncreated God himself.

Upon the strictly Judaic creationist perspective a radically manifestationist reality is superimposed. Henceforth the created and the uncreated are mystically conjoined in the Church. In an instant the luminous divine axis flares up, passing through the whole universe. Everything preceding the Incarnation and everything following it is cardinally rethought in metaphysics. In place of the two eternal answers of the angels there is disclosed the incredible, the impossible Third Answer. It is neither the hopeless mechanical non-divinity of the creature (as with the Jews), nor the optimistic natural divinity of the world (as with the Hellenes).

It is the Tidings of the grace-filled deification of the non-divine; of the adoption of the constructed; of the communion of the non-living with the Living.

Such is the foundation of the strictly Christian metaphysics. On it the whole teaching of the Church is built.

The central, principal figure of that teaching is the Immaculate Virgin Mary, through whom and by means of whom the highest and transcendent touches material, natural concreteness. In the Theotokos the Light of Tabor merges with earthly matter, forming the substance of that mystical womb from which is born the New Man — henceforth a partaker of the Second Adam, the Heavenly Adam, our Lord Jesus Christ.

Apparatus · Chapters XII–XIII

Apparatus

Source notes. The superscript numerals preserve Dugin’s footnote markers (nos. 82–88 fall in these chapters); their content, stripped by the electronic transcriptions, will be restored from the paginated source in the complete reader edition.

Scriptural citations. “The new man” is Eph. 4:24 (cf. Col. 3:10); “new heavens and new earth,” Isa. 65:17 / 2 Pet. 3:13 / Rev. 21:1; the Beatitudes, Matt. 5:3–11; the kingdom taken by force, Matt. 11:12; “for, behold, the Kingdom of God is within you,” Luke 17:21 (KJV, matching the Slavonic «внутрь вас есть»). In Chapter XIII the Creed’s «нас ради человек и нашего ради спасения» is given in the standard English liturgical wording “for us men, and for our salvation.” The patristic tag “God became man, that man might become God” condenses Athanasius, On the Incarnation 54.3, with its antecedent in Irenaeus, Against Heresies V pref. Christ’s “enigmatic saying” is Luke 17:33: the Greek and Slavonic have psyche/«душа», which the King James renders “life”; Dugin’s argument requires the literal “soul,” and the translation follows him — the reader should know the standard English Bibles obscure the word on which his exegesis turns.

Two cautions. (1) The distinction Dugin builds between “kingdom of heaven” and “Kingdom of God” is his own esoteric construction: in New Testament scholarship the two phrases are generally held synonymous, “kingdom of heaven” being Matthew’s reverential periphrasis for the divine name. Dugin converts a difference of idiom into a difference of ontology; the argument should be read as his, not as exegetical consensus. (2) His account of Catholicism — no direct action of the uncreated upon the created, grace only mediated — is the standard neo-Palamite polemic (the line of Lossky and Meyendorff) against the scholastic doctrine of created grace; Catholic theology contests this characterization, and the ethnic-confessional mapping of “rays” (Romano-Germanic peoples to salvation, Orthodox peoples to deification) is assertion, not argument.

Translator’s terms. «обожение» = deification (theosis); «срастворение» = commingling; «бог по причастию» = a god by participation; «гиперуранический» = hyperuranian (the hyperouranios topos of Plato’s Phaedrus 247c — a Platonic term inside an Orthodox argument, characteristically); «званые» / «избранные» = the called / the chosen (Matt. 22:14); «Фаворский свет» = the Light of Tabor.

Part III · The Metaphysical Aspect of the Most Holy Theotokos

Part III · Chapter XIV

The Head of the Angels

Глава ангелов

The Most Pure Virgin Mary plays a role of the highest importance not only in the Christian cult but in Christian metaphysics. This aspect, like the other fundamental questions of that metaphysics, is often described in symbolic terms, and its clarification will require us to compare the dogmatic elements with themes of Orthodox sacred tradition reflected in oral tradition and in the subjects of icon-painting.

The Theotokos disputes served in their time as a catalyst for clarifying the metaphysical position of the Nestorians, who, refusing to acknowledge the legitimacy of the term Theotokos, “Birth-giver of God,” disclosed their adherence to the Arian, crypto-Judeo-Christian position with respect to Orthodox dogmatics. What was at issue was not mere details or terminological misunderstandings, but the uncovering of deep divergences in essential metaphysical orientations: on the one side the Orthodox Christian-Christians,89 defending the purity of the third path; on the other the heirs of the Judeo-Christian line, striving to limit the whole fullness of the metaphysical consequences that flow from the metaphysics of the Good News.

The Theotokos in Orthodox teaching has two principal aspects. On the one hand, she is the collective bearer of human nature as a whole, which the incarnate Word takes upon himself. It is through the Virgin Mary that Christ becomes the Son of Man. If Jesus himself is the absolute God-man, the Theotokos is the absolute Man, who has contained in her person the essence of human nature as such. The Virgin Mary is likewise the archetype of universal man, of the perfect man with the whole fullness of his possibilities and of his ontological, created limitations. By her earthly descent she goes back to King David and, accordingly, embodies in herself the purest qualitative aspect of the era of the law. In a certain sense, she is that very law of the old existence. It is precisely in this capacity that Jesus “refuses” her in certain situations of the Gospel narrative,90 just as he calls his disciples, too, to renounce their fleshly parents. Naturally, what is at issue is not a change in the foundations of morality or a rejection of the ten commandments of Moses, but the principial ending of the whole existence under the law, of the mark of “the form of a servant,” whose sequence and continuity are secured in “fallen” humanity by the continuation of the race — which is the instrument for transmitting through time the seal of “original sin.”

In this historical, human capacity the Theotokos occupies a boundary position. Remaining a daughter of the fallen Adam, a bearer of his nature, she at the same time — by her “Let it be!” spoken to the archangel Gabriel in answer to his words, “Rejoice, O Virgin Theotokos!”, and earlier by her providential, chosen service in infancy in the Holy of Holies of the Temple — opens the way to the Incarnation and, consequently, to the beginning of the era of grace. It is no accident that in Orthodox icon-painting the Theotokos is often depicted together with John the Baptist, who is likewise a symbol of the limit between law and grace. John the Forerunner is the last of the Old Testament prophets, who announced to the world the coming of the true Christ. But he belongs to the old reality, where even righteousness and the gift of prophecy cannot radically change the ontological, principial status of a being. Hence Jesus says of John the Forerunner that “he that is least in the Kingdom of God is greater than he.”91

In the new metaphysics of “deification” disclosed in the Incarnation, all created hierarchies become insignificant and inessential; their relativity flows from the incommensurability of the uncreated Trinity with the whole of creation, including its highest regions. The limitedness of the Baptist’s created nature shows itself symbolically in this: that he sends disciples to Christ in order to resolve his doubts concerning Christ’s “messianic” nature.92

On the one hand, he recognizes in Jesus, come to the Jordan for Baptism, the true Christ (becoming the first human witness of the Theophany); yet the conjectural character of his created intuition tells, on the other hand, in the fact that doubts at times assail him. Within the frame of the Old Testament reality it cannot be otherwise, for any assertion concerning uncreated reality is here only a spiritual conjecture. At the same time John the Baptist is accounted by Christians the highest of all the Old Testament prophets, since at the metaphysical level his spiritual, celestial choice is oriented otherwise than the problem of the angelic dualism. John the Baptist anticipates the possibility of a third path in metaphysics, though his created nature “under the law” does not permit him to establish himself in this suspicion. It is no accident that he is sometimes depicted with two wings at his back. He is an atypical angel, drawing near to the solution of the paradoxical “deifying” reality brought in by God the Word. The angel nearest to the Theotokos herself.

The Theotokos fulfills a metaphysical role akin to the Baptist’s. But their relations to the Incarnation are qualitatively different. This shows plainly in the manner in which each is made a partaker of the “New Testament reality” of grace. The initiatic ritual by which a created being enters the uncreated world of the Incarnation is baptism, named “birth from above.”93 John the Forerunner baptizes with water, which is the symbol of the whole created world (the totality of the upper and lower waters). This baptism has only an intra-created significance, and symbolizes the cleansing of the old and concretized nature of beings from all secondary cosmic accretions. It is a return to the purity of created nature in its primordial state, a restoration of paradise, a precondition of the soul’s salvation. In answer to the question whether he himself is the Christ, the Forerunner proclaims that One is coming who will baptize with the Holy Spirit and with fire.94

This means that the true divine baptism is distinct from the baptism with water, for it opens the prospect not merely of salvation but of deification — it potentially leads the being out beyond the bounds of creation altogether.

It is precisely this fiery, transcendent baptism that the Theotokos receives in the instant of the descent of the Holy Spirit upon her, which brought the Nativity in its train. It was the Most Pure Virgin Mary who, first of all creatures (human and non-human), was vouchsafed the spiritual, uncreated baptism.

If during their lives the metaphysical difference between John the Forerunner and the Virgin Mary was not very clearly defined, then after the earthly death of both (in the case of the Theotokos it is customary to speak of the Dormition, not of death) their functions were strictly hierarchized. In this consists the second principal aspect of the Theotokos, which may be called the aspect “of the Dormition.”

Tradition holds that after the Dormition the Theotokos was taken up to heaven (the third world, that of formless spiritual influences) and became there the leader of the angels, the “Champion Leader,” taking the place of the “fallen” morning star. It is the Theotokos, and not John the Forerunner, who stands at the head of the angelic hosts. One may say that the Theotokos, first to receive baptism by the Holy Spirit, became thereby the first Christian Saint — the Most Holy. The Forerunner, who came “in the power of Elijah” (taken up in his time to heaven in a chariot of fire), fulfills in the celestial world a function which, though exceedingly significant, is nonetheless secondary — at least in comparison with the Theotokos herself. Her central and supreme position in the celestial world is symbolically reflected in the Apocalypse, where she is depicted as the Woman clothed in the sun. She is the sun of the spiritual world, the pole of the purified primordial creation, the chief treasure of the heaven of the First Day of creation, the center of the created circle traced by the Creator. In Orthodoxy the Virgin Mary, like John the Baptist (it would be more correct to say: “John the Baptist like the Virgin Mary”), is depicted with archangelic wings.

The establishment of the Theotokos at the head of the angelic hierarchy returns us to the problem of the “choice of the angels.” It is perfectly evident that, given the eternal nature of heaven and of the angelic world, such an “event” can belong neither to earthly time nor to any form of duration whatever. It is absurd to maintain that the Theotokos “became” head of the angels after her historical Dormition. In the “aeon” of the kingdom of heaven there exists neither “before” nor “after.” Consequently the Theotokos was head of the angels always. This follows from the very supra-temporal structure of the upper waters.

But what, in that case, does the assertion mean that “the Mother of God replaces the morning star”? The star, let it be noted, is both a symbol of the Theotokos (the three stars on the shoulders and brow of the Most Pure signify the threefoldness of her immaculateness: “Virgin before the Nativity, Virgin in the Nativity, and Virgin after the Nativity”) and a symbol of the morning star, of Lucifer, whom the ancients identified with Venus, the morning and evening “star.”

From the metaphysical point of view, this “replacement” is the affirmation of the strictly Christian view of the specificity of manifestationism — of the uncreated outpouring of the divine nature “outward.” Lucifer made his eternal choice by declaring his consubstantiality with the principle (against the “good angels,” who acknowledged their ontological “nothingness”). But at the same time he “divined” the possibility of a contact of the uncreated reality of the Godhead with the element of manifestedness. In the “Hellenic,” non-creationist form in which he rejected the very idea of creation, he was “punished.” Yet one aspect of his assertion was metaphysically true. The rightness of the manifestationist assertion, however, does not extend to the whole creature, and does not follow a natural trajectory. The Incarnation of the Son opens this possibility as sacrificial grace, not as “mechanical” necessity. The manifestationism of the Incarnation is possible, but not obligatory. If God wills to walk the path of “kenosis” to the end, He can do so; but in no case is He obliged to do so. Lucifer, the celestial archetype of the “Hellenic,” manifestationist choice of the angels, divining (even “knowing!”) of the Incarnation, deciphers it not in the Christian but in the pagan perspective — as something necessary and inevitable, as a kind of element of fate, projected by him onto the uncreated Godhead. The Theotokos, leader of the angels, formally affirms the same thing as the morning star — but with this nuance: that the Incarnation, and the possibility of “deification” that flows from it, of passage beyond the bounds of createdness, is for her a free and volitional manifestation of divine freedom and divine love.

The Incarnation may take place, and may also not take place — whereas the morning star reckoned that it must take place in any case.

Of course, in the celestial world, where the whole history of the universe is written simultaneously in the Book of Life, there can be no dispute about the fact of the Incarnation. That fact is archetypally accomplished already on the First Day and is not subject to doubt. But what is at issue is the deciphering of the metaphysical content of this Incarnation; and since metaphysics passes beyond the level of the manifested, this content is not unambiguous or evident even to the angels. It is precisely in the divergence concerning the character and conditions, and likewise the cause, of “deification” (as a strictly metaphysical possibility) that the difference between the Eternal Theotokos and the “fallen prince of the angels” consists.

It is important to underscore that the Theotokos in the heavens is precisely the angel of the Third Path, distinct not only from the morning star but from the “good angels” who took the standpoint of strict and consistent creationism. These angels may be called “the celestial causes and patrons of Judaism and of Judeo-Christianity.” They too know of the Incarnation, but they interpret its metaphysical meaning otherwise than either the Theotokos or the morning star. Their notion of the content of the Incarnation does not pass beyond the frame of creation: they number the messiah among created beings of the highest order, executing a task suggested by the transcendent, single Creator. Hence the idea of the “Christ-Angel” among the Ebionites. Without calling the fact into question, they refuse to acknowledge its uncreated ground and, accordingly, deny entirely the possibility of “deification.” The whole theology of the holy apostle Paul, and the majority of the passages of the synoptic Gospels — especially the Gospels of John and of Luke (the disciple of the apostle Paul) — are regarded by these angels as a purely Luciferic line, differing in nothing from the “idolatry” of the other pagans. Helleno-Christianity, and even the orthodox, dogmatic, Nicene Christian Christianity95 of the Third Path, appears to them a variation of “Ophite gnosis” and a “rehabilitation of Cain.” But with respect to them, too, Jesus Christ unambiguously uses the term “devil”: “Ye are of your father the devil, and the lusts of your father ye will do.”96 The Greek word “devil” (literally: “the divider, the splitter,” “the breaker of unity”), or the Hebrew “satan” (literally: “barrier,” “obstacle”), metaphysically refer precisely to that creationist obstinacy — illegitimate from the standpoint of full Christianity — which, against the evidence of the Christian Faith, against the angelic witness of the Theotokos, denies any possibility of a direct connection of the uncreated (Divine) with the created, thereby “splitting” (the etymological aspect of “devil”) the Divine Unity, and setting an insurmountable “barrier” (the etymological aspect of “satan”) between manifested beings and the Almighty Lord.

Thus the Woman clothed in the sun finds herself in the heavens surrounded by two rather grim neighbors: on the “right” stands “satan,” on the left the “morning star.” Narrow are the gates into the Kingdom of God.

And yet the “good angels” (who, from the Christian point of view, prove in the last reckoning to be not so very “good”97) of the creationist orientation have their truth, as does the morning star. If the “good angels” of the Old Testament affirm the impossibility of manifestationism, and the “evil angels” of the non-creationist religions its necessity, the Theotokos affirms its possibility, but not its necessity — thereby confirming the relative rightness and the relative wrongness of both. The Theotokos acknowledges the “nugatory” character of the creature, but hopes for the absolute miracle of “deification,” believes in that miracle, loves its potential cause. That is why she is at times identified with Sophia herself, the Wisdom of God. The Theotokos is the Angel of Love, the Angel of Faith, the Angel of Hope.

She is named in the liturgical language “Bride of God.” She answers for the whole creature, embodies in herself all primordial nature and all primordial spirit. She is present everywhere, in all the worlds, imploring the Lord for the salvation and deification of all beings — relying in this solely upon His free will, constraining Him neither toward a positive nor a negative decision. She is the Angel “who does not judge,” and consequently she “shall not be judged.” She intercedes before the Bridegroom for the fate of all subtle and dense forms, and even for the beings of the formless celestial world.

Part III · Chapter XV

The All-Immaculate and Barbelo
(Second Excursus into the Gnostic Doctrines)

Пренепорочная и Барбело (второй экскурс в гностические доктрины)

In the Christian doctrine there is one very important point touching the delicate theological theme of the “fatherhood” of the God-man. The Symbol of Faith says that the “incarnation” of Christ takes place of the Holy Spirit, and the becoming-man of the Virgin Mary. Yet the theologians, as a rule, take care to avoid any unambiguous identification of the Holy Spirit with the “father” of Jesus Christ; more than that, it is underscored that he was born “without father” — which possibly means not only “without a physical father” (which is obvious) but without a father at all. The name “Bride of God,” meanwhile, is applied to the Theotokos in a more global context, connected with her universal, cosmic function as the “Woman clothed in the sun.”

Guénon in one place erroneously (in our view) ascribed a “masculine” quality to the Holy Spirit, pointing to the illegitimacy of identifying him (against certain Christian esotericists who based themselves on the Hebrew feminine gender of the word for “spirit,” ruah) with the “feminine” aspect of the Trinity — precisely on the ground of his “paternal” role in the Incarnation. It is telling, however, that this point is practically never either confirmed or denied by the Christian theologians themselves. Most often one speaks of the “mystery” of this event, exceeding human understanding.

By way of illustrating a possible metaphysical understanding of this problem, let us adduce one heterodox Gnostic version which proposes a particular way of thinking this moment through. One should not strive to force the Gnostic material into the frame of orthodoxy: that can lead to nothing but sectarianism. But the consideration of the Gnostic version may help us draw nearer to the metaphysical truth within the Orthodox doctrine itself — just as, in its time, the refutation of the heresies contributed to the clear formulation of the dogmatic principles of Orthodoxy.

This Gnostic doctrine is called the “teaching of Barbelo,” the “Great Mother from above,” and its elements can be met in various Gnostic currents up to and including the Pistis Sophia. In brief, it comes down to the following description of the Incarnation.

At the key moment of the archangelic annunciation there takes place a descent upon the Virgin Mary of a special uncreated spiritual force called “Barbelo,” the “Mother from above.” In this way there is communicated to the Theotokos an exceptional quality which makes it possible for the Divine nature of the uncreated Son of God to be joined with the created nature of the Son of Man. The human nature of Christ is inherited from the earthly Theotokos, a created human person. The Divine nature is preserved in the Incarnation thanks to the supra-celestial, uncreated principle (“Barbelo”) that descended into the Virgin Mary — a principle, however, which figures not as an active “masculine” principle acting upon a “feminine” and passive one, but as an uncreated “feminine” principle merging with the created “feminine” principle. Thus Christ truly has no father whatever in his earthly Incarnation, taking both natures from “two mothers”: the earthly and the transcendent.

The hereticism of this doctrine consists in two points: in the introduction of a special feminine hypostasis, over and above the Trinity, into the uncreated world of principles98 (which contradicts the strict logic of trinitarianism); and in the endowing of the Theotokos herself with the quality of Incarnation, since in her there takes place the same merging of two natures (created and uncreated) as in Christ himself. Naturally, such an approach could not be accepted by the Church.

But here the parallel between “Barbelo, the Mother from above” and the Holy Spirit plainly suggests itself, since not only etymologically but dogmatically the third person of the Trinity is fitted, in many respects, by metaphysical and ontological terms of a symbolically “feminine” character. The Holy Spirit is, in the words of Basil the Great, “the perfecting cause” — but it is precisely this characteristic that belongs to natura, to nature, the immanent aspect of the principle. The extremist conjecture of the Gnostics concerning “Barbelo,” expressed in dogmatically inadequate form, may nonetheless hint at the real function of the Holy Spirit as the one communicating to the man Jesus the quality of the uncreated divine nature, which in the Trinity is one for all three persons. And this communication of nature must not be regarded as a “masculine” function, by definition acting actively upon its object (the Virgin Mary) and conditioning the irreversibility of the Nativity. The Nativity, the Incarnation, in a full Christian metaphysics cannot be conditioned at any stage, for in it there is always present the whole fullness of the spontaneity and freedom of Divine Love. The Son comes only of Himself, sent by the Father and accompanied by the Holy Spirit. The Incarnation has no prior cause. It takes place strictly “without father,” since the uncreated Father of the Trinity does not figure in such a role in the situation of the Incarnation (nor does any Orthodox theologian assert that he does). Consequently the natural precondition of the Incarnation cannot have a causally conditioning character — and this means that the Holy Spirit performs, rather, a “feminine,” “maternal” function in the Nativity of the God-man, furnishing the Virgin Mary with the special, paradoxical possibility of the uncreated nature. Thus, by analogy with the Gnostic subject of “Barbelo,” a more orthodox explanation of the Immaculate Conception may be proposed.

Let us now turn to the second argument against the Gnostic myth of “Barbelo.” It consists in this: that the descent of the uncreated nature into the Virgin Mary would mean that she herself is an Incarnation — and consequently a “Christ” and a God-man. So, indeed, certain heretical sects of early Christianity maintained. In fact such a conclusion is by no means obligatory. One may regard the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the Theotokos as an initiatic event, a baptism — not the preliminary, created baptism with the waters practiced by John the Forerunner, but the uncreated Baptism with the Holy Spirit which the Son of God brought into the world. Such a baptism is “birth from above”: the affirmation, within a created being, of a special metaphysical dimension opening for it a direct connection with the uncreated world of principles. And what is at issue here is not an Incarnation — not a personal, “hypostatic” fixation of the transcendent principle in created reality “from above downward,” a “birth of the higher in the lower” (that case is unique and pertains to Jesus Christ alone) — but the projection of a created being “from below upward,” a “birth of the lower in the higher.” The uniqueness and singleness of the hypostasis of the Son is thereby neither violated nor divided.

The Theotokos, first among human beings (and, more broadly, among all creatures), not only received baptism by the Holy Spirit but realized the whole initiatic fullness of the transfiguration, the transubstantiation, laid down in it. It was she who became the first of the “gods of the second order.”

She therefore constitutes the absolute archetype of the baptized being, with the whole fullness of the metaphysical realization of the supra-creational possibilities laid down in it. And so the second argument, too, against the “Mother from above” — and, accordingly, against the absolute immaculateness of the immaculate conception — may be set aside, given an adequate determination of the proportions and a clarification of the quality and specificity of the interrelation of the natures in the Most Pure Virgin herself.

Part III · Chapter XVI

The Virgin Mary and Spiritual Realization

Дева Мария и духовная реализация

The metaphysics of the Theotokos is set forth most fully in the hesychast tradition, which sees in her the chief figure and principal symbol of Christian initiation and spiritual realization. This doctrine is clearly presented in St. Gregory Palamas.

According to Palamas and the Orthodox patristic tradition, the Virgin Mary embodies in herself the maximum of purity possible within the frame of creation. She is the archetype of the perfect man. At times the metaphysical quality of purity is symbolized by her extraordinary, incomparable beauty. In the Virgin Mary all that is best in creation, all that is untouched by degradation and decline, converges providentially from all eternity. In her the paradisal nature preceding the fall is eschatologically reproduced. She belongs to the logic of the law, but in its most essential, paradisal quality; she is the purified substance of creation, matter, the Mother of the World in her primordial quality. From the metaphysical point of view one may say that the Virgin Mary is the essence of the universe, its archetype, its mysterious seed. And the hesychasts insisted that she was such primordially, even before the annunciation and the election — that she was such always, from the beginning of the world. In a certain sense the human personality of the Theotokos dissolves entirely in her archetypal, universal dimension, where she is wholly identified with universal and human nature as such, in its highest, “godlike” aspect.

It is very important to underscore that in Orthodox esotericism it is precisely the Virgin Mary who is accounted the archetype of the perfect man — against Guénon, who, in accordance with the Islamic esoteric doctrines, held Christ to be the archetype of the perfect man.

“Perfect man” is an initiatic term signifying the fullness of the realization of all esoteric possibilities within the three worlds of creation (of manifestation), and the actually accomplished transfer of a particular being’s existence from the cosmic periphery to the motionless center of all things. The perfect man is the axis around which the three manifested worlds revolve: bodily, soul-related, and spiritual. Guénon, in his book The King of the World, underscores that in the Gospel tradition these three aspects of the perfect man are symbolically sealed in the “adoration of the Magi,” whose three offerings to the infant Jesus he correlates with the three levels of the perfect man. Gold symbolizes the “pole” of the material world (this gold may be called “alchemical gold” or the “philosopher’s stone”); myrrh, the “pole” of soul-reality (a hint at the anointing of the monarch); and frankincense, the “pole” of the spiritual, celestial sphere (underscoring the sacerdotal, priestly function).99

It is important to note an interesting parallelism between the “three Magi” who came to worship the infant Jesus and the “three Myrrh-bearers” who set out for the tomb of Christ after the crucifixion. This parallelism is accented in Orthodox tradition, as is sealed in the paschal texts of the Flowery Triodion.100

And it is important to note that if the Magi were personages of the male sex, the Myrrh-bearers were of the female; moreover it is underscored that among them were Mary Magdalene and the “other” Mary (meaning the Theotokos herself). So we are entitled to draw an analogy between the Paradisal Adam — the perfect man of the beginning of the cycle, whose three aspects are symbolized by the Magi and their gifts — and the analogous mission of the Woman, the Virgin, at the end of the cycle, who by dual symmetry becomes the perfect man in the eschatological period. On the other hand, St. Gregory Palamas insists that the first to learn of the Lord’s Resurrection was the Theotokos herself, who came to the Lord’s tomb alone. At the initiatic level these two scenarios do not contradict one another, but only accent the threefold perfection of the Virgin Mary in the three created worlds, her central, royal function with respect to reality.101

Guénon, in his book The Great Triad102, speaks of the difference between the perfect man and the transcendent man, underscoring that the perfect man is the principle within the frame of the manifested, whereas the transcendent man is the principle in the region of the non-manifested; consequently the transcendent man is immeasurably higher than the perfect man.

In Christian terms this difference may be correlated, on the one hand, with the sphere of creation, whose general, universal principle corresponds to the perfect man, the Old Adam; and on the other, with the uncreated Trinity itself, whose second person may in a certain perspective be named the transcendent man — since Christ is named by the holy apostle Paul the “New Adam” (the “New Man”).103

But it was precisely the quality of the perfect man that was disclosed in the Most Pure Virgin at the end of times, as the reverse projection of the paradisal Adam before the fall. The transcendent man in this case is, without question, Jesus Christ himself, who combined in himself the fullness of the created human nature taken from the Theotokos (the perfect man) with the uncreated divine nature of the second person of the Trinity.

Enormous initiatic significance belongs, for the hesychasts, to the feast of the Entry of the Theotokos into the Temple. This subject — recounting the introduction of the Virgin Mary in her infancy into the Temple and her uninterrupted104 dwelling in the Holy of Holies, that is, in the altar precinct — unambiguously underscores the metaphysical identity of the Theotokos with the Holy of Holies itself, with the Tabernacle of the Covenant, with the Center of the World, with the Throne of the Lord. Palamas asserts unambiguously that the Old Testament Tabernacle (the center of the entire metaphysical tradition of the Jews) was the prefigurative image of the Virgin Mary, and the Entry of the Theotokos into the Temple was the literal fulfillment of the whole Covenant: the symbol was joined with what it symbolized.

Palamas underscores that the Virgin Mary’s dwelling in the Holy of Holies in her infancy passed in perfect silence, stillness, and contemplation.

All these moments have enormous initiatic significance:

  1. The infancy of the Theotokos symbolizes the primordial, pure, archetypal, paradisal state;105
  2. her dwelling in the Holy of Holies signifies identification with the Center of the World, with the axis of the universe;
  3. her silence points to the realization of the transcendent, principial levels preceding every actualization — for “silence,” “stillness,” in the esoteric perspective, pertain to the divine, uncreated reality itself which precedes creation, as “silence” precedes “sound.”

These are three determinations of the state of the perfect man, standing at the central place of the universe, in motionlessness, untouched by the external course of things, in complete spiritual concentration upon what lies on the far side — above the highest celestial regions of the universe, in the uncreated reality of pure principial light.

For the hesychasts this period of the life of the Theotokos is exceedingly important, since their own initiatic practice was founded precisely upon silence, motionlessness, and contemplation. Such a ritual imitation of the quality of the perfect man must, under determinate conditions, lead to genuine spiritual realization. One may say that in this case it is entirely legitimate to speak of a spiritual “imitation of the Theotokos” — in distinction from the Latin mystical ways of the “imitation of Christ” (Imitatio Christi), which is, in truth, a characteristic Judeo-Christian lowering of the transcendent man to the level of the perfect man.

The identification of the Theotokos with the Tabernacle of God, in the initiatic context of Orthodox esotericism, recalls the Kabbalistic interpretation of the “Shekhinah,” the “Presence of God,” which is accounted the immanent aspect of God abiding at the center of the manifested. It is evident that the Kabbalistic doctrine of the “Shekhinah,” when fully developed, passes far beyond the bounds of orthodox Judaism, which is founded on the directly opposite theory of the Creator’s perfect transcendence with respect to the creature.106

Be that as it may, the “Shekhinah,” the “feminine” side of God, at times regarded as His “Bride,” has in the Kabbalistic perspective many features in common with the esoteric understanding of the Virgin Mary in Orthodox hesychasm. As through the “Shekhinah,” by the Kabbalists’ assertion, one may attain union with the divine worlds of the sefirot, so through the Theotokos — and only through the Theotokos — is it possible, in Christian realization, to attain deification and become a partaker of the trinitarian light.

The perfect man is the mediator par excellence between the created and the uncreated, between the immanent and the transcendent, between the metaphysical heaven (and what is above it) and the manifested universe (the earth). The perfect man is the axis piercing the worlds and communicating to all beings a participation in the region of the principles. It is precisely this function that is characteristic of the Theotokos, who is called the “Intercessor,” the “Mediatress” for all the living and the dead before the face of the Lord. Being universal pure nature, she is near to everything created; but having become the chosen Bride of God, and having been taken “at the right hand” of the Lord (“upon thy right hand did stand the queen” — Ps. 44:10) after the Dormition, she proves nearest of all to the uncreated Light. The Theotokos, in Orthodox tradition, is compared with the tongs with which, in Isaiah’s vision, the seraph took the coal from the altar to touch it to the prophet’s lips. The coal here is the uncreated, transcendent reality, the divine nature of the Son; the lips, destined for cleansing and prophecy, are created reality; the seraph is the executor of Providence. This prosaic workshop instrument thus becomes the highest initiatic sign, symbolizing the Theotokos.

The principal events of the life of the Theotokos, corresponding to the Orthodox feasts, mark the various initiatic stages of spiritual realization, and in Orthodox esotericism they acquire a metaphysical meaning. The Nativity of the Theotokos from the Ancestors of God Joachim and Anna,107 who until old age were a barren couple (like Abraham and Sarah), is interpreted esoterically as an indication of the ascetic path preceding the attainment of the state of purified nature. The barrenness of Anna is seen by the Christian consciousness in a perspective directly opposite to Judaism: if the Jews held “fewness of children” and sterility to be a sign of divine wrath, the ascetic and transcendentally oriented doctrine of Christianity, on the contrary, discerned in it the greatest symbol of “kenosis,” whose reward is the acquisition of the quality of the perfect man. So the chastity and asceticism of Joachim and Anna are crowned by the triumphant immaculateness of the Virgin.

The Entry of the Theotokos into the Temple108 discloses the metaphysical chosenness of the Theotokos. But she leaves the Holy of Holies, following the impulse of “self-expenditure” — the voluntary renunciation of spiritual fullness for the sake of the world’s benefaction and the accomplishment of Providence.

The Annunciation109 is the culmination of the spiritual path of the Most Holy Virgin, where her quality of perfect man comes into direct contact with the region of the uncreated and transcendent. The archangel Gabriel brings the good tidings: “Fear not, Mary: for thou hast found grace with God.”110

This initiatic grace is described in these terms: “The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow thee: therefore also that holy thing which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God.”111

The purified nature, having passed through the cycle of sanctification by the rest and silence of the Tabernacle, of the Ark, receives into itself the uncreated energies of the Trinity, receiving the highest and most perfect baptism.

The Nativity of the God-man, his preaching, his sufferings on the cross, and his Resurrection make the figure of the Theotokos, for this period of the human life of Christ, a secondary one — for such is the place of everything created, even the perfect, before the face of the Creator and of the transcendent principle disclosing itself before the world.

After the Resurrection the Theotokos participates in the apostolic ministry, being by her nature the highest of the apostles, their spiritual head.

In the Dormition112 the Most Pure, first among humanity, rises again in the body before the Last Judgment and ascends into the heavens, to their highest point, to the very boundary between the created and the uncreated, taking her place “at the right hand” of God. Together with the Theotokos the whole of human nature is transfigured and becomes celestial, and the epoch of the universal Resurrection secretly begins.

Palamas in one place compares her with heaven, and Christ himself with the Sun. If in her earthly life the Virgin Mary became the fleshly throne for the Divine Presence, encompassing (as on many icons) the infant Christ, then after the Dormition she was stretched out like the heavens, which serve as the luminous throne to the source of every Light, the Father of Lights. This path is the paradigm of the completed development of every Christian who walks the way of spiritual realization. Its stages may be defined as:

  1. active askesis, the struggle to affirm the inner against the outer (“the King’s daughter is all glorious within”113) — corresponding to the righteous life of Joachim and Anna, their ascetic labors;
  2. the concentration of attention upon the heart, the immersion of the mind in the heart — the Nativity of the Theotokos;
  3. the beginning of contemplation, of noetic work; motionlessness, the practice of the breath, the deepening into the heart — the Entry of the Theotokos into the Temple and her dwelling in the Holy of Holies;
  4. the invocation of the Name of God, the Jesus Prayer, which in the fullness of time proves to be heard, and the divine answer becomes manifest — the Annunciation;
  5. the manifestation of Christ in the heart, inner illumination — the Nativity;
  6. the transubstantiation of one’s own soul-and-body nature, the opening of spiritual sight (the seeing of spirits), the science of the discernment of spirits — the Transfiguration of Christ on Mount Tabor;
  7. the complete establishment of the spirit in the uncreated Light of the divine energies, perfect “deification,” becoming god — the Dormition of the Theotokos, the Resurrection.

Part III · Chapter XVII

“He Brought Me to the Banqueting House”

«Он ввел меня в дом пира»

The Theotokos plays an exceptional role in Orthodox metaphysics, since in her figure, her destiny, her function, her nature, and her universal person is concentrated that limit in which the kenotic orientation of the absolute itself found expression — both in its transcendent, apophatic hypostasis and in its ontological manifestation. One may say that it was precisely in the Virgin Mary that the Trinitarian character of the absolute was disclosed with full metaphysical evidence — a character which is itself, as we showed in the preceding chapters, conjoined with the kenotic orientation. The Most Pure Virgin is the last ontological verge, where the sacrificial, kenotic process rooted in absolute possibility attains fullness and perfection.

The Virgin Mary at the metaphysical level embodies in herself the whole creature in its principial, universal aspect. She is the providential concentration of everything that, in the Christian optic, is not God — of everything not consubstantial with Him. In effect, the Virgin Mary is equal to the whole of creation in its First Day: she is heaven and earth, the Mother of the World, and likewise the Heavenly Ladder joining the one to the other. But principial creation itself — with the aeonic choice of the angels, with the wound of the open metaphysical problem — is the interrogative form of the kenosis of the absolute, expressed in the act of creation. This is the kenotic economy of the Father, which has not yet disclosed the trinitarian nature of Divinity. Only in the economy of the Son (the cycle of the Incarnation), and in the economy of the Holy Spirit inseparably bound to it (before Christ, the Old Testament prefiguration of the prophecies; after, the ecclesial reality between the First and Second Coming), does the economy of the Father become triumphantly affirmative, completed, and fixed. The problem of the choice of the angels, of the tense confrontation between right and left, is annulled in the Incarnation and the Resurrection — in the Church of Christ.

The Theotokos gives the single correct metaphysical answer to the problematic of the appearance of the universe ex nihilo; she becomes the third archangel; she takes in and integrates within herself the third path of Christian metaphysics.

The Virgin Mary — her election, her threefold immaculateness, and finally her Dormition and Ascension into the heavens in the body, her pre-worldly Resurrection, potentially identical with the universal Resurrection of humanity and of the whole universe — mark the completion and the result of the divine sacrifice whose beginning was laid in the apophatic, supra-ontological absolute, which endowed the possibility of being (the “smallest” of the possibilities) with the filial quality. At the following stage, principial trinitarian being goes still further along the path of kenosis and reaches the limit of ontological sacrifice, creating creation “out of nothing,” ex nihilo. But this cycle of sacrificial economy does not at once disclose the whole scope of the metaphysical message enclosed in it. The kenosis of the Son (“who came down from the heavens…, and was incarnate… and was made man… crucified…, and suffered…, and was buried”) and the kenosis of the Holy Spirit (“who spake by the prophets,” and then descended upon the apostles in tongues of flame, and indwells the baptized by the action of grace) open the primordial mystery. And all this is accomplished in the Theotokos and, in a certain sense, for the sake of the Theotokos — in whom and through whom all created being is drawn by grace into the worlds of the absolute, after all the sufferings and wanderings of an estranged existence torn from its immediate cause. The Theotokos is named the Bride of God precisely in this metaphysical aspect: as the limit of the divine kenosis; as that which is principially other than God himself; as the “nugatory,” not existing independently, having in itself neither cause nor being — and yet not repulsed, not cast off, not rejected by God, but accepted, taken, glorified, beloved by Him.

In the person of the Virgin Mary created nature is “deified” — the human and cosmic substance called into being out of “nothing” by the creative impulse (“Let there be!”) of God. As the “Fiat,” the “Let there be” of the Creator, is the timeless first beginning, so the “Fiat” of Mary — “Thy will be done,” “be it unto Me according to thy word” — is the timeless end: the completion of the kenosis, the final point of metaphysics.

In the Most Pure Virgin is disclosed the transcendent mystery of the Love of the absolute: sacrificial Love for that which He is not in himself, by his nature, by his absolute quality — Love for an other than Himself, though such an other, strictly speaking, cannot be. And yet God loves it; and not merely loves — He is, in the apostle’s expression, Love. The dynamic, forceful expansion of the Light of Lights everywhere, beyond all limits, encompassing and transfiguring everything, and even nothing, descends upon the chosen vessel, the immaculate Virgin — who is the absolute object of Divine Love on the far side of history, on the far side of humanity, on the far side of heaven and earth, on the far side of creation and of the aeonic eternity of the angels.

In the annunciation formula of the archangel Gabriel all the “thunders of the absolute” were concentrated: “The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow thee.” This is the mystery of the highest Marriage, the mysterium of absolute Love.

In Orthodox esotericism it is held that the Song of Songs of Solomon refers, in its deepest, metaphysical dimension, precisely to the mystery of this marriage — a marriage redemptive and saving. It is the Immaculate Virgin who says: “Daughters of Jerusalem! I am black, but comely, as the tents of Kedar, as the curtains of Solomon!”114 “Black,” because she is other than God himself, distinct from Him by nature. But “comely,” because she seeks God, gravitates toward him beyond measure, passionately desires the Beloved, the Father of Lights. And the kenotic will of the absolute answers her:

“Thou art all fair, my love; there is no spot in thee.”115

The hierogamy is accomplished as Resurrection. The creature is deified and transfigured. The body, the lower limit of creation, is illumined by the Divine Presence and, in a supra-rational manner, passes beyond the bounds of creation. The universe, through the Theotokos, is as it were overturned into the interior of the divine Trinity, finding itself no longer outside but within. The logic of ordinary, non-Christian metaphysics is rejected. The wisdom of the world is put to shame. Love has conquered everything — law, fate, limits, order, necessity.

“He brought me to the banqueting house, and his banner over me was Love.”116

Apparatus · Chapters XIV–XVII

Apparatus

Source and notes. This instalment is translated from the litresp transcription, which carries the complete text of Part III but strips Dugin’s footnote markers entirely (they fall in the range following no. 88); the numbered apparatus will be restored from the paginated source in the complete reader edition. The chapter title «Глава ангелов» turns on a pun the English cannot keep: glava means both “head/chief” and “chapter” — the chapter about the Head of the angels.

Scriptural and liturgical citations. “He that is least in the Kingdom of God is greater than he,” Luke 7:28; “in the power of Elijah,” Luke 1:17; the Woman clothed in the sun, Rev. 12:1; “Ye are of your father the devil…,” John 8:44 (here correctly given as Christ’s words — contrast Chapter XI); “Fear not, Mary…” and “The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee…,” Luke 1:30, 35 (KJV); “be it unto me according to thy word,” Luke 1:38; “upon thy right hand did stand the queen” and “the King’s daughter is all glorious within,” Ps. 44:10, 14 LXX (= Ps. 45:9, 13 of the Hebrew numbering); the tongs and the coal, Isa. 6:6–7 — a genuine Orthodox hymnographic image of the Theotokos as the “tongs of the divine Coal”; the Song of Songs citations are 1:5, 4:7, and 2:4 (KJV). “Champion Leader” renders «Взбранная Воевода», the opening of the Akathist kontakion. The “Flowery Triodion” («Цветная Триодь») is the Pentecostarion. The seven-stage table of Chapter XVI maps the classical hesychast method (guarding of the heart, breath-linked Jesus Prayer, vision of the uncreated light) onto the Marian feasts.

What is tradition and what is Dugin. Several elements here have genuine patristic or liturgical standing: Palamas’s homilies do present the Entry into the Temple as the Virgin’s hesychast noviciate and do argue that she was first to see the risen Christ; the liturgy does exalt her as “more honorable than the cherubim” and Queen of angels. But the load-bearing structural claims of Chapter XIV — that she occupies the vacated throne of Lucifer; that she is “the third archangel” of a Third Path over against both the “good” creationist angels (recast as “satan,” patrons of Judaism) and the morning star; that the loyal angels of the Old Testament are “not so very good” — are Dugin’s own esoteric construction, without dogmatic warrant, and they retro-fit his creationism/manifestationism scheme onto Mariology. Likewise “gods of the second order” (Ch. XV) is his coinage, closer to Neoplatonic theurgical vocabulary than to the patristic idiom of theosis, though Ps. 82:6/John 10:34 stands behind it at a distance. Note also that «Непорочное Зачатие» in Chapter XV means the virginal conception of Christ; it should not be confused with the Roman dogma of the Immaculate Conception (Mary’s own conception free of original sin), which Orthodoxy in fact rejects — an irony given the chapter’s anti-Latin polemic. The claim that Guénon ascribed a “masculine” quality to the Holy Spirit is Dugin’s gloss on scattered remarks; Guénon wrote no systematic treatment of the question.

Reading note. Chapter XIV is the answer promised at the end of Chapter XI, and it is the boldest move in the book: the Theotokos affirms formally the same thing as Lucifer — the contact of the uncreated with the manifest — differing only in modality (free gift versus necessity). Dugin concedes in so many words that “one aspect of his assertion was metaphysically true.” The rebel angel of Part I is thus not refuted but out-narrated: his truth is re-founded on grace and his throne given to the Virgin. Whether this constitutes the overcoming of the Luciferian position or its baptism is the central interpretive question of the whole book — and Golovin’s review, from the other side of the line, plainly read it as the latter.

Markers 89–116 have now been restored from the studfile transcription (pages 14–17), which preserves them: XIV carries 89–97, XV only 98, XVI a dense 99–113, XVII 114–116 (the three Song of Songs citations). All twenty-eight were anchored to their exact positions against the Russian; continuity 88 → 89…116 → 117 across Parts II–IV is now closed, and the edition’s marker convention holds without exception.

Part IV · The Initiatic Meaning of the Christian Sacraments

Part IV · Chapter XVIII

The Sacraments in the Eastern
and Western Churches

Таинства в Восточной и Западной Церквях

The division of the single grace-filled ray that shone forth in the Incarnation into two parts — the “cause of salvation” and the “cause of deification” — was reflected in the division of the Churches. This division is neither absolute nor final. Historically, however, the schism fixed a deep mystical difference between the two Churches, expressed not only in the otherness of their metaphysical perspectives but in the very quality of the Christian sacraments.

Guénon, in his article “Christianity and Initiation,”117 pointed out that the Christian Church, from a certain moment onward, identified itself unambiguously with exotericism, becoming a religion only,118 and that its sacraments lost their initiatic meaning. This means that Christianity, in Guénon’s optic, turned into a soteriological religion of the Judeo-Christian type. On this ground Guénon uses the term “Judeo-Christianity” for the whole Christian tradition.

In the same article Guénon speaks of a primordial Christianity that had another, initiatic nature, but he holds that this nature changed sharply at the moment when the Church became the official confession of the Roman Empire. From that moment, Guénon supposes, the Christian sacraments lost their initiatic meaning and turned into purely exoteric rituals oriented toward securing the “salvation of the soul” — that is, toward improving the quality of the “subtle form” within the individual sphere of the lower waters. Guénon does not thereby deny the participation of the Holy Spirit in the sacraments, but hints that His action at a certain moment radically changed its quality, affecting churched Christians only as a “saving” cause “external” to their being.

From our point of view, Guénon is right in everything that concerns the Catholic Church, which mystically and dogmatically did indeed take the path of exotericism and thereby radically changed the quality of the ecclesial sacraments. Initiation in the West became something separate from the Church proper — which explains the presence of so many independent initiatic organizations in all the regions where Catholicism spread. But the Eastern Church constitutes an entirely particular metaphysical reality, to which the considerations Guénon voiced do not apply.

A strictly exoteric line was the distinguishing mark of the “Western” fathers (Latin and North African alike) long before the final schism, and one can therefore understand Guénon, who investigated the strictly Catholic and proto-Catholic line of Christianity. Nonetheless it is illegitimate to extend this argument to the whole of Christianity, since in the East, both before the schism and after it, there always existed a sharply pronounced initiatic, esoteric, and metaphysical branch, which in large measure determined the disposition of the Orthodox tradition. One must not confuse the imperial Christianity of Byzantium, which preserved the inner, “deifying,” initiatic dimension, with the Christianity of the Western Roman Empire — with its Latinizing rationalism, its Judeo-Christian theocratism, and its clause on the filioque, illegitimately introduced into the Orthodox Symbol of Faith. And it was precisely this introduction that irreversibly sealed the exoteric and “Judeo-Christian” character of the Western Church.119

Orthodoxy (Byzantine, and subsequently Russian) never in the whole course of its history made a decisive gesture in that direction; and notwithstanding the contradictoriness and multiplicity of its paths, it never lost, to the very end, the fundamental metaphysical orientation primordially laid down in the tradition of the Good News and of the Incarnation of God. This concerns not only the dogmatic foundations, the theological specificity, the monastic practice, and the contemplative nature of the “Eastern” ascetics, but also the ecclesial sacraments themselves, their initiatic quality. This does not mean that exotericism and the line of “salvation” are absent from Orthodoxy. They are unquestionably present in it — and, more than that, in the quantitative sense it is precisely such exoteric Orthodoxy that predominates and constitutes the most frequent case. But this state of affairs depends no longer on any modification of the Church’s mystical orientation, but on the quality of the flock, which in its majority cannot lay claim to anything more than the role of the “called.” The metaphysical and initiatic dimension never disappears from Orthodox Christian reality in the process, even if its full realization becomes with time an ever more exceptional and rare phenomenon. This state of affairs explains, in particular, the circumstance that in the Orthodox world special extra-ecclesial initiatic organizations and orders are practically absent: here there is no reason whatever to carry them outside the Church, and the whole esoteric dimension opens to the “chosen” within the bosom of Orthodoxy itself.

One of the most important indicators of this difference is the presence or absence, in Christian temples, of the iconostasis separating the altar precinct from the temple proper. The Orthodox tradition holds that the threefold division of the temple into:

  1. the altar,
  2. the church (the nave, the “ship”),120
  3. the narthex (“that which stands before the temple”)

corresponds to three ontological levels —

  1. the uncreated, “hyperuranian” world of principles (“that which is higher than the heavens”),
  2. the created celestial world of the angels,
  3. the formal worlds of the lower waters —

and likewise to three categories of the flock:

  1. the clergy, the priests, ordained to the apostolic ministry;
  2. the faithful — churched, full Christians;
  3. the catechumens and penitents, who have come to know the Christian truth from outside and have not yet been vouchsafed holy baptism.121

The iconostasis in Orthodoxy symbolically separates the “hyperuranian” world of principles from the created heavens of the temple by a wall impenetrable to the gaze, adorned with particular icons, whose doors are opened only at determinate moments of the divine service. This points to the possibility of a contact of the created world with the uncreated — a unique metaphysical moment, reflecting the great paradox of the Good News. The first analogues of the present iconostases (earlier, curtains) appeared in Christian temples at the end of the third and the beginning of the fourth century, when the transition took place from the pure esotericism of the first Christians — who were, all without exception, “priests” and “hierophants” of Christ — to the dual division of the flock into “baptized” laity and ordained clergy. The iconostasis symbolizes the boundary between the created and the uncreated world — a boundary which, thanks to Christ, is not absolutely impassable, but which does not on that account disappear. Thus (against Guénon) the exotericization of the Christian tradition does not entail with necessity the loss of the initiatic and esoteric dimension, but only hierarchizes the flock, always leaving open the possibility of a direct connection between its levels. This is symbolized by the periodic opening of the Royal Doors, whence the priest comes out to the faithful for the communion of the Holy Mysteries. It is precisely the iconostasis that is the distinguishing sign of the inviolability and preservation of the esoteric level of the Orthodox Church.122

In the Catholic Church the altar precinct is not separated in any way at all from the space of the temple itself. From this only two conclusions can be drawn: either Catholicism is an exclusively esoteric organization, and all members of the flock admitted to the divine service are ordained and constitute one unbroken apostolic priesthood; or, on the contrary, the supra-celestial, “hyperuranian” element has here been brought down to the level of created celestial reality — and accordingly the sacred action pertains not to the metaphysical mystery of the union of the created with the uncreated, but to a contact of natural createdness with “supernatural” createdness (in the schoolmen’s expression). It is perfectly evident that only the second answer will be correct, since all baptized laypeople without exception may attend (and always might attend) the Catholic church — and by no means only the clergy and the priests.

Hence the justice of Guénon’s assertion about the change in the quality of the Christian sacraments in the Catholic Church: for in this case the participation of the Holy Spirit in the ritual — be it baptism, eucharist, marriage, chrismation, and so on — is henceforth effected mediately, through the created celestial reality of the “supernatural” level; and this means that the metaphysical result of these sacraments cannot pass beyond the bounds of “salvation,” or at best can give an impulse toward the realization of celestial sanctity. Every “deifying” force of the sacrament is here absent, and the light of transubstantiation or of “birth from above” (even the epiclesis itself, the descent of the Holy Spirit) figures as a supernatural action of a created, though highest, order. Among the Orthodox the same force, the same transfiguring ray, manifests itself in its primordial and unmediated nature, as uncreated reality; and accordingly the metaphysical essence of the sacrament, and its action upon human beings, is here radically other.123

The second point vividly illustrating the difference in the nature of the Churches is the canonical question of admitting or not admitting marriage for the clergy. From the Orthodox point of view, the priesthood forms a particular caste — not closed, as in India, but open. The priesthood differs from monasticism in that the monk is occupied only with personal salvation and, in determinate cases, with metaphysical realization, whereas the priest is called to accomplish the liturgical feat of the good tidings and of the mystical drawing of the outer flock into the ray of salvation and deification. Marriage is one of the forms of the extension of priestly grace, and fits beautifully into the logic of the clergy’s metaphysical function. The priest fulfills an impersonal sacred duty and is therefore guided by extra-individual norms and provisions. The idea of priestly celibacy adopted in Catholicism confuses the concepts of priesthood and monasticism, individualizing and narrowing the caste functions of the priesthood. Thus a Catholic priest cannot belong to a “caste” of priests: each time he must come out of some other estate, which makes the fulfillment of his mission something exclusively individual, and consequently limited — whereas the Orthodox clergy is often founded on the caste principle, which excludes neither the possibility of representatives of other castes entering this estate, nor the voluntary hieromonasticism of individual priests, which is, on the contrary, a necessary condition for receiving the episcopal rank.

This difference is, of course, less expressive than the presence of the iconostasis in the temples; nonetheless, for the traditionalist view it plays a very considerable role. Thus Guénon, in particular, grounded the absence of a “sacerdotal” estate in the Christian West precisely on the fact that its formal representatives, the “clergy,” are almost always scions of the families of the warrior aristocracy, the “nobles,” who, from the traditionalist point of view, possess a particular manner of perceiving the sacred and are inclined to regard the metaphysical principles exclusively mediately, through cosmological, strictly created instances.

Be that as it may, an unambiguous assertion must be made: the Orthodox tradition and all the Orthodox sacraments have preserved their initiatic dimension, and Orthodox dogmatics is transparent and non-contradictory from the standpoint of grasping and realizing the metaphysical possibility opened by the Incarnation of God. In other words, Orthodoxy is essentially the genuine and primordial Christianity in its whole scope, and this quality the Eastern Church preserves to this day, notwithstanding all historical cataclysms and tragic moments. Catholicism, for its part, irreversibly lost this metaphysical dimension at the ritual-dogmatic level, and the Roman Church substantially changed the primordial metaphysical and initiatic nature of Christianity, becoming a simple soteriological exotericism of the Judeo-Christian type.

Hence the prospect of a return to the One Church (if one proceeds from the actual duality of the churches) can be understood exclusively as the return of the Roman Church into the bosom of Orthodoxy — which is the single and all-encompassing Christian tradition, uniting both the exoteric and the esoteric sides. Properly speaking, it is precisely Orthodoxy that is the One Church, in need of no supplement whatever; and Catholicism, by and large, is nothing other than the “Latin heresy.”124

The Church is one, as the Truth is one, as the Son of God is one.

Part IV · Chapter XIX

The Protestant Question

Протестантский вопрос

We spoke above of the two Christian Churches, Eastern and Western, but made no mention of Protestantism, which is likewise exceedingly widespread in the modern world. From Guénon’s point of view (a very just one on the whole), Protestantism cannot be called a tradition in the full sense of the word at all, since it denies nearly all the dogmatic and mystical foundations of Christianity, acknowledging only the Gospel Revelation — which within Protestantism may be interpreted arbitrarily, according to the individual or collective capacities of a person or a group. This is what produced such an abundance of intra-Protestant currents and sects, founding their worldview on a fragmentary, accidental, and arbitrary interpretation of the Bible, and renouncing the fundamental dogmas, rituals, sacraments, hierarchy, and liturgy.

It is perfectly evident that in this case one cannot speak of metaphysics, nor of a full ontological doctrine. More than that: even at the level of cosmology Protestantism confines itself to the most earthly, ethico-social plane, having turned into a secularized morality and a social teaching.

But it is interesting to note another aspect, often overlooked by the traditionalists. First: within Protestantism there are two principal versions, each uniting in turn a multitude of smaller sects. The first is Lutheranism — the form of Protestantism that owes its structure to Martin Luther, who formulated an imposing code of anti-Catholic, anti-Vatican views. Lutheranism strove to return to early Christianity, holding the Vatican period of the Church to be a perversion of the primordial idea and a departure from the clarity of the Good News. At the base of Luther’s critique lay not so much historical, political, individual, or social motives (as is wrongly supposed today) as a principial dissatisfaction with that compromised mystical nature of Catholicism of which we spoke above. Luther reproached the Vatican with Judeo-Christianity, with “Pharisaism,” with distorting the metaphysical meaning of the Incarnation, which is addressed to the heart of every Christian, bypassing interpreters, “scribes,” and intermediaries. The Lutherans reject the supremacy of the Roman Pope and cast off ecclesial liturgical ritualism and the Christian sacraments as a “needless” barrier between the human heart and the fullness of divine Love.

It is curious that the Lutheran branch of Protestantism advances against Catholicism practically the same arguments as Orthodoxy. The Orthodox likewise hold that the dogmatic enactments after the first seven Ecumenical Councils do not possess the same unambiguous truth as before, and that the dogmatic creativity of the Vatican consequently only restricts the metaphysical freedom of the faithful. By and large, Orthodoxy also reproaches the Vatican with Judeo-Christianity, as does Lutheranism. The permission of marriage for the priesthood, on which Luther insisted, brings the Lutherans close to the Orthodox on yet another question — and we saw in the preceding chapter with what inner significance this question is charged in a full traditionalist perspective. The apostle Paul was the Lutherans’ favorite Gospel authority, and sometimes the whole of Protestantism is even called “the Church of the apostle Paul.” But it is precisely Paul who is the most perfect exponent of the strictly Christian metaphysics, of the genuine Christianity of the third path, where there is “neither Jew nor Greek.”125

Only on those questions which concern the renunciation of the sacraments, the liturgy, the icons, and the other attributes of the Christian cult does a deep divergence arise between Orthodoxy and Protestantism of the Lutheran type.

Lutheranism produced in its initial period profound contemplatives and theologians — such as Jacob Boehme, Theodor Gichtel, von Baader, and others — whose cosmological and theological ideas were far closer to the Orthodox contemplative tradition than to Catholic mysticism (which is precisely why Boehme, for example, was so popular in certain Orthodox circles). But for many reasons Luther’s anti-Catholic Reformation, notwithstanding its in many respects just orientation and its warranted striving to return to the metaphysical sources of Christianity, could not take its stand on the only correct — the Orthodox — point of view, which would have confirmed the Protestant-grounded anti-Catholicism while at the same time preserving the Reformation from sectarianism and heresy, and would have consecrated by its apostolic authority and mystical nature the just protest of all those seekers after genuine metaphysical life who were drawn to Lutheranism. So one may say that Lutheranism, especially in its first periods, was a rather ambiguous reality, having both a negative and a positive side. And the most negative things in it were the renunciation of the sacraments, the dogmatic revisionism, and the incapacity to take the Orthodox point of view.

The second variant of Protestantism was embodied in Calvin and Calvinism. Formally a part of the general process of the Reformation, this movement had a metaphysical meaning opposite to the Lutheran. Calvinism came out against Catholicism from almost Old Testament positions, striving to “purify” Roman Christianity of what was most Christian in it. Calvin, unlike Luther, appealed most of all precisely to the Old Testament tradition, striving to oppose to the Catholic “Judeo-Christian” compromise a still more Judaized version of Christianity. In Calvinism the most negative sides of the Reformation were absolutized, and the “materialist,” moralistic, and social aspect of Ebionism was revived. The role of Jesus Christ and of the Incarnation was brought down to the Judaic understanding of the function of the messiah, with an almost complete renunciation of all the strictly Christian metaphysical tendencies. And it is precisely in the case of Calvinism that one may speak of an unambiguously negative, destructive role of the Reformation and of anti-Catholicism. Calvinist theology was a parody of Catholic scholasticism, deprived of every metaphysical or fully cosmological element (which was preserved, in spite of everything, in Catholic theology). It is precisely the Calvinist variant of Protestantism that cannot be numbered among the varieties of Christianity as such at all, since in it all the fundamental dogmatic, ritual, and metaphysical principles of the essentially Christian Faith have been lost.

In comparison with the Orthodox Church, Calvinism constitutes something directly opposite to it — a finished and consistently anti-Orthodox doctrine to an even greater degree than an anti-Catholic one.

But on the whole, in the final reckoning, all the varieties of Protestantism were a departure from the essence of ecclesial truth; consequently no genuine mystical, still less initiatic, content can be found in them. The Reformation, in both its Lutheran and its Calvinist variants, led in the end to the loss of any effective participation, in this form of “pseudo-Christianity,” of the uncreated divine element — both direct (as in full Orthodoxy) and indirect (as in Catholicism). Consequently the whole field of the Protestant confessions stands radically outside the transfiguring reality of the Church, and nothing that pertains to the effectiveness of the metaphysical and “supernatural” (in Catholic terminology) factors constituting the meaning and essence of the Christian tradition is applicable to it.

Part IV · Chapter XX

The Meaning of Initiation

Смысл инициации

Having made these clarifications concerning the difference between Orthodoxy and its spiritual nature, on the one hand, and the other Christian confessions on the other, let us pass to the elucidation of the initiatic content of the principal Christian sacraments. This initiatic meaning — which was the source and foundation of early Christianity, itself wholly and entirely initiatic and esoteric — has been preserved in Greek, Slavic, Georgian, and Romanian Orthodoxy, and likewise in certain other Churches of the East. In Catholic reality the analogous sacraments changed their quality in a purely exoteric key, and in Protestantism they were abolished altogether. This must constantly be borne in mind, to avoid contradictions in the analysis of so complex a question as Christian initiation.

Let us begin with what is called in the esoteric tradition the “lesser mysteries.” The initiatic meaning of this ritual is excellently described by Guénon.126 Its essence comes down to the following. — The human individual, incarnating in earthly reality, falls into a regime of estranged existence, in which the true ontological proportions between subject and object, inner and outer, genuine and illusory, one and many, and so on, are inverted. The manifestationist doctrine holds this to be a consequence of “maya,” a special universal cosmic force casting over reality a distorting veil, thanks to which the true nature of things and beings is darkened. The Christian tradition names it a consequence of “original sin,” or the state of the “fallen Adam.” The individual in this state abides in a condensed, discrete world — beset by passions, by the stream of estranged reflection, subject to the external, fatal laws of social, psychic, and physical limitation. It matters little whether this is accounted the “era of the law,” as in the creationist religions, or the dark epoch of the reign over the world of the “dark goddess,” as in the Kali-yuga of the Hindus — the essence of the estrangement remains one and the same.

Such a status of the individual human being — abiding in a regime of estrangement from its essence and from the fullness of cosmic possibilities (of both the psychic and the spiritual planes), situated on the periphery at an indefinitely great remove from the center — can have three fundamental existential perspectives.

1) The first consists in the individual’s renouncing entirely any attempt to change his inner quality, voluntarily becoming the object of dim passions, psychic impulses, and the stream of his own decentered consciousness. This level may be called “profane”; and notwithstanding the seeming naturalness of such a choice, until the most recent centuries it remained a rather rare exception in comparison with the other orientations.

2) The second perspective is the exoteric one. It presupposes that the individual is guided in his existence by certain external ethical, rational, and social norms, which he accepts as an orientation toward the “good.” In this case the basic quality of the individual does not change, nor do his basic existential and ontological parameters; but his existence acquires a determinate centeredness and meaningfulness — while the pole toward which the man strives always remains external to him. This, properly, is what is called the outer side of tradition, called to draw people into a spiritual, specially organized existence — not, however, as full and conscious subjects, but as half-conscious objects, whose perfect spiritual awakening is deferred in advance for an indefinitely long term.

3) The third perspective is initiation proper. This is a process that radically and instantaneously changes the very status of the individual being, leads it out from under the yoke of estranged, fatal conditionedness, raises it to a radically other state, incommensurable with dwelling in the fallen world. It demands of a man incredible efforts, directed against the stream of fate into which he is plunged together with birth, against the whole “natural” course of events, against the most fundamental logic of the outer and inner worlds.
