In initiation even the outer “law” itself, exotericism, is partly rejected — since it is primordially oriented toward non-radicality, toward “evolutionism,” toward the gradual improvement of the quality of being. Though for chaotic and spiritually deficient individuals the religious law itself may be saving and positive, from a certain moment it too becomes a barrier to the spiritual realization of certain exceptional beings, a limitation on their way to the center of the cosmos and beyond its bounds.

Initiation has a complex structure and several stages. But from the very beginning the initiatic path is oriented quite particularly, and is distinct from the exoteric path. The man who chooses initiation is at once drawn into a special reality, conjoined with far greater risk than the existence of the profane and the exoterics. In order really to change the quality of one’s nature, one must subject one’s own individuality to a radical and dangerous transformation, comparable to such a liminal phenomenon as physical death.

The first step of initiation is precisely initiatic death: the mortification of the “old man,” the traumatic renunciation of that reality upon which individual, social, and natural actuality is founded. Such initiatic death is comparable to madness, to the loss of reason.

There follows the “new birth,” the “second birth,” or “birth from above.” This means that in the place of the man who has “died the initiatic death” a new essence is installed, a “new man” — accompanied likewise by the acquisition of a new initiatic name, corresponding to the inner quality of the essence that has manifested itself in the initiate. The “newness” of this being consists in its belonging to a qualitatively other, higher level of reality, and accordingly possessing incomparably greater possibilities than the “old man.” The “new birth” is the first stage of initiation and constitutes what tradition calls the “lesser mysteries.”

In the case of actual humanity, initiation into the “lesser mysteries” means not only the expansion or change of individual qualities, but a change in the mode of human nature itself: it is the passage from the state of the fallen Adam to the state of the first Adam — paradisal, polar, abiding on the far side of the fall. The identification of the initiate of the “lesser mysteries” with the paradisal Adam in all fullness is an exceptional case, presupposing the total and complete realization of all the possibilities opened as a result of the initiatic process. Most often a determinate aspect of this Adam is realized, limiting the archetypal fullness but at the same time effectively transfiguring the natural mode of the man’s existence. In other words, the man in such a case becomes one of the faces of Adam, and not the whole of him.

After the realization of the center of the whole individual state — after the attainment of paradise and, more precisely, of the center of paradise — the regime of the “greater mysteries” begins. This is the second principal level of initiation, though both stages may in themselves be subdivided into various degrees according to the concrete initiatic school. Be that as it may, in the process of the “greater mysteries” there takes place the passage of the being — which has restored its paradisal nature but continues to abide in the individual state, at the center of the world of the lower waters — to the higher, supra-individual level, the world of eternity and of the celestial archetypes. This is the full realization of the angelic dimension of the being, which forever quits the world of the lower waters and establishes itself in the Kingdom of Heaven. As in the initiatic death of the “lesser mysteries” the threshold of physical death is in fact overcome — death being no longer, for the being, a radical rupture in the fabric of individual existence (as it is for the profane and the exoterics) — so in the “greater mysteries” there exists a certain likeness of an “initiatic death” of the soul, thanks to which the soul, having merged with the spirit, begins to perceive the spiritual as personal and imperishable. To beings who have realized the “greater mysteries” applies the phrase of the Apocalypse: “on such the second death hath no power.”127

Such is the most general structure of the initiatic process, which has a multitude of variations and nuances according to the concrete tradition. Let us attempt to correlate this initiatic logic with the Christian sacraments, and to find the correspondences to the two principal stages of initiation in the Christian doctrine.

Part IV · Chapter XXI

Birth from Above. The Lesser Mysteries

Рождение свыше. Малые мистерии

Among the Orthodox sacraments, the initiatic ritual of the “lesser mysteries” corresponds to the rite of baptism. This ritual is called “birth from above,” and of it Christ himself said that he who is not “born from above” cannot see the Kingdom of God.128 Baptism possesses all the marks of initiation. In the performance of the rite the priest liturgically proclaims that at this mysterious moment the one being baptized “is buried together with Christ and crucified together with Christ” — that is, he passes through that initiatic death which is the first phase of the “lesser mysteries.” To this corresponds the threefold immersion of the convert in the font. This is the “baptism with water,” or the being’s passage through birth and death in the three worlds of the created universe — in the two worlds of the lower waters and in the world of the upper waters. It is the mystery of the cleansing of the heart, and the potential unlocking of the restrictive created bonds that fatally hold the being within the bounds of creation. Christ is He who “binds and looses.” In the present case, by the power and authority of the Comforter, the Paraclete, the Holy Spirit, sent by the Son of God to the Church after the Ascension, there takes place the “loosing” of the bonds — the dissolution of the causal mechanism of the dense and subtle forms of individuality, and even of the angelic createdness of heaven. This sacred action corresponds to the man’s “co-burial” with the Son of God, who kenotically diminished his uncreated nature for the sake of the descent into the three sectors of created being — which for God fully corresponds to a “burial.” But if for Christ such a “burial” of his uncreated nature was a descent, for the newly converted it is, on the contrary, an ascent, made possible by means of the divine descent of the Son. “God became man, that man might become god.”

The members of the one being baptized the priest marks with cross-shaped seals of chrism, at points corresponding to the number of the wounds of the crucified Jesus Christ. This is the “co-crucifixion.” At this moment there takes place a mysterious assimilation of the human flesh to the flesh of the God-man. In certain initiatic traditions a similar ritual is called the “quickening of the body.” The wounds of Christ — which poured forth the blood shed for the salvation and deification of men, and which symbolize the inexhaustible source of grace — being marked upon the body of the baptized, mysteriously communicate to the Christian’s body a special initiatic quality, henceforth “binding” this body to the higher ontological levels and “loosing” it from the yoke of material, physical determinism. This is the ritual of the effective exit from the era of the law and the entry into the era of grace. From this moment the initiate who has passed through co-crucifixion and co-burial with Christ potentially possesses a new body, a new soul, and a new spirit. He receives within himself the seed of another person — an ecclesial and Christ-bearing person — and this person, however paradoxical it may seem to outside observers, has henceforth an entirely different nature and an entirely different structure from those who have not been vouchsafed to pass through this rite.

This difference, however, can be registered only with the help of special spiritual methods, of whose very existence the majority do not even suspect. The “co-crucifixion,” chrismation, is a mystery performed by the Holy Spirit. It is the second and higher half of the baptismal rite.

In the baptism with water what is at issue is only a preliminary, intra-created, potential transformation of the being. It is merely a preparation for the reception of the second, incomparably more important side of baptism, which consists in the baptism with the Holy Spirit and with fire (which in determinate cases serves as a Christian symbol of the Holy Spirit). If the baptism with water is the function of John the Forerunner, the last Old Testament prophet, “preparing the ways of the Lord,” the baptism with the Holy Spirit is a strictly New Testament mystery, in the charge only of the Only-begotten Son of God.

The baptism with the Holy Spirit is the potential change of the whole nature of the created being — the opening, in the human center, of a paradoxical and supra-rational dimension joining this being directly with the uncreated, with the light of Tabor, with the radiance of the divine energies of the glory of the Most Holy Trinity. This is a baptism into the supra-celestial reality of the principles, and only those who have passed through it can truly say of themselves: “the Kingdom of God is within us.” This baptism with the Holy Spirit is also the personal Pentecost of the newly converted, since at this moment there descends upon him a tongue of the divine flame, becoming henceforth a constant inner quality inseparable from him.

The cleansing of the being and the quickening of the body must of necessity precede the culminating moment of initiation — the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the Christian. It is important to note the function in this ritual of the godparents, the godfather and godmother. These roles cannot be performed by the natural father and mother, since what is at issue is not a fleshly but a spiritual birth of the “new man,” whose “parents” (more precisely, receivers of the child) henceforth become a Christian man and a Christian woman considered impersonally, as representatives of the single mystical soul — the Orthodox Church. The godparents figure here as bearers of the same initiation which the newly baptized is now undergoing. Here one may adduce the words of Christ: “And every one that hath forsaken house, or brethren, or sisters, or father, or mother (emphasis ours — A.D.), or wife, or children, or lands, for my name’s sake, shall receive an hundredfold, and shall inherit everlasting life.”129

At this unique moment there takes place a “birth from above” in the most literal sense: a birth from God, the adoption of the created man by the uncreated Trinity, the man being called henceforth to the path of deification. Only now is the believer entitled to address God with the words of the chief Christian prayer, “Our Father.” Before baptism such an assertion sounds like blasphemy. The apostle Paul asserted in this regard that it is the Holy Spirit himself who cries in our heart: “Abba, Father!”130 — that is, only the new, potentially deified person is permitted to address God as Father; and even in this case such an assertion proceeds not from the created human nature, but from the Holy Spirit, given to the faithful through the fiery baptism.

The new person, overshadowed by the henceforth inalienable presence of the Holy Spirit, receives in baptism a new name as well. The old man dies; the New Man is born.

As a symbol of the renewal and completion of the “lesser mysteries,” the newly baptized is clothed in a white garment, the baptismal robe, symbolizing the new flesh which the Christian henceforth possesses. This white garment figures in practically all initiatic traditions and signifies, in alchemical terminology, the “work in white” — the spiritual realization of a principially purified and transfigured soul.

In Orthodoxy, immediately after baptism the faithful one comes to his first communion, his confirmation (in distinction from Catholicism, where confirmation takes place after some time, often after several years). Guénon underscores that one must strictly distinguish the ritual of initiation from the initiatic ritual — that is, the particular rite in which the consecration itself is effected, from those rites which the initiate performs with varying periodicity. The eucharist is precisely an initiatic ritual, to which only the faithful are now admitted — Christians who have passed through the mystery of baptism. Tasting the flesh and blood of the Living God, the newly baptized now receives the highest sacrifice into the bodily temple of the New Man created by the grace of baptism. Cleansed and “born from above,” the Christian becomes a sanctuary capable of receiving into itself the kenotically diminished Son. By the first communion the new structure of the new person is confirmed and strengthened; the first step is made toward the spiritual life and total transfiguration, toward the deification of the new being.

The first post-baptismal communion is the final point of the ritual of the “lesser mysteries,” their completion, their apotheosis.

It is important to observe that baptism is the most universal and important Christian sacrament, since it alone is mentioned in the Symbol of Faith. Baptism is the foundation of the whole Christian realization, of the whole of Orthodox metaphysics. On it are founded, and from it flow, all the other initiatic rituals, rites, and sacraments. And yet, more concretely, baptism pertains to the restoration of the paradisal fullness of reality, to the return to the earthly paradise, to the establishment of the individual at the center of the things and beings of the dense and subtle worlds. Though it is precisely in baptism that the foundations of sanctity and deification are laid — that is, the preconditions for the acquisition of the Kingdom of Heaven or even of the Kingdom of God — this ritual nonetheless pertains more to the first stage of initiation, in which what is at issue is the potential acquisition of the earthly paradise. To this point the various symbols of the baptismal ritual — the use of water, the clothing in white garments, and so on.

Apparatus · Chapters XVIII–XXI

Apparatus

Sources and notes. Translated from the WikiReading transcription, which preserves Dugin’s footnote markers (nos. 117–130 fall in these chapters); the note contents will be restored in the complete reader edition. One source typo («вятого» for «святого») is corrected silently.

The argument with Guénon. Part IV is structured as a point-by-point answer to Guénon’s verdict — in “Christianisme et initiation” (1949; gathered posthumously in Aperçus sur l’ésotérisme chrétien) — that the Christian sacraments descended from initiatic to merely religious rites when the Church became the imperial confession. Dugin’s strategy is concession-and-partition: Guénon is granted everything with respect to Rome and refuted with respect to the East, the iconostasis serving as the physical proof-text. Chapter XX’s profane/exoteric/initiatic triad, the lesser/greater mysteries, and the distinction between the rite of initiation and initiatic rites all reproduce the framework of Guénon’s Aperçus sur l’initiation. “On such the second death hath no power” is Rev. 20:6; “born from above,” John 3:3 (where the Greek anōthen means both “again” and “from above” — the Slavonic, like Dugin, reads “from above”); “Abba, Father,” Rom. 8:15/Gal. 4:6; the hundredfold saying, Mark 10:29–30, with Dugin’s own emphasis on “mother” preserved as marked.

Historical cautions. (1) The iconostasis argument outruns the archaeology: sanctuary curtains and low templon barriers are indeed attested from the fourth century, but the tall, opaque, icon-covered wall Dugin treats as the confession’s essential mark is a late-medieval development, reaching its classic form in fourteenth–fifteenth-century Rus'; Byzantine sanctuaries for most of the first millennium were visually open. (2) The claim that the Catholic altar precinct “is not separated in any way at all” holds only for post-Tridentine and especially post-conciliar arrangements; the medieval West separated choir and nave with rood screens and jubés, many still standing. (3) On clerical marriage the contrast is real but simplified: the East ordains married men but requires celibate (monastic) bishops, as Dugin notes; the Western discipline was consolidated only in the eleventh–twelfth centuries. (4) “Theodor Gichtel” should be Johann Georg Gichtel (1638–1710), Boehme’s editor. (5) The chrismation of the senses is real (brow, eyes, nostrils, lips, ears, breast, hands, feet, with the words “the seal of the gift of the Holy Spirit”), and calling chrismation the believer’s “personal Pentecost” is standard Orthodox catechesis; but the correspondence of the anointing points to “the number of the wounds of Christ,” and the reading of chrismation as “co-crucifixion,” are Dugin’s esoteric glosses, not the rite’s self-description. (6) The Luther/Calvin typology — Lutheranism as near-Orthodoxy manqué, Calvinism as “still more Judaized” religion outside Christianity altogether — reproduces a polemical trope of early-twentieth-century literature (Sombart’s inversion of Weber) rather than any tenable history of doctrine: Luther retained baptismal regeneration and the real presence, which the chapter’s blanket “renunciation of the sacraments” flattens, and Calvin’s sacramental theology is saturated in the Greek fathers. The anti-Judaic frame that has run through the book since Part II here becomes the sorting principle for entire confessions.

Translator’s terms. «малые/великие мистерии» = the lesser/greater mysteries; «спогребается и сраспинается» = is buried together with, crucified together with (the liturgical со- prefix of Rom. 6:4, rendered by “co-” where Dugin isolates it as a concept); «детоприимцы» = receivers of the child (sponsors); «работа в белом» = the work in white (albedo); «Царские Врата» = the Royal Doors; «корабль» = the ship (nave — the same metaphor English preserves from Latin navis).

Part IV · Chapter XXII

The Royal Priesthood.
The Greater Mysteries

Царственное священство. Великие мистерии

The Christian rite standing nearest to the “greater mysteries” is ordination (cheirotonia) — the consecration of a faithful Christian to holy orders.131 To judge by determinate places in the Gospel and by the historical sources, in early Christianity all members of the Church were ordained to the priesthood. “But ye are a chosen generation, a royal priesthood, an holy nation, a peculiar people; that ye should shew forth the praises of him who hath called you out of darkness into his marvellous light.”132 This situation lasts approximately until the third century — until the moment when curtains, the prototypes of the iconostasis, appear in the Christian temples. From that period onward the “royal priesthood” becomes the affair of a determinate group of men who have identified their human destiny wholly with the Church, with the performance of the sacraments and of the good tidings.

Ordination, like baptism, discloses its initiatic nature in this: that it can be accomplished only once, and to the very hour of death no circumstance is able to deprive of the initiation the one who has once received it. As baptism is one, so ordination is one. Ordination, moreover, is precisely a ritual of initiation and not simply an initiatic ritual, since it potentially communicates to the human being a particular metaphysical dimension which is thenceforth inalienable from him. Furthermore, at both baptism and ordination in Orthodoxy only the initiated may be present: the baptized in the first case, priests in the second. This corresponds to the initiatic requirements for the performance of a genuine esoteric mystery, in which only those already consecrated to it may take part.

At ordination there is transmitted to the man being consecrated to the priesthood the energy of the apostolic ministry — the power of the Holy Spirit in its archetypal form, as the handing-on of the fire that descended upon the apostles at Pentecost; and the newly consecrated becomes an executor of the apostolic functions and, in a certain sense, an apostle. It is precisely by virtue of this unbrokenness of the apostolic transmission of the sacrament of the priesthood that the Church is called apostolic. Here it may be objected that the personal Pentecost is already accomplished in the rite of baptism, through chrismation and the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the baptized. This is true; but the difference between ordination and baptism consists in this, that the personal Pentecost is here realized in two distinct aspects. In baptism what is at issue is the opening of the possibility of personal transfiguration: henceforth the conjunction of the created individuality with the uncreated Holy Spirit is such that the being can attain the fullness of deification and sanctity by personal spiritual efforts alone, without any supplementary sacrament or rite whatever. But here everything depends on the individual will and the other qualities of the baptized; and in determinate cases (which in our epoch are the most widespread) the baptismal grace and the presence of the Holy Spirit in the heart may not manifest themselves at all, if the being does not intend to walk the path of spiritual realization and to make use of the priceless gift of Christian freedom, capable of making him a “god by participation.” (Christ himself repeated the words of King David: “I said, Ye are gods.”133) Thus the uncreated energy in the baptized constitutes precisely a possibility and not an actuality; consequently the action of the Holy Spirit through the baptized is possible but not necessary — everything depends on his personal efforts.

Ordination transmits to the one being consecrated the uncreated quality of the apostolic flame in another form, not bound directly to the individual’s realization of the fullness of baptismal grace. In the normal case (and so it was in early Christianity) ordination ought to correspond to the individual’s objectivation of this baptismal grace by personal effort. When such objectivation takes place, it is confirmed by the initiatic ritual, in which the apostolic power is transmitted to the man in its manifested and unalterable form: as a supra-individual quality dependent on nothing. And from this moment the Christian becomes a full member of the “chosen generation, the royal priesthood, the holy nation” — an apostle, endowed with the right, in the name of Christ, to “bind and loose,” that is, to perform the initiatic and liturgical rites and sacraments.134 But in determinate situations, when spiritual realization becomes for one reason or another problematic and the portion of exceptional persons (who as a rule prefer monasticism), ordination may also be transmitted on the basis of another logic: not as the confirmation of the objectivation of baptismal grace, but as a transmission — detached from personal realization — of a supra-personal power permitting the effective performance of the ecclesial sacraments independently of the inner readiness or unreadiness of the individual. This circumstance in no way touches the power itself of the fiery consecration into the “greater mysteries,” but it limits the functions of the priest to the conduct of the cult and the performance of the rites, depriving him, in the ordinary case, of the right to be a genuine pastor of the faithful in all questions touching spiritual realization (on the ground, already, that he has not personally accomplished it). In such a case the power of ordination acts “through” the priest, touching his inner quality only indirectly. More than that: the priest himself will scarcely be able to notice the changes that have taken place in his personal structure after passing through the consecration into the “greater mysteries,” if he has not previously moved along the path of the willed realization of the potency of baptism and encountered the spiritual phenomena arising on that path.

This state of affairs, which took shape in determinate periods of church history, is one of the basic underlying causes of the schism within Russian Orthodoxy. The Old Believers insisted on a strict correspondence between the level of initiatic realization and the passage through initiation into the Christian “greater mysteries,” and they therefore perceived with such sharpness the purely functional attitude toward the order of the priesthood. From their point of view, such a rupture meant the “departure of grace from the Church,” its ritual and mystical desacralization. This coincided with the “book correction,” in which — among everything else — the question of “fire” and of the “fiery baptism,” and of the dogmatic definitions of the Holy Spirit, became a stone of stumbling between the Nikonians and the followers of the archpriest Avvakum. The Old Believers held that behind the collation of the Slavonic liturgical books with the modern Greek recensions stood a striving to fix the “desacralization” of ecclesial grace dogmatically — to acknowledge the relativity of the deifying action of the Holy Spirit. Such a position was in essence justified, since at that historical moment the distinction between the esoteric and exoteric planes of church life was gradually passing from harmonious equilibrium into open imbalance.

The role of the symbolism of fire in the Russian Schism135 points indirectly to the essence of the “greater mysteries” themselves, which are called to lead the being out beyond the frame of the “lower waters,” beyond the limits of the formal state, to the far side of the soul, into the world of heaven, into the spheres of supra-formal manifestations, to the angelic type of existence. Angels are often described in theology as fiery beings, reflecting at the created level the uncreated nature of the Holy Spirit. The priest who has passed through ordination realizes the fullness of the angelic archetype while remaining in a human body and preserving an individual soul. The essence of the ordained priest is withdrawn from the world of duration: he participates henceforth in the eternity of the heavens, in the First Day of creation, which has no duration.136 But this eternity manifests itself at the earthly level in the closedness of the liturgical cycle of divine service, whose rotation the priest furthers while remaining in his spirit motionless — like the center of a circle, around which and by virtue of which the wheel of the church year revolves. There are several moments in the liturgy where the divine service is described as flowing simultaneously on two levels: the human and the angelic. Thus “Hosanna in the highest!”, “Alleluia!”, “Holy! Holy! Holy! Lord God of Sabaoth,” and others are angelic hymns, repeated during the service on earth and in heaven. The priest is the conductor, the mediator between these realities — being, by virtue of his initiation into the “greater mysteries,” an angel, while remaining at the same time a man.

The monastic consecration stands apart; Dionysius the Areopagite accounts it the highest formula of initiation into the “greater mysteries.” In distinction from the priestly consecration, the one being tonsured a monk remains during the ritual standing before the altar, not kneeling. Moreover, like the baptismal rite, the monk in the course of the tonsure is clothed in new garments — and this underscores that what is at issue here is a “new birth.” This time it is a “third birth,” a “birth in the heavens,” since the “second birth” was baptism.

All these details of the monastic initiation are plainly correlated with the symbolism of the second order of the angelic hierarchy. To understand this more clearly, let us recall the basic characteristics of the angelic world and its ranks in Dionysius the Areopagite.

All the angels are divided into three orders, with three ranks in each. The first order — seraphim, cherubim, and thrones — is characterized by the contemplation of the Godhead and by His praise. These ranks fall down ceaselessly before the Most High. To this corresponds also the first and highest angelic action: the complete renunciation of self-identity and the glorification of what is other than the angels themselves. This action is identical with contemplation in its highest aspect.

The second order — of dominions, powers, and authorities — unites the angels whose common symbolic action is “standing”: the preservation of vertical, luminous existence. The third order — principalities, archangels, and angels — is the lowest in the celestial hierarchy. Its ranks accomplish the third angelic action: they transmit the celestial energy, the rays of light, downward — to all the beings beneath heaven.

Thus the seraphim, cherubim, and thrones are directed wholly upward and away from themselves. The dominions, powers, and authorities are concentrated upon themselves, upon the affirmation of their luminous existence. And the principalities, archangels, and angels are oriented downward and away from themselves. The general characteristic of the three angelic orders predetermines, moreover, a more concrete hierarchy among the ranks themselves; and the model of the “three angelic actions” is repeated for each rank of the hierarchy in turn.

On the basis of such a description, it is most logical to correlate the monastic order within the church hierarchy precisely with the second celestial order. This is evident already from the fact that the monk passes through the tonsure standing. More than that, the meaning of monastic practice is concentrated precisely in personal spiritual realization — and this coincides exactly with the affirmation of luminous self-identity among the angels of the second order.

By analogy, the priestly consecration should be correlated with the ranks of the third celestial order, since the chief task of priests is the enlightenment of the flock and the evangelization of the universe — the transmission of the luminous grace “downward,” to the simple Christians, to the lower levels of the ecclesial cosmos.

One may also set the three ranks of the church hierarchy beside the three ranks of the lowest angelic order: the bishops will correspond to the principalities, the priests to the archangels, and the deacons to the angels. The differing functions of the three ecclesiastical ranks in the divine service and in the performance of the Christian rites exactly confirm this connection.137

The matter stands more complexly with the threefold division of the monastic order in correspondence with the hierarchy of the second angelic order.138 Moreover, the martial specificity underscored in the angelic names of this order hints, possibly, at the initiatic participation of specifically royal sacrality in this level. It is no accident that many tsars, immediately before their end, received the monastic tonsure and departed already in monastic habit, according to the ecclesiastical rites. What draws tsars and monks together is not only the function of “standing,”139 of the affirmation of luminous self-identity, but also the ritual renunciation of “all that divides.” As the tsar symbolizes the seal of unity and singularity — standing before God personally and in the singular for the whole Orthodox people, for the whole realm — so the monk at his tonsure vows to keep the perfect unity and undividedness of his soul, in deeds and in thoughts.

Be that as it may, between the monastic tonsure and the royal initiation, on the one hand, and the second order of the angelic hierarchy, on the other, a determinate connection exists.

Continuing this analogy, one must pose the question: to what initiatic line within the Christian “greater mysteries” should the first order of angels and its ranks — seraphim, cherubim, thrones — correspond?

To a certain degree, of course, the functions of these ranks are carried over to the bishops, priests, and deacons, who in performing the sacraments accomplish sacred rituals often bound up with the celestial functions of these ranks (whence the images of seraphim, cherubim, and thrones on certain details of the vestments of the clergy). More than that: the church service is raised up not so much for men as for God, as the expression of His unceasing veneration on the part of the universe. But at the purely initiatic level there must exist a category of those consecrated into the “greater mysteries” whose representatives would be predestined exclusively for pure “contemplation” — for complete and absolute turning toward the Lord, for perfect immersion in the beholding of the Glory of the Lord, on the far side both of public service (the action of the third angelic order) and of personal salvation (the action of the second angelic order). Nearest of all to such a category stand the Orthodox elders, the hesychasts,140 who walk the path of perfect and uncompromising metaphysical realization, of finished deification. It is precisely for them that the assertion of Dionysius the Areopagite holds: “our salvation is possible only through our deification.”141 (For the remaining categories of the faithful this maximalist approach cannot be accounted an unconditional truth.)

The initiatic ritual practiced in these most closed circles of Orthodox esotericism, however, is kept in strict secrecy. The one thing that may be supposed is its connection with fire and with the coal, by analogy with the description of the initiation of the prophet Isaiah by the seraph.142 Moreover, it is possible that in the course of the initiation the one being consecrated must prostrate himself, as the ranks of the first angelic order do. Generally speaking, an attentive study of the works of the hesychasts and of their lives from this angle of vision could shed light on this mystery as well. And besides — in spite of all persecutions and oppressions — the tradition of the elders continues to exist to this day, in Russia as in Greece, and likewise in other Orthodox lands (Serbia, Romania, Bulgaria, and so on).

Initiation into the “greater mysteries” in the bosom of the Orthodox Church, however, is not confined to angel-realization. From the very moment of baptism the Christian already becomes potentially higher than the angels, since the grace of the Holy Spirit acts in him from within, and not from without. Therefore in the “greater mysteries” also, the angelic plane is realized as a certain prefigurative, intermediate stage, called to express the inexpressible, and to anticipate the uncreated by created (though celestial) realities. And this means that the Orthodox priesthood, in its metaphysical dimension, passes through the celestial hierarchies — it exercises its priesthood concerning the uncreated by means of the whole spectrum of created instruments, from the corporeal to the angelic. Consequently the celestial hierarchies themselves are saved and deified through the metaphysical realization of Christian human beings. This is why it is said that “the angels look with wonder upon the performance of the church sacraments.” Precisely “with wonder” — for their celestial, eternal omniscience is limited to the created, whereas the Orthodox priests touch directly the uncreated and pre-eternal Glory of the Lord.

Proceeding from these last considerations, a correction must be entered into the preceding exposition: it is not the priests who are likened to the angels, but the angels, in their highest aspect, who are likened to the Christian priests, monks, and elders — for only their human nature has been vouchsafed to enter the uncreated bridal chamber of the Most Holy Trinity together with our ascended Lord Jesus Christ. Consequently, in the initiation, in the service, and in the feats of the Christian initiates there is present a transcendent, uncreated dimension which the celestial hierarchies do not possess.

The angels are saved and deified through men. Through those who have passed through, and realized to the end, the whole cycle of the grace-filled Christian initiation.

Part IV · Chapter XXIII

The Order of Melchizedek

Чин Мельхиседеков

Concerning the Christian priesthood and the fundamental initiatic ritual bound up with it, we must recall the enigmatic personage of the Bible, Melchizedek — since the apostle Paul indicates clearly that the Christian priesthood differs sharply from the Judaic precisely in this: that the second is ordered “after the order of Aaron,” and the first “after the order of Melchizedek.” Elsewhere in Paul, Christ himself is named “High Priest after the order of Melchizedek.”143

Melchizedek was the king of Salem, to whom Abraham brought a tithe (the sign of an almost feudal dependence) after the defeat of his enemies, the kings; Melchizedek himself is called by the apostle Paul “the king of the world.” The apostle Paul says of him: “Without father, without mother, without descent, having neither beginning of days, nor end of life; but made like unto the Son of God; abideth a priest continually.”144 Guénon notes, further, the existence of a hierarchy between the name of the God whom the biblical Melchizedek worshipped (El Elyon — literally, the “Most High”) and the name of the God of Abraham — Shaddai, the “Almighty.” Guénon himself holds that in this biblical episode there is symbolically described the origination of the strictly Judaic tradition, the tradition “of Abraham,” out of the Primordial Tradition (higher, fuller, integral), which in the given context is represented by Melchizedek.145

In the question of the “Abrahamic” (and later “Levitical”) priesthood, on the one hand, and the “supra-Abrahamic” priesthood on the other, the metaphysical nature of the Christian initiation into the greater mysteries is visible with utmost clarity in the eschatological epoch. The Levitical priesthood — or the Abrahamic (creationist) sacerdotium in the broader sense — performs entirely different functions from the Christian priesthood. More than that: the Levitical priesthood does not possess, and cannot possess, a properly initiatic character. The apostle Paul underscores that it (the Levitical priesthood) stands under the law, and not only does not lead out beyond the law’s bounds, but cannot itself pass beyond them.

Yet in the strictly creationist perspective the movement from cause to effect is irreversible, and the lost paradisal state cannot be regained — neither by the shepherds nor by the flock. The apostle utters in the Epistle to the Hebrews a phrase whose metaphysical significance it is difficult to overestimate: “the law made nothing perfect.”146 This means that the observance of the law — the priesthood founded on the creationist theology of the Old Testament alone — is merely a compromise solution of the ontological “drama” of the universe, incapable of really “turning the current of the Jordan back”: of effectively overcoming the one-directional, constantly deepening process of rupture between Creator and creation. The waters of the Jordan flowed backward, according to tradition, when Christ entered them, coming to John the Baptist — which betokens the beginning of a new metaphysical era. “But the bringing in of a better hope did.”147

The Orthodox priesthood is the conciliar reflection of the priesthood of Christ himself. Consequently the liturgical action and the performance of the rites are accomplished by the Orthodox priests together with Christ and the Holy Spirit — and, in a certain sense, by Christ himself and the Holy Spirit through them. The Christian clergy, consisting of all ordained priests (past, present, and future), constitutes a “conciliar Melchizedek,” the king of Salem, the king of the world. In distinction from the Levitical priesthood, the Orthodox priesthood “after the order of Melchizedek” is no longer separated from the uncreated reality by that altar veil which hung in the Temple of Jerusalem. It was rent at the moment of the Savior’s death on the cross. Together with the law and with Abrahamic creationism there was also overcome the mystery of the Old Testament priesthood — which, being able to “make nothing perfect,” discloses itself, in comparison with the Christian, “Melchizedekian” priesthood, as evil and as “devil-worship,” from that moment when (in distinction from Abraham himself) it refuses to bring the tithe and humbly to acknowledge its subordination to the king of Salem, the king of the world — the Church of Christ.

Guénon remarks in this connection that the numerical value of the name “El Elyon” (“Most High”) coincides with the numerical value of the name “Emmanuel” (“God with us”), by which Jesus Christ was called. Here it is interesting to pose the question: in what manner does the Christian tradition, which insists on creationism, admit the existence of a particular figure or anthropological instance plainly falling outside the creationist norms, outside the law — not only after the coming of Christ (this we have explained), but long before that event, and moreover not in the paradisal Adamic state but in the whirlpool of concrete history, following both the fall, and Noah’s flood, and the confusion of Babel? From the point of view of Helleno-Christianity — which gravitates toward the maximally manifestationist perspective and strives, wherever possible, to relativize the rigidity of creationism even within the Old Testament (the desire to rethink the Old Testament “Platonically,” proceeding from the metaphysics of the New, is characteristic of Origen, Clement of Alexandria, Melito of Sardis, and so on) — Melchizedek was the pole of universal esotericism and possessed a direct connection with the uncreated reality: he was an axis of manifestation (and not of creation). There is no need to repeat once more that such an assertion corresponds metaphysically to the “choice of the fallen angels” (Origen, who was clearly aware of this, therefore advanced the theory — later anathematized by orthodoxy — of the “apokatastasis,” the final “salvation of the morning star”). For the Judeo-Christian tradition it is characteristic, on the contrary, to lower the role of Melchizedek by every means; there exists even a version, founded on the linguistic analysis of this passage of the Bible, according to which it was not Abraham who brought the tithe to Melchizedek but, on the contrary, Melchizedek to Abraham. Let us recall also that in the New Testament the apostle Paul, speaking of Melchizedek, was addressing precisely the Hebrews — that is, the Judaist-creationists and the Judeo-Christians of the Jerusalem community, who inclined toward the Ebionite doctrine of Christ the “man-angel” and toward the necessity of circumcision for all Christians; and the theme of Melchizedek was the central theological weapon for the radical rejection of the old creationism, of Judaism, and of Judeo-Christianity, in the doctrinal as in the cultic plane. The creationists themselves, for their part, strive to pass over the figure of Melchizedek in silence altogether — as they do the other places of the Bible (in particular, the episodes of Enoch and Elijah) which fit into the creationist context only with great difficulty.

Genuine Christianity, the Christianity of the third path, must have its own particular metaphysical view of the figure of Melchizedek, so as not to fall into either extreme — “neither the Judaic nor the Hellenic.” Evidently one must here turn to the same reality as in the case of the Theotokos, pointing to the necessity of the eternal presence of the metaphysical tradition of the third path, actualized in history only from the moment of the Incarnation.

Melchizedek, the king of the world, has existed always, exists now, and will exist until the end of history. But his sacerdotium, his bloodless sacrifice of “bread and wine,” is a service “neither Judaic” (Abrahamic) “nor Hellenic” (manifestationist). It is a particular, paradoxical cult, founded on initiation into the greater mysteries, yet resembling neither the manifestationist analogues (the mysteries of Osiris and Isis, the Eleusinian mysteries, the cults of Mithra and Attis, and so on) nor the purely exoteric fabric of the creationist Judaic liturgics.

It is difficult to make even the most distant conjecture concerning this priesthood and its nature. One can only take the path of symbolic correspondences, and continue Guénon’s line in drawing together the three Magi with the three aspects of the “king of the world” — in our case, of Melchizedek.148

The Orthodox tradition, in its interpretation of the Gospel narrative of the adoration of the Magi, specifies that what is at issue are three Persian priests, keepers of a most ancient knowledge, who transmitted along a chain of initiates the prophecy of the coming of the Savior and of the appearance in the heavens of a particular star that would announce it. The indication of Persia — of the ancient Iranian tradition — may be an indirect hint at the existence in its bosom of a particular esoteric current, which was the veiled prefiguration of the historical Christian tradition in the pre-Christian period.

In confirmation of this supposition testify many particularities of the Iranian tradition: its eschatologism (exceedingly close to the biblical prophets), its cyclology (reproduced in the prophet Daniel, in the narrative of the interpretation of Nebuchadnezzar’s dreams), and so on. But most curious of all is the fact that it is precisely the Iranian tradition — belonging on the whole to the family of typically Indo-European manifestationist doctrines — that stands, among them all, furthest from classical Vedantism or Hellenic Platonism. It is no accident that even the names of the gods and demons among the Hindus and the Persians are strictly opposed.149 On the other hand, Mazdaism (or Zoroastrianism) is distinguished by a sharply expressed dualism, a dramatic and passionate understanding of the struggle of good and evil — which is characteristic of the creationist, Abrahamic religions. In a certain sense it is precisely the Iranian tradition that comes nearest to the strictly Christian sacrality — to that combination of creationism and manifestationism which constitutes the essence of Orthodox metaphysics. It is no accident that Iranian esotericism (and later Manichaeism) so influenced the Christian gnostics, who saw in Zoroastrianism and its versions a doctrine very close to the teaching of the Gospels.

Ohrmazd, the Iranian “god” of light, may be set beside the uncreated divine reality. He is conjoined with grace and salvation, with the manifestationist line. Ahriman, the Iranian “god” of darkness, greatly resembles that sinister personage whom the gnostics called the “evil demiurge” — the author of the estranged creation, despot and usurper. The Saoshyant (Savior), as the manifestation of Ohrmazd, must come at the end of times into the universe whose power Ahriman has usurped, and manifest himself through a “virginal conception” by a chosen maiden150 (after her bathing in the lake of the Covenant), in order to save all the “children of light” and to conquer his eternal adversary.

The eschatological scenario of Zoroastrianism, as we see, has a multitude of parallels with the Christian tradition. But the matter does not rest on textual correspondences alone. The very structure of Zoroastrian metaphysics astonishingly recalls the “third choice” of the angels, of which we have spoken more than once, and which is conjoined with the figures of the Theotokos and of Melchizedek. Thus, in distinction from pure manifestationism, Zoroastrianism is piercingly aware of the tragicness and abnormality of the ontological conditions in which humanity abides. The seriousness of this drama is felt by the Iranians far more deeply than by the Hindus or the Neoplatonists. Hence the centrality of the problem of “evil.” This sharp and distinct experience of the “estrangement,” the “deadness” of the manifested makes the Zoroastrians kin to the Jews (more broadly, to the Abrahamic, Semitic tradition) and draws their doctrine near to creationism, where the distance between Creator and creature is likewise felt painfully and acutely. But in distinction from the strict monotheism which resigns itself to the creature’s insignificant lot, the Iranian tradition resolves this problem in favor of the “morning star,” the “angel of light,” who rises up against Ahriman the usurper.

It is no longer surprising, then, that the Magi followed precisely a star — a “morning star.” This was the sign bequeathed to them of the Birth of the True God, called to free the “sons of light” from the law, from the “form of a servant.” And now it is entirely comprehensible why the star is a symbol of the Theotokos: for through her is accomplished the mystery of the Incarnation — the appearance, at the center of the estranged universe, of God himself. The chief difference between the meaning of this Theotokian “morning star” and the sign of Lucifer is the gracious, free nature of the Incarnation — whereas classical Luciferism insists on the necessity, the fatal predetermination, of the creature’s deification.151

From all these correspondences the conclusion may be drawn that the mystery of the “priesthood after the order of Melchizedek” is in some manner connected with the Iranian tradition; and it is no accident that, according to the Bible, paradise lay in the East — and proceeding from the sacred geography of Israel, the “East” for the promised land was precisely Persia (Iran). Moreover, it must be recalled that the Hebrew word pardes (literally, “paradise”) is a borrowing from the ancient Persian paradesha, which meant “enchanted garden.”

The full spiritual realization of ordination must lead the priest to identification with the very essence of him who is “without father, without mother, without descent, having neither beginning of days, nor end of life.” And in the bosom of Melchizedek, the king of the world — as in the bosom of Abraham for the Jews — must rest the spirit of the highest Christian initiates, the priests of the Living God, Jesus Christ.

Apparatus · Chapters XXII–XXIII

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 131–151 are preserved. Ch. XXII: the “royal priesthood” is 1 Pet. 2:9 (a Petrine text, though the chapter leaves the attribution open); “I said, Ye are gods,” Ps. 82:6, cited by Christ at John 10:34; “bind and loose,” Matt. 16:19/18:18; the closing “angels look with wonder” echoes 1 Pet. 1:12 (“which things the angels desire to look into”); Dionysius’s “our salvation is possible only through our deification” is genuine — Ecclesiastical Hierarchy I.3 — and the detail that the monk stands (not kneels) at his consecration is likewise Dionysian (EH VI). Ch. XXIII: “after the order of Melchizedek,” Ps. 110:4 with Heb. 5–7 (Hebrews is cited throughout as Paul’s, per the traditional ascription); “without father, without mother…,” Heb. 7:3; “the law made nothing perfect… but the bringing in of a better hope did” — both halves of Heb. 7:19; the rent veil, Matt. 27:51; the Jordan turned back, Ps. 114:3, applied liturgically to the Theophany; the “form of a servant,” Phil. 2:7. The Old Rite spelling Isus continues in the running text of both chapters.

The king of the world. The chapter’s engine is a bilingual pun the reader must see to evaluate. Heb. 7:2 interprets Melchizedek’s title: king of Salem, “which is, King of peace” (Salem/shalom). Russian «мир» means both “peace” and “world”; writing «царь мира» and attributing it to Paul, Dugin silently fuses the Pauline “King of Peace” with Guénon’s Roi du Monde — the subterranean sovereign of Le Roi du Monde (1927), whose sixth chapter supplies everything here credited to Guénon: the El Elyon/Shaddai hierarchy, the primordial-tradition reading of the tithe, the gematria El Elyon = Emmanuel (= 197), and the three Magi as three functions of the King of the World. The translation renders «царь мира» as “king of the world” because that is how the term functions in the chapter; the reader should know the verse itself says “peace.” The alternative reading in which Melchizedek tithes to Abraham is a real crux (the ambiguous pronoun of Gen. 14:20; cf. b. Nedarim 32b, where Melchizedek’s priesthood passes to Abraham for blessing him in the wrong order) — Dugin reports it accurately as a “lowering” strategy.

Constructions to mark. (1) The two-aspect theory of the personal Pentecost — baptism as possibility, ordination as objectivation — maps Guénon’s distinction between virtual and effective initiation onto the two sacraments; the scheme is Dugin’s, not the tradition’s. (2) The unrepeatability claim imports the Latin scholastic doctrine of indelible character; Orthodox canonical practice (deposition, return to lay state) and a substantial line of Orthodox theology treat orders as revocable, and the question is at least disputed. (3) The “three angelic actions” (prostration, standing, transmission) systematize Dionysius in Dugin’s own vocabulary — the Areopagite’s triads run by purification, illumination, and perfection — though the bishop/priest/deacon correspondence extends a genuinely Dionysian pattern. (4) The “strictly secret initiatic ritual” of the hesychast elders is conjecture, and to his credit Dugin marks it as such; the Isaiah 6 coal is his proposed analogue. (5) The closing inversion — angels saved and deified through men — has a real patristic basis (the flesh assumed into the Trinity exceeds angelic nature; cf. the Theotokos “more honorable than the cherubim”), here pushed to a maximal formula. (6) In Ch. XXIII the anti-Judaic polemic reaches its sharpest sacramental formulation: the Levitical priesthood as “evil and devil-worship” from the moment it refuses the tithe to the Church. This is translated as written and named for what it is — the book’s structural polemic, here fused with supersessionist cult-theology. (7) Melito of Sardis sits oddly in the list of “Platonizing” readers of the Old Testament; his Peri Pascha is typological and, notoriously, itself fiercely anti-Judaic. (8) The Iranian excursus is the chapter’s boldest construction: the daeva/deva inversion is accurate comparative philology, the Saoshyant’s virgin birth from the seed preserved in Lake Kąsaoya is genuine Zoroastrian eschatology, and pardes from Old Persian paridaiza (“walled enclosure” — “enchanted garden” is a flourish) is sound etymology; but the conclusion — that Melchizedek’s priesthood is “in some manner connected with the Iranian tradition” — quietly relocates the third path’s prehistory from Salem to Persia, giving the Christian esoteric genealogy an Iranian axis. This is the seed of Dugin’s lifelong Iranophilia, and the reader should notice that the chapter distinguishes the Theotokian morning star from Lucifer’s by exactly one modal marker — grace versus necessity — the same razor’s edge on which Chapter XIV balanced.

Part IV · Chapter XXIV

Eucharist and Liturgy

Евхаристия и литургия

Communion, the eucharist, is the chief Christian sacrament and the central initiatic ritual. It must be recalled, however, that an initiatic ritual is not a ritual of initiation — of which the two varieties in Orthodoxy are baptism and ordination. An initiatic ritual is a rite practiced only by those who are already consecrated and have passed through the primary initiation. It is a determinate aspect of existence within consecration, accessible only to the consecrated themselves. That communion is precisely an initiatic ritual is plainly visible in the phrase pronounced by the priest before the beginning of the “liturgy of the faithful”: “As many as are catechumens, depart”… And then the uninitiated, who have not received holy baptism, must leave the place where the initiatic rite is performed.

Communion is the center and axis of the whole Christian divine service, the meaning of its existence. The entire service is built around the chief sacred event — the transubstantiation of the wine and bread into the blood and flesh of Christ, and the tasting of holy communion by the community of the faithful. In this is realized the factual and mysterious unity of Christians and Christ himself; the grace given potentially in baptism is confirmed by the possibility of the direct contact of created man with the uncreated God — and, correspondingly, by the concretization of the baptismal prospect of deification. At the moment of communion the Christian receives an initiatic power — fiery-luminous — for his spiritual realization, placing within himself, from without, a sacred support that helps to fix his being at ever higher stages of the spiritual development turned toward full deification. In the purely initiatic sense, communion, strictly speaking, ought to be a periodic “confirmation” — a “ratification” and fixing of the man’s personal spiritual work of converting the potential grace of baptism into the actual, realized status of the “deified man,” the “god by participation.” Precisely for this reason communion is not a single-occasion sacrament: it is necessary (or desirable) each time the man traverses a determinate stretch of the path (though this stretch may be measured in rather various “units” of objective and subjective time). The perfect initiatic nature of the eucharist discloses itself to the Christian only when he is himself inwardly prepared for an “objective” contact and fusion with the incorruptible nature of the God-man — when the grace-filled eucharistic sacrifice is perceived and received by him as the fulfillment of the divine promise (given in baptism), in faith in which, and in hope of which, the man has moved along the path of the transfiguration of the person in the uncreated light. Only in such a case is the man really ready for communion; and only in such a case does the assimilation of the “flesh and blood” of God really take place, the outer become the inner, the promise find its fulfillment. It is not by chance that the liturgical formula warns those who are not ready for communion of the possibility of the luminous divine nature turning into a “fire that burns,” should the communicant not possess a soul sufficiently illumined for the reception of the sacrament. In other words, the objective side of communion can be assimilated by the believer’s being only if he is sufficiently prepared by inner, “subjective” work — by the “work of the mind.” But since the process of spiritual development may have various phases, communion itself may be repeated in a man’s life a determinate number of times, according to the rhythm of realization of the concrete person. Frequent communion is recommended for monks, priests, ascetics, and people actively occupied with pneumatic practice. In such cases the rhythm of the alternation of subtle states is, as a rule, rather high. For simple Christians everything depends on their personal particularities, though for the majority it makes sense to take part in this sacrament not too often — several times a year.

But the action of communion does not become less effective even when it is given to people not occupied with spiritual realization at all. In that case the objective contact with the nature of the Godhead gives an indirect, not directly initiatic effect: the grace-filled energy strengthens the soul, restores the body to health, and orients the spirit, without showing itself, toward the higher spheres (though all this may be rather relative, according to the individual quality of each man). Such a non-initiatic effect of an essentially initiatic ritual in no way alters the nature of the eucharist itself; and under determinate, exceptional circumstances, the whole fullness of communion grace may manifest itself even in one who has not subjectively prepared himself for it in advance. There exists also another possibility of the action of the eucharistic sacrament, which may be called “counter-initiatic” (in Guénon’s terms). It is precisely to this perspective that the warning to communicants refers — of the possibility of the “blood and flesh” of the Lord turning into a “fire that burns.” What is at issue are beings who, having received holy baptism, do not simply remain in a neutral state, taking not a single step toward personal spiritual development while nonetheless preserving in potential form the uncreated grace of the water and the Spirit — but those who rise up against this grace and actively counteract it, committing thereby the most terrible crime, which has no “forgiveness” in the Christian Church: the slander of the Holy Spirit — that is, they deny the genuine nature of the baptismal sacrament. For such people communion is likewise a kind of “confirmation,” a ratification — but this time the ratification of their accursedness, their condemnation, their punishment. And here the supra-created nature of communion itself likewise manifests itself objectively: through a real transubstantiation of the inner nature of the man, exposed by the uncreated fire — a transubstantiation inverse to the luminous transfiguration of genuine Christians. This effect tells not only upon conscious “anti-Christians” and “Satanists” (who exist only in insignificant numbers), but upon those “Christians” who, without renouncing Christian dogmatics in general, deny all the uncreated, metaphysical, and strictly initiatic aspects of the Church. Herein lies one of the causes of the renunciation of the sacraments (and first of all of the eucharist) among those “Christian” currents which withdraw furthest from the Orthodox truth in the direction of extreme Judeo-Christianity. Such people are often simply incapable, physically and spiritually, of enduring communion — whose effectiveness depends not on subjective and volitional causes (faith or unbelief in the reality of the transubstantiation), but on the whole fullness of the objective Orthodox metaphysics, manifesting itself concretely and palpably in the eucharistic fact.

The liturgy is the ritual frame of the initiatic eucharist, called to create the conditions for its genuineness and perfection. In the liturgy the basic moments of the metaphysics of the Gospel are ritually reproduced in brief form, and the events of the Incarnation, the Preaching, the Death on the Cross, and the Resurrection of the Son of God are conciliarly commemorated. These commemorated events are understood not as historical fact, but as events of the eternal present, of the aeonic celestial cycle independent of time. The divine service, flowing in earthly time, takes place simultaneously in the celestial, supra-temporal, supra-formal reality; and therefore the assertion of the theologians that angels take part in the service together with earthly men must be understood not metaphorically but literally, as an indication of the purely spiritual, celestial level of this rite. Telling, in this sense, is the opening of the liturgy with the ritual gesture of the deacon lifting his orarion — which symbolizes the “sweep of the angel’s wing,” giving the sign for the beginning of the divine service on earth as in the heavens.

The uncreatedness of the God-man’s body lies on the far side of time, and therefore the whole liturgy is, on one of its sides, “withdrawn” from the reality of the lower waters, and even of the upper waters. The liturgical commemoration of the Gospel events is transfigured into a real contact with the archetypal, supra-temporal, metaphysical content of these events; they are actualized in their essence in the concrete period of the service, carrying that period beyond the frame of time. The Incarnation, the Preaching, the Death on the Cross, and the Resurrection are accomplished in actuality in the course of the service; and the proof of the concreteness of this accomplishment is the very sacrament of communion — of the “beholding of God,” the “tasting of God.” All the elements of the liturgy have a dogmatic and metaphysical meaning bound up with the eucharist, from which, as from a center, radiate the rays of the ritual actions, gestures, exclamations, hymns, movements, and operations with sacred objects of the priests, the clergy, and the parishioners. In the divine service Christians essentially live through the Gospel, touching — through the actualizing agency of the Comforter, the Holy Spirit — the great mystery, the chief metaphysical event of the universe. Therefore attendance at the divine service has in itself a directly initiatic dimension even when the Christian does not approach communion: in this case also there takes place an effective, universal participation in the eternal angelic and even divinely uncreated being. This concerns especially the “liturgy of the faithful,” which is correlated directly with the sacred actualization of the mystery of the New Testament — whereas the “liturgy of the catechumens” is regarded as a preliminary stage, correlated with the Old Testament, and especially with the prophecies of Christ, which permits those not yet baptized, but desiring to enter the saving bosom of the Church, to take part in it.

Part IV · Chapter XXV

The Pneumatic Aspect of Confession

Пневматический аспект исповеди

One of the most important ecclesial sacraments, likewise possessing an initiatic character, is confession — which obligatorily precedes the chief initiatic Orthodox ritual, communion.

Confession contains within itself two principial moments: the repentance of the believer, the “contrition for sins,” and the absolution of sins by the priest. For Orthodox spiritual realization both elements have exceedingly great significance. The word “pokayanie” renders in Church Slavonic the Greek term metanoia, which literally means “a change of consciousness,” or even “a going beyond the frame of consciousness, of reason, of mind.”152 What is meant is a particular spiritual operation, transferring the believer’s consciousness from one plane of being to another. A “change of consciousness,” in accordance with the gnoseology of Tradition — in which “to know” means “to be” — is at the same time a “change of being.” Consequently, in the “technical,” operative-initiatic sense, metanoia, “repentance,” is nothing other than the radical experience of passage from one state to another. Unfortunately, in the Russian language this term has a narrower meaning: “contrition,” “regret over something,” and more concretely, “contrition and regret over certain deeds committed or conceived.” In this case the initiatic content of the term is not so evident as in the Greek, and requires special elucidation.

To understand what passage, and what states of consciousness and being, are at issue, let us recall that holy baptism, in its initiatic dimension, is a “new birth” in the most direct sense of the word. In the baptized Christian, by the grace of the Holy Spirit, the germ of a new person, a new man, is laid down. This is the “seed cast into the earth.” The fact of the seed’s presence is guaranteed by baptism; but its further fate depends in great measure on whether the man himself can “grow it” — in other words, whether he can identify himself with this new person, transfer to it the center of gravity of his being, fuse with it in his consciousness. If baptism is an initiatic ritual taking place instantaneously (like the sowing), the maturing of the new man is a long process, having several phases. Up to the stage of full illumination, sanctification, deification, the Christian abides still in two realities, between two poles, between two essences — the old (created) and the new (uncreated).153 The old part drags him downward, to the ontological periphery, to passivity, to the dark sides of existence; the new part (which of necessity is at first the weaker) strengthens his “standing,” his vertical position, and draws him toward creativeness, contemplation, and illumination. The meaning of repentance as an initiatic ritual comes down precisely to this: that the man, by an act of will, undertakes an effort — a wrench, as it were — to establish himself in the new and to leave the old, altering thereby the proportions between these two foundational aspects of his Christian existence. Such an effort has an essentially spiritual, pneumatic significance, since what is at issue is the change of the man’s innermost nature, and not simply his assessment of deeds committed (or conceived). Repentance must touch the man’s essence, his deepest core, and not simply the outer aspects of the person or the “moral” characterization of behavior. One must repent, in essence, of this: that one is still man and not God — still the Old Adam, and not the New Adam.

Repentance constitutes one of the chief practices of Orthodox realization. Ritually it should be performed at least once a day, at Compline; but in an intensive spiritual life it becomes an almost constant state of the Christian who stubbornly strives to grow the inner man. But repentance before communion (confession proper) has a particular significance, since in it the “second party” takes part ritually and initiatically — the priest, endowed by the Holy Spirit and the power of the apostolic ministry with the capacity to “absolve sins,” to “bind and loose.” The one confessing makes a spiritual effort for a sharp passage from one state to another; and the priest who hears the confession, by grace-filled power, certifies this action, as it were — bears witness to it, acknowledges the Christian’s intention as a spiritual datum. “Absolution of sins” means, theoretically, a confirmation from the uncreated reality of the Holy Spirit, acting through the priest, of the effectiveness of the pneumatic effort of the one who has confessed — a trust in the new man within him, and a “disregard” of those remnants of the “old” which are still preserved. (For the uprooting of these remnants the penance serves.) Confession is thus the cleansing of the soul, the initiatic confirmation of the new person born from above in each concrete believer. And only after such confirmation may the Orthodox Christian approach the Holy Gifts, the eucharist, where he receives within himself the Divine Presence.

It is important to note that in the early periods of the Church, down to the Middle Ages, the faithful confessed for the most part not simply to parish priests but to elders — men who had attained a determinate (high) stage of spiritual realization and belonged to a particular initiatic chain (these might be priests, or simple monks, or in exceptional cases even laymen). This means that besides the supra-human, partly supra-personal mystical aspect of the sacrament of confession — which has been preserved in the Church down to the present time, being independent of human and historical concreteness — it once possessed an important psychic content. The assertion that the one who hears the confession takes upon himself the confessed sins, in the case of confession to an elder, has a particular “technical” significance: in this case the spiritually realized Christian (the elder-confessor) by personal effort helps the disciple (the “spiritual child”) to pass consciously through a certain stage of realization. In doing so the teacher really enters into direct contact with the old nature of the disciple and helps to tame it, to subordinate it to the new person. Accomplishing this, he himself is exposed to a determinate risk from the negative, at times demonic energies composing the fabric of the old nature of the disciple’s soul. In order to perform such a dangerous operation in full measure, one must be excellently oriented in “psychic topology,” must know thoroughly the laws of the “subtle plane.”154 Only in this case can the “spiritual father” really and personally participate in the spiritual, initiatic realization of another man, dissolving in his soul the dark barriers of the old existence, fed from without by the demonic hosts. It is not hard to understand that active participation in such a form of confession demands of the “spiritual father” a high level of personal spiritual realization155 — since otherwise the whole operation will be unsafe and problematic both for himself and for his “spiritual child.” The situation here is analogous to the one we examined in investigating the initiatic meaning of ordination. If one speaks of full realization, then only exceptional persons should be ordained — those who have realized the whole fullness of baptismal grace and confirm the fullness of the growth of the new man within them by the reception of the apostolic grace. But gradually, by force of historical causes, this state of affairs — ordinary in early Christianity — became, on the contrary, the exceptional case; and a multitude of ordained priests stand very far from the level of spiritual realization corresponding to their rank. Yet the power of the order of Melchizedek, acting through them, has not thereby been diminished in the least. Exactly so in the case of confession: from the moment confession came to be made not before an elder but before an ordinary priest, the initiatic content of this sacrament became merely potential — acting only at the level of the secret new man (through the believer, as it were) and of the supra-individual grace of the priesthood in the priest, bypassing the psychic, properly human, subtle level of both. The initiatic content of the sacrament itself has in no way suffered from this; but the degree of individual consciousness of all that takes place, the degree of involvement of the psychic powers of both participants in the confession, has diminished considerably. (Let us note that here, as in all analogous situations, there are exceptions — when the confessor even in our time is a spiritually realized person.)

In parallel with this, it became usual to single out in confession (in repentance and the absolution of sins) a strictly moral aspect, connected not with the transfiguration of the Christian’s essential nature but with the harmonization of his outer manifestations — deeds, designs, actions, and so on. At the same time the “spiritual father,” from a teacher of pneumatic realization, became a moral adviser, a preceptor in the rather limited and conditioned sphere of personal or social behavior. Henceforth the concept of “sin”156 was identified not with the whole old nature of the person, but with only one part of it — with that which, in the moral respect, Christian morality recognizes as unacceptable or simply undesirable. Confession, at the discursive level, came more and more to be interpreted as a ritual connected exclusively with the moral side of a man’s deeds, and the consciousness of its initiatic significance gradually dwindled to practically nothing. But in the Orthodox Church — in distinction from Latinism or Protestantism — this has in no way touched the mystical essence of the ritual itself, which continues to preserve its pneumatic dimension in all its fullness, independently of the loss of the keys to its adequate interpretation.

Apparatus · Chapters XXIV–XXV

Apparatus

Sources and citations. No footnote markers fall in Chapter XXIV; Chapter XXV carries nos. 152–156, preserved. “As many as are catechumens, depart” is the dismissal opening the Liturgy of the Faithful. The “fire that burns” the unworthy is from the prayers before communion (“…lest it be to my judgment: for it is fire, consuming the unworthy”); the scriptural root of the whole negative-effect doctrine is 1 Cor. 11:29–30 (“eateth and drinketh damnation to himself… for this cause many are weak and sickly among you”) — a real Pauline and liturgical kernel, which the chapter then extends well beyond its sources (see below). The unforgivable “slander of the Holy Spirit” is Matt. 12:31–32; the seed imagery draws on Mark 4 and John 12:24; “bind and loose,” Matt. 16:19/18:18.

Terminology. Dugin’s word for the eucharistic change is «пресуществление» — the Latinate transubstantiation, a seventeenth-century import into Russian theology — rather than the older and neo-patristically preferred «преложение» (“change”); the translation follows his choice, noting the small irony of an Old-Rite-leaning author reaching for the scholastic term without comment. His «павечерница» for Compline is the Old Rite form of the word — one more lexical confessional marker, invisible in English. His idiosyncratic use of “confirmation” for repeated communion continues from Chapter XXI. The etymology of metanoia as “going beyond mind” is homiletic, not lexicographic — the Greek means change of mind or heart; the “beyond-nous” reading is a staple of twentieth-century hesychast-revival writing that suits his initiatic frame.

Constructions to mark. (1) The counsel of infrequent lay communion (“several times a year”) reproduces synodal-era Russian practice; the twentieth-century eucharistic revival (Kollyvades fathers, John of Kronstadt, Schmemann) argued precisely the opposite, and frequent communion is now general Orthodox counsel. Dugin’s rationale — communion as rhythmic “confirmation” of initiatic progress — is his own, and quietly converts a historical accident of piety into esoteric doctrine. (2) The “counter-initiatic” inverse transubstantiation of the unworthy communicant, and above all the claim that Judaizing Christians are “often simply incapable, physically and spiritually, of enduring communion,” extend the Pauline kernel into polemical psychophysiology with no warrant in the tradition; this is the Part’s anti-Judaic frame reaching into sacramental physiology itself. (3) The eternal-present reading of the liturgical anamnesis, and the literal co-service of the angels, are genuine Orthodox liturgical theology, here stated in radicalized vocabulary; the deacon’s orarion as angel’s wing is attested in the Byzantine commentary tradition. (4) The history of confession in Chapter XXV — monastic elders, including the non-ordained, as the original confessors, with parish penance a later routinization — is broadly supported by scholarship on Byzantine and early Russian penitential practice; but the “technical” mechanics of the elder’s assumption of sins (“psychic topology,” the “subtle plane,” demonic energies engaged by contact) are Guénon’s occult categories mapped onto the eldership. (5) Note the structural echo: the chapter’s narrative of confession degraded from pneumatic operation to moral counseling is Guénon’s master-plot — initiation decaying into religion — applied inside Orthodoxy, the very move Dugin forbade Guénon to make against the Eastern Church in Chapter XVIII. The contradiction is managed, not resolved, by the closing assurance that the “mystical essence” persists intact beneath the lost keys.

Reading note. With these two chapters the sacramental mapping begun in Chapter XXI is complete in its core: baptism the Lesser Mysteries, ordination the Greater, the eucharist the initiatic ritual par excellence, confession the operative technique binding the person to the process. What remains of Part IV (XXVI–XXIX) extends the scheme to marriage, monasticism, and unction, closing on the “consuming fire.”

Part IV · Chapter XXVI

The Sacrament of Marriage.
The Soteriological Function of Woman

Таинство Брака. Сотериологическая функция женщины

Crowning — the marriage rite — is a ritual bound up with the lesser mysteries. In it is mystically realized the restoration of the lost primordial androgynate proper to the Adam of paradise. A man and a woman, crowned according to the Orthodox rite, become henceforth a single being — on the fleshly and on the spiritual levels alike.
