In a certain sense, ecclesial marriage symbolizes the installation of Eve, taken from Adam’s rib, in her primordial place. The woman is integrated, as it were, into the man, completing his being with her breath of life (Eve, in Hebrew, is “life”).

Christian marriage differs sharply from that of the Old Testament. What is at issue in it is not an indulgence of the fleshly desires of fallen humanity, but a new, evangelical, grace-filled form of the saving economy. Christ, by his sufferings on the cross and his Resurrection, rejects once and for all the irreversibility and one-directionality of the current of sacred history “under the law,” in which only a progressive removal from the primordial paradise is realized. Now the entrance into paradise is open once more, and all who are faithful to Christ become partakers of the paradisal mysteries — one of which is crowning: the restoration of the androgynate. Before Christ no marriage could have such an initiatic meaning and effectiveness. Only in His Church did this sacred ritual receive its final and perfect significance, whose prefigurations are met not only in the ritual of Judaic marriage but in the nuptial ceremonies of other, non-Abrahamic traditions.

The restoration of the paradisal dignity bound up with the androgynate has the most direct relation to royal status — since the paradisal Adam, standing at the center of reality and governing the universe, is the archetype of the royal function in its highest significance. Hence the very word “crowning” as applied to the Orthodox marriage rite. The entrance into paradise is at once the union of the two principles and the acquisition of the royal nature. With this is connected also the leading of the newly married into the altar. This is the single case (besides baptism) in which a layman is permitted to enter the Holy of Holies. Such an exception points to the extraordinary significance of this ritual and to its initiatic nature.

After the crowning there appears a new conciliar being, in which husband, wife, and future children compose a single spiritual organism — a “little Church.” In fact, from the esoteric point of view, the full Orthodox family ought to be a finished prefiguration of a compact initiatic organization, conjoined with the spiritual realization of the content of the lesser mysteries. The husband prefigures in it the King — and likewise the shepherd, the priest, the teacher and preceptor. The wife — the Realm, the flock, the hieros laos, the “holy people.” And the “little Church” of the family has in Orthodoxy two aspects: the outer and the inner. On the outer, socio-ethical plane, primacy here belongs, unconditionally, to the man, the head of the family. Here the hierarchy is affirmed unambiguously and irreversibly. But at the inner level — in distinction from the purely creationist religious models of the ordering of the family hearth, founded on absolute patriarchy in all spheres — in the Orthodox family the proportions are inverted.

The Orthodox woman, as spouse and mother, is bound to the Most Pure Virgin Mary herself, to the absolute archetype157 — and consequently, at the level of spiritual realization, she occupies the place of the perfect man. Therefore her role in the mysterious side of the family economy is, in Orthodoxy, enormous. In certain Old Believer concords this aspect of the feminine principle was so far developed that the salvation of the husband was held possible only through the salvation of the wife — in complete opposition to the purely exoteric socio-ethical doctrine. Behind this evident exaggeration is concealed a reality often forgotten in our time: the esoteric mission of the Orthodox woman, which has the most direct relation to the metaphysical realization of baptismal grace.

Now for the “technical” side of the curse and of salvation. The curse of the first parents came about through Eve, who served as mediator between the serpent and the Adam of paradise. The Nativity of the Savior is likewise from a Virgin — the New Eve, who once more came forward as mediator, only this time between the Trinitarian God himself and the fallen Adam. Consequently, at the initiatic level also, what is at issue is the use of one and the same instance (the feminine principle), which has already served toward the curse and must henceforth serve toward salvation. Therefore the woman’s entry into marriage is both her salvation (through the organizing, luminous, affirmative, vertical energy of the man-king) and, with her help, the salvation of the man himself — for whom the wife must serve as the image of the churched universe, of the transfigured cosmos, the sign of the perfect man. In the obedience and self-renunciation of the wife with respect to himself, the husband grasps the initiatic model of his own relation to God — in which likewise there must be no trace of self-will or egoism, as there is none in the normal Orthodox woman. In the final reckoning, before the face of the uncreated Godhead the man himself, in his created aspect, is nothing other than purely feminine passivity and receptivity; his dignity and affirmative power he draws from that which he himself is not, but whose functions he ritually performs — from the uncreated Lord and the Holy Spirit. The Holy Spirit the Orthodox Christian, after baptism, bears always in his heart, as a wife bears in her womb a son who will be greater than she, by the rules of the providential hierarchy.

One may say that at the exoteric level the Orthodox “little Church” establishes hierarchical relations between the man, as the deputy of the Lord himself, and the woman, as the imperfect flock, torn by the base energies of the passionate world. What is at issue in this case is a taming, a disciplinary limitation of the feminine principle. This is the daytime aspect of the family. Here the paradisal proportions abide in struggle with the raging forces of the outer, non-paradisal, perverted cosmos. In the nocturnal, esoteric aspect all is otherwise. The passionate world, with which the struggle was waged, dims and withdraws. The woman discloses her second, spiritual dimension, which is not without but within; and consequently the husband no longer teaches her, but himself learns from her; he no longer subdues her emotional chaoticness, but is himself penetrated by the supra-rational energies exceeding the rigid decrees of the reason. In a certain sense the man himself spiritually becomes a woman, abiding in the passive openness of transcendent contemplation, entrusting his consciousness to the Godhead itself, which has no common measure with man. This time the proportions of the most genuine paradise are restored.

Moreover, in the eschatological perspective the sex of the whole creation changes. If in the primordial, archetypal, paradisal state creation is the projection of the Husband — of Adam, or the man-androgyne — in the final state it acquires the features of the Virgin, or the woman-androgyne. This is indirectly visible, in particular, in the Christian tradition which asserts that the Virgin Mary became the head of the angels in the place of the morning star — who is symbolically described, unambiguously, as an angelic being of the masculine sex. And at the level of initiatic realization, the archetype of the perfect man becomes not a man, but a woman.

Part IV · Chapter XXVII

The Monastic Path and the
Transcendence of Love

Монашеский путь и трансцендентность Любви

The same stage of spiritual realization that pertains to the sphere of Orthodox marriage may also be passed in another way. In the monastic work, too, the paradisal proportions are restored — only this takes place without the participation of a representative of the other sex. The one being tonsured a monk, at the moment of initiation, ritually promises to cast off all that introduces division into him, all that violates his unity. This is precisely the mystical marriage, the restoration of the androgynate. Only in this case the place of the spouse — of the inner woman, the wife — is taken by the monk’s own soul. Since the monk chooses the purely inner path and renounces the outer, no need arises in him for the exoteric side of marriage. He begins at once from that moment which is the crown of the spiritual realization of Orthodox married laypeople. So too the women-monastics have no need of the preliminary veneration of the Lord through service to a husband, but immediately and directly receive the rank of “brides of the Lord.” But the sacrament of marriage, in the case of wedlock as in the case of monasticism, always remains real and sacred — though in monasticism its initiatic content is immediate, while in ordinary wedlock it is mediated.

More than that: the further monastic realization pertains already to the greater mysteries, and not to the lesser, as crowning does. Therefore the nuptial symbolism here changes its level. It is held that the paradisal state has already been attained by the one being tonsured monk (or nun), and that what is now at issue is fusion with the spiritually vertical, celestial and — more than that — uncreatedly transcendent principle. Therefore the monastic tonsure is drawn close not to the royal symbolism (as crowning is), but to the consecration to the priesthood. In the case of the tonsure of men this is not simply an approximation but an initiatic ritual of the same order. In the case of the tonsure of women, one may discern in the ritual echoes of the initiatic custom of the first times of the Christian Church, when women too were admitted to ordination in the rank of deaconesses.158 Though this was abolished already from the first centuries, the possibility of a purely spiritual, angelic path for the woman always remained open through the monastic feat. The prohibition was laid only upon the conduct of services and the exercise of the outer priestly functions; the metaphysical realization could be, for women also, full and perfect — including the consecration into the greater mysteries.

Here we should dwell somewhat longer on the Gospel symbolism of marriage. In many places of the Gospels themselves and of the apostolic epistles, Christ is named the “Bridegroom,” and the faithful the “brides of the Lord.” Such expressions are used as the “marriage supper of the Lamb,” the “wedding feast,” and so on. In a certain sense this symbolism of the wedding, of the union of Christians with the Lord, is central in the Christian Tradition159 and is identified, generally, with its emotional-aesthetic form. Behind this stands also the fact that Christianity is the religion of Love; God himself is named in it Love, and the chief commandments of the New Testament speak precisely of Love — first, of love toward God, and second, of love toward one’s neighbors. Love, in the Christian perspective, is union without confusion.

In this definition is contained the whole specificity of Christian metaphysics. In Judaic creationism there is no prospect whatever of the union of creature with Creator (and hence no initiatic aspect of love); while in “Hellenic” manifestationism every union leads to confusion, to the identification of the one with the other (there love is excessive160). In Christianity Love comes forward in its total, highest, transcendent aspect. In it nothing merges, nothing loses its particularity — yet neither does anything remain in eternal division, in eternal parting. Not only are the persons of the Most Holy Trinity themselves undivided and unconfused in their uncreated Love for one another; upon creation too fall the rays of this great pre-existent power — it too is raised, through all who have put on Christ, beyond its “insignificant” limits, without thereby losing its unique nature.

The highest initiatic aspect of Christian Love is the re-creation of a paradoxical Unity that does not abolish particularity and differences. Christ is the Bridegroom for the whole of creation; but each soul, each thing, enters with Him into an intimate, strictly personal spiritual marriage, merging neither with Him nor with the other souls. Nothing vanishes, nothing is lost, in the mystery of Orthodox transcendent Love; and Love itself therefore acquires the attributes of the uncreated Godhead. God is Love. This means that neither the object nor the Subject of Love ever disappears to the end.

The marriage of the Lamb is on the far side not only of time but of eternity. It has no duration. It is the eternal glory poured forth by the Holy Trinity. And the Orthodox become participants in the eternal crowning, being deified in the rays of the Light of Tabor, the uncreated radiance of the holy Crown.

This transcendent perspective of absolute Love, of the mysterious Marriage, overshadows even the very first levels of the spiritual realization of Christians. In the lesser mysteries of Orthodox wedlock, as in the greater mysteries of the monastic feat, there is present a particular dimension which gives to the whole initiatic work its strictly Christian character. All things are raised up here in Love, but nothing is lost. The unity, the androgynate of the paradisal state, is restored — but the quality of “conciliarity” remains, since each part of the restored whole brings into the new, saving, grace-filled synthesis not only itself, but also the highest providential cause of its former “separation.” Therefore the New Eve does not become, in the full sense, the rib of her husband, but fuses with him together with the fullness of the memory of the fall and of the longing of separate, sundered existence. And this memory becomes the fuel of the mystery of Love, feeding the saving economy with living energy.

So also in the case of the monastic feat: contrary to non-Christian mysticism, the soul of the “brides of the Lord” never merges with the Son of God to the end. Createdness, and the memory of the “created exile,” is always preserved — which constantly, with ever new power, discloses the whole fullness of the Love of the Godhead for men, and the whole fullness of the answering Love of men for God.

And this will never end.

Apparatus · Chapters XXVI–XXVII

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 157–160 are preserved. Eve from the rib, Gen. 2:21–23; the etymology of Eve as “life” (Ḥavvah) is sound, per Gen. 3:20. The Bridegroom, Matt. 9:15, 25:1–13; the marriage supper of the Lamb, Rev. 19:7–9; God is Love, 1 John 4:8; the two chief commandments, Matt. 22:37–40; “put on Christ,” Gal. 3:27. “Union without confusion” transposes the Chalcedonian adverbs (“without confusion… without division”) from Christology to the metaphysics of love — an elegant and characteristically Dugin move. The chapter spells the New Eve «Евва», the Church Slavonic double-v orthography: one more quiet confessional marker, invisible in English.

The androgyne. The restoration-of-androgynate doctrine has genuine patristic cousins — above all Maximus the Confessor (Ambigua 41: Christ overcomes the division of male and female among the five cosmic divisions), with Gal. 3:28 behind him, and Gregory of Nyssa’s double-creation scheme adjacent — but as stated (primordial androgyne, marriage as its ritual reconstitution) it belongs to the esoteric lineage: Plato’s Symposium myth, the kabbalistic two-faced Adam, Boehme, and most proximately Evola’s Metaphysics of Sex, whose day/night polarity the chapter’s “daytime and nocturnal aspects of the family” reproduce. The eschatological sex-change of creation — masculine at origin (projection of Adam), feminine at the end (the Virgin enthroned, the archetype of the perfect man become woman) — is Dugin’s own construction, consistent with Chapters XIV and XVI, and one of the book’s most original.

Factual cautions. (1) The claim that the newly married are led into the altar — “the single case besides baptism in which a layman enters the Holy of Holies” — corresponds to no rubric of the Byzantine marriage rite known to this translator: the crowned pair processes three times around the analogion; the sanctuary entry Dugin may have in mind is the churching of male infants, who are indeed carried into the altar (and baptism itself does not enter the sanctuary either). Confidence: moderate-high that this is an error or a highly idiosyncratic local usage; translated as written. (2) The Old Believer concords in which “the salvation of the husband is possible only through the salvation of the wife” cannot be identified from the text; what is well attested is women’s religious leadership (nastavnitsy) among the priestless concords. Reported as his claim. (3) The deaconess note is right in substance and wrong in chronology: the female diaconate is genuinely ancient and was ordained by cheirotonia, but it was not “abolished already from the first centuries” — the Byzantine euchologia preserve the ordination rite well into the medieval period; the office faded rather than fell.

Reading note. The gender metaphysics should be stated plainly rather than laundered: the exoteric doctrine is unqualified patriarchy (“hierarchy affirmed unambiguously and irreversibly,” the wife as model of obedience without “self-will”), and the esoteric inversion — husband learning from wife, man become spiritually woman, woman as archetype of the perfect man — operates only on the far side of that submission, as its reward and secret. The structure is Evola’s: hierarchy outward, alchemy inward. What redeems the pair of chapters as literature is the closing movement of XXVII, among the finest pages of the book: the memory of the fall retained inside restored unity as the “fuel” of Love, and the refusal of fusion — “neither the object nor the Subject of Love ever disappears” — as the precise boundary between Orthodox theosis and the monist mysticisms the book has opposed since Part I. “And this will never end” closes the sacramental doctrine on its highest note before the two contemplative chapters that end the Part.

Part IV · Chapter XXVIII

The Seraphic Sacrament
(The Anointing with Oil)

Серафимское таинство (елеопомазание)

The anointing with oil is an initiatic ritual directly connected with contemplation and with the angelic level. Its general meaning comes down to the Christian’s communion with celestial reality — and, more concretely, with the reality of the highest angelic rank, the seraphim. Dionysius the Areopagite, in his interpretation of the ecclesial sacraments, underscores the strictly spiritual, “intellectual,” “noetic” character of this sacrament, called to open in the Orthodox Christian a particular celestial sight. It is telling that Dionysius himself — who does not very clearly divide the created from the uncreated aspects of the “Divine hierarchy” (a trait characteristic of “Helleno-Christianity” in general) — compares the ritual of the consecration of the oil with the eucharist itself, since the “oil,” upon the pronouncement of particular sacred formulas, is transfigured on the altar into a “noetic” celestial substance of intellectual fragrance.

Strictly speaking, such an approximation is not entirely legitimate — for the eucharist is unambiguously and directly bound to the fullness of the uncreated Divine Presence in the Holy Gifts, whereas the “transubstantiation of the holy oil” is saturated with the Holy Spirit indirectly and mediately, through the “prism” of the celestial but created rank of the seraphim. In the Orthodox initiatic ritual the anointing with oil justly occupies a secondary, though very important, place — in no case comparable with the dread and unique sacrament of the eucharist.

In the “transubstantiation” of the oil there participate various images and symbolic numbers connected with the six-winged seraphim. The oil itself, the “sacred oil,” is not only a fragrance but a combustible material; and the word “seraph” in Hebrew means “fiery,” “burning.” Consequently the consecrated oil is converted, in the course of the ritual, into a concentration of celestial fire, or into a substance of the upper waters. Let us note that the spiritual world, heaven, is described in the sacred texts both as the world of the upper waters and as the world of fire. Therefore the very substance of oil symbolically combines in itself both potential fieriness and moistness — which makes it the natural basis for the metaphysical manifestation, in the corporeal world, of celestial, angelic energies.161

By analogy with the angelic rank, one may speak also of the strictly Christian aspect of this initiatic ritual, conjoined this time with the supra-celestial reality — with the reality of the Holy Spirit, whose symbol is likewise fire. Therefore in this case too, as in the other initiatic rituals of Orthodoxy, a certain supra-created element participates, incomparably exceeding the created eternity of the heavens. And this is why the consecration of the oil takes place on the holy altar, which is the symbol of the supra-celestial, uncreated reality.

Oil is used for the consecration of the altar, of the font, at the consecration to holy orders, at funerals and burials, and likewise in other cases. The range of the initiatic, “seraphic” action of the oil is extremely diverse. In its very highest aspect it actualizes the effective presence of the radiance of the Most Holy Trinity — it lays open, discloses, the ceaselessly outpoured energies of the Godhead. This aspect is central at the consecration of the altar, of the water in the font, at ordination, and so on. At the same time, the anointing with oil in the narrower sense acts effectively upon the contemplative capacities of the man: it purifies the angelic sight, opens the possibility of a direct, “prophetic” observation of the celestial hierarchy. When the priest anoints the Christian’s brow with oil in the form of a cross, there is initiatically laid into the latter’s consciousness the “burning coal” that purified Isaiah and breathed into him the gift of prophecy. If the ordinary Christian, not especially concerned with metaphysical realization, does not notice this action — living it through, rather, psychologically or even physically, as a stirring, pleasant feeling bringing harmony and calm — the active Orthodox contemplatives, on the contrary, vividly experience precisely the intellectual significance of this sacrament. Through the anointing with oil the Christian is given the grace-filled possibility of glancing upon the borders of the “second death,” of seeing the reality of the eternal motionless world, the open Book of Life. In fact this is the gift of prophecy, an immersion in the apocalyptic realities. Often the Orthodox contemplatives, after the anointing, read the Revelation of John the Theologian — the single book of the New Testament not used in the divine service, as something closed, secret, accessible only to the prophetic gaze. The initiatic aspect of the anointing with oil is bound up, in its turn, precisely with the Apocalypse. To this may be added the line of ascending flight that defines the whole existence of the seraphim, ceaselessly soaring upward, glorifying the Lord of hosts — “Holy, holy, holy, Lord God of Sabaoth!” A kindred feeling of ascension is engendered by this ritual as well.

And finally, at the very lowest level, the anointing with oil heals physical infirmities better than all medicines — for upon the body of the faithful, too, the consecrated substance of the heavens exerts its grace-filled action. Strengthening the essential, spiritual principle in the man, it indirectly restores the violated harmony in the bodily composition as well.

The oil is likened to the “seal” of the sacraments. And in this quality the “sacred oil” is used to anoint the dead — confirming thereby their entrance into a reality where, until the instant of the Last Judgment, no fundamental changes will any longer take place. As the birth of a man in the bosom of the Church begins with the cross-shaped pouring of oil upon the water in the font, so his death also is marked with oil: the whole of life is likened to a brief fragment of the single angelic eternity, taken separately, in isolation from the whole ontological context, yet constantly raised toward the fullness of celestial wisdom in the measure of the Christian’s diligent participation in the cycle of the ecclesial sacraments. One may say: the deeper the realization founded on this sacrament, the less accidental and the more meaningful the Orthodox life of the man becomes — the more consecutiveness and logic there is in it. Thus the mysterious communion with the seraphic bodiless consciousness is bound, unnoticed, to the eventful fabric of human destiny — or, more precisely, to the degree of clarity with which the man is conscious of what happens to him and in what direction he is moving through life; more precisely, through life, to the far side of death.

In the final reckoning, the full realization of the anointing with oil must restore the primordial quality of the human reason to such a degree that the mind itself becomes as fiery as the six-winged angel — converted into an eternally blazing soaring upward, into an unceasing praise of the Name of the Lord, into a ceaseless contemplation of the eternal heavens.

Part IV · Chapter XXIX

“A Consuming Fire”

“Огнь поядаяй”

Summing up the basic positions on Christian initiation, one may say that Orthodoxy is an essentially initiatic tradition. In the terms of traditionalism this means that Orthodoxy is something greater than a religion. It is a tradition possessing both the esoteric and the exoteric dimensions — one which has preserved, in distinction from Catholicism, the initiatic connection with primordial Christianity. The Orthodox sacraments bear an initiatic character, and the chief among them are baptism and ordination. And the rays of Orthodox initiation converge upon one common point, a single center — the eucharist, in which all the elements of Orthodox esotericism are conjoined: personal spiritual work, priestly ministry, the liturgical actualization of the supra-temporal events of the New Testament, the union of the living, the dead, and the angels — of heaven and earth, of creature and Creator — in a single act. Holy baptism must, in the normal case, lead to the laying open, within the human person, of a certain being radically distinct from the created and habitual “I” of the individual. This new “I,” the “new man,” is the still breathing of the Holy Spirit — the initiatic person of the Christian.

In the measure of the growth of the “new man,” of his concretization, of his manifestation, the Christian naturally begins to be immersed in “noetic being” — when Holy Scripture, the subjects of the icons, the words of the prayers, the phenomena of the outer world, natural and social events, disclose their inner content, their logic imperceptible at first glance, their luminous structure. Communion and the divine service draw him to themselves without any effort, since the need for the angelic work of the liturgy becomes natural and spontaneous.

If the immersion in the spheres of the “new being” proceeds swiftly and radically, existence outside the Temple, or outside some other form of unworldly existence (hermitage, monastery, and so on), becomes simply impossible. The thirst for movement inward — to the true homeland of the Spirit, into the worlds of the Most Holy Trinity, into the abode of Love — overpowers the inertia of ordinary existence, and the second step of consecration becomes urgent: ordination, or the monastic feat.

Of the further experience it is difficult to say anything concrete — perhaps impossible altogether. As the apostle Paul spoke of the ascent to the third heaven in indefinite terms — “whether in the body, I cannot tell; or whether out of the body, I cannot tell: God knoweth”162 — so it is difficult for us to describe the existence of the saints and ascetics: whether there is still a body there (and likewise a soul and a spirit), or no longer, is unknown. God knoweth.

Initiation, in all its varieties, is the translation of metaphysical premises, of doctrinal assertions, into the region of concrete, verifiable, evident fact. Christian initiation is the factual realization of the strictly Christian metaphysics; and consequently there is accomplished in it the supra-temporal coincidence of the two sacred moments — the First and the Second Comings of our Lord Jesus Christ. In distinction from the outer faith of exotericism, which always remains only faith, Christian initiation converts this faith into concrete and evident knowledge — more resembling, in its rigor, the scientific constatation of a phenomenon than the dim and barely graspable subjective experience with which the phenomena of the spiritual plane are associated among the profane.

The path of initiation coincides with active co-participation in the realization of the End of the World — for it is precisely at the moment of the Last Judgment that the truths of the Gospel become an objective phenomenon for the whole universe, whereas until that moment they remain the possession of the partakers of the Covenant, albeit the New one. In the measure of the movement into the depth, toward the center of things, the Christian who walks the path of initiation comes face to face, visibly, with the objectivity of the Last Judgment and of the apocalyptic phenomena. Paradise and hell are converted for him from metaphors and images into a palpable and weighty reality. The illusions of this world are dispersed; through its husks the fire of true being shows through ever more brightly. A terrifying and beautiful reality stands before his gaze. The other world visibly conquers this world, drawing it into itself — illumining what is to be saved, mercilessly annihilating what deserves destruction. Light and fire in one countenance. “For our God is a consuming fire.”163

Apparatus · Chapters XXVIII–XXIX

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 161–163 are preserved. The Sanctus is Isa. 6:3; the burning coal, Isa. 6:6–7 (resuming the conjecture of Ch. XXII); the “second death” and the Book of Life, Rev. 20:6, 12; Paul’s third heaven, 2 Cor. 12:2–4; the closing verse, Heb. 12:29 (= Deut. 4:24), which gives the chapter and the Part their last word. The seraph/“burning one” etymology is sound. The observation that the Apocalypse is the one New Testament book never read in the Byzantine liturgy is accurate — a genuinely telling detail, whatever one makes of the initiatic construction placed upon it. The Old Rite spelling Isus appears once more in the final chapter’s “First and Second Comings of our Lord Jesus Christ.”

The myron problem. The chapter’s Dionysian warrant contains a substitution the reader should see. Dionysius’s Ecclesiastical Hierarchy IV — the chapter Dugin summarizes, with its “intelligible fragrance” and its ranking of the rite alongside the eucharist — concerns the consecration of the myron, the perfumed chrism of chrismation, consecrated by the hierarch; not the simple blessed oil (elei) of anointing. The tradition keeps the two substances carefully apart (the myron episcopally consecrated, the oil blessed by any priest), and transferring the Areopagite’s chrism-theology to the whole oil-economy fuses them. Confidence: high. Similarly, the chapter’s single “seraphic sacrament” absorbs rites the standard taxonomy distinguishes: the sacrament of Holy Unction proper (the euchelaion, for healing), the non-sacramental anointing at the festal vigil, the pre-baptismal “oil of gladness” on the font, and the funeral oil. The fusion is Dugin’s systematization — and, remarkably, the classic scriptural warrant for unction, James 5:14–15 (“is any sick among you? let him call for the elders…”), is never cited, perhaps because its plain therapeutic sense (“anointing him with oil… and the Lord shall raise him up”) sits awkwardly beneath a chapter that ranks healing as the rite’s “very lowest level.” The seraphic ascription itself — oil as the substance of the first angelic order — completes the angelological grid begun in Ch. XXII and is his own construction.

Reading note — Part IV complete. The Part opened by conceding Guénon everything about Rome and denying him the East; it closes with the full counter-system in place: baptism and chrismation as the Lesser Mysteries, ordination and the monastic tonsure as the Greater, the eucharist as the central initiatic ritual, confession as the operative technique, marriage as the androgyne restored, unction as seraphic contemplation — all seven sacraments remapped as a complete initiatic economy. Chapter XXIX then states the epistemological maximalism underneath the whole: initiation converts faith into “concrete and evident knowledge,” rigorous “like the scientific constatation of a phenomenon” — which is Guénon’s ideal of effective realization, transplanted whole into Orthodoxy, with its characteristic disdain for mere belief (“the outer faith of exotericism, which always remains only faith”) left intact. And the claim that the initiate co-enacts the Last Judgment — First and Second Comings coinciding in the rite, the contemplative already standing amid “the objectivity of the apocalyptic phenomena” — quietly interiorizes the eschatology that Part VII will exteriorize into history and empire: the same fire, turned inward here, outward there. “Light and fire in one countenance” is the book’s theology in six words.

Part V · The Christian Year

Part V · Chapter XXX

The Metaphysics of the Year

Метафизика года

Tradition regards every existence as a cycle. In accordance with the threefold division of the manifested (or of creation), one may construct a hierarchy of the mutual relation of three principial cycles.

In the earthly material world, taken separately, time moves progressively, in one direction, and irreversibly. Material reality, torn away from the remaining levels of the cosmos, is pure entropy, gravitation, a vector of attraction toward the ontological bottom, toward non-existence. In principle, the sole content of such a reality is gravitation, inertia, the pull toward wearing-out, toward zero. If one were to imagine an absurd world in which only the material plane existed, in it, indeed, no cycle would exist. But this is impossible in principle, since such a reality would be purely quantitative and wholly deprived of quality — that is, of all ontological content — and consequently it could not exist. To speak in the terms of the Hindu tradition, such a material world would be pure “tamas,” that is, “quantitative darkness.”164

But reality has a triple structure, as every sacred tradition asserts; and therefore even the earthly world possesses, of necessity, supplementary non-material dimensions, which to a considerable degree influence matter itself and its laws.

The subtle plane, the “world of the soul,” the “middle world,” has a duration which is pure rotation. Since in this subtle world there is no space — which, together with time, is an a priori condition of the existence of material reality — this circular rotation (cycle) encounters no “hindrances” proceeding from the “medium of rotation”; by analogy with earthly reality one may say that in the subtle world “friction,” “inertia,” the reaction of the medium, are entirely absent.

The spiritual world, for its part, has no duration at all — neither circular nor progressive — and all things abide there in simultaneous constancy. The one thing that distinguishes this world from the strictly uncreated divine nature is that it has a beginning and an end, whereas the divine nature is without beginning and without end. Heaven, the abode of the angels, is related to the world of the soul as a point to a circle. The point is the center and the content of the circle, its source and its essence. Thus one may say that the rotation in the subtle world is accomplished around the eternal celestial center, which belongs to the Kingdom of Heaven. The soul of men, of beings, and of things is a ceaseless rotation around the eternal axis. To this may be referred the words of Christ: “the Kingdom of God is within you.” The higher the quality of the soul, the smaller the symbolic radius separating the trajectory of rotation from the center.

In its turn, the pure rotation of the soul, combining with the material plane — or with the potential progressiveness of time proper to that plane — yields that form of cycle in which certain moments repeat and certain do not. This is precisely the basic characteristic of the earthly cycle, manifesting itself in various aspects in every being and every thing. Everything repeats, yet everything changes. Everything returns, but in a new form. The dynamics of earthly development (of history) is archetypally predetermined by the eternity of the spiritual heaven, which constitutes the meaning of history, its content, and is quickened by the circular rotation of the psychic plane. The best and most expressive disclosure of such a structure of reality is the earthly year, in which the revelation of the metaphysical structure of reality is naturally imprinted. The repetition of the seasons and of the natural phenomena corresponding to them points to the constancy of the motionless eternal structure, whose unfolding the annual movement is. The alternation of the seasons, the dynamics of the year, discloses the presence of the psychic, subtle force turning the wheel of the year. And finally, the eventful particularity of each annual cycle testifies to the spiral progressiveness which permits the construction of a chronology.

It is telling that such progressive chronology — corresponding to the most material side of the cycle and bound up with the lowest, gravitational, entropic levels of reality — appears in history only at the late stages, adjoining the end of the cycle, the eschatological epoch. The more ancient sacred civilizations knew no chronology at all, since for them a far greater interest attached either to the eternity and constancy imprinted in the year (this relates to the golden age, to the paradisal state of humanity, and to polar symbolism), or to the living dynamics of circular rotation (this relates to the subsequent epochs and is bound up with solar symbolism, since the sun embodies in itself precisely circular, cyclic movement).165

It is precisely by force of this natural symbolism, which visibly illustrates the whole structure of reality, that the year and its hieroglyphs, the calendar circles, were held sacred in all the sacred civilizations. More than that: it is precisely the annual cycle that is the secret foundation of the most ancient traditions, myths, sacred texts, esoteric and initiatic doctrines. And what is at issue, naturally, is not a divinization of nature, nor a “religion of agricultural cycles,” but a strictly metaphysical understanding of the triple structure of creation, expressed directly and visibly in the symbolism of the year. The fact that nearly all the mythological subjects of the Indo-European peoples — and of the Old Testament tradition as well — have a substratum directly connected with the structure of the annual cycle must be interpreted precisely in this metaphysical sense. The symbolism of the year, the seasons and the natural phenomena corresponding to them, the dynamics and logic of the annual movement of the sun, the rotation of the celestial sphere and the cyclic displacement of the constellations — all together they describe the concreteness of the sacred paradigm of reality, give being its directedness and orientation, mark the rhythm of cosmic existence.

It is very important to note that it is precisely the annual, and not the diurnal, cycle that lies at the foundation of the single universal sacred tradition — which, in the Christian perspective, may be identified with the primordial revelation of the Word, given through nature, the cosmos, and the world, and gradually lost by “fallen” humanity. The annual cycle of the alternation of the seasons unfolds before the human gaze the whole fullness of the sacred content of the circle of being — in which there participates not only the sun, descending and rising again, not only the moon, the stars, and the constellations (as in the diurnal cycle), but the whole surrounding nature, answering the sun’s descent to the nadir at the winter solstice with frost, sleep, and death, and its summer ascent to the zenith with warmth and heat, with the flowering of life.

Between the year and the day there exists a symbolic analogy, on which the daily ritual practice of the various traditions is founded. But it is precisely the year that is the most general period, which may be identified with sacred time itself; and it is not by chance that human life is reckoned in years, and not in days or months (nor in five-year or ten-year spans, and so on).

Part V · Chapter XXXI

The Great Circle

Великий Круг

The year, as the expression of the “rotation” of the soul of the world, has a strict symbolic structure, visibly represented in the image of a circle divided by a vertical cross into four parts. Each point of the cross’s intersection with the circle marks a key moment of the cycle — one of the solstices or equinoxes.

The lower point of the cross is the winter solstice, the day of the sun’s lowest ascent above the horizon in the year. The upper is the summer solstice, where the sun rises highest of all. On the right of the circle the cross coincides with the point of the spring equinox. On the left — with the point of the autumn equinox. The sun moves through the annual cycle counterclockwise.

The winter solstice is the transitional moment from the sun’s descent to its ascent. The summer solstice — the reverse, from ascent to descent. The spring equinox marks the beginning of the upper semicircle, where day is longer than night. The autumn equinox — the end of the upper semicircle and the beginning of night’s superiority over day.

In accordance with this simplest principle, the various traditions and religions marked the beginning of the year, or the festival of the New Year. Only rarely did this festival fall on some intermediate point — for example, on the middle between the autumn equinox and the winter solstice (as, for example, the Samhain of the Celts, or the sipapu of the North American Indians, and so on) — but even in that case the cultic meaning of the festival was correlated with the four basic events of the year corresponding to the great cross.

Not only time submitted to this sacred logic, but space as well, since the orientations of space acquired, in such a calendrical paradigm, a particular qualitative meaning. The North was correlated with summer and the summer solstice, the South with the winter solstice, the East with spring, and the West with winter. And it is very important that such a correspondence is adequate only within the bounds of the annual cycle. The diurnal cycle has a somewhat different structure: North — midnight, South — noon, East — morning, West — evening. In both cases South and North exchange their symbolic attributes, while East and West preserve theirs. Incidentally, it is precisely this state of affairs — and the incapacity of researchers to separate adequately those aspects of symbolism which relate to the annual circle from those which relate to the diurnal — that has led to confusion in the understanding of the foundational structure of the primordial sacred subject reflected in the most ancient traditions, signs, calendars, and symbols.166

Part V · Chapter XXXII

Orthodox Time

Православное время

The Christian tradition, in its liturgical, ritual, and cultic aspects, is wholly unintelligible without a conception of sacred time and its structure. The daily offices, the annual liturgical cycle, the fixed and the movable feasts — all of this rests upon the laws of the cycle, upon its logic and its symbolism. The services of the Church, like the hours appointed for prayer, draw the believer by their rhythm into that “circling motion” in which the intensive life of the soul is accomplished. In effect, the liturgical rhythm consciously and methodically leads a man into the current of the soul’s life, drawing his attention away from the entropic reality of matter, which pulls him down into the gravitational stream of non-existence and inertia. In going regularly to church, or in praying alone in his cell, a man performs an inner action opposite in its qualitative orientation to profane existence.

At that moment he returns to his inner dimension, turns from the dense to the subtle, from forward motion to circling motion, and thereby draws near to the eternal and unchanging. In the daily office this “rotary,” cyclical time appears in a still more articulated form.

First, the regularity of the services, their unaltering course day after day, discloses the soul-life of the Church — its constancy, its steadfastness, its independence of external material conditions.

Second, the service itself is filled with cultic repetitions — of liturgical and prayer formulas as much as of the appointed gestures of clergy and worshippers — and this too reproduces the circling of the subtle world.

Third, the movements of the priests in the course of the service repeatedly take on a “rotary” character: they go about the altar, they pass through the church with the censer, and so on. Of especial importance is the circling of the altar,167 that is, of the place of God’s presence, for in this action is contained the fullest and most vivid expression of the sacred essence of Life as revolution about an unmoving, uncreated point which, like the altar itself, stands above the heavens.

It matters, too, that the principal services are appointed twice in the day, morning and evening, marking thereby the sacred points of East and West, of Rising and Setting, the two extremities of the Cross’s horizontal beam. Only at the great feasts (the Nativity, Pascha) does the service last the whole night through, marking above all the point of midnight, the cosmic North (in the symbolism of the day), where the great mystery of the Resurrection — the Birth — of the Sun of Righteousness is accomplished.

So stands the matter with the daily symbolism of the Christian day. Within the year the structure of the Orthodox cycle is more complex, for besides the great feasts, which pertain directly to the principal figures of Orthodoxy (to Isus Christ, the Holy Spirit, the Virgin Mary, John the Forerunner, the apostles and the angels), there is the Old Testament rank of the prophets and patriarchs, and there are the days of the Christian saints. One may say that the chief feasts mark the cardinal points of the sacred Orthodox year, which recapitulates eternity and, more than this, takes into itself the supra-celestial reality that opens to the Orthodox through the sacrificial ever-presence of the Son, the uncreated God.

The winter solstice, the lowest point of the year’s Great Cross, coincides with the Nativity of Christ, the birth of the Spiritual Sun. This is the chief feast of the Orthodox winter, opening within the “rotary” cycle a saving, supra-created dimension, by grace of which the Christian receives direct contact with the uncreated principle168 standing above eternity itself — with the pre-eternal and pre-worldly God, who “came down from heaven, and was incarnate of the Holy Spirit and the Virgin Mary, and was made man” at the holy midnight, the great midnight of the world.

The spring equinox is marked by the Orthodox Pascha, the Resurrection of Christ. Christ tramples down death by death, conquers night and cold, and by His Resurrection proclaims the coming of a new dawn, a universal spring. In this season the sun by its natural course imitates, as it were, the Saviour: it quits the winter frost, calls back to life the nature that has slept since autumn, and enters the half-year of “day” after the half-year of “night.” The summer solstice is the summit of the light’s ascent. The risen Christ leaves the earth of men and ascends into the uncreated light of the Trinitarian Unity. To this corresponds the feast of the Lord’s Ascension.

Close upon the Ascension at the summer’s peak comes the feast of the Holy Spirit, the Day of the Spirit, marking the fulfilment of Christ’s promise and the coming of the Comforter, the Paraclete. This descent of the Holy Spirit upon the apostles nature “illustrates” by the beginning of the sun’s descent toward autumn. There begins that half of the Orthodox Year which belongs to the cycle of the economy of the Spirit. And to autumn fall the three chief feasts of the Mother of God — the Dormition of the Most Holy Theotokos, the Entry of the Theotokos into the Temple, and the Nativity of the Theotokos.

The Virgin Mary, as the image of the whole of created nature, is set at the foot of the year’s circle. To her the Son descends for the Incarnation; the Spirit of the Lord overshadows her, coming down from heaven. Like the Jews, the Orthodox place the day of the Church’s new year — the Orthodox New Year — at the autumn equinox. And it is no accident, as the hesychasts observe,169 that the liturgical New Year lies close beside the Nativity of the Theotokos: with that event began the accomplishment of the world’s salvation, and for Christians, therefore, the Most Pure Virgin is indeed the true beginning.

Of the other evangelical figures it is John the Forerunner who is bound most intimately to the Cross of the year. He symbolises the descending half of the year, for he closes the prophetic chain of the Old Testament. It is appointed to him “to decrease,”170 as it is appointed to the sun to descend toward winter.171 The winter solstice is linked with Nicholas the Wonderworker, so venerated among the Orthodox, and especially in Russia. And the summer solstice has passed, in part, to Elijah the Prophet, who hurls lightnings from the heavens and pours down rain — who brings down, that is, the heavenly fire, the living water, from on high: symbolic forms of the descent of grace, which in its highest, uncreated hypostasis is the economic work of the Holy Spirit.172

But all these sacred feasts, the services devoted to them, the liturgical formulas and Gospel passages bound up with them, do not merely express the constancy of the Church’s “rotary” life; they are appointed to lead the faithful out beyond the soul, to the aeonic heaven where all sacred events are present at once, in the angelic reality of the eternal present. And the wonder-working reality of the eucharist, accomplished at every liturgy, opens a direct way not only to the eternity of the Heavens but beyond its bounds — to that secret centre of the Orthodox Year around which the liturgical cycle revolves. This central point is the uncreated light of the energies of the Most Holy Trinity, poured out before all ages and before all worlds, independently of the presence or absence of creation, as the absolute abundance of the Glory of the Lord.

At whatever point of the year’s liturgical cycle the believer may stand, his chief aim is to move inward along the Cross, toward the centre where the beams intersect. That centre no longer lies in time; it stands on the far side of duration. It is the source of the Year. The Word of God.

Apparatus · Chapters XXX–XXXII

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 164–166 are preserved; no markers fall in Chapter XXXII. “The Kingdom of God is within you” is Luke 17:21. “Tamas” as “quantitative darkness” fuses the Sāṃkhya guṇa with the idiom of Guénon’s Reign of Quantity, which governs the whole entropic-matter doctrine of Chapter XXX. Chapter XXXII stands in the source transcription as a single paragraph — the shortest chapter of the book so far; whether this is the author’s bridge-chapter or a transcription loss will be checked against the paginated edition during the reader build, and the continuity of the note numbering (166 before it, and the next marker after it) will decide.

The unnamed master of Part V. Guénon recedes in these chapters and another authority silently advances: Herman Wirth — the Dutch-German theorist of a polar primordial script and calendar, first head of the SS Ahnenerbe, whom Dugin had introduced to the Russian reader in The Hyperborean Theory (1993). The signature doctrines are all Wirth’s: the year (not the day) as the vessel of the primordial revelation; the circle quartered by the cross as the proto-calendar from which scripts and myths descend; the sequence polar-then-solar symbolism; and Chapter XXXI’s closing methodological complaint — that researchers confuse annual with diurnal symbolism — is Wirth’s own signature argument. The “single universal sacred tradition… given through nature and gradually lost” is Wirth’s Urreligion baptized: Dugin identifies it, in a subordinate clause, with the primordial revelation of the Word. The reader should know that the calendrical science of this Part rests on an author whose reconstructions academic prehistory has never accepted.

Factual cautions. (1) The claim that “the more ancient sacred civilizations knew no chronology at all” is untenable as history — Sumerian king lists, Egyptian annals and regnal dating, Assyrian limmu-lists are all chronography, and all ancient; it functions here as Guénonian typology (orientation toward eternity versus process), not as archaeology. (2) The source prints “Simhain” for the Celtic festival; the translation gives the standard Samhain. (3) The sipapu of the Puebloan peoples is not a New Year festival: it is the small hole in the kiva floor symbolizing the place of emergence from the underworld — an architectural-cosmological feature, not a feast (the Hopi year-opening festival Dugin may have meant is Wúwuchim). Confidence: high. (4) The annual orientation table as printed assigns the West to winter — which contradicts both the South = winter solstice assignment two sentences earlier and the chapter’s own observation that East and West “preserve their attributes” between the two cycles (which requires West = autumn, matching West = evening). Almost certainly a slip for “autumn”; translated as written per the project’s convention. A book whose method is calendrical precision misprinting its own compass is an irony the apparatus records without comment.

Reading note. The theoretical frame of Part V is now set: three durations (entropic line, psychic circle, spiritual point), the year as their visible synthesis, the quartered circle as its hieroglyph, and the liturgy as the machine that moves the soul from the line onto the circle. The chapters that follow apply the frame — the Cross (XXXIII), the two mountains of Transfiguration and Ascension (XXXIV), and then the most Wirthian stretch of the book: the Russian folk calendar read as esoteric text (XXXV–XXXVIII), where Elijah’s chariot and Paraskeva-Pyatnitsa will be made to carry the polar tradition inside Orthodox popular piety.

Restoration (14 Aug 2026). Both texts were restored on 14 August 2026 from the studfile.net transcription (a .docx upload, file 7681830, 79 web pages), an independent lineage from wikireading, litresp, and booksonline. Chapter XXXII appears there in full (fourteen paragraphs against wikireading’s one), with note markers 167–172 in place — closing the 166→173 marker gap exactly as the batch-17 diagnosis predicted, and confirming that the wikireading loss was transcriptional, not authorial. Confidence: high.

Restoration (14 Aug 2026). Two incidental double-attestations from the same fetch: the “Simhain” spelling and the assignment of West to winter in Chapter XXXI both appear in studfile as in wikireading. Both flags are therefore authorial, not corruption in either transcription. Confidence: high.

Chapter XXXII markers. 167 — the circling of the altar; 168 — the uncreated principle above eternity; 169 — the hesychasts on the New Year and the Nativity of the Theotokos; 170 — “to decrease”; 171 — the sun’s descent to winter; 172 — Elijah, grace, and the economy of the Spirit. Note contents are not preserved in this transcription (markers only), per the standing decision of 14 August 2026 to retain markers without contents.

Restoration — The Church New Year is not the equinox. The Byzantine ecclesiastical new year (the Indiction) falls on 1 September, roughly three weeks before the equinox; and the Jewish new year, 1 Tishrei, is a lunisolar date that wanders relative to the equinox. Dugin’s “like the Jews, the Orthodox place the new year at the autumn equinox” assimilates both to the solar cross of the year. Translated as written; confidence in the error moderate-high. His subordinate observation, however — that the liturgical New Year stands close beside the Nativity of the Theotokos (1 September before 8 September), and that the year therefore opens with the beginning of salvation — is a genuine liturgical commonplace, fairly credited to homiletic tradition.

Restoration — The four-point mapping trims the movable cycle. Pascha is bounded by the equinox but movable across thirty-five days; the Ascension and Pentecost float with it through May and June and only occasionally approach the solstice. The clean assignment of Pascha to the equinox and the Ascension–Pentecost pair to the solstitial summit is the Wirthian cross imposed on a calendar that, in its movable half, refuses the geometry. This belongs to the same schema-trimming family as the silent omission of the Apostles’ Fast flagged in the Chapter XXXVIII apparatus. The Nativity-at-solstice equation repeats the fourth-century liturgical fixing already flagged there; the assignment of the Dormition (15 August) to “autumn” repeats the trimming noted in the same place.

Restoration — The Forerunner and the descending year: genuine. John 3:30 (“He must increase, but I must decrease”) read against the two solstitial nativities — the Forerunner’s on 24 June, the Lord’s at the year’s nadir — is authentic patristic solstitial symbolism, played upon in Augustine’s sermons on the two feasts. Credited without reservation. The Nikola-winter / Ilya-summer folk pairing is likewise attested (Uspensky’s classic study; see the Chapter XXXV–XXXVI apparatus).

Restoration — Reading note. XXXII is the hinge on which Part V turns: Chapter XXXI supplies the Wirthian wheel, and this chapter gives the wheel a Palamite hub — the centre of the liturgical circle identified with the uncreated light of the Trinity’s energies, “poured out before all ages… independently of the presence or absence of creation.” The chapter then dissolves the calendar into the Logos in four closing words. Whatever one makes of the rim, the hub is the book’s own: no page of Wirth ends in “the source of the Year. The Word of God.”

Part V · Chapter XXXIII

The Symbolism of the Cross

Символизм Креста

The Christian tradition uses, for the expression of its teaching, symbols which admit of multiple interpretation. The most “material” aspect of a symbol relates to the historical side of the Gospel narrative, of the Old Testament, or of the church traditions and the texts of the lives of the saints. The highest, spiritual aspect of the symbolism opens the mysteries of Christian metaphysics. To the intermediate level belongs everything connected with the three-part structure of creation — of the sacred cosmos lying between man and the uncreated Godhead. This cosmic, or cosmological, symbolism contradicts neither the historical concreteness nor the highest metaphysical significance of this or that Christian subject, sign, or personage. All three levels combine harmoniously with one another and serve as steps for the raising of the contemplative spirit from the outer to the inner, from the human to the Divine. And this intermediate level of Orthodox symbolism is bound, more than to anything else, precisely to the liturgical year — which is itself the middle instance between earth and heaven, between the motionlessness of the angelic archetypes and the gravitational inertia of matter.

The threefoldness of the meaning of Christian symbols can be shown on the example of the cross — one of the chief signs of Christianity, the foundation of the tradition, a sacred object and a ritual gesture which has, among Orthodox Christians, a purely initiatic meaning.

At the first (historical) level the cross points to the passion of the Savior. It is the sign of the execution of the Son of God, the dread symbol of the sacrificial tragedy of the Lamb, of his suffering, of the unbearable torments which He endured for the redemption of humanity (and of the whole world), for the granting to the creature of the grace-filled possibility of deification. At this level the cross is a symbol of grief and sorrow, a testimony to the depth of the estrangement of creation at the last stages of the cycle — where the most high God himself is condemned to the humiliating execution of a slave. It is telling that the Catholics (and to a still greater degree the Protestants) in fact reduce the whole meaning of the Lord’s cross precisely to this tragic aspect — since in the Western Church the material, historical, strictly earthly side of Christianity predominates and is almost the only one, which makes its teaching an exclusively moral, and not a metaphysical or esoteric, doctrine.

At the highest, metaphysical level the cross is the new tree of life, the axis of the world, the concentration of the uncreated being of the Trinity, which pours itself out in the form of luminous energies in all ontological “directions.” It is likewise the four rivers of paradise173 — not of the earthly, nor even of the celestial, but of the divine, Trinitarian paradise. And in this aspect what matters most of all is the point which is the center of the intersection of the two beams of the cross. This is the symbol of the divine reality from which the luminous grace flows forth. Such a metaphysical understanding of the cross is conjoined with the glorious, triumphal, conquering side of this symbol as the sign of absolute victory. This is the celestial and supra-celestial cross, whose signs are the four sacred living creatures, the cherubim, the four-winged angels. This is the cross radiant, grace-filled, “most honorable and life-giving” — a sign pure and deprived of all materiality. And the joyful veneration of the cross flowing from this meaning is characteristic precisely of the most spiritual, supra-historical currents in Christianity, and especially of Orthodoxy. It is perfectly evident that one and the same sign, in the two cases (in the historical aspect and in the metaphysical), has a diametrically opposite meaning, and must call forth in the faithful a different spiritual disposition.

The intermediate aspect of the cross is precisely its cyclic symbolism — that great cross of the solstices and equinoxes which divides the annual cycle. This cross is a symbol neither of pure suffering nor of pure triumph. In it there is both the one and the other. This specific duality of the cross of the year is especially expressive in the eight-pointed Orthodox cross. Here the upper part of the vertical relates to the light of the summer solstice. This is the positive part of the year, bound up with the Divine nature of Christ and with the Ascension. The lower part of the vertical symbolizes His Descent from the heavens and the Incarnation, whose result was the martyrdom on Golgotha. And the slant of the lower horizontal (third) crossbar points, in the case of its lowered end, to the negative symbolism of autumn (this is also interpreted as an indication of the “evil thief” crucified together with Christ), and in the case of the raised end, to the symbolism of spring (this is the “good thief,” who acknowledged Christ at the last moment and, by the promise of the God-man, entered paradise first).

It is very important to underscore also the analogous cyclic symbolism in the schematic representation, traditional in Orthodoxy, of the cross upon the steps,174 with two attributes of the Savior’s sufferings on the cross: on the left, the reed with the sponge of vinegar; on the right, the spear of the centurion Longinus.

Sometimes to the right and left of the upper part of the cross’s vertical the sun and moon are also depicted — which points unambiguously to the cyclic, annual symbolism of this subject. Here it is easy to correlate the image of the “skull,” placed inside the “hill” — that is, in the “cave” — with the winter solstice, with the place where the sun dies. Moreover, in the most ancient calendars the winter solstice was represented hieroglyphically precisely as a “cave.” The reed with the sponge fastened to it appears as a circle set at the summit of a straight line. This part of the schema is correlated with the East and the spring equinox,175 the “place” of the sun’s ascent, which is encoded in the circle of the sponge. It is astonishing that precisely such a hieroglyph corresponded to the spring season and the spring equinox in the most ancient cave calendars; and, according to Professor Herman Wirth, it is precisely this figure that lies at the foundation of the representation of the most ancient letter “Ρ” (“rho”) of the Greek alphabet.

The spear of Longinus, depicted in the left part of this symbol, is a shaft with a head — like a stylized letter “T” with the ends of the upper crossbar lowered. But the most ancient sacred calendar circles designated by precisely this symbol the autumn equinox — the point where the sun descends toward winter, as the man schematically depicted in this manner lowers his “arms.” According to the researchers of mythology, the most ancient religious subjects in which a spear figures are always, in one way or another, connected with the calendrical meaning of this sign.176

Thus the historical particulars of the sufferings on the cross depict, at the cosmic level, the ordering of the cosmos, the sacred rhythmics of its cycle, the luminous structure of creation. At the metaphysical level the same symbols signify more transcendent realities. Thus the sponge with vinegar, which the mocking executioners held out to the suffering Christ for the quenching of his thirst, symbolizes God’s taking into himself the poisonous taste of immanent creation — into which He is immersed (or which He draws into himself) at the moment of the Incarnation.177

The spear of Longinus178 symbolizes, in metaphysics, the sacred “instrument” (the “contemplative will”) thanks to which the transfiguring Blood of God pours out in grace upon humanity, thirsting for salvation and deification.

Part V · Chapter XXXIV

The Two Mountains

Две горы

On Orthodox icons one and the same symbolic detail is very often repeated, invariably accompanying the most various sacred subjects — independently of whether what is at issue are Gospel and Old Testament motifs or the depiction of the lives of Christian saints. This detail is the placing, in the background of the icon, of two mountains. Unquestionably they have an exceedingly important sacred significance — which, however, is rather difficult to derive from the Orthodox tradition itself, since the two mountains are depicted independently of the dogmatic meaning of this or that episode.

Here, as in all other cases, the symbol may have three different levels of description, corresponding to historical, cyclic, and metaphysical reality. And in this concrete case it is not always possible to establish unambiguously the historical meaning of the two mountains.

As for the highest, metaphysical significance of this symbol, it consists in two aspects. On the one hand, the mountain is the image of the axis of the world, of the central point of creation, around which it “rotates.” It is the image of the pole, of the center. And as such it is present on sacred images, on icons, underscoring their metaphysical consecratedness, their connection with the polar, central, essential side of reality.179

On the other hand, mountains may symbolize created reality as mass, as prime matter, as the cosmic background subject to transfiguration in the process of spiritual work. To this relate, in particular, those iconographic subjects where, instead of two identical mountains, there are symmetrically depicted, on one side, a wild mountain (nature, matter, created being), and on the other — a temple, a city,180 and so on: that is, the result of the divine action upon the structure of the universe. Thus this subject may signify the passage from the natural state to the supernatural, and even from the created to the uncreated.

At the level of the annual cycle the same two mountains acquire a new significance, bound this time to the hieroglyphic symbolism of the seasons. The point is that the two mountains were the most ancient calendrical hieroglyph designating the winter solstice — the place where the mystery of death and rebirth is accomplished, the mystery of the passage from the old to the transfigured (the new). This moment of the annual cycle was the most important from the initiatic point of view, since in it was summed up the whole fullness of initiation as such — which is the passage from one state of existence (material, natural, inertial) to another (spiritual, supra-natural, conscious).

The two mountains are the two arcs of the solar trajectory before the winter solstice and after it181 (at least, so the matter stands in the North, in the Hyperborean regions, whence, according to Tradition, began humanity’s descent to the South, into the historically known regions of the ancient civilizations). According to Herman Wirth, these two arcs, or two mountains, gave rise to the most ancient sacred hieroglyph, which is the Latin (and Greek) letter “B” turned through 45 degrees. Indeed the letter itself developed, according to Wirth, out of this sign. Hence, incidentally, derives the particular veneration of the letter “B” in certain initiatic traditions, which appeal to the fact that the Bible itself, in its ancient Hebrew original, begins with the letter bet — “Bereshit bara Elohim.” It is interesting to note that in the Greek translation the first letter of the Bible becomes ε, epsilon — Ἐν ἀρχῇ ἐποίησεν ὁ θεὸς τὸν οὐρανὸν καὶ τὴν γῆν — while in its turn, in the Slavonic translation, the same hieroglyph “В” — the two mountains — takes its sacred place once more: «В начале сотвори Бог небо и землю» (Gen. 1:1).

What takes place between the two mountains possesses, in calendrical symbolism, the highest spiritual significance. It is the mystery of the Divine Birth, the becoming-man of God and the opening of the road to the deification of man. Consequently the whole initiatic content of Orthodoxy stands under the shadow of precisely this symbol (the two mountains) — which is why it appears so often on the icons, which are, in their turn, visual depictions of initiatic realities.

Apparatus · Chapters XXXIII–XXXIV

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 173–181 are preserved. The four rivers of paradise, Gen. 2:10–14; the four living creatures fuse Ezekiel 1 (four-winged) with Revelation 4; “most honorable and life-giving” is the standing liturgical epithet of the Cross; the good thief’s promise, Luke 23:43; the first words of Genesis are given in Hebrew, Greek, and Slavonic (the source transcription garbles the Greek through a font artifact — По profession restored here to the Septuagint text). A textual discovery concerning Chapter XXXII: the markers of the present chapters run 173–181, while Chapter XXXI ended at 166 — six note markers (167–172) therefore belong to Chapter XXXII, whose transcription in the working source consists of a single unannotated paragraph. The chapter as issued in the sixteenth instalment is thus certainly incomplete — a transcription loss, not an authorial bridge — and will be restored from the paginated edition; the reader edition will carry the full text. Confidence: high.

Wirth, now named. What the previous instalment inferred, these chapters declare: Herman Wirth is cited by name twice — for the sponge-circle as the proto-form of Greek rho, and for the two mountains as the rotated “B” of the winter-solstice gate. The whole hieroglyphic method here — cave as the sign of the dead sun, the lowered-armed man of autumn, seasonal signs evolving into alphabet letters, the Hyperborean descent southward — is the apparatus of Wirth’s Heilige Urschrift der Menschheit, whose sign-tables Dugin is applying to the Golgotha schema. The reader should hold two things at once: the iconographic base is genuine — the schematic Calvary cross with spear and reed, the sun and moon flanking the upper beam, the skull of Adam in the cave beneath Golgotha, and the icon-mountains (gorki) are all real conventions of Byzantine-Russian sacred art — while the calendrical decoding laid over them has no standing outside Wirth’s system, which academic prehistory and palaeography have never accepted (the derivations of rho and beta from solstice hieroglyphs are fantasy philology). The traditional readings the chapters displace are worth recording: the slanted footbeam’s received exegesis is the balance-beam of judgment, its raised end pointing the good thief to paradise (Dugin integrates this and adds the seasons); the skull is Adam’s, buried at Golgotha (“the place of the skull”), so that the Blood falls on the first man — a legend the calendrical reading flattens into solar death; and art history derives the icon-mountains from Hellenistic landscape scenography via Byzantine models, not from solstice signs. The Slavonic «В» flourish is elegant and empty: the letter is the preposition “in.”

Cautions. The claim that Catholicism and Protestantism reduce the Cross “to the tragic aspect alone” simplifies to the point of caricature — the Christus triumphans of the first Western millennium, the Exaltation feast common to both churches, and the entire Easter liturgy of the West say otherwise; the polemic recycles Chapter XVIII’s method of dating the whole confession by its late devotional style. The metaphysical glosses on the instruments (vinegar as the “poisonous taste of immanent creation” absorbed by God; the spear as “contemplative will”) are Dugin’s own constructions, without patristic source, though the first has a certain dark brilliance.

Reading note. These two chapters are the hinge on which Part V turns from theory to application: the three-level hermeneutic (historical, cyclic, metaphysical) is announced, and immediately the middle level — the one Wirth owns — does all the work, with the metaphysical level reduced to brief codas. The direction of interpretation should be noticed: the Passion is not read into the year; the year is read out of the Passion, as its prior and deeper stratum. What remains of Part V (XXXV–XXXVIII) applies the same machine to the Russian folk calendar — Elijah’s chariot and Paraskeva-Pyatnitsa — where the stakes of the method become fully visible.

Part V · Chapter XXXV

The Russian Year and the
Orthodox Tradition

Русский год и православная традиция

The Orthodox liturgical calendar was superimposed, in Rus', upon a more ancient circle of the Sacred Year, in which all the most important points of the annual symbolism were bound up with diverse pagan myths. The discovery of a multitude of evident parallels between the system of strictly Christian feasts and pagan rites and calendar myths compelled the researchers of this question to speak of a “paganization” of Christianity — of a reinterpretation of its subjects and doctrines in a pagan key, of its being fitted to the preceding non-Christian paradigm. Most often the argument adduced in favor of such a view is the noticeable rethinking, by the Russians, of the functions of this or that saint on the basis of a formal resemblance of their names to certain Russian words (so-called “folk etymology”). Thus the unmercenary martyrs Cosmas and Damian become the patrons of blacksmiths — the Christian equivalent of the mythic subterranean Smith, the Slavic Hephaestus — solely on the ground that the Greek name Cosmas182 (Russian Kuzma) resembles the Russian word for “smith” (kuznets). The same is evident in the case of St. Blasius (Vlasii), who became the patron of cattle (“Vlasii” resembles “Volos”). The holy great-martyr Helen was converted into the patroness of flax-spinning (“Elena” chimes with lyon, flax), and the feast of St. Barbara was marked by the boiling (“Varvara” — varit’, to boil) of kashas, and so on…

Most often in such cases one speaks of “double faith” (that is, of the preservation of pagan beliefs beneath the mask of Christianity), or of the deep ignorance and childish naïveté of a “wild” people. In reality, from the point of view of traditionalism, the matter stands far more complexly. The point is that the pre-Christian tradition (in our case, the Russian sacred tradition) was neither the result of the primitive fantasies of an “undeveloped people,” nor a particular form of independent and finished demon-worship, as Christian authors sometimes polemically asserted. The “Hellenic” tradition — and in the symbolic sense the Russian, Slavic, Indo-European tradition has every ground for inclusion in the category of the “Hellenic”183 — was, from the full Christian point of view, a distorted continuation of the Japhethic sacrality, which developed, however, in a channel distinct not only from the law of Moses and the people of Israel, but from the whole Abrahamic line bound up with Shem — a son of Noah just as Japheth was. Consequently, in denying “Hellenism” as a finished theological construction, Christianity could not fail to acknowledge the presence in it of certain adequate sacred elements, connected not with metaphysics but with cosmology and the other secondary spheres. Thus nothing prevented the holy fathers from studying Hellenic philosophy, rhetoric, aesthetics, and physics, introducing all these auxiliary disciplines into the general order of purely Orthodox theology as supplementary components. Incidentally, even the language of the Gospel is Greek — “Hellenic” — and language plays an enormous role in a sacred tradition.184

The very sphere of the Sacred Year has to do precisely with cosmology, with the sacred structure of the world — concerning whose general order there are practically no disagreements among the full traditions. Disagreements begin where the metaphysical evaluation is at issue, the transcendent meaning and theological significance of this or that cosmic phenomenon; and there, indeed, both “Hellenism” and “Judaism” enter into irresolvable contradiction with genuine Christianity. But at the lower level — the level of description, of constatation (albeit a sacred one!) — all the traditions coincide in general outline.

According to this logic, the identification of strictly Christian feasts with certain pre-Christian sacred subjects of the calendar among the Russians should be regarded as a providential, spiritually grounded, and entirely non-accidental phenomenon — in which was reflected the Christianization of the cosmos, the churching and transfiguration of one of the branches of the most ancient Japhethic tradition, brought in Orthodoxy to the highest metaphysical perspective. Not the abolition of the one for the sake of the radically other, but the conversion of the one into the other — herein lies the sacred meaning of the Russian Christian Year. Not naïveté, not survivals of paganism, not “popular wildness” stood behind the elaboration of the Russian local Orthodox traditions bound up with the calendar, but the providential return of the sacred Slavic language — consisting not only of the names of objects but of symbols, myths, calendar correspondences, and images — to the fullness and celestial harmony of the source.
