Through the Incarnation of the Word, through the Church, there took place the restoration of the almost effaced and faded traces of the creative, primordial participation of the Logos in the making of the world — of the sacred memories that “by Him all things were made,” that everything arose through Him and was created by Him. If “folk etymology,” at the rational level, contradicted historical etymology, then at the level of the most ancient primal meanings of words — of the pre-Babel language, the “language of the angels” — the calendar identifications were by no means accidental, and restored a long-forgotten wisdom, revived by the Church: the initiatic knowledge of the interconnection of all natural and supernatural phenomena.

Therefore the specificity of the Russian Orthodox calendar, and of the rites and traditions bound up with it, helps one to understand the harmonious logic of the full Tradition, which found its highest completion and transfigured embodiment in Orthodox metaphysics and Christian doctrine.

One may liken the Christianization of the Sacred Year among the Russians to the symbolic gesture performed by the apostle Paul in Athens. In the Areopagus the apostle points to the altar of the Unknown God, the Deus Absconditus, saying that this is the God-Trinity.185 It is important to note that this “unknown god” of the Greeks was understood not simply as “some further god, as yet unknown” within the general polytheistic ensemble, but as the highest transcendent apophatic principle, abiding on the far side of manifestation. The eschatological Revelation of this transcendent God in Jesus Christ transfigured “Hellenism,” annihilated its “pagan,” “polytheistic” side, and pointed out the fitting place, within the frame of Orthodoxy, of those sides of the Greek tradition which combined harmoniously with the metaphysical truth of the New Testament. So also in the case of the Slavic calendar: in the course of its conjunction with the Christian liturgical cycle there took place the providential disclosure of its “unknown center,” its saving cross, its highest metaphysical meaning. In place of scattered myths, and the veneration of the sacred symbolic events of the year as independent essences, gods, idols, in the Russian Orthodox calendar the true proportions were restored: the most important cosmic phenomena and symbolic correspondences of the spatio-temporal plane were included in a non-contradictory and harmonious picture of the Orthodox cosmos — subordinated to the Lord Jesus Christ, pierced through by the energies of the Holy Spirit, ordered according to the pre-eternal counsel of the Most Holy Trinity. And in this everything true was preserved, while the accidental or the perverted was cast off.

Properly speaking, it is precisely this that explains the disappearance of the traces of paganism among the Russians to a far greater degree than among the peoples of the West. The point is that the pre-Christian tradition of the Slavs passed over almost wholly into the sphere of the Russian Orthodox traditions; and its traces must be sought not outside the Church, but precisely in the Church itself — where it acquired metaphysical fullness, transfigured in the rays of the Good News.

Part V · Chapter XXXVI

The Summer Chariot of the
Prophet Elijah

Летняя колесница пророка Илии

Let us give only a few examples of how the Christianization of the Sacred Year proceeded among the Russians. One of the most expressive conjunctions of the pre-Christian tradition with the Orthodox cycle of the Russian Church is the feast of the prophet Elijah, marked on the 20th of July (Old Style). Long ago the researchers noticed that the cycle of legends and traditions bound up with this feast has an ancient, archaic, pre-Christian dimension. The hypothesis is often advanced that earlier, on this day, the Slavs celebrated the day of Perun, the “god” of thunderstorm and rain, the analogue of the Greek Zeus or the Roman Jupiter. And so it is customarily held in our time: faithful to their pagan habits, the Slavs, it is said, “paganized” the Old Testament prophet, and beneath the mask of Elijah continued to offer sacrifices to the ancient divinity and to venerate him in pagan rituals.

This point of view is characteristic of profane researchers, who (consciously or unconsciously) proceed from essentially atheistic conceptions of religion, holding it to be a social convention, an artificially elaborated ideology, a product of “human hands.” In that case, naturally, one can speak only of accidental and fragmentary coincidences, and the sacred, the providential, the spiritual, the cyclic is perceived only as metaphor. From the point of view of Tradition, all history is, on the contrary, providential and sacred. There are no accidents in it, and the human factor, consciously or not, submits to a determinate Divine plan, which is fulfilled with unswerving consecutiveness both through the subject of history (man) and through the object of history (the outer cosmos). God governs the world doubly — through the reason of men and the elements of the outer world, bringing all to a single harmony and final goal. Consequently the pre-Christian tradition, too, is not the product of the primitive, simplified fantasies of an “underdeveloped” humanity, but, in the worst case, the result of the degeneration of a once full and spiritual picture of the world, inherited by the peoples from the sources of history, from the earthly paradise — from that state of the world in which the traces of the Divine Word were evident and intelligible in it.

In our concrete case, the Slavic Perun himself is not some independent being, artificially conjoined afterward with a wholly independent Old Testament personage, but the personification of a certain cosmic force which can be raised to a celestial archetype; and the latter, in its turn, necessarily has a determinate place within the context of the full Christian doctrine of the structure of reality. In other words, instead of explaining one particular and individualized figure through another (Perun through the prophet Elijah, or the reverse), it would be more correct to turn to that common sacred meaning which stands behind both personages, on the far side of historical or pagan contexts.

In the Russian feast of the prophet Elijah, the following is immediately striking: it falls on that point of the calendar circle which, in the runic calendars of the ancient Germans (and possibly of all the Indo-Europeans), corresponded to the rune Ilx [glyph lost in transcription], which designated at once an antlered elk, a lily (water-lily), a sheaf of arrows, a tree with roots and crown. This rune relates to the second half of July. The root il, according to modern linguists, means “light,” “whiteness,” “fire” in many Indo-European languages. Thus, in particular, the Russian word solntse (sun), the Greek helios, the Latin sol, the Sanskrit surya, are formed precisely from this most ancient base. But most astonishing of all is that the Semitic root Il, Ilu, meaning “god,” “divinity,” and entering etymologically into the composition of the name Elijah (literally, “stronghold of the Lord” in Hebrew), has, in accordance with the newest researches in the field of linguistics, the very same origin.186 In other words, the Indo-European name of the calendar rune and the name of the Old Testament prophet are connected not only at the level of “folk etymology,” but entirely directly and historically.

Now let us turn to the hieroglyph itself, depicting a sheaf of arrows or lightnings. The very same hieroglyph is met in the archaic depictions of the Greek Zeus or the Roman Jupiter, mythologically kindred to the Slavic Perun. But if the rune Ilx is more ancient and primordial than the name of Perun (and it is known that, time and again, the attributes of a mythological personage precede the personage himself), then a certain hero or symbolic being by the name of Il may prove to be far more ancient than the properly pagan divinities of the pre-Christian mythologies. Perun’s arrows, then, should with greater justice be called the arrows of “Il,” or of “Elijah.” And this means that the Orthodox calendar feast of the prophet Elijah must have been perceived by the newly converted Slavs not as an innovation, nor as the drawing into the sacred context of some alien or obscure personage, but as a return to the more ancient and primordial, “paradisal” times of their own sacred tradition — preceding the Babylonian confusion of tongues.

On the other hand, the Old Testament Elijah himself can be perceived as an exclusively historical person — whose whole existence is a sequence of unique individual deeds and occurrences having no symbolic or prefigurative meaning — only within the frame of strict Judaic creationism. From the Orthodox point of view, on the contrary, all the subjects of the Old Testament without exception have in them a symbolic dimension. In the first place they prefigure the New Testament; but in the second, they serve as a description of the structure of the sacred cosmos. Thus the Old Testament prophet Elijah, taken up in a fiery chariot into heaven, is both a herald of the Ascension of Christ and a symbol laying open the structure of the sacred cosmos — where, on the far side of the firmament, lies the world of immaterial fire and immaterial water. Lightning and rain are the natural forms of the disclosure of supernatural celestial realities. The righteous man who accomplishes personal spiritual realization, ascending into the heavens, enters — besides the subjective psychic phenomena and states — into interaction with the objective natural aspects of the cosmos as well,187 co-participating in the accomplishment of the world’s harmony both spiritually and corporeally. Consequently the Old Testament prophet Elijah himself, in the fullness of his spiritual feat, touched the objective side of the cosmos and realized that angelic, luminous archetype which is imprinted in the most ancient hieroglyph Ilx. The historical in him touched the supra-historical; he accomplished the passage from the periphery of the Great Year to its center, and in the course of this was identified with the archetype of the cycle, with the solar rune of light and heaven.

Taking all these considerations into account, one may say that the celestial archetype of the prophet Elijah — of that same Elijah whom the Orthodox Christians venerate on the 20th of July — is more ancient and more primordial than Perun, than Zeus, and than the Old Testament righteous man himself. It ascends to the paradisal epoch, when time was created. And this initial point of sacred history manifested itself anew in the last times, when the Revelation of Christ restored in all its fullness the paradisal paradigm of the cosmos.

Apparatus · Chapters XXXV–XXXVI

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 182–187 are preserved. The altar to the Unknown God, Acts 17:23; “by Him all things were made,” the Creed with John 1:3 behind it; Elijah’s fiery ascent, 2 Kings 2:11; the immaterial fire and water beyond the firmament resume Chapter VII. The rune-glyph in Chapter XXXVI stands in the source as an empty pair of parentheses — the image did not survive transcription; the paginated edition will be checked for it during the reader build. The Old Rite spelling Isus continues throughout. In the source’s folk-etymology list, «Власий похоже не волос» prints “не” for “на” (corrected silently); the reference understates its own best case — the resemblance that made Blasius the cattle-patron is to Volos/Veles, the Slavic cattle-god, the one instance where the “folk etymology” school has its strongest evidence.

Chapter XXXV: the inversion of double faith. The chapter’s target — the reduction of Russian folk Christianity to “paganization” and peasant ignorance — is a real school, and Dugin’s complaint against the crudity of the dvoeverie concept has, curiously, a parallel in revisionist scholarship (which has likewise attacked “double faith” as a polemical construct of medieval churchmen adopted uncritically by ethnographers). But his alternative is not history; it is providentialist metaphysics: folk etymology vindicated at the level of a “pre-Babel language of the angels” — which is Wirth’s Ursprache baptized, the same operation as Chapter XXX’s primordial year-revelation. The Japhethic/Shemitic frame extends the book’s Part II grid into ethnology. The Areopagus analogy (Acts 17) is the chapter’s legitimate patristic precedent — the fathers did baptize Hellenic materials — though Paul’s altar was a rhetorical bridgehead, not an apophatic doctrine attributed to “the Greeks.” The closing claim, that Slavic paganism vanished because it passed into the Church, elegantly inverts the standard account; as explanation it is unfalsifiable, but as description of where the folk material now lives it is simply true.

Chapter XXXVI: the machinery exposed. This chapter is the clearest single specimen of the Wirthian method in the book, and its philological claims require flat verdicts. (1) The “rune Ilx” assigned to late July belongs to Wirth’s reconstructed year-circle — the sign is the elk-rune (ᛉ, algiz) under Wirth’s naming; no academic runology knows a calendar rune “Ilx” of the second half of July. (2) The claim that Russian solntse, Greek helios, Latin sol, and Sanskrit sūrya derive from a root il meaning “light” is false: all four descend from Proto-Indo-European *sóh₂wl̥/*seh₂wel-, with initial *s-; there is no such root il. Confidence: high. (3) The identification of Semitic El/Ilu with this nonexistent Indo-European root, credited to “the newest researches in linguistics,” is Nostratic-style speculation with no standing; and the gloss of Elijah as “stronghold of the Lord” is wrong — Hebrew Eliyyahu means “my God is YHWH,” which, ironically, contains exactly the El element Dugin wants, correctly, without the fantasy etymology he builds on it. Confidence: high. (4) What is genuinely traditional in the chapter should be credited: Elijah’s ascent as a type of Christ’s Ascension is standard patristic typology, and the reading of the prophet’s feat as engagement with the objective cosmos is a coherent extension of the book’s Chapter VII cosmology. The chapter’s conclusion is the full Wirthian inversion, stated without disguise: the “celestial archetype” of Elijah is declared older than Perun, older than Zeus, and older than the biblical prophet himself — the saint’s feast becomes a window onto the pre-Babel polar tradition, and Christianity the restoration of that primordial paradigm rather than its judge. Folk Orthodoxy is thereby made the carrier of the Hyperborean Ur-religion. This is the maneuver the whole Part was built to perform, and here it is performed on a thunder-prophet with fantasy philology as its instrument.

Reading note. Notice the direction of subordination, for it is the tell of the entire method: not the archetype measured against the prophet, but the prophet measured against the archetype. Where the Church’s own typology reads Elijah forward — toward the Ascension he prefigures — Dugin reads him backward, toward a solar rune that precedes and outranks him. The next chapter performs the same operation on the feminine side of the folk calendar, and its title concedes the method in a single word: Polar Paraskeva-Pyatnitsa.

Restoration (14 Aug 2026). Checked against studfile while access held: Chapter XXXVI reads “руне Ilx ()” — empty parentheses here as everywhere. The glyph was a symbol-font character lost in the shared ancestry of every plain-text transcription; no online text preserves it, and only a scan of the 1996 printing could. The editor-supplied ᛉ (with its flag) stands as the only honest option. Confidence: high. The two star glyphs of Chapter XXXVII remain safely reconstructible from the prose, which names the shapes.

Part V · Chapter XXXVII

The Polar Paraskeva-Pyatnitsa

Полярная Параскева-Пятница

Alongside the feast of the prophet Elijah, the particular veneration in Rus' of Paraskeva-Pyatnitsa (the holy martyr Paraskeva — commemorated 28 October, Old Style), with whom a multitude of legends and traditions were traditionally bound up, is often taken as a specimen of pagan influence. The majority of researchers see in this the survivals of the cult of some feminine pagan pre-Christian divinity.

In reality, here as in the case of the prophet Elijah, one should speak of the existence of a most ancient sacred archetype, preceding its pagan degeneration and restored in its whole full measure precisely in the context of the Orthodox year. Let us try to unravel what symbolic conception, properly speaking, is at issue in this case.

In Greek, “Paraskeva” means precisely “the day of preparation (before the sabbath)” — that is, “Friday,” the “fifth day of the week.” Consequently, in the pre-Christian context a particular role must be played precisely by the number 5 and the day of the week corresponding to it. It is common knowledge that Friday among the Indo-European peoples was dedicated to a “feminine divinity” — to Aphrodite among the Greeks, Venus among the Romans, Freyja among the ancient Germans,188 and so on. One may suppose that among the Slavs, too, there existed some analogous mythological figure.189 Most likely the concrete personage is of no great significance, since the common element here is precisely the drawing-together of the fifth day of the week, the number 5, with the feminine element and the symbolic complex corresponding to it — from ritual objects, ornaments, and patterns to the name of the planet or the element of water.

Concerning the symbolism of the number 5 and the most ancient (indeed primordial) hieroglyph corresponding to it — the five-pointed star [glyph lost in transcription] — the most convincing works, in our view, are those of Herman Wirth,190 who showed that this sign corresponds to the hieroglyph of the year — the six-pointed star [glyph lost in transcription] — without the lower stroke. According to Wirth’s conception, this means that in the polar regions the sun in winter does not appear at all — which explains the absence of the lower (= southern) stroke on the most ancient sign of the year. Winter, with the autumn adjoining it (which were once regarded as a single season in the threefold division of the year) — or night — as the lower regions of creation, into which the sun of the spirit (Il) descends, is likened in natural symbolism to the maternal womb, from which new life (= the new spring sun) appears. It is this natural symbolism that makes of the five-pointed star the sign of the mother, the woman, the sacred night, the earth, the water; and consequently the number 5 itself is firmly bound to it in the sacred consciousness of humanity. The memory of this connection is preserved even many millennia after the departure from the northern regions; the symbolic complexes formed in immemorial times continue to live in traditions, legends, customs, and rites, which are preserved even when their primordial meaning itself is lost.

Confirmation of this is easily found in the cycle of Russian traditions about “Pyatnitsa,” according to which “Friday” spoils the distaffs of those girls who work, rather than rest, on the day of the week dedicated to her. Distaffs in Rus' were traditionally fashioned in the form of a wheel with six (or eight) spokes, and symbolized the sun, the light of the world, or the whole year entire. The “Pyatnitsa who spoils the distaffs” is the image of the ancient northern winter (mother-earth), who steals the light, who thieves the solar wheel. Often a feminine personage with a distaff — the Slavic Dolya, the Germanic Norns, the Greek Gorgons, and so on — signifies fate, the future: for the wheel of the distaff symbolizes the cycle, the subtle world, pure rotation, while the yarn itself symbolizes progressive, one-directional movement — which together correspond exactly to the content of the year as cycle (as we showed above).

Besides winter and earth, “Pyatnitsa” may also signify the northern ancestral homeland itself — the place where the natural meaning of the five-pointed star was a naturally observable calendrical phenomenon. In this significance the image describes the North Pole in the sacred cosmography of the ancients.

One may trace also the connection with the symbolism of apples — since the core of an apple, in transverse section, yields precisely a five-pointed star,191 a “Pyatnitsa.” Hence the image of the maiden-appletree in the legends of the most various peoples. The chain of symbols “woman — appletree — paradise (pole, center)” is met both in the Old Testament description of paradise, and especially in the subject of the fall of the first parents. At the level of cosmic symbolism, the eating of the apples of paradise and the expulsion from paradise that follows it is a description of the autumn movement of the sun (often identified with the ripened fruit) toward the earth-winter, the grave, death.

A symbolic equivalent of Pyatnitsa is also the palm of the hand, which served as the most ancient calendar192 — the memory of which is preserved in the Russian custom of the vrutseleto, the reckoning of calendar feasts proceeding from the anatomical structure of the palm, the joints of the fingers, and so on.

To the same symbolism belongs Great Friday of Passion Week — for it is precisely then that the Savior’s descent into hell takes place: under the earth, into the regions of darkness and cold, into the winter regions of creation. The weekly Friday fasts are entirely correlated with this.

It is characteristic that the Orthodox Church honors the memory of the holy martyr Paraskeva in autumn, when the sun inclines toward the winter solstice. And in this particular moment the Orthodox tradition combines with the pre-Christian one.

Part V · Chapter XXXVIII

The Calendar “Expectation of the Creature”

Календарное “чаянье твари”

Such are but a few examples of the providential harmony of the Russian Orthodox Tradition and the sacred Year of the ancient Slavs. In principle, the same regularities could be found in other subjects as well.

Thus the first Russian saints, Boris and Gleb, often symbolize the old and the new year (Boris with a beard, Gleb without one). Moreover, Gleb is sometimes depicted with ears of wheat, symbolizing the grain193 (the phonetic resemblance of Gleb and khleb, bread). The first Russian saints, further, revive the most ancient subject of the sacred tradition concerning the twin brothers — which is likewise connected with the two halves of the year, or of the solar cycle. With the symbolism of the New Year, of the birth of the sun, is drawn together the holy hierarch Nicholas (Nikola), likewise venerated beyond measure in Rus'. His very name — Nikola — recalls the Russian kolo, that is, circle, year, rotation. Sometimes the winter saint Nikola is linked with the summer prophet Elijah, and then they form a symbolic pair of the two opposite sides of the annual cycle — summer and winter. Often it is precisely this pair — Nikola and Elijah — that comes forward in the most various legends and traditions, in connection with other symbolic complexes of a distinctly calendrical character.

This series could be continued; but the frame of the present work does not permit it in full measure, since such an investigation would require, at the least, a separate book.

It is important also to point out one circumstance which will doubtless help toward a clearer understanding of the correspondences between the Orthodox liturgical year and the ancient sacred structure of the Slavic calendar. The point is that in its primordial form the sacred circle of the year had, as its chief point, precisely the winter solstice — the true New Year. To this “revolutionary” moment of the cosmic cycle related the majority of symbols, traditions, hieroglyphs — since at this moment a miraculous event takes place: the passage from death to life, from winter to summer, from decline to rebirth, from the old to the new. And it takes place visibly, convincingly, and macrocosmically — displaying the divine harmony through the whole order of nature. Even on the basis of purely geographical particularities it becomes intelligible that the most vivid picture of the New Year, of its natural symbolism, of its visible significance, is observed in the North, where the annual rhythm of the seasons is marked out, distinct, and expressive in the extreme. One may say that the celebration of the New Year on the day of the winter solstice is the most ancient and the most correct, the primordial one. And it is not by chance that the Nativity of our Lord Jesus Christ providentially took place precisely at this moment.

But in later periods the point of the New Year, and the feasts and traditions bound up with it, were transferred to other calendar dates — especially to the autumn and spring equinoxes. The single symbolic calendar complex split into three points, undergoing, naturally, certain changes. The autumn equinox became bound to the figure of the woman-mother, the earth, winter. Often (though not always and not necessarily) she has, in calendrical symbolism, a sinister character, combining with the below, with water, cold, death. But even in this state there is foreseen in her the prospect of the coming birth. To the autumn equinox correspond the feast of the church New Year and three of the four great feasts of the twelve dedicated to the Virgin Mary.

The spring equinox, on the contrary, underscores the victorious, triumphal phase of the light that has been born (has risen) after the winter captivity. In Russian paganism this is Maslenitsa, the round solar loaf or pancake. In Orthodoxy it is the great feast of the Holy Pascha of Christ.

But in all these points — the winter solstice, the autumn and spring equinoxes — the chief thing is, in essence, the moment of initiation: the passage from the old to the new, death and the trampling of death, abasement and exaltation. Hence the resemblance of certain rites and symbolic elements proper to all three festal cycles. In the Orthodox year these key initiatic “New Year” points are marked by particular feasts, which are preceded by fasts. The fast signifies purification, and likewise suffering — a conscious immersion in a qualitatively diminished reality corresponding to the old — in order to co-participate in full measure in the coming victory of light over darkness, of spirit over flesh. Upon autumn falls the Dormition fast. Upon winter, the Nativity fast (the fast of Philip). Upon spring, the Great Fast.194 One may say that all three fasts, and likewise the ritual order of the feasts to which they relate, have an initiatic character — whose detailed study would open before us the incredible riches of Orthodox esotericism, and would likewise give more information about the pre-Christian tradition of the Russians and the Slavs than all the profane searches of archaeologists and secular historians.

If one clearly understands the symbolic synonymity of the three chief “New Year”195 points and their primordial connection with the mystery of the winter solstice, a multitude of symbolic details difficult to explain will fall into their places and acquire meaning and inner harmony. And such an investigation will not undermine the Orthodox Faith, but on the contrary will strengthen it — since the discovery of the Wisdom of God, so evidently present in the very fabric of the being that surrounds us, must convince even the skeptics of the Triumph of Orthodoxy.

Be that as it may, there is no doubt that the Sacred Year has enormous significance in the Orthodox tradition. Its structure is worked out with care at the liturgical level. The movable part of the liturgical calendar — the paschal cycle — displays the variable, dynamic aspect of the Christian year; the fixed feasts, the static aspect. In the computation of the paschalia the totality of the cosmic calendar cycles is taken into account — the solar, the lunar, the weekly. Thus the mystical being of the Church does not withdraw from natural existence, but transfigures that existence, illumines it, gives it a particular luminous dimension. The natural, through the Church, passes over into the supernatural. For in the apostle’s words: “the earnest expectation of the creature waiteth for the manifestation of the sons of God.”196 Through the Orthodox Year — its liturgical cycle, the rhythm of its feasts, and the dynamics of the divine service, whose daily fabric depends directly on the calendar — the mystery of the ecclesial economy is unceasingly accomplished; from the eternity of the Heavenly Church, the center of the great circle, straight rays go forth to every day of the year, extending to Christians — and through them to the whole cosmos — the grace of deification and of the ascent into paradise, into the heart of the world, into the Holy of Holies of creation.

Apparatus · Chapters XXXVII–XXXVIII

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 188–196 are preserved. The closing verse is Rom. 8:19, quoted in Slavonic — the chapter’s title phrase. The paraskeuē = “preparation/Friday” etymology is sound; the commemoration date (28 October O.S., Paraskeva of Iconium) is correct; Friday as the day of Aphrodite/Venus/Freyja is accurate comparative calendar-lore; the apple’s transverse section does show a five-pointed seed-star; and the vrutseleto — the Slavonic hand-computus reckoning feasts on the joints of the fingers — is a genuine and well-chosen reference. Two more glyphs are lost in the source transcription (the five- and six-pointed stars of note 190’s passage, both reduced to empty parentheses) — added to the recovery checklist beside Chapter XXXVI’s rune. The Old Rite Isus continues (“the Nativity of our Lord Jesus Christ”).

The unnamed goddess, and Wirth’s arithmetic. The “analogous mythological figure” of the Slavs whom Dugin declines to name has a standard name in scholarship: Mokosh, the one goddess of Vladimir’s pantheon, whose functions (spinning, women, moisture) the Paraskeva cult absorbed. The refusal to name her is method, not ignorance — as with Perun, the pagan personage is deliberately dissolved into the archetype so that the archetype may outrank it. The derivation on which the chapter turns — the pentagram as the hexagram-year minus its southern stroke, because the polar winter sun never rises — is Wirth’s sign-arithmetic, and has no standing in the history of symbols; the “sun of the spirit (Il)” imports the previous chapter’s phantom root. What is genuinely attested should be credited: the folk prohibition on Friday spinning and the Pyatnitsa who punishes spinners are solid ethnography; so, in the next chapter, are the Nikola–Ilya winter/summer pairing in folk legend (the classic study of the Nikola cult documents it) and the beardless, grain-bearing iconography of Gleb. The data are real; the polar decoding is the imported layer.

Errors and trimmings. (1) The Greek spinners of fate are the Moirai; the Gorgons are not fate-figures, and no distaff belongs to them. Confidence: high; translated as written. (2) The claim that the Nativity “took place precisely at” the winter solstice is theology of providence resting on a fourth-century liturgical fixing, not a birth date — legitimate as typology, not as history. (3) The count “three of the four Marian great feasts correspond to the autumn equinox” works only by assigning the Dormition (15 August) to autumn’s approach — Nativity of the Theotokos (8 September) and the Entry (21 November) fit; the Annunciation is the spring exception. (4) Most tellingly, the three-fast scheme silently omits the Apostles’ Fast — the fourth canonical fast of the Orthodox year, falling in early summer, where the schema has no slot for it. The year is trimmed to fit the triangle. A method that finds its pattern by discarding a quarter of the data has told you what kind of method it is.

Reading note — Part V complete. The Part’s architecture closes cleanly: theory (XXX–XXXII), hieroglyphs (XXXIII–XXXIV), application (XXXV–XXXVIII); and with Paraskeva the year receives the same two-pole gender metaphysics as Part IV gave the family — the masculine summer of Il-Elijah at the zenith, the feminine winter-mother at the nadir, day and night, patriarchy and the nocturnal reversal, now inscribed on the calendar itself. The apparatus has spent the Part flagging Wirth’s machinery, so honesty requires recording where the Part ends: on Romans 8 — the creature’s earnest expectation, creation groaning toward the manifestation of the sons of God. That verse is the one place where the Wirthian year and Pauline cosmic eschatology genuinely meet: both hold that nature is not scenery but a participant awaiting transfiguration. Dugin’s strongest defense of the whole Part is its last page — which is also the only page of it that needed no Wirth at all.

Part VI · The Mysteries of the Church

Part VI · Chapter XXXIX

The Three Enclosures of the
Heavenly Church

Три ограды Небесной Церкви

The Orthodox Church is named the Apostolic Church. Such a definition not only points to the historical fact of the Church’s foundation by the apostles of Christ, but underscores the qualitative role of the apostles as a particular metaphysical category — central for the whole Orthodox economy.

The apostles are human persons and, more broadly, human types, who embodied in themselves the fullness of the consecratory realization of that “revolutionary” metaphysical Good News which the Son of God brought by his Incarnation, Preaching, Death on the Cross, and Resurrection. The apostles constitute the circle nearest to the Savior not only historically but metaphysically. They abide around the divine Throne, on the far side of time and history. The number of the chief apostles is 12, and in Tradition it is directly correlated with the circle, the cycle, fullness and completion. In the Apocalypse the 12 apostles are set beside the 12 tribes of Israel and the 12 gates of the Heavenly Jerusalem.

The correlation with the gates underscores especially that the apostles constitute the “entrance” into the reality of the Kingdom of God; consequently all the members of the Church of Christ are thereby divided into 12 foundational types. The representatives of each enter the Heavenly City through their own gates, corresponding to a determinate apostle. And it is important that it is precisely the apostles, and not the angels, who play the foundational role in Orthodox ecclesiology: God the Son is incarnate in man, and it is to man, not to the angel, that He gives the prospect of deification. Thus the initiatic fullness of Orthodoxy is realized through the apostolic Church and the apostolic ministry. The apostles are not simply equal to the angels — they are higher than the angels; and the angels themselves partake of the saving Good News precisely through deified human beings, and not immediately. This ontologically unique position of the apostles is bound up with the problematic we have defined as the “third path” in the pre-eternal choice of the angels.197

In the soteriological economy, and in the personal spiritual realization of the Christian, there always exists a determinate instance bound to the person of one or another apostle, who presides over the particular anthropological type to which that Christian belongs. Thus the Orthodox Church is apostolic for yet another reason: in the process of personal salvation (deification), the believer, as a conciliar part of the Church, realizes a certain apostolic archetype — quickens it, fills it with the concreteness of personal spiritual experience. This may be called the “apostolic economy.”

The 12 apostles are 12 aspects of the archetype of the perfect man — whose Orthodox image, as we have seen, is the Immaculate Virgin Mary, the “New Eve” (in the expression of the hesychasts). Consequently it is precisely the Theotokos who may be regarded as that which is common to the apostolic economy.198 To this points directly the enigmatic episode of the Gospel concerning the Savior’s dying command from the cross — the adoption of John the Theologian by the Virgin Mary herself.199

Through the apostle John (who occupies a particular place among the apostles — not by chance does the Gospel underscore: “whom Jesus loved”) there takes place the adoption by the Theotokos of all the apostles, and through them, in turn, of all Christians in general. Those baptized into Christ are adopted not only by the uncreated Heavenly Father (through the eucharist), but also by the created yet deified Mother (through participation in the apostolic Church) — who, as the result of the fullness of Christian God-realization, became the head of the angels. Thus the ecclesial spiritual “birth from above” becomes perfect — from the supra-celestial Father and the celestial Mother. And the ordinary Christian is conjoined with the single nature of the perfect man — the Theotokos — precisely through the 12-membered circle of the apostles, who constitute the Christian analogue of the archangels, the “princes of the lights.” The 12 apostles lead the host of saints, venerables, martyrs, and righteous ones, as the archangels lead the angelic hierarchies, grouping them in ranks according to spiritual families and symbolic functions. Within the frame of Orthodox initiation they symbolize 12 rays, or 12 paths, binding the periphery to the center, multiplicity to unity. Their work is the accomplishment of conciliarity — integration, the gathering of all into one. But this unity of the ecclesial center — of the uncreated Lord and of the created but deified cosmic nature of the Mother of God — cannot be realized at once, from the concrete individual being straight to the fullness of deification. It passes by stages, by strata of approach to the inner, along the initiatic heavens of Orthodox sanctity; and at the nearest “radius” from the point of the center, from the Heavenly Throne — where precisely the apostolic circle is disposed — there takes place the “meeting with the apostle,” with one of the 12, who, having been sent to this or that people, to this or that human type, is thereby directed also to the concrete human person: as a sacred hierarch, a transcendent preceptor, teacher, bishop, true spiritual father.

Besides the 12 apostles, the Orthodox tradition speaks also of the 70, or 72, apostles. This figure (especially 72200) is by no means accidental. According to the Judaic tradition, alongside the 12 tribes of Israel — the chosen people, typologically summing up all humanity and standing before God on behalf of that humanity — there exist 72 “tongues,” peoples, to each of which, from the creation of the world, a particular angel was sent for enlightenment and sanctification.201 One may correlate the 12 tribes of Israel with the 72 tongues, and conclude that each of the tribes corresponds to 6 peoples — or, at the celestial level, that the angel of each tribe of Israel (an archangel, in this case) leads 6 angels relating to 6 peoples of the earth. In the passage to Orthodox metaphysics, the role of the 12 tribes of Israel, in the transcendent and specifically Christian sense, is transferred to the 12 apostles; and the 72 apostles become the guides and evangelizers of the corresponding peoples of the earth. Thus one should suppose that the 72 apostles were distributed among the 12, as their disciples, 6 men to each, and were correlated with the human nations. With this, evidently, is bound also the phenomenon of “glossolalia,” the “speaking in tongues,”202 which first occurred at Pentecost, at the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the apostles, who suddenly began to speak in languages and dialects previously unknown to them. To judge by the epistles of the holy apostle Paul, “speaking in tongues” was an ordinary phenomenon for the whole early Christian Church.

Thus, somewhat farther from the inner circle of the 12 apostles is disposed the second circle of the 72 apostles, corresponding to the symbolic number of the peoples of the earth. The churched nations, under the leadership of one of the 72, are thereby united into groups of 6 and placed under the headship of one of the 12 chief apostles.203 Each of the 72 apostles becomes an archetype of national sanctity, which unfolds consecutively in the host of national local saints. These saints, in their turn, form the third circle on the path of deification — being, as it were, a multitude of doors into the narthex of the Heavenly Jerusalem, which through 72 passages is joined to the “altar part” of the supra-celestial abode, access to which is accomplished through the 12 principal doors. Together these three holy and apostolic circles form the fabric of the Heavenly Church, turned by one of its sides to every “tongue,” to every people of the earth. On this ground it is customary to give the newly baptized a name in honor of some saint, who henceforth becomes the patron of the baptized, his celestial preceptor and guide (together with his guardian angel).

It is very telling that in the Orthodox calendar every day of the year is dedicated to a determinate saint, whose name, in the normal case, is given to the person baptized on that day (in Orthodoxy, most often on the eighth day after the infant’s birth). The year itself, as we showed above, is understood by Tradition as the disclosure of eternity in time; consequently the concrete element of the year (the day) is an initiatic entrance into the Kingdom of Heaven. And since the liturgical day is obligatorily correlated with some saint (or group of saints204), this saint becomes the esoteric essence of the concrete day, its inner dimension. One may say that he is the luminous entrance into the supra-human space of the Heavenly Church for those who liturgically commemorate him during the service — and especially for those who receive his name, and thereby acquire him as preceptor and teacher, as an apostle sent personally to them.

Let us note that both the figure 12 and the figure 72 belong to the class of “cyclic” numbers, which traditionally lay at the foundation of the division of the annual circle and of the other units of time. Thus, in particular, there are 12 months in the year. In a vaster cycle — the cycle of the precession of the equinoxes — in 72 years the point of the spring equinox is displaced by exactly 1 degree, that is, by one division equal to 1/360 of the circle. It is known also that the number of days in the year equals 360, apart from 5 supplementary days, to which particular attention was paid in many sacred calendars and which were regarded apart from the remaining ordinary 360. One may say that these particular 5 days symbolize the four points of the great cross of the year and its secret center (of which we spoke in the preceding chapter). Thus the three apostolic circles — the 12 apostles, the 72 apostles, and the host of saints corresponding to each day of the year — give a harmonious picture of the division of the annual cycle first into 12, then into 72, then into 360 parts. The apostolic hierarchy of the Heavenly Church acquires a pyramidal structure, reproducing in general outline the angelic hierarchy, but in a new, strictly Christian sense. The 12 lead the 72; and each of the 72, in his turn, stands over the 5 most venerated national saints.

Of course, such a mathematically calibrated schema does not answer to the real state of the liturgical Orthodox calendar, and the ideal harmony of the Heavenly Church differs in some measure from the historical concreteness of the earthly Church. But sooner or later the secret becomes manifest; and at the final eschatological moment, prepared by the Holy Spirit, the ecclesial economy will coincide, literally and exactly, with the Divine Design — as the Old Testament prophecies were literally realized in the Incarnation of the Son. Then in truth “the righteous shall shine forth as the sun”; and the comparison “as the sun” will disclose its cosmic, transfiguring content — the righteous will become the sun of that day over which they kept patronage (in which they abode, celestially and mystically lived) in the liturgical year. The eschatological tradition says that at the moment of the Second Coming time will stop; consequently all these suns will then shine simultaneously.

Part VI · Chapter XL

The Legacy of Peter and the Legacy of Paul
(On the Outer and Inner Church)

Наследие Петра и наследие Павла (о внешней и внутренней Церкви)

If the 72 apostles are conjoined with the evangelization of the tongues, they must stand, symbolically and historically, in a particular connection with the apostle Paul, the “apostle of the nations” par excellence. More than that: though Paul himself did not belong to the 12, it is precisely he (together with the apostle Peter) who is accounted the most important among the apostles, chief among them.

On the other hand, the apostle Paul in the Christian tradition is constantly correlated with the apostle Peter, who “is the rock on which the Church is built.” Between Peter and Paul there exist complex symbolic and metaphysical relations. They complete one another, as it were, often coming forward together in sacred subjects (thus one and the same day of the year is dedicated to them). John Chrysostom so underscores the archetypal character of the conjunction of these two apostles that he foresees in Adam himself a primordial duality, providentially realized in them in the primordial Church.205 On the other hand, already in the “Acts of the Apostles” there is the subject of a dispute between Peter and Paul; and besides this, in early Christianity there existed a multitude of traditions concerning serious disagreements and contests between them — until the place of Paul in these subjects was taken by the outright heretic Simon Magus.206

The typology of the opposition of Peter and Paul comes down, on the whole, to the theme of Judaism as the early Christians understood it. If the symbolic function of the 12 apostles could still be combined with the Judaic tradition within the frame of a Judeo-Christian synthesis (practiced, in particular, by the sect of the Ebionites), the passage to the evangelization of the tongues — that is, to the function of the 72 apostles, to the second circle of the apostolic economy — was radically incompatible with the doctrine of Judaism. Therefore it is precisely the figure of Paul, his theology, his metaphysics, that have the highest and absolute significance for the Church as a universal reality, for Christianity as the third path, for Orthodoxy, for Christian-Christianity. Before Paul and outside Paul, the question of the metaphysical nature of the Christian choice in the pre-eternal dispute of the angels had no particular distinction from the general disposition of Judaic piety, and the fact of the Incarnation could easily be interpreted in a strictly creationist and monotheist perspective. To judge by the uncertainty of the 12 apostles themselves, even these chosen vessels of grace could not at once, and not to the end, become conscious of the “revolutionary” significance of the metaphysical gift they had been vouchsafed to receive. Without Paul there would have been no full, intellectually formed Christian teaching; there would have been no Orthodoxy. And it is precisely his metaphysical, theological role — and not simply his particular activity in the sense of preaching among the “tongues” — that makes him the central figure among the apostles, occupying a particular place: the place of the absolute apostle, the apostle par excellence.

In Paul was disclosed Orthodox esotericism, the fullness of the teaching on Christian metaphysics; and its most important postulate was the assertion that “there is neither Jew nor Greek.”207 Around this assertion the principal disputes were waged — fixed partly in allegorical form in the “Acts,” and more openly in the epistles of the apostle Paul himself; and with this precisely were bound the subjects of the apocryphal legends opposing Paul to Peter. Peter himself was the first of the apostles to transgress the Judaic laws, entering the house of the centurion Cornelius after the threefold vision of the beasts in the sheet.208 But this turn to the churching of the pagans was censured by the adherents of the strict Judeo-Christian line in the early Church. Most often the chief figure of this current is James, the “brother of the Lord,” head of the Jerusalem community. His direct opponent is precisely the radical apostle Paul, the fiercest adversary of Judeo-Christianity. Peter occupies an intermediate position in this dispute. But in the course of the formation of church dogmatics — especially after the sharp demarcation from the Ebionites — the Judeo-Christian line of the radical (heretically deviating) followers of James was already beyond the bound of orthodoxy, and Peter became the principal figure at the Judeo-Christian metaphysical pole of the Church, while Paul remained at the opposite end. Incidentally, the very images of Peter’s vision preceding his coming to the centurion Cornelius bear an evidently Judaic character — since the “pagans” appear here, in the Old Testament optic, as “unclean animals.”

One may say that every full tradition has both an exoteric (outer) and an esoteric (inner) side. At the outer level God comes forward, in relation to man, as a transcendent and inaccessible object — as something radically other than man himself. The extreme formulations of such an approach are proper precisely to creationism, and especially to the purely Judaic, Abrahamic view. Properly speaking, the basic creationist postulate of the abyss between Creator and creation is the extreme form of consecutive and finished exotericism. This is the position of the pure “Jews.”

At the inner (esoteric) level God comes forward, in relation to man, as subject — as his highest spiritual “I,” as the “inner man” or the “new man.” Such a relation is characteristic of esotericism and of the initiatic doctrines. In principle all the manifestationist traditions — traditions of the “Hellenic” type — bear precisely this “emanationist” character; which, in the case of their degradation, leads to polytheism and idolatry, since the discovery of God within a thing (more broadly, a creature: a man or a sacred object) may go as far as the veneration of that whole thing as such — which violates the unity and unicity of the principle, of the Godhead.

In the Christian tradition these two aspects are correlated with the figures of Peter and Paul — where Peter is answerable for the exoteric side, and Paul for the esoteric. Peter is the head of the outer; Paul, of the inner. Both the one and the other, in the full Orthodox tradition, are neither Jew nor Greek: both are genuine Christians. And yet Peter personifies precisely the outer Church, and Paul the inner.209

Peter is one of the 12. More than that, he is the first to acknowledge in Christ the Messiah. Paul does not enter into the number of the 12. But he is the first to affirm and confess, unambiguously and with a loud voice, the God-manhood of Jesus Christ and the Trinity of the Godhead. Thus it is precisely with Paul that Christianity becomes metaphysically itself — on the far side of the purely messianic factology which some from among the Jews could acknowledge (and did acknowledge). And it is not by chance that Paul receives the command to the apostolic ministry from the Risen and Ascended Jesus — directly from the uncreated God in his celestial nature210 — whereas the 12 apostles had communion with the Son in human form. Paul, blinded on the road to Damascus, was vouchsafed to behold the Light of the Second Coming — to see the Savior-in-Strength himself. Therefore his teaching, too, describes the structure of Orthodox metaphysics with such clarity and unambiguity — with eschatological definiteness.

Depending on one’s view of the symbolism of the apostles, the relation of the figures of Peter and Paul may be represented, at the metaphysical level, in different ways. In a determinate case (outside the Orthodox teaching) one may accent their opposition and even hostility.

This was characteristic of the gnostic Marcion, who acknowledged only and exclusively Paul and his metaphysical, esoteric Christianity — rejecting James and even Peter, and together with them the texts of all the Gospels except the Gospel of Luke, since Luke was a disciple of Paul.211 Later a similar position appeared among the Manichaeans, the Bogomils, and the Cathars, who held the God of the Old Testament to be the “evil demiurge” and rejected Peter, the founder of Catholicism. The same argumentation surfaced again in the Reformation, when Luther, in his critique of the Vatican and of the Papacy founded on the centrality of the apostle Peter, had recourse to the alternative authority of the apostle Paul.

One may also meet the inverse version of this opposition, on the part of the priority partisans of Peter. Western Catholicism in many cases affirms precisely the unconditional supremacy of Peter among all the apostles — placing all the rest in dependence on him. The line of Paul is then often regarded as the private and almost “heretical” opinion of a particular historical person, participant in the early ecclesiastical reality only thanks to a totality of accidental circumstances. The further the Judeo-Christian tendencies in Catholicism go, the sharper becomes the question of the “orthodoxy” of Paul’s epistles — up to the proposals, constantly repeated in recent years, to subject certain of his texts to censorship and cuts. In fact the “Latin heresy,” in the understanding of the Orthodox, is de facto the rejection of the metaphysical and initiatic legacy of Paul.

On the other hand, one may regard the two apostles as mutually completing figures, with priority given to one of them. This already corresponds not to a heretical but to an adequate, orthodox point of view. And such a choice does not demand of the Christian the acceptance of any particular dogmatic formulations. In it there tells, rather, the specificity of the believer’s spiritual type: the moral-ritual aspects of the Church are the priority for the followers of the apostle Peter; metaphysics and initiation attract, rather, the followers of the apostle Paul. In the Orthodox Church both apostles are honored as equals — though, in accordance with the metaphysical hierarchy, priority is implicitly given precisely to the line of Paul. And the very fact that the Eastern Church sharply resists the Catholic exaltation of Peter and his placement above the other apostles — on which the Vatican attempts to found the claims of the universal ecclesial authority of the Pope — points to the unambiguous choice of Orthodoxy.

Among the remaining apostles one may likewise single out several archetypal figures fitting into this dual division of exotericism and esotericism (Peter–Paul). Thus, without any hesitation, the apostle John the Theologian, the “beloved disciple” of Jesus, may be assigned to the esoterics. John the Theologian evidently expresses the most hidden, esoteric aspects of Christianity. It is for this reason, incidentally, that he is the author of the “Apocalypse” — the book which completes the whole “New Testament,” and which contains the exposition of the last eschatological mysteries of the Church. Moreover, the Gospel written by him stands fourth, and is accounted, in accordance with the Orthodox tradition, the innermost — where the whole metaphysics of the Incarnation is laid open.

The apostle John enters into the trio of the most chosen apostles of the 12, together with James and Peter. One may say that among these three highest apostles John constitutes the innermost pole of the Church, James the outermost, and Peter the middle. In a certain sense the apostle John is bound to Paul and to the whole metaphysical line of Paul.212 A formal sign of belonging to Christian esotericism is the positive relation of this or that apostle to the question of the churching of the pagans; and on this ground one may also correlate with the line of Paul–John the apostles Philip (he preached in Samaria and baptized the Ethiopian), Bartholomew (who brought 5 pagans to the apostles), and Andrew (who evangelized in the north of Greece and in the Scythian lands).

Nearest to Judeo-Christianity stand the apostles James the Elder and James of Alphaeus; the apostle Jude, “brother of the Lord,” also called Thaddeus (concerning the last two, tradition asserts that they were physical kinsmen in the flesh of the Virgin Mary or of Joseph the Betrothed — which underscores their connection with the Old Testament reality); likewise Simon the Zealot (being a Zealot, he must have been bound to Judaic nationalism), and the apostle and evangelist Matthew (of whom tradition asserts that he alone among the evangelists wrote his gospel in the Hebrew language and addressed it to the Jews).

Of Thomas, and of Matthias chosen in the place of Judas Iscariot, it is difficult to say anything definite — though the behavior of Thomas at the appearance of the risen Christ hints indirectly at his possible connection with the sect of the Pharisees, which denied the resurrection in the flesh. Though, on the other hand, tradition asserts that the apostle Thomas, “once weaker than the other apostles in faith, became by the Grace of God more courageous, more zealous, and more tireless than them all — so that he traversed with his preaching almost the whole earth, not fearing to preach the Word of God to peoples savage, wild, and bloodthirsty.” It is telling that he died a martyr in India, in the city of Mylapore. The apocryphal tradition ascribes to him a gnostic “Gospel” of manifestationist, “Hellenic” orientation.

The division into esotericism and exotericism is clearly present also in relation to the four evangelists, who are identified in Orthodoxy with the four sacred living creatures — the cherubim — from the vision of Ezekiel and the Apocalypse of John the Theologian. John the Theologian (his symbol is the cherub with the face of a lion) and the evangelist Luke (his symbol is the cherub with the face of a calf), the disciple of the apostle Paul, evidently relate to the esoteric side of the Church. And many Orthodox icons and murals depict John above Luke on the right side of the eight-pointed rhombus, in the subjects connected with the “Unburnt Bush” or the “Savior-in-Strength.” Thus John, an apostle of the twelve, is regarded as more inner than Luke, an apostle of the seventy-two. Matthew (his symbol is the cherub with the face of a man) and the evangelist Mark (his symbol is the cherub with the face of an eagle), the disciple of Peter,213 correspond to the exoteric side of Christianity; and the placement of Matthew above Mark in the same icon subjects points to the superiority, in this domain, of Matthew, one of the twelve, over Mark, one of the seventy-two.

Possibly it is precisely this non-equivalence of the apostles, their correlation with different aspects of the Church, that is symbolically reflected in the Orthodox iconographic subject often met on the iconostasis at the center of the deisis row. This subject depicts the Last Supper, where the Savior distributes to the apostles the eucharistic Holy Gifts — and to one half of the disciples He extends the bread (the flesh of the Lord), and to the other half the wine (the blood of the Lord). At the initiatic level the bread (flesh) is correlated with exotericism, and the wine (blood) with esotericism.

Apparatus · Chapters XXXIX–XL

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 197–213 are preserved. “Whom Jesus loved,” John 13:23 etc.; the adoption from the cross, John 19:26–27; “neither Jew nor Greek,” Gal. 3:28; Cornelius and the sheet, Acts 10; the Jerusalem dispute, Acts 15 with Gal. 2; “the righteous shall shine forth as the sun,” Matt. 13:43; the 70 (or 72) of Luke 10:1 — where the manuscripts genuinely divide between the two numbers, so Dugin’s “70 or 72” reflects a real textual variant; the 72 nations with their angels rests on the Table of Nations (Gen. 10) with Deut. 32:8 in its Septuagint form (“according to the number of the angels of God”). The astronomical data are sound: the precessional point does move one degree in about 72 years, and the 360 + 5 epagomenal days are the Egyptian sacred calendar. One transposition to note: in Rev. 21:12–14 the twelve gates bear the names of the twelve tribes, while the apostles’ names stand on the twelve foundations; Dugin’s entry-through-apostolic-gates quietly swaps the assignments. Confidence: high on the text of Revelation. The chapter title’s “three enclosures” is Guénon’s triple enceinte — the triple druidic enclosure of Symboles fondamentaux — here churched into ecclesiology. The Old Rite Isus continues throughout.

Chapter XL: the map and its liberties. The genuinely learned substrate should be credited before the liberties are charged. The Pseudo-Clementine literature really does transpose the anti-Pauline polemic onto Simon Magus — Dugin is alluding, accurately, to the Tübingen reading of those texts. Marcion’s canon (an edited Luke plus the Pauline corpus) is correctly described. The Matthew-in-Hebrew tradition is Papias. And — a point in his favor against the casual reader’s suspicion — his assignment of the lion to John and the eagle to Mark is not an error but the Irenaean scheme (Adv. Haer. III.11.8), which much Russian iconography of the Savior-in-Strength follows, against the later Hieronymian scheme (lion-Mark, eagle-John) that became standard in the West. The Communion of the Apostles is a genuine iconographic subject; the exoteric-bread/esoteric-wine reading laid on it is his own. Against these: (1) the “possible connection of Thomas with the sect of the Pharisees, which denied the resurrection in the flesh” repeats the error already flagged at Chapter VIII — it was the Sadducees who denied the resurrection (Acts 23:8); the Pharisees affirmed it. Confidence: high. (2) The Ethiopian of Acts 8 was baptized by Philip the deacon-evangelist, not the apostle; the conflation is traditional but should be named. (3) The claim of “constantly repeated proposals in recent years” to censor Paul’s epistles is given without source and, as stated, is polemical invention. (4) The Chrysostom citation on Adam’s duality is reported as Dugin gives it (marker 205); the present translator has not verified the passage.

Reading note. Part VI converts ecclesiology into initiation-geometry: three concentric enclosures (12 — 72 — the sanctoral host of the year), with the calendar of Part V now revealed as the outermost wall of the Heavenly Church, and a closing image of real beauty — time stopped at the Parousia, every day’s saint blazing at once. Chapter XL then maps the book’s master-dichotomy onto persons: Peter the exoteric pole, Paul the esoteric — with the notable choice that the esoteric principal is Paul, not John (the usual pick of the esoteric tradition from Joachim to Merezhkovsky); John is then bolted onto the Pauline line as its innermost member. Two consequences follow and should be seen clearly. First, the anti-Catholic application: papal Peter-supremacy is glossed as creeping Judaization, and the “Latin heresy” as de facto rejection of Paul’s initiatic legacy — the book’s standing polemic given an apostolic genealogy. Second, the apostles themselves are sorted into more and less inner — a ranking the Church’s own tradition, which calls Peter and Paul equally chief, does not perform. The stage is now set for the Part’s closing chapter, where the typology acquires its shadow: if the Church has an inner circle, Dugin will argue, so does its adversary.

Part VI · Chapter XLI

Judas, Israel, and Counter-Initiation

Иуда, Израиль и контринициация

Judas Iscariot plays a particular role in the apostolic typology. He is the archetype of evil in the Christian tradition, combining in himself and prefiguring everything that is opposed to Orthodoxy — to its ethics, its metaphysics, its initiation, its soteriology. In a certain sense he serves as the prefiguration of the antichrist himself. If from the ethical point of view the image of Judas is crystal-clear — since in him all the basic vices converge (treachery, mendacity, cunning, greed, and so on) — metaphysically his function demands particular examination.

Judas Iscariot is the first and, at the historical level, the chief link in the chain of the Savior’s sufferings on the cross. It is with him that the mystery of the Crucifixion of Christ by the Jews begins, since it is precisely he who delivers Jesus into the hands of the Sanhedrin. Since the murder of God is the collective crime of the Jews, who rejected and crucified Immanuel, Judas Iscariot serves as the archetype of the whole of Judaism which remained itself in the period after the preaching of Christ. It is of precisely such Jewry that it is said: “them which say they are Jews, and are not, but are the synagogue of Satan.”214 Thus Judas is not only the betrayer of God but the murderer of God. To him, in the “Acts,” the apostle Peter applies the prophecies of the psalmist: “Let his habitation be desolate, and let no man dwell therein.”215 This has direct relation also to the devil, with whom Judas is directly identified. In John the possession of Judas Iscariot is described thus: “And after the sop Satan entered into him.”216 The entry of Satan into Judas upon the eating of the bread extended by the Savior symbolizes the entry of the spirit of the prince of this world into Judaism after the Incarnation, in the midst of the Jews, of the Son of God, the Heavenly Bread. The indwelling of the devil, then, corresponds in the Christian perspective not simply to abiding outside the truth, not simply to “paganism” or “idolatry from ignorance,” but precisely to the rejection of the truth after the tasting of it. On this foundation is based the whole radical and consistent anti-Judaism of Orthodoxy, which from the beginning was in the extreme hostile to the Judeo-Christian tendencies within the Church itself (Arianism, Nestorianism, and so on), and especially to Judaism, which continues to insist on the law and to deny the grace of the Gospel. Judas Iscariot was identified with post-Christian Judaism not only among simple believers, on the ground of “folk etymology” and the resemblance between the words “Judas” and “Judeans,” but precisely by force of the metaphysical identity between these two realities — personified in Judas, collective in the Jews.217

At another level Judas presents the image of that which Guénon calls “counter-initiation” — that is, a consecration which, leading a man out beyond the limits of the individual state, orients him, however, not upward but downward. And here it is telling that the evangelist underscores the process of Satan’s entry into Judas as proceeding together with communion. This same episode is commemorated in the communion prayers at the liturgy, where the believer asks the Lord not to turn the Holy Gifts into a consuming fire, even in the case that the communicant’s soul is unworthy of the deifying mercy and black with sins. The eucharistic indwelling of God is thus fraught with the indwelling of the devil — with the opening of the non-human, demonic dimension in man, his transformation into a “fallen angel,” possession. And in the image of Judas the counter-initiatic moment is present in its most dreadful quality: for the betrayal of God is the most terrible and unforgivable sin, the “perfect sin,” if one may so express oneself concerning sin — and this means that here it is not simply one of the demons that indwells the man, but the leader of the demons, Satan.

Since Judas Iscariot is metaphysically identical with the whole Judaic community that rejected the Savior, one may examine an analogous “devil-indwelling” in the context of the people of Israel as well. This has direct relation to the “choice of the angels,” of which we have spoken repeatedly in the preceding chapters. Let us recall that, from the point of view of Orthodox metaphysics, the problem of the pre-eternal choice of the angels is transformed from a twofold into a threefold one; the doctrines of both the Jews and the Hellenes disclose their fundamental insufficiency, and the celestial dual opposition of the “good angel” (often symbolized by the archangel Michael) and the “evil angel” (the “dennitsa”) is resolved in the Dormition of the Theotokos, who became the Third Angel — in reality the First, the one who made the only correct choice. The Christian tradition of necessity accomplishes a significant transformation of Judaic angelology, since henceforth the Christian “good angels” and their truth differ sharply from the “good angels” of Judaism — though without being identified, for all that, with the retinue of the “dennitsa,” contrary to the excessive and too “Hellenic” assertions of certain gnostic Ophite (and especially Cainite) sects. If the Judaic tradition (and correspondingly the Judaic angelology) had existed only up to Christ, the goodness of the Hebrew angels would not be placed in doubt, and all the “good angels” without exception could pass unhindered into Christian angelology, as humble spirits who had diligently awaited the fulfillment of the promise and were fully adequate to the epoch of grace. But the fact of the existence of Judaism, and of the Jews as a religious community, after Christ as well, changes the whole matter sharply. In that case a most profound change takes place at the level of angelology itself. It consists in the following. The national angel of Israel was the archangel Michael, the highest of the angels, governing all the angelic hierarchies — just as Israel was the chosen people, called to rule the other peoples.218 But together with Christ, Israel as an Old Testament reality, as the community of the law, ceases to exist — since the New Israel is born, the new chosen people: the Christian Church, the community of grace, the nation of the faithful. The Old Israel stands toward the New in a twofold position. On the one hand, the apostles, and especially the apostle Paul (and together with them all the Hebrews baptized into the incarnate God), enter into the new era, lay the foundation of the Church, establish the pillars of Orthodoxy. In them and through them is accomplished also the conversion of the angels who acknowledge the uncreated nature in Christ and voluntarily pass under the command of the Theotokos, “more honorable than the cherubim and more glorious beyond compare than the seraphim.” By analogy with the Jews one may suppose that something similar occurred also in the case of certain angels from the retinue of the dennitsa — these “fallen angels,” as leaders of the pagan nations, were churched in the process of the baptism of the peoples, likewise entering under the command of the Queen of Heaven.

But it is evident that the angels governing the Judaic community after the coming of Christ are, in the Christian perspective, profoundly distinct from those angels who stood before Israel until Christ. This transformation proceeds in parallel with the entry of Satan into Judas Iscariot: not having accepted the incarnate God and Savior — more than that, having crucified Him, betrayed Him, mocked Him, subjected Him to tortures and to a shameful (from their point of view) execution — the Jews let into themselves the devil, who firmly established himself in the place of the archangel Michael, usurping his functions. Thus the Jews became, from this moment, the people-bearer of counter-initiation, an “accursed nation,” a community in which supremacy belonged henceforth to Judas Iscariot. “And their court became desolate, and there was none dwelling in it,” in the words of the apostle Peter in the “Acts of the Apostles.” To the Jews henceforth became fully applicable the assertion of the same Peter: “Now this man (Judas — A.D.) purchased a field with the reward of iniquity; and falling headlong, he burst asunder in the midst, and all his bowels gushed out. And it was known unto all the dwellers at Jerusalem; insomuch as that field is called in their proper tongue, Aceldama, that is to say, The field of blood.”219 Thus the promised land becomes the “field of blood,” the sons of Abraham the “sons of the devil,” and the archangel standing before the Jews, repeating the trajectory of the dennitsa, is “cast down” into the abyss.

Here one should turn again to those considerations which we expressed concerning the apparent contradictoriness of the Christian assertion that the Theotokos became the head of the angels — whereas, proceeding from the specificity of the celestial world, the laws of eternity reign there, and nothing becomes or endures, diminishes or increases. We were compelled then to acknowledge the eternity of the secret Third Archangel, the Most Pure Virgin, who in a certain sense was the head of the angels always. That is: in the Kingdom of Heaven there always existed a third possibility of resolving the problem of the relation of Creator and creature — neither in the Judaic way nor in the Hellenic. One may say that in parallel with the dual hierarchy of the two angelic hosts there always existed a third, secret hierarchy — the host of the Queen of Heaven, or, in other terms, the “angels after the order of Melchizedek.”

In this same perspective one should understand also the counter-initiatic transformation of the angel of Israel, reflected in the dreadful fate of Judas Iscariot. The angel of Israel, naturally, could not turn from “good into evil” in the course of earthly history — since in the heavens the whole of history exists simultaneously and at once, from the first instant of creation. Consequently the “good angel” of Israel was from the beginning not so unambiguously “good” as it might seem at first glance. The creationist choice of this angel could, on the one hand, serve as the expression of a profound humility — of a surmise concerning the kenotic orientation of the absolute itself, and of an imitation of that orientation (in which case the “good angel” stood from the beginning on the side of the Queen of Heaven); but it could also be “satan” in the etymological sense of that word — an insuperable barrier, arbitrarily set between Creator and creation. This is the dark aspect of the archangel Michael, who is sometimes named Samael, the “prince of this world.”220

The Incarnation of the Son of God, at the eternal (angelic) and the earthly (historical) level, already potentially accomplishes the mystery of the Last Judgment and separates the goats from the sheep: the dark aspect of Michael, the angel of Israel, is severed from his light aspect. And as Judas is cast down from the apostolic election into the abyss of the curse, so the Jews, who identified their destiny with the line of Samael, are scattered through the world; their Temple is destroyed; and they are turned into eternal wanderers, hardening in God-resistance and rejected by God and by the Christian peoples of the earth. Michael, for his part, in his light aspect, becomes henceforth the protector of Christians — the churched archangel, standing before the face of the new universal Orthodox nation, the community of the faithful, the One, Holy, Catholic, and Apostolic Church.

Apparatus · Chapters XLI

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 214–220 are preserved. “Synagogue of Satan,” Rev. 2:9 (par. 3:9) — in its own context a verdict on a specific first-century conflict in Smyrna, here universalized into a standing sentence on all post-Christian Jewry. “Let his habitation be desolate,” Acts 1:20, citing Ps. 69:25. “After the sop Satan entered into him,” John 13:27, quoted in Slavonic. The Akeldama passage is Acts 1:18–19. Michael as the prince of Israel is Dan. 10:21 and 12:1, presumably the matter of marker 218 — though “called to rule the other peoples” is Dugin’s gloss on election, not the Torah’s, whose formulas are a kingdom of priests (Ex. 19:6) and a light to the nations (Isa. 42:6), not dominion. The Old Rite Isus continues; “Immanuel” stands as he writes it.

The altered verse. One textual operation deserves isolation, because it is the chapter’s whole method in miniature. Acts 1:20 reads, of Judas, “let his habitation be desolate” — singular, as Dugin first quotes it. When he reapplies it to the Jewish people, the quotation silently changes number: “and their court became desolate.” Scripture said nothing of the kind; the plural is Dugin’s emendation. The transfer of the curse from the one man to the whole people — which is precisely the thesis the chapter must prove — is accomplished inside the quotation marks, by rewriting the pronoun. Confidence: high; the alteration is verifiable against any text of Acts.

Guénon’s category and Dugin’s application. “Counter-initiation” is genuinely Guénon’s term (The Reign of Quantity, chs. 34–38 and elsewhere): an inverted spiritual realization, oriented downward, served by unnamed agents whose localization Guénon deliberately refused. What must be stated flatly is that Guénon never identified counter-initiation with Judaism or with the Jewish people — he treated the Hebrew Kabbalah as an orthodox esoteric tradition of the primordial stock. The identification of the category with a living people is Dugin’s move, made under Guénon’s borrowed authority, and it converts an abstract (and already conspiratorial) doctrine into a theological warrant for ethnic demonization. Confidence: high. The same must be said of the angelology: Samael, in the Jewish sources where the name lives, is a distinct figure — accuser, angel of death, prince of Esau/Edom — and no tradition, Jewish or patristic, knows him as “the dark aspect of the archangel Michael”; “prince of this world” is the Fourth Gospel’s title for the devil (John 12:31), not a name of Michael in any received angelology. The bifurcation of Michael into light and dark aspects is Dugin’s private construction, engineered to solve a problem his own eternalism created.

The deicide doctrine and the tradition. The chapter’s foundation — “the murder of God is the collective crime of the Jews” — is the charge of collective deicide, and the apparatus owes the reader precision about its standing. It is not the doctrine of the New Testament record, in which a Roman prefect executes Jesus by a Roman method, Peter tells the Jerusalem crowd they acted “in ignorance” (Acts 3:17), Paul writes that the rulers “would not have crucified the Lord of glory” had they known (1 Cor. 2:8), and the Lord himself prays “they know not what they do” (Luke 23:34) — verses the chapter does not quote. Nor is it Orthodox dogma: no council defines it, and the catechetical tradition’s answer to “who crucified Christ” is: the sins of all. What Dugin maximalizes is real but lesser material — the harsh anti-Judaic strand of some patristic polemic (Chrysostom’s Adversus Judaeos homilies above all) and of certain Holy Week hymnography — a strand modern Orthodox theology has increasingly treated as a problem to be repented of, not a metaphysic to be completed. Dugin completes it: and his completion goes beyond even the harshest supersessionism. Patristic supersessionism held Israel’s election genuine and the fall historical; Dugin’s eternalism retrojects the flaw into heaven itself — the angel of Israel “was from the beginning not so unambiguously good.” The curse is thereby written into the eternal structure of creation, a step no father took.
