Attendant claims require flags of their own: Arianism and Nestorianism were Trinitarian and Christological heresies of Greek intellectual provenance, and calling them “Judeo-Christian tendencies” repeats the ancient heresiological smear rather than history; the “eternal wanderer” is the medieval Ahasuerus legend, not doctrine; and the scattering-as-punishment narrative is false as demography — the great diaspora communities of Alexandria, Asia Minor, and Babylonia flourished for centuries before the Crucifixion, so the dispersion the chapter dates from the curse in fact preceded it. Confidence: high throughout.

What is internally consistent, and what it costs. Honesty about the chapter’s architecture: within the book’s own system it is rigorous. The eternal Third Archangel of Chapter XIV, the communion-fire of Chapters XXIV and XXIX, the Cainite refusal (Judas must not be rehabilitated, against the gnostic temptation — the Cainites of Irenaeus’ report did venerate Judas and possessed a “Gospel” in his name), the three enclosures of XXXIX now mirrored by a counter-church: every prior gear engages. That is precisely what makes the chapter the gravest in the book. The machinery of the whole work — eternalism, initiation-geometry, the modal razor between grace and necessity — is here assembled into a single device whose output is the demonization of a living people. The reader who has admired the system’s ingenuity for forty chapters is shown, in the forty-first, what the system is for.

Reading note — Part VI complete. The Part’s logic closes with grim economy: a Church with an inner circle requires an adversary with one; the three enclosures demanded a counter-hierarchy, and Dugin has supplied it with an address. This chapter is also the hinge of the book: behind it, the sacramental and calendrical mysticism of Parts IV–V; ahead of it, Part VII’s imperial eschatology, whose katechon needs exactly the enemy-image manufactured here. And it is the chapter Golovin’s review was warning about, from the pagan side, in the sentence already quoted in this edition’s supplement: “from a chosen confession it is not far to a chosen people.” The translation has been kept faithful and unsoftened precisely so that the apparatus could be this exact: the chapter’s authority-claims dissolve under citation — the verse is altered, the Guénon is misapplied, the angelology is invented, the history is false — and what remains is the oldest accusation in Christendom, wearing a Traditionalist mask.

Part VII · The Kingdom and the Realm

Part VII · Chapter XLII

Priests and Warriors

Священники и воины

From the point of view of traditionalism, the question of the mutual relation of the two highest types of men, the two castes — priests and warriors (brahmins and kshatriyas in Hinduism) — is of the utmost importance. According to this relation one can determine the character of any concrete tradition and the sacred orientation of the society founded upon it. The isolation of these two types (the sacerdotal and the martial) and of the functions corresponding to them is one of the principal methodological instruments for the sacred deciphering of history. For the Christian tradition this typology likewise has the utmost significance — though here, as in all other cases, Christianity proposes an entirely particular solution without analogue. But in order to grasp the specificity of the strictly Christian understanding of the “caste” problem, a brief excursus into the traditionalist theory of the castes, and of the social types of society bound up with them, is necessary.

This theme is worked out in greatest detail in the Hindu tradition, which may be taken as the basis. That tradition affirms the existence of three foundational castes, corresponding to three types of men: priests (brahmins), warrior-kings (kshatriyas), and producers — artisans and tillers of the soil (vaishyas). If in India these castes are erected into strictly defined social institutions, in other civilizations the division manifests itself more flexibly. But even where the caste principle has no clear social equivalent, men nonetheless divide of necessity, by the quality of their inner nature, into these three basic categories.

Men of the sacerdotal type incline to contemplation. This type is primordially predisposed to the immersion of consciousness in the inner worlds, to “introversion,” to the centripetal tendency of existence, to concentration. The contemplatives grasp reality of the intellectual, spiritual order without any effort — as naturally as ordinary men perceive sensible objects. Parallel to this pull toward the inner, whose weight the person of the sacerdotal, priestly cast never doubts, interest in outer actuality, in the events of the psychic and material world, is here blunted and diffuse. In its most finished variant, material reality is for the priest as much a convention and an abstraction as the world of pure ideas or of general concepts is for the common man. In the threefold structure of the created world the sacerdotal type corresponds to heaven, the world of “formless manifestation,” to “angelicity,” to spirit. It is important to note that in this case the general existential orientation is supra-individual, “witness-like.” From the standpoint of the symbolism of the cycle, the priestly type stands nearest of all to celestial eternity, to the motionless point on the far side of all duration (progressive and rotational alike).

Hence the ritual “motionlessness” of the contemplatives, so often met in ascetic practice. To the second type — the warrior-king — belong men of pure action. Precisely pure: for to them the material consequences and results of the action are unimportant; it is an end in itself. Warriors are men of constant movement, of circular rotation (to use the cyclic symbolism). To them corresponds the middle world, intermediate between the material earth and the spiritual heaven. The orientation of the martial type is exactly described by rotational movement, in contrast to the centripetal tendency of the priests and the centrifugal tendency of the producers. Warriors are neither introverted nor extraverted. They are, rather, concentrated upon their own person, which they affirm and fortify as a kind of fixed, independent reality. It is no accident that in history the martial type is bound up with the horse and the symbolism of the horse: what is at issue is the lightness, mobility, and lightning speed that characterize the inner constitution, the nature, the soul of the warrior. Warriors incline to perceive the surrounding world in the same terms in which they perceive themselves — that is, individually and dynamically.

The third type of man is the producer. His chief characteristic may be taken to be the principle of material action, of centrifugal orientation. The producers correspond to progressive, forward movement in the cyclic symbolism. They are extraverted and gravitate toward directing their forces upon the outer world — transforming, arranging, and cultivating it. For the producers, psychic reality (transparent to the warriors as an open book) is perceived dimly and fragmentarily, while the spiritual worlds seem so distant that they scarcely interest them at all. As a rule the producers are bound up with the symbolism of the tools of labor — the plow, the hammer, the scythe, and so on. Their energies are oriented outward from the personal, psychic principle, and so it is natural for them to evaluate real events on the basis of physical sensations and material objects. These are the men of the earth, of the lower world.221

The circumstance that warriors and producers are characterized by a common term — “action” — is not accidental. Indeed, in distinction from the priests, who symbolize an entirely particular tendency of existence, there is considerable resemblance between warriors and producers. One may say that the warriors are “disembodied” producers, and the producers are warriors “immersed in the material, objectual medium.” What distinguishes the warriors from the producers is only the degree of density of the medium in which they act. And so, strictly speaking, the producers represent not so much progressive movement as a combination of purely progressive and purely rotational movements — just as the real year carries in itself, on the one hand, the repetition of seasons and natural phenomena, and on the other, the eventful difference proper to this year alone and no other. The third type, the third caste, contributes no particular quality to the structure of the martial archetype; it merely darkens that archetype, makes it fragmentary and “turbid.”222

One may therefore speak of two principial types — the sacerdotal (priestly) and the martial (royal) — since the third type adds nothing qualitatively new to the two preceding. In agreement with this logic, most traditionalists (and first of all Guénon himself), in analyzing the sacred vector of civilizations, speak precisely of the domination of one of the two archetypes — contemplation or action — including the producers as a “subspecies” of the warriors, one of their varieties. This, incidentally, corresponds exactly to historical reality, in which the third caste often became the basis of armies or military detachments under the leadership of professional aristocrat-commanders.

Thus in human society a fundamental dualism of types may be traced: the type of the contemplative and the type of the doer — priesthood and warriorhood. According to Guénon, the normal hierarchy exists where contemplation dominates action and subordinates it to itself — where, accordingly, the priesthood directs the warriorhood. Guénon defines the basic quality of the priesthood as “spiritual authority,” while the warriorhood, for its part, is characterized by “temporal power.”223 The violation of this order of things Guénon calls the “revolt of the Kshatriyas” (that is, of the warriors), and he holds that the degradation of the modern world is precisely the consequence of such a “revolt” — the uprising of the men of action against the men of contemplation.

The history of the Western Middle Ages offers a broad panorama of how the distortion of the proportions between these two archetypes begins from both sides: on the one hand the priesthood degenerates and interferes ever more in secular problems, laying claim to “temporal power”; while the secular rulers, warriors, and kings struggle against the authority of the sacerdotal type for complete independence in political and spiritual questions alike. The most vivid specimens of these two aspects of “caste” perversion are the Catholic Vatican and absolutist France.

As an alternative model in the West itself, representing an adequate distribution of the archetypal tendencies, the traditionalists (Guénon as well as Evola) point to the Ghibelline idea, in which the universality of the imperial principle — the highest martial archetype — was combined with a purely spiritual, supra-temporal understanding of the Church and its authority. The line of the German emperors of the Hohenstaufen embodied two lines at once: anti-papism (anti-Vatican) and anti-absolutism (anti-France).

The Holy Empire is thus the synthesis and summit of the adequate combination of the sacerdotal and martial principles. It stands opposed both to pure theocracy, where the priesthood is compelled to secularize itself, and to the “revolt of the Kshatriyas,” where the warriors illegitimately appropriate to themselves the functions of the highest instance in the sphere of pure spirit.

Part VII · Chapter XLIII

The Symphony of Powers

Симфония властей

To the two types of men there correspond, in the Orthodox tradition, two concepts of the utmost importance for understanding the history of the Church. These are the concepts of the “Kingdom” and the “Realm.” The “Kingdom” was understood by Christians as the Kingdom of God, for whose coming the Orthodox pray in the “Our Father” (“Thy Kingdom come”). It corresponds to the purely spiritual, “sacerdotal,” priestly reality, since the Kingdom of God belongs precisely to eternity;224 and though it immeasurably exceeds the created eternity of the heavens, for the gaze from within creation it is identified with the Kingdom of Heaven, with the primordial spiritual, motionless point. The Kingdom of Heaven is the chief object of contemplative concentration, the center of theological attention, the focus of the spiritual gaze of all those called to inner being, to the angelic immersion in the Holy of Holies of creation.

The Realm, in distinction from the Kingdom, pertains to cosmic reality, to the outer world, to temporal actuality. In the Realm is accomplished the ordering of the environment, the organization of men’s existence in accordance with determinate sacred norms. The Realm is a function of “standing” — of the preservation of the vertical position against the entropy of material reality. It is likewise the struggle against inertia, the bridling of the lower, bottom-most forces of creation. It is the region of concrete action, psychic or corporeal. And the Realm is not simply the region of the lower, but of the organized lower, brought into accord with certain higher prescriptions. Only in that case can one speak of a Realm properly so called; from the beginning it had the meaning of an intermediate reality between the inertness of the earth and the purity of heaven. Such an intermediate function suits precisely the type of the warriors, the “kings.”

Between the Kingdom and the Realm there exists not a dualistic contradiction but complementarity. In the normal case the sacred society — the Holy Empire — presupposes the fullness of the saving hierarchy, thanks to which all the levels of cosmic and human reality are conjoined with one another in a single chain, ascending from the floor of creation to its summit and back. In this consists the esoteric symbolism of Jacob’s ladder, upon which, in the patriarch’s vision, the angels descended and ascended. The Kingdom is the summit of the Realm, its transcendent dimension: the triangle crowning the trapezoid. But the participation of the ordinary man in this transcendent dimension cannot be realized except through the Realm — for in order to receive full illumination one must first order the chaotic elements of material nature, and only afterward transfer the ordered person into the higher, supra-individual spheres.

On this principle is founded the mystery of royal power or, in diminished form, of “martial consecration.” For it is precisely the King, as the summit and peak of the whole Realm, who is the universal figure of the warrior par excellence, the absolute warrior, in whom the direct contact of the lower with the higher, of the earthly with the heavenly, is accomplished. He stands at the very highest point of the immanent Realm and, at the same time, at the very lowest point of the transcendent Kingdom. This is distinctly traceable in all the traditional rituals of “royal consecration,” and among others in the Old Testament, where the anointing of Saul to the Realm by the prophet Samuel is described — that is, by a representative of the sacerdotal type, a bearer of “spiritual authority.”

Orthodoxy in its historical form fully inherited the sacred understanding of the Holy Empire and of the adequate combination of Kingdom and Realm. This is reflected in the conception of the Orthodox symphonia, where the spiritual status of the Patriarch and of the Emperor is clearly defined. The Orthodox Tsar, the Basileus, the Emperor, is understood as a bishop of the Church, spiritually dependent on the authority of the Patriarch (or metropolitan), but absolutely free in the conduct of secular affairs. The conception of the Orthodox symphonia corresponds exactly to the Ghibelline ideal, which in the West was never realized. It would be more correct to put the comparison the other way: the Ghibelline ideal of the Holy Empire was an echo of the genuinely Orthodox symphonia, which really existed in the East (and later in Rus') at the very time when the most traditional esoteric organizations of the West were desperately (and almost always unsuccessfully) attempting to realize something similar in practice through the Staufen princes, or through the wholly utopian Rosicrucian projects. And it is altogether strange that modern traditionalists, who painstakingly investigate the most insignificant details of the esoteric organizations of the imperial type in Europe, at the same time loftily pass over the Byzantine-Slavic experience — where all the aspects of the adequate combination of “spiritual authority” and “temporal power” were theoretically fixed in the doctrine of the symphonia and existed in practice for many centuries, down to the very last times!225

The merging of Kingdom and Realm in the person of the Tsar, who thereby becomes as it were a bridge between the two worlds, spiritual and secular, is reflected in many aspects of the Orthodox ritual. It is displayed with particular vividness in the rite of crowning — of marriage. The very word “crowning,” and the symbolic use of crowns (held above the heads of those entering into marriage), indicates that any man, at the most spiritually responsible moments of his existence, is ritually identified with the Tsar himself, actualizing in himself the royal dimension. In other words, the ordinary man is joined to the Kingdom of Heaven only through the archetype of the King — by identifying himself with the King, by ritually becoming him. Hence, in particular, royal attributes are used in many initiatic rituals: the entrance into celestial reality passes through the peak of the earthly Realm. In like manner the doors separating, in the Orthodox temple, the ship of the nave from the altar precinct are called Royal.

It may be observed that, proceeding from the foundational duality of human types, contemplation and action can stand in another relation besides the harmonious and ideal symphonic conception. Thus, in distinction from Orthodoxy, the Western Church almost from the very beginning gravitated toward the opposition of Kingdom and Realm — or at least toward a distortion of the proportions between them. Already in St. Augustine, who before his acceptance of Christianity had been a Manichaean, an inclination is noticeable toward the counterposition of the “Heavenly City” and the “Earthly City.” Contemplation and action are regarded as two mutually exclusive concepts. And though in this case what is at issue is an unambiguous choice in favor of contemplation, of the “Heavenly City,” the very framing of the question provokes the expansion of the functions of the priesthood into spheres which, from the Orthodox point of view, ought to have no relation to it. After such an “anti-symphonic” counterposition there had logically to follow the answering step of the “Earthly City” — that is, the “revolt of the Kshatriyas,” the uprising of the Realm against the Kingdom. This process was gradual: beginning with the French absolutist monarchs of the fourteenth century, it manifested itself sharply in the Reformation and ended with the complete desacralization of the West — that is, with the complete victory of the “Earthly City.”

In Orthodox Byzantium, and later in Holy Rus' (of the Muscovite period), such a sharp counterposition never existed (though tendencies toward it there were, as we shall see further on), and the Orthodox world therefore succeeded much longer in withstanding desacralization. But even in the last, catastrophic periods of the history of Orthodoxy, the quality of its decline is nonetheless entirely other than the line of the degeneration of the West.

Apparatus · Chapters XLII–XLIII

Apparatus

The part title. «Царствие и царство» opposes two words English collapses into one: Tsarstvie, the liturgical word of “Thy Kingdom come” (Царствие Твое), and tsarstvo, the ordinary word for a kingdom or realm of this world. The translation renders them throughout as “the Kingdom” and “the Realm.” This resumes the distinction Dugin built in Chapter XII between the uncreated Kingdom of God and the created kingdom of heaven — now redeployed politically. Note markers 221–225 are preserved; two source typos («влыдычество», «Висилевс») are corrected silently to “authority” and Basileus.

Sources of the framework. Chapter XLII is a compressed restatement of Guénon’s Autorité spirituelle et pouvoir temporel (1929) — “spiritual authority” / “temporal power” and the “revolt of the Kshatriyas” are its coinages — crossed with Evola’s Ghibellinism (Rivolta contro il mondo moderno, the imperial idea of Il mistero del Graal). One move, however, is Dugin’s own accent: the reduction of the third caste to a “darkened” variety of the second, so that only two principial types remain. Guénon’s scheme keeps the vaishya as a genuine type; the collapse into a warrior-priest duality is Evola’s aristocratism — and it conveniently clears the stage for the Empire as one of only two possible summits. Note also the inversion of precedence in XLIII: where Evola derived his ideal from the Ghibelline West, Dugin makes the Ghibelline idea a mere “echo” of Byzantine symphonia — the Traditionalist canon corrected in Orthodoxy’s favor, as everywhere in this book.

Historical cautions. (1) The account of symphonia idealizes: the classical formula (Justinian’s Sixth Novel; the ninth-century Epanagoge) did envision the concord of sacerdotium and basileia, but Byzantine practice oscillated toward what its critics have always called caesaropapism — emperors deposing patriarchs, dictating dogma at Hiereia or over the Union councils; the “many centuries of practice” were considerably stormier than the chapter allows. The Tsar-as-bishop formula echoes Constantine’s ambiguous “bishop of those outside” (Eusebius, Vita Constantini IV.24), a phrase whose meaning is disputed to this day. (2) The reading of Augustine is tendentious twice over: the two cities of De civitate Dei are constituted by two loves (love of God unto contempt of self, love of self unto contempt of God), not by the castes of contemplation and action; and the insinuation that the doctrine is residually Manichaean inverts Augustine’s own anti-Manichaean intent. The genealogy Augustine → papal theocracy → absolutism → Reformation → desacralization is a schema, not a history. (3) The marriage-crowning symbolism (венчание) is genuinely Orthodox — the rite is indeed called “crowning” and the crowns are royal as well as martyric — though the standard liturgical commentaries give the martyric sense at least equal weight, which the chapter omits.

Reading note. These two chapters perform the pivot the whole book has been preparing: the metaphysics of Parts I–III becomes political theology. The Realm is justified from above — the ordinary man reaches the Kingdom “only through the archetype of the King” — which makes empire not an arrangement of convenience but a soteriological instrument, exactly the position Chapter X seeded when grace was activated by the entry of “a man, a people, an empire” into the Church. The next two chapters (XLIV–XLV) will name the stakes: theocracy and tyranny as the Judaic and Hellenic deviations, and Byzantium as the katechon — the restrainer of II Thessalonians — whose withdrawal opens the reign of the Antichrist.

Part VII · Chapter XLIV

Theocracy and Tyranny /
Jewry and Hellenism

Теократия и тирания/иудейство и эллинство

The problem of the relation of Kingdom and Realm (of priests and warriors) may also be examined at the level of the counterposition of the two basic metaphysical approaches: creationism and manifestationism (of which we spoke in detail earlier). The creationist perspective corresponds strictly to the affirmation of the transcendent Kingdom at the expense of the earthly Realm. Contemplation is in that case opposed to action, and on the social plane we are dealing with one of the varieties of that regime which it is customary to call “theocracy.” Strict creationism proceeds from the premise of the absolute insignificance of creation before the face of the Creator, and in consequence all aspects of the immanent lose all meaning. But the sphere of the cosmos is precisely the sphere of the immanent, whose common denominator is the principle of action — which corresponds to the martial archetype.226 Consequently the principle of action, of warriorhood, of royal power is metaphysically devalued. Only the sacerdotal caste, which answers for “deciphering the will of the Creator” or for the “keeping of the Covenant,” is allotted the right to figure as the chief instance in society. Late Judaic society, in the period following the return from the Babylonian captivity, constituted precisely such a “theocratic” reality, with the undisguised domination, in all social, domestic, and practical questions, of the caste of scribes and interpreters of the Law.

Characteristically, a similar situation repeats itself with Islam, in which the typically Semitic creationism renewed by Muhammad, and the strict Abrahamic monotheism, engendered the strictly theocratic system of the early caliphate with scribes and faqihs at its head. And as always in such cases, theocracy devalued the immanent entirely, arranging the social landscape on the model of a barren desert. It is telling that early Islamic (as also Judaic) architecture abounds in constructions of the square type with a flat roof — which is meant stylistically to serve as a constant reminder of the perishability and nullity of the Realm and of its incommensurability with the transcendent Kingdom. Just as creation in creationism is radically divorced from the Creator, so under theocracy the priests are set apart as a particular caste of the pure, the men of faith, who have no common measure with the remaining types of society. In place of organic bonds and a continuous, elevating hierarchy there arise genetically segregated clans — in Judaism, the Levites — or classes, which impose upon the whole society their totalitarian rule, whose logic is held in advance to exceed the mental capacities of ordinary men. Analogously to the rupture of the bond between God and the world, theocracy ruptures the bond between the priest-rulers and everyone else (the men of action). Such a social type, by analogy with the “anti-sacerdotal revolt of the Kshatriyas,” may be called the “usurpation of secular power by the priests.”

In the Christian terms of the apostle Paul, such a society should be called “Judaic,” in the very broadest sense of the word.

The opposite type will accordingly be the “Hellenic.” This is the Realm setting itself in the place of the Kingdom. It is the typical result of the “revolt of the Kshatriyas.” Here the sphere of the immanent is held to be so thoroughly pierced by the real presence of the principle that every difference between manifestation and its source is entirely effaced. The whole of reality is proclaimed the sphere of action, and the questions of the cult pass into the charge of the caste of warriors. Such a society may with full justice be called “pagan,” in the most negative sense of the term.

Often there takes place, in the process, a complete “divinization” of the King or Emperor: instead of the executor of a mediating function, he begins to be regarded as the highest source of all authority.227 The Greece of the late Hellenistic epoch, and the last centuries of pagan Rome (with the exception of brief periods of relative restoration of the normal proportions), offer a multitude of examples of such manifestationist, Luciferic degeneration, with terrifying figures of tyrants and despots of the type of Caligula or Nero.

In such societies, with the domination of the warriors, the priests pass into the rank of an auxiliary estate, called to sustain the social regime; and the very type of sacerdotal spirituality is brought down to the level of “social service” or, at best, of “psychic work.” The whole of metaphysics is in this case reduced to cosmology, and even to rituals and practices operating with the two lower spheres of the universe — the world of flesh and the subtle world (the world of the soul).

Thus “Jewry” in the social sphere corresponds to theocracy, and “Hellenism” to tyranny and absolutism. At the period of the Savior’s preaching these terms coincided exactly with Judea and the Roman Empire.

Later, already within Christian reality, the corresponding social archetypes were embodied in the Judeo-Christian tendencies of the Vatican, which gravitated toward theocracy (the Guelph party), and in the humanist pathos of the Renaissance (later of the Enlightenment), with its typically pagan, immanentist statehood. Beyond this, determinate analogues of these two types of social system, corresponding to the two types of human nature, can be found in the history of all peoples and civilizations — whether at the documentarily attested level or at the mythological and legendary one.

Part VII · Chapter XLV

Byzantium, the Katechon,
and the Millennial Kingdom

Византия, катехон и тысячелетнее Царство

The metaphysical choice of the third path, which is the foundation of Christianity as a tradition, projects itself onto the social sphere as well. Here, just as in theology, the Christian solution flows from the universal formula of the holy apostle Paul: “there is neither Jew nor Greek.” If one interprets this in the terms of Realm and Kingdom (which corresponds to the affirmation of the domination of one or the other human type — either priests or warriors), one may say that the Orthodox doctrine denies both theocracy and tyranny: both the Judaic domination of the priests and the pagan system of the undivided rule of the warriors. Christianity, to be sure, gives precedence to the sacerdotal, priestly function, to the reality of the transcendent Kingdom. But in distinction from theocracy, the powers of the priesthood are strictly limited to the region of the spirit — for “My Kingdom is not of this world,” in the words of Christ. Consequently the Christian priesthood is empowered to occupy itself with the strictly transcendent sphere, without interfering directly in the affairs of the world.

But at the level of human and cosmic immanent reality, the Orthodox social teaching unambiguously acknowledges the competence of royal power and empowers the warrior-kings to exercise the fullness of ordering control over the earthly element. The principle of the Realm acts in this world, submitting symphonically to the unworldly, supra-cosmic principle of the Kingdom. And it is precisely thus that one must understand Christ’s answer to the Pharisees: “To God the things that are God’s, and to Caesar the things that are Caesar’s.” In a certain sense Christianity restored the true proportions of the relation between the sacerdotal and martial types — which contrasted with particular sharpness against the social degradation and the various forms of usurpation of power in the societies in which Christianity arose. The Christian symphonic solution of the problem of the ordering of the state was an overcoming both of the local Judaic social tradition, in whose bosom the first Christian communities appeared, and of the wider social context of the Roman Empire, into which the whole of Judea juridically entered at that period. In other words, the theory of the Orthodox symphony of powers put an end both to the “usurpation by the priests of full social power” and to the pagan “revolt of the Kshatriyas.”

The Byzantine Empire became the historical embodiment of the Orthodox symphonia, and Constantinople, as the New Rome, displayed a unique synthesis of Kingdom and Realm — becoming that providential millennial kingdom in which the eschatological prophecies were realized. And here it is important to underscore the very name New Rome. Let us recall that in the initiatic sense the term new does not simply indicate temporal sequence, but pertains to the region of esotericism — to those aspects of reality which participate in the process of initiation. Thus the “new man” was understood by Christians as the “initiated man,” the man who has passed through the ritual of consecration (baptism). The “new heavens” and “new earth” of the Apocalypse point to a particular transfigured reality, possessing a qualitatively different ontological characteristic from the cosmic objects of the old creation. So also must the New Testament be understood: it signifies not simply what is historically nearer to the present time, not simply what succeeds the Old Testament for purely temporal reasons, but a radically Other Covenant, opening for man and the universe the unheard-of prospect of deification, of kindred relations (by grace) with the Godhead. All this is applicable to the term “New Rome” as applied to Constantinople, which meant precisely a special, initiatically understood state formation — the Christian Empire, related to the former Roman Empire as the strictly Christian, New Testament theology was related to the Old Testament theology of Judaism. One may say that for the New Rome, the First Rome was the Old Rome. But if in the sphere of metaphysical doctrine Orthodoxy affirmed a radically new understanding of the Kingdom in comparison with Judaism, in the social sphere it advanced the conception of the Christian Realm, radically new in comparison with the Roman Empire. One may say that the theory of the Orthodox symphony of powers was a completely revolutionary doctrine for the entire world in which it was affirmed — for Jews and Hellenes alike. “Unto the Jews a stumbling-block, and unto the Greeks foolishness.”

From the point of view of Orthodoxy, the Byzantine Empire was the millennial kingdom in the most genuine sense — the kingdom to which the Old Testament and apocalyptic prophecies referred. Thus for approximately a thousand years — from the foundation of Constantinople and its transformation into the capital of the Roman Empire (324–330) to its taking by the Turks (1453) — there was affirmed in the world a sacred order corresponding to the highest and most harmonious hierarchical structure. For an adequate contemporary Christian consciousness, the chiliastic expectations — in distinction from the Jews, who did not receive the Lord — thus refer not to future time but to time already past: to the period of the existence of the Byzantine Empire, in which the Orthodox symphonia was embodied in perfect and finished form.

The Orthodox Empire had, in the strictly Christian perspective, an emphatically eschatological meaning. The perfect hierarchy restored in it between “spiritual authority” and “temporal power” reproduced, as it were, the proportions of the golden age, of the paradisal state of humanity. More than that: Byzantium was the earthly prefiguration of the Heavenly Jerusalem, with whose descent to earth human history will end. On this is founded the Orthodox “theology of Empire.” This eschatological “theology of the Orthodox Realm” is dogmatically founded on the following words from the Second Epistle of the apostle Paul to the Thessalonians: “For the mystery of lawlessness is already at work; only it will not be accomplished until he who now restraineth (emphasis ours — A.D.) be taken out of the midst.” And a little above: “And now ye know what withholdeth, that he (the antichrist — A.D.) should not be revealed until his own time.”228 In these spare words a brief eschatological scenario is described, and the role in it of the Orthodox Realm — the millennial kingdom, the Empire. The “man of lawlessness” and the “mystery of lawlessness” are the antichrist as personage and “his kingdom” as condition, whose appearance will immediately precede the moment of the Second Coming. The antichrist and his kingdom will establish themselves in the very last period of history, and their end will come about not mediately but immediately, in the Dread Coming itself of the Savior-in-Strength. But before the accomplishment of the “mystery of lawlessness” there stands, in the way of the antichrist’s coming, a certain barrier which “does not permit him to be revealed.” This is “he who restrains” — in Greek, the katechon. The Orthodox tradition unambiguously understands by “him who restrains” the Orthodox Tsar,229 and by that which “does not permit him to be revealed,” the Orthodox Realm, the Christian Empire. Thus the restoration of the normal correspondence between Kingdom and Realm, between the sacerdotal and martial elements, on the foundation of the New Testament truth, was understood by Christians as a really palpable, visible, concrete obstacle to the appearance of the antichrist and to the “falling away,” the “apostasy.” For this reason the theme of the preservation or absence of a full Orthodox sovereign power had the greatest importance in determining the signs of the immediate nearness of the end of times.

It is important to observe that in such a perspective every violation of the symphonic harmony between priesthood and kingship within the Christian tradition instantly summoned up the motifs of the “mystery of lawlessness,” of the “coming of the man of perdition,” of the “antichrist.” The preservation of the Orthodox Empire and of its “caste” order served as the visible expression of the fact that “he who now restraineth is not yet taken out of the midst” — and consequently that some time must still pass before the coming of the antichrist. Consequently the social aspect in Christianity has a deeply sacred, eschatological significance; and wherever a departure from the theory of the symphonia — as from the chiliastic reality bound up with the “katechon,” with “him who restrains” — is in evidence, there the Orthodox consciousness immediately divines apocalyptic notes and foresees the beginning of the process of universal apostasy.

It is not surprising, therefore, that the Eastern Church so stubbornly refused to accept Latinism and the papacy: for in the departure from the symphonic, Orthodox-imperial (“Ghibelline”) model a theocratic, “Judaic” deviation is plainly noticeable — and this in turn signifies the potential withdrawal, or at least the concealment, of the “katechon.” The Orthodox Byzantines, and after them the Slavs, from a certain moment justly discerned in papal Rome a listing toward the placing of the Kingdom over the Realm; and the violation of the symphonia in this direction could not fail to provoke the reverse process — the “revolt of the Kshatriyas” in a new, post-pagan and nominally “Christian” context (which finally took place in the Reformation). Generally speaking, the rupture of the West with Constantinople and its cultural-political separation could not, within Christian eschatology, be understood in any way other than as apostasy in the most genuine sense — since at the esoteric level it signified a return from the New Rome to the Old Rome (and at the theological level, a departure from the Christian-Christian New Testament to a Judeo-Christianity of almost Old Testament style).

Outside the symphonic, chiliastic Orthodox Realm there can exist only “Judaic” or “Hellenic” state types — that is, societies built upon the illegitimate usurpation by one caste of the powers of another. And it is not surprising that the Catholic world, immediately after the rupture with the East, displayed both variants of social apostasy at once: the lapse into theocracy and into pagan tyranny. Nor is it accidental that it was precisely the West that became, geographically, the space in which the openly anti-Christian and, more than that, antichrist-ic undertakings appeared — the complete rupture with tradition, profanism, atheism, progressism, rationalism, individualism, and so on.

From a certain moment — after the final failure of the attempts at a Ghibelline restoration in the West — the theme of the Christian Empire, of the symphonia, of the eschatological Realm became the possession of individual esoteric orders, acting as it were in defiance of the outer Latin apostasy. And this explains the anti-Catholic orientation of those spiritual currents which were still conscious of the whole significance of the Christian mystery of Empire. In place of real Orthodox Tsars there appeared the purely ritual “emperors” of the esoteric orders; the Christian Realm became invisible, “parallel,” potential; it withdrew into the region of legends and traditions. It is precisely around this theme, incidentally, that the medieval initiatic legends revolve — of the “kingdom of Prester John,” of the knights of the Round Table and the Holy Grail.230 But be that as it may, the West became the homeland of the “man of lawlessness,” whence the poisonous anti-Christian energies of the modern world began to creep across the whole planet.

Apparatus · Chapters XLIV–XLV

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 226–230 are preserved; two source typos («конктекста», «Фессолоникийцам») are corrected silently. “My Kingdom is not of this world” is John 18:36; the Caesar saying, Matt. 22:21 (Dugin reverses the canonical order, God before Caesar — the reversal is his and is kept); “unto the Jews a stumbling-block…,” 1 Cor. 1:23. The load-bearing citation is 2 Thess. 2:6–7, which Dugin quotes (verse 7 first) from the Russian Synodal version; the translation renders his Russian directly, with his emphasis and his bracketed gloss preserved. Readers checking the King James should know its verse 7 — “only he who now letteth will let” — uses “let” in the obsolete sense of hinder: the “letter” of the KJV is the restrainer, the katechon (ὁ κατέχων).

The katechon identification. Reading the restrainer as the Roman Empire and its emperor is genuinely ancient — Tertullian (Apology 32; On the Resurrection 24) and John Chrysostom (Fourth Homily on 2 Thessalonians) both take it so, and this became the dominant Byzantine reading. But “the Orthodox tradition unambiguously understands” overstates: patristic exegesis also proposed the Holy Spirit and the preaching of the gospel as candidates, and the personalization of the katechon as the Orthodox Tsar specifically is a Russian development, hardened into a fixed theologoumenon above all after 1917, when the Tsar’s removal could be read as the restrainer “taken out of the midst.” Note also the modern genealogy running beneath the ancient one: the rehabilitation of the katechon as a category of political theology belongs to Carl Schmitt (The Nomos of the Earth, 1950), and Dugin had been Schmitt’s chief importer into Russia since 1992 (“Carl Schmitt: Five Lessons for Russia,” Elementy no. 1). This chapter is where Schmittian katechontics is fused with Muscovite eschatology — the fusion that Dugin’s later geopolitical writing, and the “Russia as katechon” discourse of the 2010s–20s, presupposes. This 1996 text is its seed.

Realized chiliasm. The chapter’s most original — and most tendentious — move is temporal: the millennium of Revelation 20 is declared past, identified with Byzantium from Constantine’s foundation (324–330) to 1453. Mainstream Orthodox exegesis (Andrew of Caesarea in the East, convergent with Augustine in the West) reads the thousand years as the whole age of the Church between the two comings — a symbolic number, not a datable empire — while futurist chiliasm was marginalized after the Creed’s “His Kingdom shall have no end” (Constantinople, 381). Dugin literalizes the number, closes it at 1453, and thereby manufactures the dramatic situation the remaining chapters exploit: if the millennium has ended, everything after Byzantium is post-millennial borrowed time — which is exactly the frame in which the Third Rome (Ch. XLVI) and the “brief aeon of the Derzhavnaya” (Ch. XLVII) will be presented. Arithmetically, 324–1453 is some 1,129 years; “approximately a thousand” is doing quiet work.

Cautions on Chapter XLIV. The regime typology is the book’s Part II heresiology turned into political science: theocracy = Judeo-Christianity in power, tyranny = Helleno-Christianity in power, with symphonia as the regime-form of the third path. As with the parent scheme, the taxonomy is generative but procrustean: post-exilic Judea knew kings, Hasmonean priest-kings, and Roman procurators, not scribal “totalitarian rule”; the early caliphate was led by commanders, not faqihs (juristic authority consolidating only later); and the flat-roof architectural physiognomy of “Semitic creationism” is impressionistic essentialism, not archaeology. The Guelph/Ghibelline mapping repeats Evola. The closing identification of the West as “homeland of the man of lawlessness” completes the transfer of the book’s anti-Judaic polemic into geography: the eschatological adversary now has a compass direction — the premise of the whole of Dugin’s subsequent geopolitics.

Part VII · Chapter XLVI

On the Third Rome

О Третьем Риме

The Russian Orthodox Church was from the beginning a constituent part of the Orthodox world and, in a certain — spiritual — sense, a province of the Byzantine Empire. The Russians received Christianity from the Greeks (either directly, or through the somewhat earlier churched Bulgarians) and entered as a full component into the bosom of the Eastern Church. The whole history of the Russians is the history of Orthodoxy, from which the history of the people and of the state is inseparable. Together with Orthodox metaphysics, dogmatics, and ritual, Rus' received the Orthodox eschatology bound up with the hierarchy of the “castes.”231 Consequently Constantinople — Tsargrad — was the highest symbol and exemplar for Russian Orthodoxy in both the dogmatic and the social senses. The Russian Tsars and metropolitans, down to the fall of Constantinople, exactly reproduced in the local context the Christian symphony of powers, distributing the spheres of “spiritual authority” and “temporal power” according to the exactly indicated proportions of the providentially restored chiliastic harmony. As a constituent part of the Orthodox world, Rus' stood under the “protection” of the katechon and belonged with full title to the Holy Realm.

In this respect the Russian Church at once inherited the true understanding of the social problem in its mystical key; and therefore, from the very beginning of Russian history, contacts with the West had an unambiguously negative character (for Rus' and her tradition). In whatever direction the influence of the Latin world upon Rus' was exercised, it always carried with it, beneath the surface, a deviation from the harmonious symphony of powers — in either the “pagan” or the “Judaic” key. In either case the providential proportion of the Orthodox Realm of the last times was violated. Catholic theology, for its part, threatened the metaphysical integrity of the Orthodox creed altogether, and the activity of the “agents of papism” throughout the whole history of Russia had consequences of extreme destructiveness in the spiritual and social senses.

But it was not only the influence of the West that carried a threat to the Russian Orthodox Realm. Tendencies toward the counterposition of Kingdom and Realm existed among the Russian people themselves. This alarming theme appears for the first time in the disputes of the non-possessors of the Trans-Volga (the followers of St. Nilus of Sora, convinced hesychasts) and the Josephites (the partisans of St. Joseph of Volokolamsk). The non-possessors belonged to the type of the radical contemplatives, immersed in the transcendent reality of the Kingdom. The Josephites, on the contrary, understood the Church as almost a social institution, as the feat of public service. The Josephites were otherwise called the “zealots.” It is clear that in this dispute — especially fierce among the followers of the two saints rather than in the saints themselves — a loss of the strict proportions concerning the mystical structure of the Orthodox Realm shows itself. Two moments which within the full Orthodox doctrine complement one another — contemplation and action, priesthood and warriorhood (taken here more narrowly, within the sphere of ecclesial-monastic economy) — are in this case counterposed to each other; consequently, in both variants there is present a certain illegitimate expansion of the competences of one or the other “caste” position. In the Josephites a tendency plainly shows through toward the excessive socialization of the Church, toward its too-close approximation to the state. In the non-possessors, on the contrary, there is a striving toward complete abstraction from the affairs of the Realm — which threatened the appearance of a special caste of the “pure” and a movement toward theocracy.

The turning point in the history of the Russian Church was the fall of Constantinople. This event had a grandiose significance for the Russian Orthodox consciousness. The taking of the New Rome by the Turks could not fail to signify a profound shaking of the foundations of the eschatological worldview of the whole Orthodox world. In effect, it was the “taking out of the midst of him who restraineth” — the withdrawal of the “katechon.” Consequently, in the Orthodox community the theme of the coming of the antichrist became more than actual.

The Russian Church, however, found itself in a complicated position. On the one hand, Constantinople had fallen, and consequently the “millennial kingdom” had ended. On the other hand, the Russian Sovereign Power continued to exist; it was strong and mighty, kept faith with the Orthodox symphony of powers, and was founded wholly upon the teaching of the Church and Orthodox tradition. Thus appeared the theory of Moscow the Third Rome. From the beginning this conception was called to give a theological and deeply eschatological interpretation to the factual continuation of the Orthodox symphonic Realm after the fall of its traditional center, Tsargrad. In such a perspective Moscow was seen as a certain temporary and pre-apocalyptic bulwark of Orthodoxy, to which it was appointed to delay the coming of the “son of perdition” for yet some term. And at the outset the theory of Moscow the Third Rome was colored in darkly apocalyptic tones — as the affirmation of a factual state of affairs which might last only a very short time, since the fall of the New Rome leaves no great temporal margin for some further, additional cycle of Christian civilization. This flows logically from the identification of the Byzantine Empire with the “millennial kingdom.” When this “kingdom” comes to its end, the devil is once more permitted to persecute humanity — now up to the very Second Coming.232 This is the so-called “little season” (in the expression of the Apocalypse), the “short term” separating the end of the “millennial kingdom” from the moment of the Last Judgment. In this interval Moscow the Third Rome — the whole of Holy Rus' — was conceived by the Russians as a providentially chosen “island of salvation,” as a particular land marked by the Holy Spirit, for which, for a certain time, an exception is made amid the universal apostasy, and which therefore continues to preserve the symphonic order of the genuine and unique Orthodox Realm.

The Third Rome was therefore connected with the third person of the Trinity, the full revelation of whose economy, according to Orthodox theology, must disclose itself in its whole scope only at the very end of history. Holy Rus' was identified, in this perspective, with the State of the Holy Spirit — with the apocalyptic sovereign power endowed with a paradoxical status and marked by chosenness. And the conception of the Third Rome itself carried no triumphal affirmativeness whatever.233 Moscow was understood as a certain gracious exception, as an elect particularity in the total sea of apostasy, as a small delay. Though the mission of the katechon, of “him who restraineth,” was in a certain sense transferred to Holy Rus' and to the Russian Tsar, no stability or long duration was implied by this. More than that: the signs of the nearness of the appearance of the “son of perdition” were plainly sensed by the Orthodox within Rus' herself.

The fall of Constantinople was directly connected, in Rus', with the Union of Florence, concluded by the Greeks with the Catholics in the endeavor to save themselves from the Turkish conquerors. But this did not help, and the capital of Byzantium fell in 1454. The Russians bound this sequence of catastrophes into a single logical chain. The Greeks had departed from the symphonia and from the purity of Orthodoxy by accepting the union with the Catholics — who embodied in themselves the unsymphonic ordering of society and a heretical deviation in the faith. For this they were subjected to the Lord’s chastisement at the hands of the Turks. The bulwark of Orthodoxy, the Second Rome, fell physically in consequence of its spiritual fall. This was understood as the end of the millennial kingdom.

The symbolism of numbers played an enormous role in the question of eschatology. The falling away of Rome from Orthodoxy was accomplished at the turn of the second millennium from the Nativity of Christ. The fall of Constantinople took place approximately five hundred years later; and the year 1500 (more precisely, 1498) signified, by the Orthodox reckoning, the end of the sixth millennium from the Creation of the world — which was interpreted as a highly probable date for the End of the World. The last boundary separating the fallen world from this grandiose event was the Muscovite realm from the second half of the sixteenth century.

But the Muscovite eschatological pause dragged on. At the turn of the seventh millennium from the Creation of the world there occurred events of far smaller scale than the End of the World, but likewise interpreted in an eschatological key. Following fallen Constantinople, certain Orthodox circles of Little Russia also began to incline toward the Latin heresy. In this period the Unia reached its flowering in Western Rus'. Those Christians who kept faith with the Orthodox Church interpreted these changes as the advance of the antichrist upon Rus' herself — as the repetition of the very scenario that had only recently destroyed the Greeks. Thus arises an extensive eschatological Russian-Orthodox literature in which the Uniate tendencies are considered in an apocalyptic key (the famous Book of Faith of Zakhariia Kopystensky; the apocryphal composition The Eagle, ascribed to Esdras; the Book of Cyril; and others).

In the sixteenth century the teaching of Moscow the Third Rome receives further development and becomes more affirmative. The Moscow council of 1551, called the Stoglav (the Hundred Chapters), definitively affirms the teaching of the superiority of Orthodox Rus' and her ecclesial-social order over other lands, directly connecting the political independence of the Russian State with the keeping of fidelity to all the norms of Orthodoxy. There takes place the real accomplishment in history of what the Kievan metropolitan Hilarion had divined as early as the eleventh century: “the last have become first.” Muscovite Rus' becomes the Third Realm, the New Israel, the last empire, in which alone the whole fullness of the Faith has been preserved. In this same period the Patriarchate is established in Rus', which betokens the fullness and self-sufficiency of the sacred Realm. Though there are other Patriarchs in the Eastern Church (the Patriarch of Constantinople, the Patriarchs of Alexandria, Jerusalem, and Antioch), nowhere are there Orthodox Tsars — that is, the preconditions for a genuine and finished symphony of powers, for the existence of the “katechon,” of “him who restraineth,” are absent. Rus' becomes the sole and last “restrainer.” For this reason she is henceforth named, in the full sense, Holy. Holy Rus'.

The catastrophe of the Third Rome was the book correction of Patriarch Nikon. Nikon himself was formed in the circle of the Lovers of God together with his future opponent, the archpriest Avvakum, and like all the Lovers of God he shared in full measure the eschatological theory of the universal mission of the Russian Church and the Russian Realm. Having become Patriarch, however, he preferred to translate this complex historiosophical doctrine into a secular-optimistic variant, entirely losing sight of the alarming apocalyptic subtext contained in it — for Rus'-the-“katechon” was only a temporary barrier to the coming of the “son of perdition.” Nikon, from entirely patriotic motives, conceived the design of uniting around the Third Rome and the Russian Tsar all the Orthodox lands and peoples then under the yoke either of the Muslims or of the “papists,” so as to spread the saving light of Russian Orthodoxy everywhere and to transform Rus' into a soteriological Empire. Into this eschatological project fitted also the construction of the New Jerusalem outside Moscow — a characteristic sign of all the eschatological and apocalyptic currents in Christianity, from Montanus to the Anabaptists. But in realizing his project Nikon did not choose his means; and in order to remove in advance all friction with the other Orthodox Churches in the matter of their integration under the aegis of the Moscow Patriarchate and the Russian Tsar, he began, willfully and precipitately, to adjust the ritual-symbolic aspects of Russian Orthodox liturgical practice to the modern Greek standards accepted, on the whole, by the majority of the Orthodox Churches beyond the borders of Rus'. In doing so he lost sight of the most important point: the very fact of the might and independence of the Muscovite Realm was connected, by the Russians, precisely with fidelity to the uncorrupted faith, with stubborn resistance to all innovations coming from the West — from which Constantinople had fallen (the Union of Florence). In other words, Nikon resolved to sacrifice, for the sake of the universalization of the Third Rome, that which constituted one of its religious and ritual particularities. This became the point of departure of the book correction. And altogether careless appear the explanations given by Nikon concerning an alleged deviation of the Russian Church from the ancient Greek liturgical norms, concerning the “corruption of the books” and of the “liturgical rituals.” In reality, Rus' after the moment of the fall of Constantinople weakened her contacts with it and froze the situation in the domain of liturgical practice in the state in which it stood in Greece herself at the period of the mid-fifteenth century. And at that moment there was taking place in Byzantium the gradual replacement of the ancient Studite typikon by the typikon of Jerusalem.234 In Rus' this replacement was not completed — though both these typika had been accounted fully Orthodox and acceptable from ancient times. But after two centuries of the relative isolation of Rus', where the process of this transition had congealed, a new comparison with the Greek exemplar disclosed certain differences. It was these that Nikon hastened, without reflection, to correct — without especially entering into the essence of the matter, hurrying to realize his own version of the Third Rome.

His opponents, who formed the core of the Old Believers, saw in the book correction only the confirmation of the apocalyptic alarms that had been gathering in Rus' since the middle of the fifteenth century. From their point of view, the chosenness of the Third Rome was founded precisely on firm adherence to the existing ecclesial tradition, on the literal fulfillment of all the norms of tradition in the small and in the great. The price Nikon proposed to pay for the universalization of the Third Rome seemed to them exorbitant, and contrary to the very essence of the whole enterprise. The partisans of Avvakum judged that the book correction was a subversive, antichrist-inspired initiative, analogous to those which had earlier destroyed Constantinople and drawn the Orthodox of Little Russia into the Unia. The contemporary post-Byzantine Greeks, in the consciousness of the Muscovite traditionalists, could not serve as a criterion of the purity of the faith, since their loss of political independence was connected precisely with their defection toward the Latins. And therefore, if the Orthodox typikon and the liturgical books were to be corrected at all, this ought to be done by the other Orthodox peoples after the Russian exemplar — and not the reverse. If Rus' is the “katechon,” the Old Believers reasoned, then the Orthodox faith has been preserved in her in the best manner, as an example to all the other peoples. But since Nikon set about the reforms and the correction sharply and willfully, the difference in method grew over into the deepest ideological and spiritual conflict. And after a certain time the reforms themselves came to be interpreted by the traditionalists exclusively in a negative, antichrist-ic sense.

The harder Patriarch Nikon insisted on his course, the more radical was the opposition to him.

But in parallel with the dubious (though idealistically justified) “correction,” Nikon violated yet another important condition of the stability of the saving Realm, of the “katechon” — the symphony of powers, the providential and unique combination of temporal dominion and spiritual authority. He began to shift ever further toward a theocratic, almost papo-caesarist rule, imagining himself the autocrat of Rus' and of the whole future Orthodox Empire — which, on the western marches, the Tsar Alexei Mikhailovich, submissive to him for the time being, was diligently building. But since the violation of the symphonia in one direction is always fraught with opposite consequences, the usurpation of secular functions by a representative of the priesthood was not slow to call to life a “revolt of the Kshatriyas.” And in the end it was now the Tsar who rose against the Patriarch — whereupon followed the removal of Nikon from Moscow, and later his deposition.

As a result of these oscillations a real spiritual catastrophe descended upon Rus'. The Third Rome was undermined structurally, ideologically, and mystically. Exactly in the year which the Book of Faith had foretold as the year of “apostasy” (1666), there in fact took place in Moscow a Council unprecedented in its destructive consequences, lasting into 1667 — at which Nikon himself was deposed while his innovations were approved; the royal power was absolutized; the modern Greek liturgical practice was taken as an unimpeachable exemplar (there were introduced, among other things, the obligatory three-finger sign of the cross; the four-pointed Latin cross — the kryzh; baptism by pouring or sprinkling was recognized; priests were directed to process against the sun; the designation of the Holy Spirit as “True” was excluded from the Symbol of Faith; the name Isus came to be written with two “i”s, and so on); and the whole history of Muscovite Rus', the Council of the Hundred Chapters, and the theological conception of “Moscow the Third Rome” were condemned and given over to anathema. And the chief judges in this matter were representatives of the Eastern Patriarchs from abroad, long dwelling under the secular power of heterodox sovereigns. In fact, however, the chief instance was the Tsar himself, concerned only with the strengthening of the throne, who entrusted the theological questions to visiting hierarchs. Possibly he was moved also by the desire to realize Nikon’s external imperial plans — but now in a secular, not a theocratic, variant.

This was the end, in the most genuine sense, of the “katechon,” of Holy Rus', of the Third Rome. From this moment it was no longer possible to speak of the Russian State as fully Orthodox and traditional. The Schism touched all sides of her religious and social being.

The Old Believers turned away from the “Nikonians” definitively, insisting on the literal observance of the Old Faith — for which they were anathematized and subjected to terrible persecutions by the authorities. Many were tortured, executed, or burned alive. All these catastrophic events were interpreted by them as the onset of the last times and the defection of the last bulwark of salvation — Moscow — from her soteriological mission. The spirit of apostasy, which had destroyed first the West, then Byzantium herself and the Little Russians who had gone over to the Unia, had reached, at last, the Holy of Holies. The sanctity of the official Church was violated; grace departed from her; she was turned into the abomination of desolation — so the Old Believers held.

Henceforth the Third Rome itself went into hiding, and Holy Rus' was divided from secular Russia. Whatever the goodness of Nikon’s original intentions, the result of his reforms was catastrophic and apocalyptic in every respect. As a complete confirmation of the tragic rightness of the Old Believers came the reign of Peter the First, who set about accomplishing the final secularization of Russia on an unheard-of scale. Now the Catholic-Protestant West was openly taken as exemplar and model for imitation; the Patriarchate was abolished, and the official Church placed under the rigid control of the secular power after the manner of the Anglican monarchy. More than that — it is in the highest degree symbolic that Peter the First transfers the capital of Russia to the West, away from Moscow,235 thereby setting the last symbolic period to the question of the Third Rome, which from that time was in effect bracketed out — fallen. In its place was enthroned the New Babylon.

Apparatus · Chapters XLVI

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 231–235 are preserved; one source typo («компонентном») is corrected silently. The “little season” is Rev. 20:3 (“he must be loosed a little season” — Slavonic «мало время»), the hinge on which the whole chapter’s temporality turns: if Byzantium was the millennium (Ch. XLV), then everything after 1453 is the devil’s parole. Metropolitan Hilarion’s “the last have become first” compresses the argument of his Sermon on Law and Grace (Kiev, c. 1037–50). The Eagle “ascribed to Esdras” is the eagle vision of 4 Ezra (2 Esdras) 11–12; the Book of Faith (Moscow, 1648) drew on Zakhariia Kopystensky’s Palinodia, though its compilation is usually credited to others — Dugin’s attribution is loose but not baseless. Its year-1666 apostasy computation (1000 + 666) is reported accurately.

Factual corrections. (1) Constantinople fell on 29 May 1453, not “1454” — an error the previous chapter did not make; translated as written per the project’s convention. (2) The end of the seventh millennium from Creation (Byzantine era, AM 7000) fell in 1492, the year the paschal tables ran out and end-of-world expectation peaked; no standard reckoning yields Dugin’s “1500 (more precisely, 1498).” (3) The Stoglav Council of 1551 standardized ritual and church discipline; it did not promulgate a doctrine of Muscovite superiority — though it did canonize precisely the usages (the two-finger sign, sun-wise processions) that Nikon would abolish a century later, an irony that in fact serves Dugin’s narrative better than the claim he makes. (4) Conspicuously absent is the classic documentary source of the doctrine itself: the monk Filofei (Philotheus) of Pskov, whose epistles of the early 1520s contain the canonical formula — “two Romes have fallen, the third stands, and a fourth there shall not be.” Modern scholarship (Ostrowski, Rowland) adds that the Third Rome idea was ecclesiastical-eschatological in its own century and became a doctrine of state only in its nineteenth-century revival — which, ironically, is closer to Dugin’s “darkly apocalyptic, non-triumphal” characterization of the early theory than to his claim of its Stoglav-era official consolidation. (5) The Patriarchate was established in 1589; Peter’s Synodal system (1721) was modeled on Protestant consistories — “after the manner of the Anglican monarchy” is Dugin’s comparison.

The Old Belief. The chapter’s account of the Nikonian reform — Russian usages as frozen archaic Greek practice rather than corruptions, the correction as needless and precipitate, the 1666–67 Council as the true catastrophe — follows the scholarly rehabilitation line of Kapterev and Golubinsky and the émigré synthesis of Zenkovsky, and it is defensible history. But the reader should know it is also confession: Dugin’s own affiliation is with the edinoverie (Old Rite in communion with the Moscow Patriarchate), and the chapter’s spelling Isus, its tenderness toward Avvakum, and its verdict that grace-abandonment talk was the Old Believers’ “tragic rightness” are party positions presented as analysis. The catalogue of 1667 changes (three fingers, kryzh, pouring, counter-sunwise processions, “True” dropped from the Creed, Iisus) is the Old Believer indictment, accurately itemized; the anathemas against the old rites were lifted by the Moscow Patriarchate only in 1971.

Reading note. Structurally this chapter performs a second iteration of the book’s master operation. Chapter XLV closed the millennium at 1453 and made all subsequent history borrowed time; this chapter now runs the same tragedy on Russian soil — the katechon migrates to Moscow, holds for two centuries, and is destroyed not by conquest but from within, by its own Patriarch and Tsar, in 1666–67. Twice fallen, once remaining: the stage is set for the “brief aeon of the Derzhavnaya” (Ch. XLVII), where the third and strangest bearer of the restraining function — an icon — takes up the office the Romanovs vacated.

Part VII · Chapter XLVII

The Brief Aeon of the “Derzhavnaya”

Краткий эон “Державной”

From the point of view of the eschatological history of the Orthodox Church, there exists one rather strange period, which began in Russia in 1917 and lasts to the present time. Together with the overthrow of the Romanov dynasty and the Bolshevik revolution — which on the outer level carried with it the complete negation of Orthodoxy, of its spiritual, dogmatic, and ritual foundations (under the Bolsheviks men were persecuted for the confession of Christ in the most direct and terrible sense of the word) — there took place two events which had enormous significance for the Orthodox consciousness. First, the Patriarchate was restored in Rus'; and second, the capital was transferred once more from Petersburg to Moscow. The two events, from the mystical point of view, are bound together in the closest way — since the appearance of the teaching of Moscow the Third Rome (and the later introduction of the Patriarchate in Rus'), in the one case, and the abolition of the Patriarchate together with the transfer of the capital to Saint Petersburg, in the other, were interconnected in Russian sacred history.

Moscow as the Third Rome is the eschatological continuation of the “millennial kingdom” after the fall of Constantinople, in which, in spite of everything, the symphony of powers was preserved — expressed in the presence of a Russian Patriarch and a Muscovite Tsar. The Petersburg period of Russian history is characterized precisely by the complete absence of “symphonia,” and possesses all the symbolic marks of apostasy. The role of the Church in it was radically other than in the Muscovite period, and her gracious action was accomplished not thanks to the Realm but in spite of the Realm, which had acquired an exclusively secular, “pagan” character.

But the end of the Romanov dynasty (and correspondingly, the end of the Petersburg period) was marked by symbolic signs of an eschatological restoration — of a return to the mystical symbolism of Moscow. This, however, stood in sharp contrast with the atheism and anti-Christian teaching of the communist authorities. To understand this eschatological paradox, one must turn to the eschatological subject of the icon of the “Derzhavnaya Mother of God,” whose miraculous appearance in the Revolution is the metaphysical key to the whole Soviet period of Russia as the last Orthodox world power.

The finding, at Kolomenskoye — the residence of the Russian tsars — of the “Derzhavnaya” icon, in which the Theotokos is depicted seated on a throne with the attributes of royal power in her hands, was accompanied by her miraculous appearance to a simple believing woman, to whom a most important apocalyptic truth was disclosed. — Henceforth, after the martyric end of the last (nominally) Orthodox tsar, power in Russia passes directly to the Mother of God, who takes upon herself the fullness of royal responsibility for Orthodox Rus' until the end of times. In other words, both the transfer of the capital to Moscow and the restoration of the Patriarchate betoken the beginning of a new eschatological period, in which a paradoxical restoration is accomplished — of a state of affairs more normal than the two-hundred-year period of the “Petersburg apostasy” — though this touches not directly the social, but the mystical, spiritual stratum of the ecclesial-national being of Russia.

If the directly secular Bolshevik authorities of Soviet Russia display the complete absence of any sacred legitimacy whatever (veiled, earlier, by the outer attributes of the Romanov system), in the spiritual sense the Russian Church, and with her the whole of mystical Russia, was mysteriously renewed — arose, was reborn — in a particular eschatological aeon under the direct leadership of the Queen of Heaven, who out of mercy (and fulfilling the economy of salvation) became the Queen of Russia.

Of course, such a mystical enthronement of the Most Pure Virgin is by no means a triumphal and victorious return to the “millennial kingdom” — which, according to the Orthodox doctrine, will never again take place. In a certain sense this supplementary aeon of grace is similar to the epoch of Moscow the Third Rome — to a time graciously given to the Russians in defiance of the universal defection. But in our case the paradoxicality and brevity of this economic “exception” are incomparably higher than in the first Muscovite period, and the raging of the beast on the outer level is likewise incomparably more terrible and monstrous.

More than that: the particular initiatic and metaphysical role of the woman, the wife, in Orthodox eschatology236 indicates that the enthronement of the Theotokos over Russia is a sign adjoining still more closely the very point of the End of Times than all other apocalyptic events. The cycle of the Russian Orthodox Realm is characterized by the same symbolic picture as the general logic of the whole of sacred history — for the history of a Christian people repeats, in general outline, the basic key moments of the sacred history of humanity. Thus the first Orthodox sovereign — Vladimir the Fair Sun, Equal of the Apostles — was a man, a male and a human being, like the Adam of paradise. He was the beginning of Russian Orthodox statehood. At the end of this statehood there sits upon the throne the “Wife clothed with the sun,”237 who is more than a human being, and who providentially saves Orthodox Rus', mysteriously raising her toward the uncreated light after the period of the “social fall” — whose peak was the late-Romanov epoch, which ended with the coming of the Bolsheviks.

Taking all these considerations into account, the true meaning of the monarchist restorationist tendencies in contemporary post-Soviet Russia becomes entirely comprehensible — tendencies in which the nostalgic cult of the Romanovs and of the Petersburg period of Russian history is again becoming actual. It is evident that this not only contradicts the sacred economic logic of Orthodox eschatology, but betokens the approach of the very last, briefest stage of the mystical path of Russia — when for one instant the darkness will thicken over her to the final limit, and when there will appear the terminal parody of that which was itself a parody: of the Petersburg Russia of the Romanovs.

If — God forbid — the neo-monarchical projects in Russia are realized, this will be a direct blasphemy against the “Derzhavnaya”; and the outer signs of complete apostasy will be a new transfer of the capital and the abolition of the Patriarchate. God knows whether this will come to pass. But if it comes to pass, it will mean that the Last Judgment has drawn right up to us.

Part VII · Chapter XLVIII

“Thy Kingdom Come”

“Да приидет Царствие Твое”

The Christian tradition unambiguously regards the modern world as the “kingdom of the antichrist.” From the Orthodox point of view, the fall of Constantinople was in fact the “taking of the restrainer out of the midst.” Following it, the Petrine reforms in Russia betokened the destruction of the symphony of powers in the last free and mighty Orthodox sovereign state. In the West this eschatological symphonia never existed in finished form at all; but even from the purely formal point of view, the fall of the Austro-Hungarian Empire at the beginning of the twentieth century put an end to the last Catholic state formation formally possessing the “imperial” quality (though in truncated form). At the same time the Russian Empire, too, finally collapsed.

Christian eschatology binds closely together the following symbolic events:

  1. the cessation of the existence of Christian Statehood founded on the symphony of powers;
  2. the coming of the antichrist and the universal apostasy conjoined with him;
  3. the Second Coming of Jesus Christ, which will follow upon the appearance of the “son of perdition.”

The first two events can be defined in strictly social terms — since the presence or absence of the symphony of powers, that is, of the adequate combination of contemplation and action, of Kingdom and Realm, is directly conjoined with the metaphysical reality of the “reign of the antichrist.” In other words, by the quality of the social aspect of the Christian states and their socio-political ordering, one can judge to which phase of the Christian scenario of the last times this or that historical event belongs. Thus between the metaphysics of the antichrist’s coming and social transformations the tradition establishes a direct connection. One may say that every departure from the Byzantine model of the relation of “spiritual authority” and “temporal power” is an expressive sign of the antichrist’s presence. It is perfectly obvious that in our time, for several centuries now, nothing resembling Byzantinism has existed anywhere in the world; consequently every full Christian can qualify the modern world in no other way than as the “kingdom of the son of perdition.” The fact that such an assessment is not common to all Christians means only that the Church herself has undergone, in the last times, a deep decline. For it is said by the apostle Paul that “he (the ‘man of perdition’ — A.D.) as God sitteth in the temple of God.” In reality, in our world the proportions between Kingdom and Realm are utterly violated. More than that: neither pure contemplation nor pure action exists any longer — or at least, no place whatever is allotted to them in social reality. In the world there reign the laws of materiality, of entropy, of gravity, where all normal relations are inverted. Not only has the priesthood long since been forced out to the social periphery: the very concept of the Realm, even in its immanent aspect, has been discredited by democratic liberal theories, which deny autocracy and sovereign rule from “humanist” motives.

In other words, we live in that “little season” when Satan is once more let loose, restrained no longer by the fetters of the Orthodox Realm. And all the attributes of this “little season” are manifest in social actuality.
