From this the conclusion must be drawn that the normal Christian — who has received the fullness of the Orthodox teaching and thereby inherits also the eschatological aspect of the Church’s social doctrine — must be spiritually hostile to the social medium in which he today finds himself, like the first Christians, who lived under the conditions either of the Judaic theocracy or of the pagan tyranny (often of both at once). Only, the first Christians lived before the onset of the “millennial kingdom,” whereas contemporary Christians live after its end. But the spiritual, social, and worldview nonconformism must in our time be by no means less (if not greater!). Besides the “millennial kingdom” bound up with the “restrainer,” there exists another — this time purely transcendent — doctrine of the Kingdom, which is sometimes called the “coming of the Kingdom of God in power.” What is at issue is the last point of earthly history, when the Heavenly Jerusalem will descend to earth. This will be not simply the restoration of the true proportions between Kingdom and Realm, but the complete absorption by the Kingdom of everything else — the all-encompassing revelation of the transcendent reality, which will transfigure all things in the rays of its glory. The secret of this event consists in this: that the Kingdom, which up to the last moment of the cycle was accessible only to spiritual contemplation, will appear as a Realm — that is, as a concrete, physically palpable reality; and this eschatological concreteness is closely conjoined with the “resurrection in the flesh.” The dualism between contemplation and action will vanish: the objects of contemplation will become actual, and actions will be transfigured into noetic realities. “The hidden shall become manifest.” And the Church as the affair of the priesthood, and the Realm as the affair of the warriors, will stand forth in their heavenly unity. “And I saw no temple therein (in the Heavenly Jerusalem — A.D.),” prophesies John the Theologian in the Apocalypse, XXI:22. Just as there is in it no social hierarchy. “He that overcometh shall inherit all things (emphasis ours — A.D.); and I will be his God, and he shall be my son” — Apocalypse 21:7. The phrase “he that overcometh shall inherit all things” means that he who enters the New Jerusalem becomes a fully empowered co-heir of God the Trinity, a son of God, requiring no further mediator for communion with the Godhead — no King.

The Kingdom of God in power will be a transcendent event — and, moreover, an event which will immediately and at once put an end to the reign of the antichrist. It is important to underscore this suddenness and instantaneousness of the Second (Dread) Coming of Jesus Christ. Of this the Christian texts speak clearly more than once: “suddenly the Judge shall come”; “behold, I come as a thief”; “the Son of Man cometh as the lightning from one edge of heaven to the other”; and so on. What is at issue is that the coming of the Kingdom of God will take place not gradually and successively, in the course of some evolution, but sharply and suddenly.

More than that: this suddenness will have a terrifying, destructive character — for in annihilating the evil of the world, Satan, and death, the Judge will turn his dread side also toward all those who, voluntarily or through weakness, were caught up in the stream of apostasy. And since this apostasy, as we have seen, has a direct social expression — and, more than that, is practically identified with the social system that replaced the Orthodox Realm with its reigning symphony of powers — the Second Coming will be the explosion of the politico-state order of the whole modern world, the absolute Revolution, the dread retribution upon all those who accepted the mark of the beast and recognized without resistance the legitimacy of the presently ruling system.

From this a definite conclusion may be drawn: in the period of universal defection, after the fall of the Orthodox Realm, the Christian must stand in radical opposition to the social reality recognized as the regime of the antichrist. Any positive evolution whatever is thereby excluded in advance, since in the sacred apocalyptic scenario of the tradition no “progress” of any kind is mentioned.238 The Christian’s opposition to contemporary social reality must bear an absolute character — for any compromise with the prince of this world and the spirit of this world is ruinous for the soul and offensive to the Lord Jesus Christ, who suffered for men, who are simply obliged to answer Him with at least the same — that is, to suffer for His name. And only after a determinate term of time, filled with desperate and uncompromising spiritual struggle, accompanied by the outward triumph of the “son of perdition,” will the Kingdom of God come — as the confirmation of the glory of the faithful, and a dread execution for all who have given themselves over to the devil.

Apparatus · Chapters XLVII–XLVIII

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 236–238 are preserved. “He… as God sitteth in the temple of God” is 2 Thess. 2:4; the “little season,” Rev. 20:3; the “Wife clothed with the sun,” Rev. 12:1; “behold, I come as a thief,” Rev. 16:15; the lightning saying, Luke 17:24 (par. Matt. 24:27); “And I saw no temple therein,” Rev. 21:22; “He that overcometh shall inherit all things,” Rev. 21:7, with Dugin’s emphasis preserved. One item in his chain of “Christian texts,” however, is not Scripture: “suddenly the Judge shall come” («внезапу Судия приидет») is liturgical — a troparion of the midnight office and the panikhida — cited here without distinction alongside the Apocalypse, as with the Dogmatikon in Chapter XII. Throughout both chapters Dugin writes the Savior’s name in the Old Rite spelling Isus (one “i”), the very spelling whose abolition the 1667 Council decreed: the orthography itself is a confessional act, invisible in English but recorded here.

The Derzhavnaya. The historical core is accurately reported: the icon — an eighteenth-century image of the enthroned Theotokos with crown, scepter, and orb — was found in the crypt of the Ascension church at Kolomenskoye on 2 March 1917 (O.S.), after the dream-visions of the peasant woman Evdokia Adrianova; that was the day of Nicholas II’s abdication, and the synchrony became the seed of the devotion. The theologoumenon Dugin reports — the Theotokos assuming the regalia and the royal responsibility for Russia — is the standard monarchist reading, cherished above all in the emigration. What is not standard is what he does with it, and the inversion should be measured precisely: in the received monarchist doctrine the Derzhavnaya holds the power in trust until the restoration of the Tsar — the devotion is restorationist by construction. Dugin reverses the term of the regency (“until the end of times”), declares the Soviet period the Marian aeon and Soviet Russia “the last Orthodox world power” in mystical essence, dismisses Nicholas II as only “(nominally)” Orthodox — the Old Believer verdict on the whole post-Petrine dynasty — and pronounces any Romanov restoration “a direct blasphemy against the Derzhavnaya,” a terminal parody of a parody. This is the National Bolshevism of Dugin’s 1996 political engagement translated into Mariology: the émigré devotion is expropriated and turned against the émigrés. The chapter is thereby the book’s clearest seam between theology and program — and the ancestor of his later “sacred Soviet” constructions.

Historical details. The Patriarchate was restored by the All-Russian Local Council with the election of Tikhon on 5/18 November 1917; the capital returned to Moscow in March 1918 — both facts correct, and the pairing with their Petrine negations (Synod 1721, Petersburg 1712) is genuinely elegant. The Austro-Hungarian Empire (1867–1918) can be called “the last Catholic state formation formally possessing the imperial quality” only loosely — the Holy Roman Empire, the actual bearer of the sacral-imperial idea in the West, ended in 1806, and the Dual Monarchy claimed no such sacrality; Dugin’s “though in truncated form” quietly concedes this. Vladimir “the Fair Sun” (Красно Солнышко) is the epic epithet of the bylina cycle, not a liturgical title; the Adam–to–Woman-clothed-with-the-sun arc spanning Russian statehood from Vladimir to the Derzhavnaya is Dugin’s own construction — one of the book’s most striking — with no source in the tradition.

Reading note. Part VII ends by politicizing the Parousia itself: the Second Coming as “the absolute Revolution,” dread retribution on all who “recognized the legitimacy of the presently ruling system.” The eschatological frame is patristic in its elements (suddenness, judgment, the loosed adversary); the application — mandatory radical nonconformism toward the post-Soviet order, salvation aligned with political refusal — is the idiom of the National Bolshevik Party in the year of the book’s publication. The three bearers of the restraining function across the Part — Emperor, Tsar, Icon — form a descending series in visibility and an ascending one in paradox; and the Part’s last word converts waiting into militancy. With this the political theology of the book is complete: what remains (Part VIII) is cosmic eschatology proper.

Part VIII · Christianity and Eschatology

Part VIII · Chapter XLIX

First Times — Last Times

Первые Времена — Последние Времена

The Christian tradition, like every genuine tradition, not only possesses a developed and complete eschatological teaching — a theory of the End of Times — but is in itself eschatological through and through, since the question of the End of Times has central significance within it. Christ comes into the world not at an arbitrary moment of cosmic history, but precisely in the last times, immediately before the End of the World — “in the last days.” This means that the whole of Christian metaphysics, founded on the Good News of the Incarnation and the Resurrection, becomes manifest, opened, only in eschatological time. Such eschatologism constitutes the essence of the Christian message as a particular tradition without analogues. All this compels us to investigate more attentively the theory of the End of Times as the metaphysical tradition as a whole understands it — which will permit a clearer picture of the specificity of the properly Orthodox approach to this theme.

Every full tradition asserts that the history of the universe is a history of decline, of degradation. Both manifestation and (with still greater reason) creation presuppose the primordial estrangement of the principle from itself, its self-diminution, its entry into a paradoxical reality founded on “insufficiency,” incompleteness, deficiency, and so on — which is the direct opposite of the defining quality of Divinity (self-sufficiency, fullness, perfection). Thus the manifested, from the very first moment of its existence, has a twofold meaning: on the one hand it bears upon itself the imprint of the divine fullness from which it proceeded; on the other, it is saturated with the taste of estrangement and insufficiency, of negative otherness in relation to the Godhead. In the sphere of the eternal angelic powers, in heaven, both aspects exist synchronously and motionlessly, fixing the archetypal limits of the universe’s resemblance and difference in relation to its source. (This bears also on the problem of the “choice of the angels” — only at another level.)

In the worlds of the lower waters, where — unlike heaven — duration and time exist, the complex of eternal paradise and eternal hell, synchronous in the upper waters, acquires another, dynamic, sequential mode of existence: it becomes a process, the meaning and content of duration. One may say that the history of the universe, in its dynamic, sequential aspect, is a movement from paradise to hell — from the state of the manifested’s maximal resemblance to the principle to the state of their maximal difference. Such a vision is imprinted in all the sacred doctrines: the idea of the four ages in Hesiod (gold, silver, bronze, and iron); of the four yugas among the Hindus (Krita-yuga, Treta-yuga, Dvapara-yuga, and Kali-yuga); of paradise, or pardes, in the Iranian and Abrahamic traditions, and so on. This means that sacred history moves from the state of fullness to the state of poverty — from the state of perfection and nearness to God to the state of God-forsakenness and degeneration. Thus time has two limits, a beginning and an end, where the beginning signifies fullness and the end, poverty. And all of time is simultaneously contained in the eternity of the heavens, whence it flows forth and whither it returns. But if it proceeds from one aspect of the heavens (the “good”), it returns to the other (the “evil”); which is why the Christian tradition speaks of the beginning of times as the good paradise, and of the end as the Terrible Judgment. Let us note especially the word “terrible,” which has the metaphysical sense of the negative relation of the first cause to the consequence — of the source of the universe to the universe itself.239 One may say that between cause and consequence, from the metaphysical point of view (as distinct from formal logic), the following relation exists. The cause (the principle of manifestation, the Creator) at first comes forward as the positive sufficient ground of the consequence (the universe, creation). The term “at first” may bear the sense of logical precedence (relative to the celestial reality of the upper waters) or of temporal precedence (relative to the reality of the lower waters). In the intermediate state, the positive aspect of the consequence — consisting in the fact that it is not simply a consequence but the consequence of the cause — is combined with the negative aspect of the consequence, consisting in the fact that it is, all the same, only a consequence and not the cause. And finally, at the end of the cycle, the consequence — having identified itself wholly with its limitative side, that is, having become only a consequence in itself and nothing more — encounters the cause once again, but now in a third way: as the sudden revelation of the fullness of that principle which lay at the foundation of the consequence, but resemblance to which the consequence has gradually lost.

Thus the cause is related to the consequence in a threefold manner:

  1. as generating source;
  2. as an abiding, permanent bond; and
  3. as fullness, “terribly” negating the pretensions of incompleteness to completeness.

Here a comparison suggests itself with the Hindu conception of the trimurti — the three “gods” Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva, who correspond strictly to these three modes of the relation of cause to consequence (Brahma creates the world, Vishnu preserves it, Shiva destroys it).

In the Christian perspective, to these three phases correspond three periods of sacred history:

  1. the creation and the paradisal state of Adam before the fall;
  2. the Old Testament period of the “law”;
  3. the Terrible Judgment.

It is not by chance that the first book of the Christian Bible is the Book of Genesis (or of creation), and the last is the Apocalypse — the Revelation of the End of Times and the Terrible Judgment.

Formally, Christianity affirms the very same logic of the metaphysical understanding of time and cycle as all the other authentic traditions. It inherits from the Torah of Moses the idea of paradise, and from the prophets the idea of the Terrible Judgment. But the specificity of the Christian perspective consists in an entirely different understanding of the proportions of the cycle, of the meaning of time; and it is precisely this specificity that makes this tradition atypical, paradoxical, standing out sharply against the background of the remaining traditions.

Part VIII · Chapter L

Pistis Sophia
(Third Excursus into the Gnostic Doctrines)

Пистис София (третий экскурс в гностические доктрины)

Christianity is founded on a central and absolute metaphysical fact — on the Incarnation, the life, and the Resurrection of the Son of God, Jesus Christ. It is Christ and his “descent from the heavens,” his crucifixion and his “trampling down death by death,” that are the axis of Christian metaphysics and of the Christian cult. No “golden” paradisal periods of history possess for the Christian such absolute significance as those 33 years which the Savior spent among men. But the Christian tradition itself does not deny that this period adjoins, immediately, the instant of the End of Times — for Christ comes “in the last days,” before the End of Times. Consequently, for Christians it is precisely this period that is endowed with maximal significance, singled out from all others, filled with a metaphysical meaning surpassing the remaining stages of history. Here, as in other questions, Christianity rests on a fundamental paradox: the End of Times, the apocalyptic twilight of the world, the time of the “abomination of desolation,” the “dark age,” the epoch of degeneration and degradation, becomes the moment at which the supreme transcendent Godhead is incarnated in the lowest of the worlds — in human material reality. And more than that: not only is He incarnated, but He passes through the whole abyss of dread trials — poverty, suffering, tortures, humiliation, betrayal, death.

Thus, reflecting on the Christian understanding of cyclical history, we at once encounter a point of view contrasting sharply with the eschatologies of other traditions. What those traditions describe with a negative sign, as the period most negative from the spiritual point of view (the Kali-yuga), is on the contrary regarded by the Christian tradition as the unique ontological conditions chosen for the accomplishment of the highest metaphysical mystery — by comparison with which the creation of the universe itself seems something secondary and of small significance. The clear consciousness of this paradoxicality moved certain Christian theologians to advance the theory of the “blessed sin,” the felix culpa, according to which the fall of Adam was a metaphysically positive phenomenon — since it was precisely the fall, and the decline that followed it, that “provoked,” in a certain sense, the Divine compassion to the Incarnation of the Savior, which opened to humanity not simply the possibility of a return to paradise, but something incomparably more important and precious from the metaphysical point of view. Here it is easy to see echoes of that Ophite gnosis which insisted on the “Luciferic” interpretation of the subject of the fall.240

This paradoxical specificity of the Christian tradition was actively pondered by the various gnostic schools, who gave themselves a lively account of the impossibility of directly combining the Christian perspective with the general logic of the traditional doctrine of cycles. This showed itself most vividly in the doctrine of the “evil demiurge,” where the End of Times of the demiurgic cycle was regarded as a wholly positive phenomenon, and the coming of Christ coincided with the beginning of a new uncreated era — the era of grace. Still more deeply was this eschatological problematic examined by the gnostics connected with the circles that shared the teaching of the “Pistis Sophia.” Let us set out in general strokes the gnostic theory of the “Pistis Sophia,” since in it — in mythologized and heterodox form — there show through astonishingly deep surmises concerning the metaphysical essence of orthodox Christian eschatology.

The “Pistis Sophia” asserts that the chief metaphysical principle is the “Inexpressible,” arretos, the absolute. He has three levels — the self-identical Absolute (the Father), the First Mystery (the Son), and the Country of Light (the Holy Spirit). And each of the named realities encloses in itself whole conglomerates of transcendent worlds. In the process of the pre-eternal and uncreated radiation of the “Light of Lights” — by which is understood the totality of the aspects of the absolute — there occurred the first catastrophe. One of the luminous essences (one of the three tri-unities), called “Authades,” that is, the “insolent,” the “arrogant,” resolved to detain the light within himself and thereby to receive power over the remaining luminous essences, which duly continued to let this light pass through themselves. Thus Authades created the “thirteenth aeon” — the central archetype of the created world, consisting of twelve angelic pairs. Then around the “thirteenth aeon” arose twelve other aeons — the periphery — and further the whole fullness of the secondary worlds, down to matter and the outer limit of the universe, the “dragon of the outer darkness.” Over all this Authades received unlimited power, and compelled all the aeons and all beings to submit to him alone. But at a determinate moment the feminine essence of one of the angelic pairs — Pistis Sophia, that is, “Faith-Wisdom” — “noticed” the transcendent light on the far side of Authades, and divined the usurpation on which creation is founded. More than that: the transcendent origin of the whole universe and of the beings inhabiting it became evident to her, as did the falsity of Authades’ pretensions to primacy. Pistis Sophia began to “pray to the Light of Lights” — and paid cruelly for it, since Authades began to persecute her, and by cunning, perfidy, and brute force cast her down out of the “thirteenth aeon” and out of the whole aeonic pleroma, plunging her into the abyss of demonic material existence.

Pistis Sophia suffers thus until the coming of the transcendent Savior, Christ, the First Mystery. And it is underscored that Christ opens the basic secrets of his metaphysical message to the disciples only after the Resurrection, whereas during his life he speaks only in parables and hints. (This helps one understand the central role of the epistles of the apostle Paul in gnostic circles — since Paul met the Savior only after his Resurrection, and was therefore symbolically initiated into the most closed aspects of Christian metaphysics.) The First Mystery lightens the sufferings of Pistis Sophia and explains to her the structure of the universe, and of the transcendent Country of Light. And the Savior says that the whole drama — including the usurpation of Authades and the fall of Pistis Sophia — was conceived by the highest principle for the sake of a particular event, the “accomplishment of all accomplishments”; and the whole history of manifestation and creation serves only as a drawn-out prologue to this event.

This event bears, in the text of the “Pistis Sophia,” an unambiguously eschatological sense, and signifies a radical, instantaneous, “revolutionary” change of the whole metaphysical and ontological hierarchy — one which must shake the foundations of the Absolute itself. The coming of Christ to Pistis Sophia is the announcement of the nearness of this event, but not yet its arrival. Before it becomes fact, a determinate time must pass — a particular supplementary aeon. Only at the completion of this aeon will that take place for whose sake all the preceding existed. The meaning of the “accomplishment of all accomplishments”241 is given in the text of the “Pistis Sophia” in rather enigmatic terms; it is clear only that what is at issue is a metaphysical event of a scale incommensurable with the ordinary laws of the sacred cycles as traditional eschatology understands them.

In this gnostic scenario there is plainly disclosed the particularity of the whole Christian doctrine, though the idea is expressed in terms characteristic only of the gnostics. The point is that, from the purely Christian point of view, the End of Times is metaphysically far more important than the Beginning of Times, and the iron age carries in itself something greater than the golden age. And the paradoxicality of such a perspective by no means denies the cosmological evidence of the superiority of the paradisal state of the beginning over the hellish state of the end. Hell does not become paradise, and poverty is not perceived as fullness. The essence of this Christian eschatology is that it acknowledges the objective proportions of the universe, and underscores the superiority of Pistis Sophia’s primordial status in the “thirteenth aeon” over her fallen state. But upon this objective and strict constatation there is superimposed another, transcendent system of coordinates, which instantly explodes the whole logic of the non-Christian approach to the cyclical theme.

What is at issue is that same kenotically oriented metaphysics whose basic features we brought to light in the preceding parts. Sacrifice, self-diminution, kenosis, the conscious choice of the lesser of two possibilities predetermine the whole order of the Christian world-view, and tell, in particular, on the Christian interpretation of eschatology. It is precisely fallen humanity, living near the End of Times — and by no means the best representatives of that humanity: fishermen, publicans, harlots — who become the chosen vessels for the particular, highest metaphysical Revelation, for the apostolic ministry of the Good News, which surpasses in its content the spiritual fullness of the heavens themselves. The Son comes to the fallen and the insignificant, diminishing himself and descending from the heavens Himself. And He carries with him the Good News of the nearness of the “accomplishment of all accomplishments” — of the paradoxical metaphysical event surpassing in its significance both the Con-creation and the Dis-solution of the world. To the full sacred logic of the cyclical understanding of the universe’s history there is added a particular dimension: the consequence (not in itself, but in its non-evident, secret aspect) becomes here, in a certain sense, more principal than the cause, and poverty more significant than fullness, than the “pleroma.”

Most vividly of all, this idea is demonstrated by the Gospel parable of the prodigal son.

Thus, in the excessiveness of the gnostic interpretation, we encounter the laying bare of an essential, orthodox Christian truth — “blessed are they that mourn: for they shall be comforted.” One may paraphrase it thus: “blessed are the fallen, for they shall be raised.”

Apparatus · Chapters XLIX–L

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 239–241 are preserved; their continuity (Chapter XLVIII closed Part VII at 238) confirms that Chapter XLIX, despite its abrupt pivot-ending, is complete in the source. “In the last days”: 1 Pet. 1:20 and Heb. 1:2 stand behind the Slavonic «в последняя». “Abomination of desolation,” Matt. 24:15 after Daniel. “Trampling down death by death” is the Paschal troparion. The Hesiodic four ages and the four yugas are accurately reported; the trimurti create-preserve-destroy schema is the popular systematization and serves Dugin’s purpose fairly. The cause-consequence triad, and the metaphysical gloss on “Terrible” (страшный) in the Judgment’s Russian name as the source’s negative relation to its own product, are his constructions — tidy ones. “Blessed are they that mourn,” Matt. 5:4; the prodigal son, Luke 15. The closing beatitude — “blessed are the fallen, for they shall be raised” — is not Scripture and is not presented as such: it is Dugin’s own coinage, flagged by him as paraphrase, and the apparatus returns to it below. The Old Rite Isus continues.

The gnostic text and Dugin’s retelling. The “Pistis Sophia” is a real book — the Coptic gnostic treatise of the Askew Codex (3rd–4th century), framed as the risen Jesus’ instruction of the disciples. Dugin’s summary is, in its main lines, faithful: the Ineffable (arretos); Authades the “self-willed” whose arrogance precipitates the drama; Sophia of the thirteenth aeon who sees the higher Light, prays toward it, and is cast down into chaos by deceit; her repentances and her raising by the Savior; and — the detail on which Dugin’s whole use of the text turns — the doctrine that Jesus imparts the true mysteries only after the Resurrection, which is indeed the treatise’s framing conceit. The deviations should be recorded: the mapping of the Ineffable’s levels onto Father, Son, and Spirit is Dugin’s trinitarian systematization, not the text’s; Authades does not “create” the thirteenth aeon in the treatise (the aeons are emanations; Authades is the usurper within their order); and Paul appears nowhere in the “Pistis Sophia” — the parenthesis deriving Paul’s esoteric primacy from his post-Resurrection encounter is Dugin’s own insert, splicing Chapter XL’s thesis into the gnostic frame. The felix culpa requires a firmer correction: the “blessed sin” is native, mainstream Latin theology — the Exsultet of the Easter vigil sings it, Augustine reasons it, Aquinas cites it — and Dugin’s suggestion that it echoes Ophite “Luciferic” gnosis is tendentious genealogy: structural resemblance is the chapter’s legitimate subject, but no filiation from the Ophites to the Latin liturgy is documented. Confidence: high on the provenance of the felix culpa; the resemblance-claim itself is interpretation, not error.

Where the chapter exceeds Orthodoxy. Two formulations pass beyond what any Orthodox eschatology asserts, and both should be seen, because they are the book’s deepest commitments surfacing. First: the eschatological event “must shake the foundations of the Absolute itself.” No conciliar or patristic eschatology holds that the eschaton alters God; the immutability of the divine nature is dogma. Dugin reports this as the gnostic text’s idea, but his framing (“astonishingly deep surmises concerning the metaphysical essence of orthodox Christian eschatology”) adopts it — and it is of a piece with Chapter XLVIII’s Parousia as “absolute Revolution” and Chapter XXIX’s initiate who co-enacts the Judgment. Second: the closing beatitude. “Blessed are they that mourn” blesses the afflicted; “blessed are the fallen” blesses the fall. The paraphrase is one modal step from the Luciferian rehabilitation the chapter officially refuses — the same razor’s edge the edition has tracked since Chapter XIV, where Lucifer’s partial truth was conceded and his throne reassigned. Dugin walks the edge knowingly: hell does not become paradise, he insists, poverty is not fullness — and then coins a beatitude for the fallen. The sentence is the whole book in miniature.

Reading note. The third gnostic excursus completes the series begun with the “evil demiurge” (XI) and Barbelo (XV), and the method is now fully visible as method: at each of the book’s load-bearing joints — creation, the Virgin, the End — Dugin brings on a gnostic myth to state “most truly” the problem, then claims Orthodoxy answers what the gnostics could only pose. Part VIII’s opening pair establishes the frame the remaining chapters will fill: the End outranks the Beginning; the effect, in its secret aspect, outranks its cause; the darkest hour is the chosen hour. What follows — “time no longer,” the Eighth Day, kenosis and eschatology — will convert this inversion into the book’s final doctrine.

Part VIII · Chapter LI

“And There Shall Be Time No Longer”

“И времени больше не будет”

Traditionalists of the Guénonian orientation often fall into perplexity when they encounter Christian eschatology — where, in distinction from all the other traditional eschatologies (and first of all from the Hindu), the Terrible Judgment and the End of Times are not followed by the beginning of a new cycle. This singularity of Christianity moves some to speak of the incompleteness of the Christian doctrine, or of its loss of important elements; others attempt to interpret the Gospel words about the “new earth and new heavens” in a cyclological spirit — as an indication of a new aeon following upon the end of the present humanity. In reality the matter stands otherwise; and Christianity in this case, as indeed in all the rest, requires a metaphysical interpretation having no analogues in the other traditions.

Christianity, unlike Hinduism, knows neither of the beginning of a new “golden age” after the end of the iron one, nor of the “mahapralaya” (the final dissolution of the universe); but it affirms, at the end of our actual cycle, such a metaphysical event as the Terrible Judgment — as a result of which the universe is not annihilated to the end (as in the mahapralaya), yet neither does it enter into a new paradisal state (into the Krita-yuga of a following cycle). According to the Christian apocalyptic vision, reality is transfigured in the Second, Terrible Coming of Jesus Christ, the Savior-in-Strength, in such a way that it passes out beyond the limits of time and cyclical history — but does not disappear finally.

The new heavens and the new earth of Christianity are not the paradisal conditions of a coming cycle, but a particular timeless reality standing on the far side both of presence and of absence. They are called “new” in a specifically Christian, initiatic sense — they are, as it were, uncreated and infinite, deified in the rays of the Glory of the Lord, in the radiance of the divine energies of the pre-eternal Trinity. The whole of creation, seized by the “accomplishment of all accomplishments,” is drawn at the End of Times into a particular paradoxical process called the Judgment — whose verdict, in each concrete case, is conjoined with a transcendent and non-evident truth, with the kenotic pre-eternal plan conceived by the Absolute in its sacrificial Trinitarian project, and not with the rectilinear and strict logic of the cosmic hierarchy of the manifested levels of being (as in conventional metaphysics). The meaning of the Incarnation is so metaphysically unique that its fact overturns the whole structure of the traditional cyclical vision of reality; and henceforth “neither Jews nor Hellenes” can contain the eschatological paradox of the Christian News.

The eschatology of Christianity is centered on the idea of the revelation, at the very lowest point of the universe’s cycle, of the very highest transcendent reality; and therefore, after such a revelation, the course of reality no longer submits to the general ontological and created laws. The grace of the presence of God at the center of the universe, disclosed at the point of cosmic midnight, explodes the normal sacred proportions, annuls all that would follow, and compels a radical review of all that went before. The Christian reality of the Church falls out of the universe, drawing into its metaphysical whirlpool the hitherto clear and strict logic of the law, which instantly loses its warrant and its efficacy. The “age to come” and “the life of the age to come,” in Orthodox terminology, relate not simply to the life of the soul after the death of the body, but to the reality which will begin after the Terrible Judgment — and which will have no common measures either with time or with other forms of duration, and, more than that, will be transcendent in relation to eternity itself. In the “age to come” “there shall be time no longer,” as there shall be no death. There will be neither beginning nor end, nor boundary between existence and non-existence, between “is” and “is not.” Created reality will be “drawn into” the Uncreated — yet it will not itself turn into the uncreated, but will preserve its former quality, acquiring simultaneously a new one.

Part VIII · Chapter LII

The Eighth Day

День Восьмой

Saint Gregory Palamas, in his “Homilies,”242 described more clearly than others the specificity of Christian eschatology, showing its singularity and uniqueness. In the homily devoted to commentaries on the Flowery Triodion he expounds the mystery of the “Day of the Lord” (as the day of resurrection was called among the Greeks and Romans). From the metaphysical point of view his interpretation of the symbolism of the week, of the six days of creation, and of the Old Testament Sabbath is very important.

St. Gregory begins with the elucidation of the metaphysics of the Sabbath as that Day on which God rested from the labors of the preceding six Days. From Palamas’ point of view, the rest of God signifies his turning from centrifugal action (the six preceding Days of creation), characteristic of his creating state, to centripetal action — when God turns to the self-identity of his unchanging and uncreated nature. Thus the Seventh Day, the Old Testament Sabbath, symbolizes the transcendent, inner aspect of the Godhead, whereas the preceding Days represent the unfolding of the divine creating actions outward, into the sphere of the immanent. The six days of the week are labor; the seventh is repose. The term “repose” (from the word for “repose” is formed the word “reposed,” that is, “fell asleep,” and also “died”), “rest,” has an initiatic sense and signifies the “central state,” free from the dynamics of the ontological periphery. “Repose” is metaphysically correlated with the terms “peace,” “tranquility,” and also “silence” (whence the series of initiatic synonyms beloved of the “hesychasts,” that is, “those abiding in stillness”). What is at issue is the self-identical and constant state of the transcendent aspects of the Godhead — the “divine darkness” (in the expression of Dionysius the Areopagite), in which all the kinds of duality characteristic of the spheres of the manifested are absent in principle: beginning with the first duality of object and subject, of earth and heaven, of hearer and heard, of perceiver and perceived, and ending with the duality of the sexes, and so on.

In the terms of classical esotericism, the eschatology embodied in the Old Testament tradition in the symbol of the Sabbath is “return” — the turning toward the principle, toward its non-manifestation and immutability; the passage, after the exhaustion of creation (manifestation) and its possibilities, back to the single metaphysical source.

The mystery of the Sabbath as the “return” of the divine selfhood (or to the divine selfhood) is divided, in Palamas, as it were into two parts.

The Sabbath is seen to have two metaphysical limits: one from the side of Friday, the Sixth Day; the other from the side of the mysterious Eighth Day, “non-existent” in the ordinary weekly count. From the side of Friday — that is, of the full activation of principial creation through man and the cycle of his history — the Sabbath is the boundary between the extreme degeneration of creation, its utmost decrepitude (this is the quality of “Friday evening”), and the detachment of the Godhead “reposing in its selfhood,” which “permits” the decrepitude of the end of the Sixth Day, acting as it were from the side of the Sabbath. Thus God’s turning to himself, his “repose,” his withdrawal from “works,” is simultaneously the dooming of creation to a certain God-forsakenness. It is precisely this aspect that correlates most closely with Judaic eschatology, which speaks not of return, but only of the “saving” messianic understanding of the inner “positivity” of God’s withdrawal into Himself away from the world. In this consists the specificity of the Judaic theology of the Shabbat, or the creationist understanding of the teaching of the “thousand-year kingdom,” “chiliasm.” “Repose,” for the Jew, is not the “return” of the creature to the Creator, but something opposite — the final detachment of the Creator from the creature. On the Sabbath, when “God rests,” humanity dies, is “laid to rest,” becomes “reposed.” For the Jews the whole Sabbath is such, and in this consists its deepest meaning. It is the return of God to Himself, and not the return to God of what he created.243

For the Christian consciousness of Palamas, such a Judaic eschatology — accepted on the whole — constitutes, however, only the tragic prelude to the most important event: the coming of the Son of God. This coming has nothing in common with the Judaic theory of the mashiach, who among the Jews opens the Shabbat, the Sabbath, rather than closing it — and who, moreover, is in general only a “sublunar spirit,” in no way consubstantial with the transcendent Yahweh. Jesus Christ comes at the end of the Seventh Day, at the completion of the Sabbath, when the non-participation of God in the world has set that world on the brink of non-existence. Christ comes in the last hours of the Sabbath; he descends to the earth and goes down into hell — where, in the words of Palamas, far more souls await him than lived on earth together with him, or would be born after. The potential transfiguration of human (and through it, of cosmic) nature is accomplished by the Son of God at the limit of the Sabbath opposite to its boundary with Friday; and here what is at issue is no longer the Judaic “return” of God to Himself, but the “Hellenic,” manifestationist doctrine of the fusion of the world with the principle — though not by nature (as among the Vedantists and Platonists), but by grace, as a result of the initiatic possibility brought into the world by the voluntary, free, redemptive, deifying sacrifice of Christ. This is the second part of the Sabbath, immediately adjoining Sunday — the mysterious Eighth Day.

In the Eighth Day what is at issue is no longer either the “Hellenic” or the “Judaic” eschatology, whose synthesis, in Palamas, is the conception of the Seventh Day, the Sabbath. Palamas insists that the Resurrection Day of the week is not the Seventh Day, not a day of rest, of repose. It is something greater — something more mysterious, more paradoxical and transcendent than simply the Judaic “chiliastic” doctrine transferred into a Christian context, or the Hellenic “apokatastasis” of the mysteries. The Christian Sunday is not the Seventh Day: it does not belong to the Week at all — neither the potential nor the concrete, neither the temporal nor the archetypal. It fits into no norms, falls out of all rules, violates the whole of metaphysical logic. Sunday, the Day of the Lord, is not something that takes place by force of metaphysical necessity, as a conditioned and inevitable part of the general plan. If all the remaining Days of the divine Week flow with inevitability from God’s first design concerning the creation of the world out of nothing — and the Seventh Day, the Old Testament Sabbath, is no exception in this (God, from creating — action outward — must of necessity pass over to “repose,” “action” inward) — then Sunday comes not by necessity, but by the free Love of the Lord, by his grace, by his kenotic sacrificial Will, explicable by nothing and conditioned by nothing.

On Sunday the potential transfiguration, renewal, spiritualization, new birth of the world, accomplished by Christ — incarnate, suffering, and descended into hell on the Sabbath — is realized in all its metaphysical fullness. And the “renewal” of the Resurrection is not a cyclical improvement, but the radical and absolute transfiguration of the inner quality of reality. On Sunday, on the Eighth Day, coinciding with the Second, Terrible Coming of Jesus Christ in strength, created, dead, decrepit being does not simply return to its primordial state, but is transposed above it, nearer to God — so that even the angels envy the lot of men, who have received the right to co-participation in the luminous and unending being of the Most Holy Trinity.

The concept of the “Eighth Day” is of the greatest importance for Christian eschatology. This Day does not, as it were, exist in the normal division of cycles; it is foreshadowed, in the words of Palamas, only in the “Feast of Trumpets” and the Jubilee Year. Palamas says:

“The veneration of this Eighth Day — that is, of the Lord’s Day — Moses too introduced covertly; for the Jubilee Year, which was decreed by him and named the ‘Year of Remission,’ did not belong to the number of the years reckoned by the lawful sevens, but came after them all, and was the eighth.”

And further: “…the all-glorious and sacred dignity of the Lord’s Day, which is to come when all the Old Testament shall have passed away.” In principle it “coincides” with the First Day (the Day of creation); but on the other hand it is mysteriously identical also with the Sabbath (the Day of the Divine Repose). In this supra-rational paradox is contained the mystery of the whole Orthodox teaching. The Resurrection is a new beginning, and therefore the First Day — or, more exactly, as Palamas says, the Day One. But it is such a beginning as, unlike the ordinary First Day of creation, has no end, and is not, strictly speaking, “created.” On the other hand, it is the Great Repose — the abiding of God in himself, unchangingly and inwardly. Yet the fullness and self-concentration of the Divine Darkness, abiding in its transcendent dimension, gathers into itself the whole fullness of the creation “estranged” outward. The Eighth Day, Sunday, is not immanent, like the beginning, and not transcendent, like the end. It stands on the far side of law, outside logic, refuting the metaphysics both of the “Jews” and of the “Hellenes.” The Eighth Day is beyond count and sequence. It follows after the Sabbath, but precedes the beginning of the Week. It is the paradoxical and unthinkable “creation inward.”

It is the instant when the impossible becomes possible.

Of it nothing is said directly in the “Book of Genesis.”

Part VIII · Chapter LIII

Kenosis and Eschatology

Кенозис и эсхатология

At the End of Times the whole process of kenosis — rooted in the most transcendent depths of the Trinitarian absolute — is exhausted and summed. If in the act of creation of the First Day the self-diminution of the Godhead reaches its lower ontological limit, then at the end of creation itself, on the Seventh Day, the metaphysical meaning of this kenosis is laid open and disclosed to the end. After this follows the unthinkable and blinding Eighth Day.

The Godhead, beginning from its highest apophatic aspects, moves along the path of kenosis. In this is the axis of Christian metaphysics. It is for this reason that God is revealed as the supra-essential apophatic Trinity — where the “smallest” part of universal possibility (the possibility of being) is suddenly disclosed as the only-begotten and beloved Son of infinity. This is the absolute source of Christianity as a metaphysical doctrine.

Further, kenosis passes over to the sphere of the ontological principles, where the possibility of being is actualized into being — that is, descends further into concreteness along the path of self-limitation. Finally the matter reaches creation, where kenosis becomes total, seizing into the process of divine self-estrangement the “lower” limit of all possibilities — “nothing,” nihil. On the First Day of creation God reaches the bottom of all possible reality.

In the two lower worlds, in the sphere of the lower waters, the movement toward “nothing” has a dynamic character (in distinction from the eternal, aeonic nature of the heavens, the world of the upper waters). Thus the First and total Day of creation is split into the cosmic week, in which time moves from fullness to lack. Thus kenosis continues now within creation itself. The world and humanity go from catastrophe to catastrophe: from the Flood to the tower of Babel, from Sodom and Gomorrah to the Egyptian captivity, and so on. And the number of the “righteous” diminishes, while that of the “sinners” grows. And at the limit of degeneration, hard against the lower bound of history, on the threshold of the earthly hell, there flashes the transcendent lightning — the Good News.

The kenotic cycle is exhausted completely. From the absolute through being to creation, and within it downward, to the “abomination of desolation,” leads the metaphysics of Love — the Love of the Greater for the lesser, of the Great for the small, of the Rich for the destitute. And when destitution and poverty reach their limit, when no path lower any longer exists, when the generations hopelessly lose not only paradise but the elementary norms of the estranged law — the desert of the universe is illumined by the transcendent flame, the flashes of the Eighth Day, the gleams of the Resurrection, the dread and dizzyingly bottomless revelation of the Sermon on the Mount: “Blessed are the poor in spirit.” Blessed are those who have nothing — the wretched and the outcast, the robbed and the sacrificed, those who have suffered unjustly and been humiliated without cause. Blessed is the “nothing” that served as the base of creation; blessed is the non-divine, for it shall be deified. Only that which has died shall rise, like the grain of the Gospel parable. Only he who loses shall find. And those alone shall reign together with Christ who have tasted the experience of slavery to the full.

The “accomplishment of all accomplishments,” the triumphal and absolute affirmation of the paradoxical transcendent metaphysics of Love, will take place on the Eighth Day, the Day of Resurrection; and it was for the sake of this Day, this instant, that all the rest was prepared — beginning from the Trinitarian Unity of the apophatic absolute, through being and creation, through history and its sufferings. At this moment of the total affirmation of the Triumph of Orthodoxy there will be laid bare the inadequacy of the other metaphysical theories — those which affirm the unchanging and inviolable constancy of a single (non-trinitarian) absolute, and therefore see the universe as a play of mutually succeeding cycles of possibilities of manifestation, not obligatory and not endowed with any higher metaphysical meaning or message.

Christianity does not know the cyclical doctrine — not through misunderstanding or error, but because it is itself the metaphysical refutation of the vision of the world as an arbitrary, accidental, or merely possible reality. Creation, the fall, and Salvation are equally necessary to the absolute and inseparable from it, since they express the fullness of its kenotic sacrificial orientation; for the Christian absolute does not leave without its Love even that which is unworthy of this Love — “nothing,” the lower limit of reality. In the Second Coming all time, the whole universe, all the cycles end and are annulled. After this moment nothing will be as before. This is neither the golden age nor the mahapralaya of Hinduism. It is the paradoxical gap between the cycles, which gathers into itself both past and future, drawing immanent reality into the funnel of the metaphysical vertical — which goes through time and space, through the waters and the celestial pleroma, through being and non-being, through the absolute, to the far side, straight to the secret transcendent source of Love.

This is the marriage supper of the Lamb — of the Sacrifice slain before all ages. This is the End of Times in the fullest and most radical sense. Here is disclosed, at last, in all its fullness, the meaning and significance of the kenosis of the absolute — its goal, its secret essence, unriddled until now.

Apparatus · Chapters LI–LIII

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note markers 242–243 are preserved; Chapters LI and LIII carry none, and the continuity 241 → 242 across Chapter LI confirms its completeness. The title of LI is Rev. 10:6 (“that there should be time no longer”); “new heavens and new earth,” Rev. 21:1 with Isa. 65:17 and 2 Pet. 3:13; “the life of the age to come” is the Creed’s closing article. In LIII: “Blessed are the poor in spirit,” Matt. 5:3; the dying grain, John 12:24 (with 1 Cor. 15:36); “he who loses shall find,” after Matt. 16:25; the marriage supper of the Lamb, Rev. 19:7–9 — and “the Sacrifice slain before all ages” fuses Rev. 13:8 (“the Lamb slain from the foundation of the world”) with the Creed’s “begotten before all ages,” a deliberate splice that compresses the book’s eternal-sacrifice doctrine into a single phrase. The Palamas quotations on the Jubilee Year and the Lord’s Day are given as Dugin cites them from the Russian “Homilies” (marker 242 presumably names the edition); the passage is consonant with Palamas’ genuine homiletic teaching on Sunday as the Eighth Day, and the “day one” point (Gen. 1:5 «день един») is authentic patristic exegesis running from Basil’s Hexaemeron onward. The doctrine of the Eighth Day itself is genuinely traditional — Epistle of Barnabas 15, the Cappadocians, Palamas — and this chapter is the book at its most authentically patristic: where Dugin has Palamas to expound, he is at his best, and the closing page of LII (“It is the instant when the impossible becomes possible. Of it nothing is said directly in the Book of Genesis”) earns its effect honestly.

The “Jewish Sabbath” construction. The chapter’s account of Judaic eschatology — the Sabbath as death, as the Creator’s final detachment from the creature, “when God rests, humanity dies” — must be flagged as Dugin’s construction, not Judaism’s self-understanding. In Jewish tradition the Shabbat is delight (oneg), sanctification, and precisely a foretaste of the world to come (me‘ein olam ha-ba) — the inverse of God-forsakenness. What Dugin has done is restate his own Part II grid (creationism as estrangement) and attribute it to the Jewish Sabbath as its “deepest meaning.” Likewise the mashiach as “sublunar spirit”: the Jewish messiah is a human king of David’s line — “sublunar spirit” is Dugin’s idiom, polemically deployed. Confidence: high on both. The reader should also note that Palamas is made the warrant for a Friday–Saturday–Sunday mapping of the book’s own Judaic/Hellenic/Christian triad — the synthesis grid is Dugin’s signature scheme, laid over the homilies rather than found in them.

The necessity problem. Chapter LIII contains the Part’s most consequential sentence for anyone auditing the system: creation, fall, and Salvation are “equally necessary to the absolute and inseparable from it.” Two things must be said. First, this contradicts Orthodox dogmatics, for which creation is God’s free act — the world adds nothing to God and is necessary to him in no respect; necessity-of-creation is the position of emanationism, which the book has spent fifty chapters calling the “Hellenic” error. Second, it contradicts the immediately preceding chapter, whose entire argument was that the Eighth Day — unlike every other day — comes not by necessity but by unconditioned Love. The modal razor between grace and necessity, which the book has wielded since Chapter XIV as the one blade separating the Theotokos from Lucifer and Orthodoxy from its rivals, is here quietly traded away in the final summation: the drama that Chapter LII declared gratuitous, Chapter LIII declares necessary. Confidence: high that the contradiction is real and not verbal. It is the price of the book’s deepest ambition — to make the End not merely happen to the Absolute but matter to it — and Dugin pays it on the last page of his eschatology, where the vertical is said to pass “through the absolute, to the far side” of it: the Super-Godhead of Part I, returning at the end as the secret source of Love beyond God.

Reading note — Part VIII complete. The Part’s architecture: the inversion stated (XLIX–L: the End outranks the Beginning), the non-cyclical claim defended against the Guénonists (LI — where, for once, Dugin’s position is simply the Church’s, and Augustine against the circuitus stands silently behind him), the patristic ground exhibited (LII), and the system’s signature re-imposed at dogmatic cost (LIII). One political note belongs in the record, because the reader of Part VII will hear it: “those alone shall reign with Christ who have tasted the experience of slavery to the full” is eschatology in the idiom of 1996 — the beatitude of the dispossessed shading into the enthronement of the dispossessed, the same voltage that ran through the Derzhavnaya chapter. What remains is the Conclusion: three chapters on witness, election, and the trumpet — the book’s summons to its reader.

Conclusion · To Become a Son of Thunder

Conclusion · Chapter LIV

A Witness Concerning Orthodox Metaphysics

Свидетельство о православной метафизике

Christian metaphysics is a spiritual paradox, a message unique and without analogue. This metaphysics is not the product of later interpretation, elaboration, and conjecture: it lies at the foundation of the New Testament Revelation itself, an adequate understanding of which is possible only with the co-working of the Holy Spirit. The Good News brought to mankind and to the world by Isus Christ carries a meaning supra-created, supra-celestial, supra-human, and supra-angelic. To understand this News, then, one must pass beyond the bounds of creation by Faith, by Will, and by Power. Only by the Holy Spirit can the mysteries of Orthodoxy be made clear. A purely human interpretation is doomed to remain incomplete and, at the limit, false. The Gospel narrative and the foundations of Orthodox tradition cannot be “fitted” to a strict system. It is evident that the uncreated Trinity stands beyond all systems and limitations, and that everything bound up with the Incarnation of one of its three persons therefore carries in itself, from the first, an apophatic, unknowable, transcendent aspect.

Knowing this clearly, the Christian consciousness must learn, with persistence, a contemplation above reason — the direct seeing of the uncreated Light, in which the “known” and the “familiar” become “unknown” and “transfigured,” and the “secret” is explained by a still greater Secret. The contemplation of Tabor has no limit: he who is immersed in it moves endlessly inward into the luminous absolute, in which, strictly speaking, there is neither Light nor Darkness. Metaphysics is that region in which contemplation follows the inward path to the very limit dividing the created, the individual, and the formed from the uncreated, the supra-individual, and the formless. From this definition alone it is plain what exceptional qualities a man must possess to gain an adequate conception of this sphere. And yet, for all the uniqueness and exclusiveness of the metaphysical approach, it is precisely this that forms the common, universal base on which rests the structure of creation and, more concretely, the natural and rational structure of thinking beings. Ignorance of metaphysics does not release a being from its laws and its logic.

The teaching of the Church is addressed to all — not only in its ritual, dogmatic, and ethical form, but in its inner, mysterious side. In a certain sense there exists a “metaphysical catechumenate,” and its logic and its history will be revealed in their fullness only on the Eighth Day. The tradition of Orthodox hesychasm bears the most direct relation to this “metaphysical catechumenate.” The metaphysical interpretation of the principal moments of the Orthodox tradition presented in this book lays no claim to novelty, nor to originality, nor — still less — to an exhaustive elucidation of the problem (which is in any case impossible). The purpose was only to consider Orthodoxy from the position of traditionalism of the Guénonian type, to describe its various sides in the terminology of that traditionalism, showing at the same time its difference both from other sacred traditions (“Judaic” or “Hellenic”) and from other versions of Christianity. And in the course of this there took shape, quite objectively, a picture testifying clearly and convincingly not only to the “regularity,” the “orthodoxy,” of Orthodoxy within the rank of the other sacred traditions, but to its singularity and its metaphysical superiority. We have striven, at the same time, to demonstrate impartially the likeness of the Orthodox teaching to other religious doctrines — where such likeness truly exists — while showing the uniqueness of the Orthodox Good News.

Setting out from this book, and taking one’s bearings from the works of other traditionalists, analogous investigations may be developed in any direction. In our view, enough has been said here to give a firm point of departure for the metaphysical interpretation of more concrete aspects of the Orthodox teaching. Much might yet be said of the symbolism of icons, of the liturgy, of the yearly rhythm of the Church’s services, and so on. Setting out from any dogmatic element, any Orthodox symbol, tradition, life, or rite, one can in principle — and must — ascend to the luminous contemplation of the uncreated Trinitarian reality, and to the corresponding transfiguration of the human subject and of the world about him in the rays of the Glory of the Lord. In Orthodoxy there is nothing accidental. Its every least element — universal or local, fixed in dogma or rising out of local tradition — possesses a great supernatural power, able to draw the material darkness of things after itself into the vortex of the light of Tabor.

Conclusion · Chapter LV

The Called, the Chosen, and Alienation

Званые, избранные и отчуждение

The very division of tradition into two levels — the esoteric and initiatic (inner), on the one hand, and the exoteric (outer) on the other — is the consequence of a deep anthropological dualism, which the various traditions explain in various ways, but the fact of whose existence is acknowledged by all without exception. Of those going the way of esotericism it is often said that they are the “chosen,” the “sons of light,” the “men of God,” “the Lord’s own,” and so on. These terms can be met in the Gospel text itself. The point is that a determinate portion of men remains in the position of the “called,” and even in accepting the tradition and its norms confines itself to its outer aspects. In principle even this is no small thing — since it is always better to be at least outwardly conjoined to the Truth than to harden in the unstructured chaos of demonic existence, or to hold to the fragments of cults long degraded and deprived of metaphysical fullness. Nevertheless, between the “inner” and the “outer” there remains in any case a rather significant distance; and under determinate conditions this distance may lead to conflict and direct contradictions.244

The “outer,” “exotericism,” may under certain circumstances come forward against “esotericism,” whose meaning remains inaccessible to it. One may say that the “called,” in this situation, deny altogether the very fact of the existence of the “chosen,” and begin to treat all men without exception on the basis solely of those conceptions of the human type which they work out proceeding only from themselves and their like — that is, from the type of the “outer.” This is one of the possibilities of the beginning of the degradation of a full Tradition.

But in other cases the representatives of esotericism themselves may, with equal want of warrant, declare their own exclusiveness against the position of the exoterics — either denying the fact of anthropological dualism and transferring their status of “chosen” to all without exception, or haughtily opposing themselves to the “outer” and denying them any truth whatever. This phenomenon lies at the base of all forms of “sectarianism.” Every “sect” begins by affirming the exclusiveness of the esoteric interpretation of Tradition — and proceeding, moreover, from some one plane of that interpretation. This interpretation is then opposed both to the outer side of Tradition and to other versions of esotericism. Esotericism steps into the place of exotericism; and the drawing into the “sect” of people “outer” (that is, exoteric) by their nature finally washes away the fragments of truth lying at the base of the given sectarian movement.

For the characterization of both these processes — militant exotericism, and esotericism degenerated into heresy — one may apply the term “alienation,” which describes the essence of the degradation of Tradition as nothing else could. “Alienation” is possible at all the levels of Tradition that have relation to immanent reality, and especially to the “human factor.” In the fact of “alienation” the whole fullness of the distance between principle and manifestation, between the Godhead and created, non-divine nature, speaks of itself. This alienation may touch not only the lower, material aspects of existence, but the higher regions of creation as well. In a certain sense the problem of “alienation” stands even before the angels — on which is built the dramatism of their supra-temporal choice, and consequently the possibility of “falling.”

“Alienation” is the translation of the essential into the non-essential — the passage from the Reflected to the reflecting, from the authentic and self-identical to imitation and dependence on another. “Alienation” is not simply proper to the whole of creation: it constitutes its basic quality. Consequently everything created — and even Tradition itself, founded on the Supra-created principle — falls in a determinate dimension under this law. It is precisely for this reason that Tradition distinguishes the outer and the inner. The inner is that sphere in which the purely Divine element is livingly and effectively present — not subject to the laws of universal entropy, to alienation. The outer is a certain intermediate aspect belonging to the boundary zone between the transcendent (God) and the immanent (the world). Therefore the outer side of Tradition, exotericism, extends both to those beings who are capable of passing radically beyond the frame of createdness — that is, of being vouchsafed full deification — and to those who remain essentially on the side of the creature, submitting to the norms of tradition as to a reality outer with respect to themselves, yet good and saving. In the normal case, esotericism and exotericism stand toward one another in harmonious combination, as the fullness of Tradition — and the reckoning with the factor of “alienation” and its consequences — requires. But at determinate crisis moments “alienation” begins to advance upon Tradition itself, attempting to project its logic, first of all, onto the proportions of the inner and outer elements.

The phenomenon of anti-esoteric tendencies on the part of militant exotericism is the consequence of the outer side of Tradition’s alienation from its own role. When Tradition loses the distinct living experience of its most transcendent, initiatic dimension, it of necessity perverts the proportions of its doctrine, and devotes unjustifiably much attention to details and secondary moments (most often psychological, social, or moral), forgetting or frankly denying metaphysics. The outer tradition may thus become so alienated from its essence that it turns into a secularized ethical or socio-psychological teaching (this is most vividly seen in the case of Protestantism).

But the esoteric side too, in determinate exceptional cases, may become the victim of the same process. Here the alienation takes place at a subtler level: the initiatic dimension does not cease to be inner and secret, but elements of the esoteric doctrine are projected onto a level in principle lower than the original. This is a more complex and elusive phenomenon than the degeneration of exotericism, since initiatic reality operates with things less evident and more hidden — which makes the analysis less graphic than in the case of the alienation of the outer side of Tradition. One can only assert that alienation at the level of esotericism begins somewhat earlier than the initiatic organization degenerates into a sect. The sect is only the last and visible product of deeper transformations. Alienation, then, cannot be equated only with the violation of the norms of the “anthropological dualism” of which we spoke above. Both the “called” and the “chosen” are theoretically subject to it; and in both cases alienation manifests itself through departure from the true proportions, which must be exactly observed in order that Tradition be living and full.

The considerations expressed above make it possible to explain the numerous transformations of the Orthodox Tradition in various historical periods. And most importantly, at the practical level, they will help one to understand that incongruity (at times crying) which can exist between the purely human, cosmic level of the Church — of the faithful, of the hierarchs, of the monastic brotherhood, and so on — and the highest mystical content of the sacraments, rituals, dogmas, Orthodox doctrines, initiatic practices. Alienation may touch, to a significant degree, certain aspects of the earthly Church; but upon the whole divine, uncreated reality — the Church heavenly (and supra-heavenly) — lying at the foundation of the Orthodox Tradition, it does not extend.

From this last remark one may draw an extraordinarily important (in our view) practical conclusion: in order, in our complex apocalyptic time and in our specific social and psychological conditions, to break through to the eternal, unclouded, deifying Truth of Orthodoxy, it is necessary first of all to learn to peel away from Tradition the products of alienation — attaching no serious significance to them, neither resisting them nor yielding to them. In other words, one should strive to minimize the “human,” earthly, worldly dimension of the Church — of necessity penetrated to a significant degree by the poisonous presence of the aggressive antichristian medium in which the Orthodox have dwelt for centuries now, and whose darkness does not cease to thicken. Of the Church as a mystical reality it is said that “the gates of hell shall not prevail against it.” And to affirm this Gospel Truth by personal confession, it is necessary to bear witness to the imperishable fullness and perfection of the great Orthodox Tradition in all its dimensions — yielding to none of the compromises imposed by the perfidious world of universal apostasy.

However alienated the human level of Orthodoxy may be (and in various periods of history it has sunk unbelievably low in the spiritual, intellectual, psychological, and other senses), it is necessary, against everything, to insist on the mystical fullness and perfection of the Church — singling out its supra-temporal, grace-filled, transfiguring aspect. This means the overcoming of alienation and, simultaneously, genuine renewal — understood not in the sense of ecclesial “modernism” (which in practice means the profanation and desacralization of Orthodoxy, its destruction to serve the son of perdition), but in the initiatic sense of the “new” as the eternal, as the only real and imperishable.

Orthodox esotericism is not a heresy, and not the importation of “mystical” elements alien and external to the tradition. Nor is it a sectarian insistence on this or that interpretation of the various doctrinal, ritual, and symbolic aspects of the church’s teaching. Orthodox esotericism is the secret foundation of Orthodoxy — that level where the whole content of the Tradition is realized literally, where metaphor becomes reality and faith passes over into concrete experience. It is not a particular “Orthodoxy for the chosen”; it is the comprehension and the living-through, by the chosen, of the one and universally true Orthodoxy — to which, in this, nothing is added, and from which nothing is taken away.

Orthodox exotericism is not a moralistic, conventional religious doctrine dependent on historical concreteness, but the totality of strict canons and norms called to determine the whole order of human existence, the principles of the social structure. This exotericism must not deny esotericism, nor fit its norms to the decrees of the “temporal power.” It determines unambiguously what is Orthodox and what is not; and to stand on the affirmation of this against the attacks of the antichristian world is its highest task. Into its functions enter likewise the strict observance of the rite, the preservation of orthodox dogmatics, and the uninterruptedness of the sacraments.

The struggle against alienation is the common cause of all the Orthodox. It is steadfastness in the Faith, fidelity to the tradition, courage in the confession of the Truth.

Conclusion · Chapter LVI

The Trumpet Voice

Трубный Глас

Thus the Orthodox teaching distinguishes in the Church two aspects: the Church earthly and the Church heavenly. The heavenly Church lies on the far side of time, as the eternal center around which the earthly Church is organized. This earthly Church belongs, in its turn, to history — that is, it is drawn into the course of time. But we have seen that Tradition regards time as a cycle, as a circle. Consequently it is warranted to picture the history of the earthly Church, too, in the form of a circle, whose center and motionless pole will be the Church heavenly.

The Nativity of Christ, corresponding to the winter solstice, may also be taken as the point of departure of the earthly Church — for the body of Christ is the Third Temple, the Temple of Salvation. To this corresponds the catacomb period of Christianity’s formation, when the news of the Birth of the Sun of Righteousness is accessible only to the chosen. These chosen abide in the night of a desacralized world that has forgotten the metaphysical dimension of its own tradition. To such a state correspond, approximately, the first three centuries of Christianity — during which it is, on the whole, initiatic and esoteric, preserving the consciousness of the metaphysical meaning of the Incarnation. This is the time of the dogmatic disputes, of the formation of the Orthodox teaching. The very fact of lively church polemic testifies that this or that dogmatic formulation was, for the Christians of that time, something inner, crystal-clear, and touching their spiritual experience directly.

In other words, at this stage the Church is sensitive in the extreme to the structure of Christian dogmatics — giving itself an excellent account of the historical, social, political, and even cosmic consequences of the acceptance of this or that dogmatic formulation. The Orthodox consciousness is fully awakened and vigilant, passionately seeking the Holy Spirit for the illumination of heart and mind. This is the time of noetic Christianity.

After Constantine, and around the fifth century, the earthly Church enters its spring period. The secret and esoteric becomes the possession of all. Out of the “winter” underground of the catacombs, Christianity comes forth onto the surface of the earth (and precisely such is the sacred subject bound up with calendar spring and the spring equinox). Orthodoxy is clothed in imperial vestments. The prophecies of the thousand-year Kingdom are fulfilled not in the transcendent, supernatural aspect (which will take place only in the Second Coming), but as the establishment in the oikoumene of a mighty Christian Empire. In parallel with this, dogmatics becomes strictly fixed, cast into outer formulations not subject to development or revision. Henceforth the apprehension of the Christian teaching proceeds not only through the grace-filled prophetic acquisition of the Holy Spirit, but also through the “outer” assimilation of strictly determined formulas. The same concerns the ritual, and the hierarchical structure of the Church. It is not by chance that the Orthodox limited the number of recognized Councils to the Seven Ecumenical and nine Local. Thanks to this, the Eastern Church preserved fidelity to the spring state of Christian consciousness — occupying an intermediate position between the still-living esoteric spirit of the first centuries and its necessary adaptation to the mass, “imperial” reception of the teaching. It is evident that at this stage of the cycle the clear understanding of the metaphysical foundations of Christianity is somewhat effaced, retreats to the background. At the least, so the matter stands with the majority of Christians. The number of the “called” grows, while the number of the “chosen” either remains as before, or even diminishes.

The peak of this imperial period falls on the tenth to twelfth centuries, when the earthly Church enters the apogee of its glory.

Here the dogmas embodied in final formulations, the strictly established rituals, the ecclesiastical hierarchy attain their maximal development. All aspects of social, political, economic life are encompassed by the influence of the Church — which now corresponds to summer and the summer solstice. The Christian Kingdom spreads across the whole Eurasian continent, stably occupying its northern limits (let us note that the summer solstice, in sacred calendar symbolism, corresponds precisely to the North). This is the Christian Middle Ages — the point cyclically opposite to the Nativity and the initial stages of the Church. It is precisely in this period that there arise in the West esoteric organizations and orders separate from the Church — since the preservation of the esoteric content of Christianity within the frame of an earthly Church gradually becoming an exclusively social institution appears there, henceforth, exceedingly difficult, if not altogether impossible. The Eastern Churches escape this fate, and continue to combine both sides of Christianity (the “winter” — esoteric, and the “summer” — exoteric) within the frame of one and the same institution. It is curious to note that Orthodoxy comes to Rus' precisely in this “summer-solstitial” quality. Therefore, throughout the whole of Russian history, Christianity is conceived only in combination with Empire — as the eternal Kingdom embodied in dust.

Soon after the “summer solstice” occur the schism and the separation of the Catholic Church from Orthodoxy. This is the first sign of the decline. At the time of the autumn equinox (the fifteenth century) Byzantium weakens and, in the end, Constantinople falls. The end of the medieval synthesis arrives. The earthly Church enters the period of decline. Prophecies of the coming of the antichrist spread ever wider. The Russian Schism lays bare the dramatism of the church situation. The primordial esotericism of Christianity, clothed in the outer vestments of exotericism, is gradually forgotten to such a degree that the outer side of the earthly Church itself begins to crumble. Gradually there disappear from the field of vision not only Christian metaphysics, but the consciousness of the inner logic of Nicene dogmatics. Catholicism, carried away by the stream of dogmatic adaptation of religious formulas to changing historical actuality, departs ever further from the primordial orthodoxy, conferring on the accidental, the historical, the relative, and the transient the status of unconditional truth. The unity of the Church disintegrates within the West itself.

Winter begins, and the reign of night.

The last five hundred years present the final decline of the earthly Church and the degeneration of Christian civilization, down to complete secularization and de-Christianization. The Church moves toward the lowest point of its history, toward the winter solstice — but not from the side of spring, as in early Christianity, but from the side of autumn, from the side of the “old year” and the “old world” that have forgotten Christ and his Good News. This is the epoch of “apostasy,” of the appearance of the “son of perdition.” The night shadows and the cold of winter strive with deadening breath to extinguish the life in the Third Temple of Christianity. The catacomb situation of the first centuries of the cycle is repeated from the “other” side of the circle. In communist Russia this correspondence is realized literally: in the persecution of religion. In the West, the Vatican enters into direct conciliation with the antichristian “fallen” world (which is still worse).
