Not only is the esotericism of Christianity no longer understood: the dogmatic side too loses its persuasiveness and force. Thus in Protestantism there takes place the renunciation of dogmas and rituals. Christianity degenerates to a socio-ethical teaching.

But the prophecy is also fulfilled that “the gates of hell shall not prevail against the Church.” In spite of everything, almost throughout the whole world the sacrament of communion is celebrated; the services go on uninterruptedly; children are baptized and priests are ordained. Against everything, every man still has the saving possibility of turning to the bosom of the Church, of acquiring the grace of the Holy Spirit, and of going the way of deification toward the uncreated, supra-heavenly Kingdom of Light. Even if “exotericism” becomes as difficult of attainment as esotericism, it does not vanish altogether; and the metaphysical dimension of the Church is likewise preserved. It can be discovered at any moment — so long as there are baptism, the service, communion, and the transmission of the priesthood “after the order of Melchizedek.”

Ever nearer stands humanity to the point of the New solstice. After a brief period of complete apostasy and the illusory triumph of the anti-Christian forces will come the fiery reality of the Eighth Day, of the Second Coming. Then the metaphysical fullness of the Good News will open in all its ontological and supra-ontological scope. If one has fine hearing, one can already now make out the peals of the transcendent Thunder — the trumpet voice of the Last Day, which will be the conclusion of the full cycle of the earthly Church and the descent to earth of the Heavenly Jerusalem. Not only the apostles James and John were named “Boanerges,” “sons of thunder.” All the apostles became such “sons” at the moment of Pentecost, when the Holy Spirit descended on each of them like tongues of flame. More than that: every baptized Christian is endowed with the first-fruits of eschatological hearing, introduced into inexpressible, mysterious contact with the Thunder of the Day of Judgment. On that Day the secret will become manifest; the perfect fullness of the economy of the Holy Spirit will be disclosed; the providential meaning of the whole of history, and of the history of the Church, will grow clear in the uncreated Light.

This will be the cosmic mystical Supper of the Lamb, his Marriage Feast — the winter solstice of the universe.

And to Orthodox Rus' it will fall to play, in this last eschatological mystery, the most important, the central role.

“It is near, even at the doors.”

The time is fulfilled.

Apparatus · Chapters LIV–LVI

Apparatus

Sources and citations. Note marker 244 (in Chapter LV) is preserved; the continuity 243 → 244 across the marker-free Chapters LIII and LIV is consistent, though the extreme brevity of Chapter LIV — a single paragraph, for a chapter titled “A Witness Concerning Orthodox Metaphysics” — places it on the verification list beside Chapter XXXII; the paginated edition will decide whether this is Dugin’s coda or a transcription loss. Suspicion here is lower than for XXXII (no marker gap testifies against it), but the reader edition will confirm. Chapter LV’s title is Matt. 22:14 (“many are called, but few are chosen”); “the gates of hell shall not prevail,” Matt. 16:18; the “son of perdition,” 2 Thess. 2:3; “after the order of Melchizedek,” Ps. 110:4 with Heb. 5–7, closing the circle opened at Chapter XXIII. In Chapter LVI: “Boanerges, sons of thunder,” Mark 3:17 (the source prints «Ваонергес»; the translation gives the standard form, and the reading list will check the paginated edition); Pentecost’s tongues of flame, Acts 2:3; the marriage supper, Rev. 19:7–9; the descent of the Heavenly Jerusalem, Rev. 21:2. The book’s last words are Scripture in Slavonic cadence: «Близ есть при дверех» is Matt. 24:33 — “it is near, even at the doors” — and «Исполняется время» is Mark 1:15, “the time is fulfilled.” The seven Ecumenical and nine Local councils is the accurate Orthodox canonical count. The Old Rite Isus holds to the last page.

“Alienation.” The master-term of Chapter LV deserves a provenance note the chapter does not give: otchuzhdenie is the Russian for Entfremdung — the alienation of German idealism and of Marx — here silently baptized into Guénonian decline-theory as “the essence of the degradation of Tradition.” For an author who in 1996 was co-founding the National Bolshevik Party, the fusion of the revolutionary left’s central category with Traditionalist metaphysics is not an accident of vocabulary; it is the book’s politics showing through its theology one last time, as “those who tasted slavery to the full” did in Chapter LIII. The chapter’s practical program — peel the human shell from the Church, minimize its earthly dimension, concede nothing to the “temporal power” — is the zealot temperament of the book’s Old-Believer sympathies made method; its risk (a Church whose human reality is treated as antichristian residue) is the ecclesiological mirror of the book’s standing habit of saving essences by discarding existences.

The circle closed. Chapter LVI performs the book’s final synthesis: the Wirthian year-circle of Part V is laid over church history itself — catacombs as winter, Constantine as spring, the medieval empire as summer solstice (with Rus' baptized precisely at the zenith, whence, Dugin concludes, Russian Christianity is thinkable only as Empire), schism as the turn, 1453 as autumn equinox, the last five centuries as winter approached “from the old year’s side.” The periodization is conventional in outline; the calendar mapping is his, and it fuses the book’s two great machines — the sacred year and the katechon-history of Part VII — into one wheel whose midnight is now. The claims requiring flags are familiar ones: “realized chiliasm” (the millennium as the Christian Empire) restates Chapter XLV; the Vatican’s conciliation judged “still worse” than communist persecution restates Chapter XLVII’s inverted loyalties; and the penultimate sentence — Orthodox Rus' assigned “the most important, the central role” in the last mystery — compresses the whole of Part VII into a single line, placing Russia inside the eschaton as the book’s parting act. One should notice, finally, what Chapter LIV does to the whole: after fifty-three chapters of system, the Conclusion opens by declaring the Gospel unsystematizable — an apophatic disclaimer that is both the book’s most orthodox sentence and its least practiced one. And Chapter LV’s definition of Orthodox esotericism — that to the one universal Orthodoxy “nothing is added and nothing taken away” — is the final orthodoxy-claim of a work whose additions this edition’s apparatus has spent twenty-four instalments recording: Guénon’s initiation, Wirth’s year, the Super-Godhead beyond the Trinity, the eternal Third Archangel, the counter-church of Chapter XLI. The claim and the record stand side by side; the reader now holds both.

Editorial afterword. With this instalment the translation of the main text — fifty-six chapters in eight parts with introduction and conclusion — is complete; the four appendices are omitted from this edition by decision of its commissioner. Outstanding for the reader edition: restoration of the truncated Chapter XXXII (notes 167–172); verification of Chapter LIV; recovery of three lost glyphs (the Ilx rune of XXXVI, the five- and six-pointed stars of XXXVII); and the full text of Dugin’s 244+ endnotes from the paginated edition. Golovin’s contemporary review accompanies the edition as a supplement. The apparatus has aimed throughout at a single discipline: translate as written, and name what is written — the genuine and the invented, the patristic and the smuggled, the beautiful and the poisonous — each in its place, so that the book can be read whole and judged exactly.

Restoration (14 Aug 2026). Chapter LIV proved to be truncated as well: four paragraphs in studfile against wikireading’s one. The batch-24 clearance of LIV was therefore wrong, and its instalment file is superseded here. The error is instructive: the lost paragraphs of LIV carry no note markers, so the marker-continuity test (243→244 across LIII–LIV) passed while a three-paragraph loss went undetected. Standing methodological caveat, now on record: marker continuity detects only losses that contain markers. Chapters or passages verified solely by continuity remain unverified against marker-free loss.

Restoration — Superiority inside the disclaimer. The third paragraph asserts, in one sentence, that the book’s picture took shape “quite objectively” and testifies to Orthodoxy’s “metaphysical superiority” — inside a paragraph that disclaims novelty, originality, and system. The conclusion thus compresses the book’s standing tension into a single page: the apophatic humility of the first paragraph (already noted in the batch-24 apparatus as the book’s most orthodox sentence and its least practiced one) against a claim of objective demonstrated supremacy three paragraphs later. Translated as written; the tension is the author’s.

Restoration — The Super-Godhead returns. The restored second paragraph’s limitless contemplation of Tabor, moving “endlessly inward into the luminous absolute, in which, strictly speaking, there is neither Light nor Darkness,” reprises the apophatic Super-Godhead of Part I at the book’s close — beyond the Light itself, as Part I stood beyond being. The recovery of this paragraph strengthens, rather than alters, the edition’s reading of the book’s architecture.

Restoration — Closing image. “Able to draw the material darkness of things after itself into the vortex of the light of Tabor” — the conclusion’s finest sentence, and it was among the lost ones. Credited.

Supplement

Supplement · Contemporary Review

Evgeny Golovin,
Review of The Metaphysics of the Good News

Рецензия на книгу А. Дугина «Метафизика Благой Вести» (1996; Golovin Foundation archive)

The text that follows is not an evaluative review (an absurd idea, in our view), but an attempt at critical commentary. So then: why was this book written at all? The question is not as idle as it may seem. In Giordano Bruno’s dialogue “The Friday Supper,” one of the participants asks: why write books at all? Only the oral transmission of knowledge is effective, only its content is effective. He receives the answer: books are written not for remembering, but for reminding.

By this entirely Platonic thought, The Metaphysics of the Good News justifies its existence — the book reminds one of a great deal and summons up a wide associative circle. The sheer scale of the subject matter involved, however, does not permit a detailed characterization; we shall pause only on certain drastic particulars. But first of all, let us try to take in this or that concept employed in the text, guided by Heidegger’s well-known maxim: “A concept is not a frozen term, but at once a measure and a posing of the question.” This will aid a more spacious understanding of the work. For such a purpose we shall make use of a composition by the celebrated Christian humanist and Kabbalist Johannes Reuchlin (fifteenth–sixteenth centuries), “A Characterization of Ancient Hebrew Meanings and Greek Words.” Alongside the familiar meanings one comes upon curious variants. For instance: “Exoterica: tidying up around the house — a woman’s occupation”; “esoterica: tidying up inside the house — a man’s occupation”; “aeon — time spent in the company of wife and children.” Reuchlin, unlike many modern researchers, is well pleased with the bodily, concrete understanding of highly abstract terms — with the naïveté of the ancients. The Greeks of antiquity unquestionably felt the world to be a single organism. Diabole — the principle of divisibility, in their understanding — had not yet cut into that organism, at least not before the coming of Socrates and Aristotle. Hence the dichotomies of “life and death,” “sensible and supersensible,” “flesh and spirit” were entirely foreign to them. Nicholas of Cusa expressed this situation well in his book of conjectures: “Each of two opposites has something of the other; each can become a unity in relation to the other.” To this pronouncement Cusanus appended a figure called the “Paradigm”: two isosceles triangles entering into one another, each touching the other’s base with its apex — something quite analogous to the double spiral or the yin-yang circle. And it corresponds to Vedanta, where the “absolute” is radically transcendent with respect to manifestation and, at the same time, integrally immanent to it, according to advaita, non-dualism.

The author of The Metaphysics of the Good News inventively and with virtuosity extrapolates the chief Christian dogmas — the Trinity and kenosis — onto the Absolute, through a complex system of ontological and metaphysical triads. Have analytical precedents of this kind occurred in Orthodox theology or religious philosophy? Hardly likely. The triadic method of investigating the “one,” the “intelligible center of manifestation,” is typical of the Neoplatonists — provided, of course, one sets aside the pathos of the exposition. But the author, coming forward as a paragon of Orthodoxy, is inclined to interpret the possible structure of the absolute exclusively in the Orthodox sense. We do not feel entitled to enter into the Trinitarian dispute; let us remark, however, that in Catholicism, notwithstanding the official standing of the dogma of the Filioque, there existed various interpretations of subordination within the bounds of the Holy Trinity. It is difficult, for example, to speak of any subordination whatever in the scheme of Catholicism’s great authority Thomas Aquinas: God the Father — the unoriginate first principle; God the Son — the Logos, the meaning-giving, form-giving principle; God the Holy Spirit — the life-giving principle.

Kenosis (self-diminution, sacrifice) is necessarily postulated by the author in regions wholly beyond the reach of reason. This is the immemorial difficulty of Christian theology. Why did the Creator make the world and condemn the creature to a creaturely vegetating between two “nothings”? Christianity in general, and Orthodoxy in particular, find only one worthy explanation: kenosis, sacrifice. Aleksandr Dugin writes of a “mysterious conflict in the bosom of the Transcendent.” And then:

The passage from metaphysics to ontology is, in Christianity, an act of extraordinary significance, charged with a mysterious dimension not decipherable in the terms of conventional metaphysics. The entry of the Godhead into Being, and then into Manifestation — its “kenosis” — here acquires the quality of “necessity,” of “purpose,” ceasing to be one of the possibilities of the Absolute, a simple proof of its abundance and omnipotence, and becoming the expression of the secret side of the Absolute, of its innermost, hidden kernel.

A certain heaviness in this passage is explicable; but the cause of the expression “the secret side of the Absolute” is not very clear. When an epithet (the absolutely one, the absolute groundlessness) becomes a substantive, a bearer of action, the tension of its abstraction is considerably heightened. Against the term “Absolute,” introduced at the end of the eighteenth century by Jacobi and Schelling, not a few intellectual assaults have been dashed to pieces. Schelling, in the article “On the Possibility of the Absolute’s Love for That Which Is Not Absolute,” refused to acknowledge such a love and advanced the thesis of an “irrational fall of the Absolute,” subsequently disputed by Hegel. Let us leave the vertiginous search for the Absolute. In reading Aleksandr Dugin’s book the following serious doubt arises: is it possible to speak of “esotericism” without stepping beyond the bounds of the creationist doctrine? An attempt to resolve this question lies in reflection on the role of the Holy Spirit. The author writes: “His participation in Creation is enigmatic and often incomprehensible, like everything connected with this person in ontology and metaphysics.” Is the Holy Spirit identical with the biblical Spirit of God? Is the biblical God-Creator identical with the Christian God the Father?

Christian and non-Christian theologians alike have always been troubled by the, so to speak, “purely masculine” composition of the Trinity. In the Gnostic heresies of the first centuries, in the teachings of the Albigensians, the Waldensians, the Adamites, the Holy Spirit was often replaced either by the Virgin Mary or by some other feminine equivalent. The author of the work that interests us likewise experiences difficulties on this plane: “The third person (God the Holy Spirit) remains to be correlated only with ‘first matter,’ with the substance of Manifestation, Natura Naturans.” This sentence, in our view, requires special commentary. The author is inclined to interpret the basic principles (Natura Naturans and Natura Naturata) in the substantial sense of Spinoza, whereas in the present context one must use, in our view, the conception of John Scotus Eriugena (ninth century), who introduced these notions. Eriugena understands Natura Naturans as the independent, masculine, celestial Principle. The Traditionalist Ananda Coomaraswamy quite justly correlates it with the Purusha of Hinduism, and Natura Naturata with Prakriti. Eriugena in his book De divisione naturae, following the Platonists Iamblichus and Proclus, interprets hyle (Natura Naturata) as “pantheistic chaos.” A caveat is needed here: “pantheism” is often understood in the sense of a “divinized nature,” by appeal to the second meaning of the word pan — “all.” The Neoplatonists have in mind the great Pan, whom the Romans called Panis (whence “penis”). So then: “pantheistic chaos” is totally living nature, the cosmic kteis (vagina), ithyphallically pierced through — and the feminine principle here unquestionably dominates and is (in Cusanus’s sense) a unity in relation to the masculine. This riotous, shoreless, pansexual, infinitely aggressive nature horrified the Jews and the Christians. Here there is no death as a passage from being to non-being; death is only a passage from one form of existence to another, an uninterrupted metamorphosis. Panta rhei, mobile in mobili, the moving within the moving — the realm of Gaia, of Cybele, of Aphrodite Pandemos. Karl Kerényi, in his book Dionysos, reproduces curious photographs of archaic amphorae: on one of them Aphrodite Pandemos sits bent over, a penis in place of a tongue — auto-erotics, auto-generation; on another, the horned Dionysus-mediator, an enormous ithyphallos passing between voluminous female breasts and propping the chin. And in these turbulences, in endless metamorphoses, in “eidetic matter” (Proclus), a germ flares up: Uranus, the future Heaven, Natura Naturans (Eriugena). In counterposition to the penis — “the hound of Diana, drugged by female flesh” (Pausanias) — there arises the upward-straining phallus, bearer of the seminal logoi (logos spermatikos). Each of these logoi creates the first matter proper to it (hyle, Natura Naturata) and is created by it. The phallic god begets and organizes, but does not “create” in the biblical sense — a spiral process of emanations of the one into the “other.” In the “pantheistic chaos” a circle of metamorphoses takes shape, specific to this or that phallic god — Uranus, Kronos, Poseidon, Zeus. In the divine amorous adventures this or that cosmos is organized. But such a demiurge-organizer arouses the monstrous fury of the pantheistic chaos. Hence the drama of divine castration, the titanomachy, the battle with Typhon — the parthenogenetic offspring of mother Night.

We make so long a digression in order to clarify “manifestationism” — the pagan picture of the universe. The following point must be noted: the pantheistic chaos can be organized in two ways — phallically and kteically; more simply, patriarchally and matriarchally. When the feminine divine unities succeed in organizing (taming) the riotous masculine substance, there arise more or less calm and stable civilizations subject to “mother-right”: the civilizations of Demeter, of Proserpina, of Arachne. The activity of the men there — plowmen, herdsmen, artisans — is conditioned by seasonal cycles and lunar phases; this radial activity is bridled by the centripetal force of the feminine principle.

“Men are busy with hoarding, with acquisition, with the raising of fences and walls,” wrote the Neoplatonist Plutarch of Athens (fifth century); “these are not men, but man-like women.” The skepticism is understandable: the pagan Neoplatonist acknowledged only the emanations of the celestial-phallic “one,” and for him the concepts “man” and “hero” are entirely synonymous.

Thus, from the point of view of Neoplatonism (an unfortunate term, incidentally, if one bears in mind that school’s sharp anti-Socratic orientation), there are no grounds whatever for identifying the Christian “heavenly Father” and “Holy Spirit” with the biblical Creator and Spirit of God. More than that: from this point of view one may identify the Virgin Mary with first matter at its quintessential level, with the “white earth” — as was in fact done in Christian alchemy. Illa terra virgo, “a certain virgin earth,” is how Tertullian defined the Holy Virgin. What can Christianity have in common with the creationist dogma of the Jews? Let us quote Aleksandr Dugin:

In the “nihilistic” (ex nihilo) nature of Creation lies that sharp boundary which divides the universe from its Maker. The “nothing” which all Creation essentially is can under no conditions pass qualitatively into “something”; accordingly, the strictly creationist model holds that the creature receives existence from outside, from God, “on loan,” and sooner or later will be compelled to give it back, becoming “nothing” once more.

It is difficult to add anything to this brilliant characterization. The Jews themselves have always underscored the uniqueness of their creationist dogma, at times violating the rules of good manners entirely. For example, the Talmudic school of “Mishnat” (thirteenth–fourteenth centuries) maintained that the Pentateuch speaks of the creation of the chosen people, and that the biblical events have no relation to the rest, who cannot even be counted as human. It must objectively be acknowledged that the peoples of the Mediterranean in antique times repaid the chosen people in the same coin. The polytheistic civilizations of Egypt, Greece, and Rome detested the Semites generally, holding Typhon and Set to be the terrible gods of the Jews. Even the efforts of the famous Philo of Alexandria (first century) to unite Judaic and Stoic-Platonic philosophy — efforts which exercised a serious influence on patristics — could not change the psychological situation. Even in the sixth century the Neoplatonist Priscian called Jehovah “Typhon in revolt.”

Yet Philo’s role is very great: the extraordinary boldness of his thought, contemptuous of all dogma, contributed to the conversion of Augustine. Casting aside the barrier between the “created” and the “uncreated,” Philo interpreted the Creator as “the super-existent one, conquering chaos.” In paradise — on the outpost, so to speak, of the chthonic abyss — the Creator posted as “sentry” the heroic Adam, an androgyne, a being half celestial, half earthly. In the battle with the chthonic monster (satan) Adam was cleft into man and woman. The world being created (Philo held to the idea of perpetual creation) now stands in need of a mediator between the one transcendent God and the all-devouring abyss. Philo, naturally, did not know of the coming of Christ — which gave Augustine the opportunity to announce the mediator’s name: the “new Adam.” The idea of the “fall” Philo denied entirely, adding in his characteristic style: “It does not befit a thinking man to occupy himself with such rubbish.” (Cited from Siegfried, Philo von Alexandria als Ausleger des Alten Testaments, 1875, p. 117.) This Jew of genius earned the same hatred from his fellow tribesmen as Spinoza did after him.

The reception of Christianity in the first five centuries of the new era proceeded painfully — far more painfully than Christian historians suppose. The antique consciousness, “erotically educated” in a certain sense, had no wish whatever to assimilate the thought of kenosis and agape, the idea of the sacrifice of the Son of God and the drama of Golgotha.

Following Philo, Clement and Origen (second–third centuries) sought the secret meaning of the Bible — and that meaning, as a rule, came down to the discovery of a doctrine of emanations in the biblical pages. Clement, for example, interpreted the Christian triad (it was still too early to speak of a Trinitarian dogma) as “the tension of silence, the Logos, the energy of the Logos.” In Origen, Christ is Logos-Kosmokrator, the golden axis, the celestial phallus, the fish. This widespread Christian symbol he understood as an independently sublimated phallus: the male of the fish fertilizes without touching the female. And indeed the Western Fathers of the Church, in their patriarchal orientation, mentioned the Virgin Mary rarely and little, reasoning like Apollo in the Eumenides of Aeschylus: “The mother does not participate in the conceiving of the child. The mother is only the bearer of a life already awakened.” Aleksandr Dugin writes very justly of the dubiousness of the “salvation of the immortal soul.” This idea in no way fits into the creationist doctrine. Both Clement and Origen believed “heretically” in the pre-existence of the soul, and interpreted its “salvation” in the spirit of Plato’s Phaedrus, which tells of the danger of the dissolution of the “body of the soul.” The soul perishes when torn from the source of the fiery pneuma. “What is that source?” asks Origen. And answers: Christ.

Opinions have often been voiced concerning the patriarchal character of Western Christianity and the matriarchal character of Eastern. Grigory Skovoroda, the Ukrainian philosopher and hermeticist, an ardent admirer of Origen, grounded this as follows: in the hermetic zodiac the East corresponds to the “Virgin,” the West to the “Fishes” (Jacob Boehme, Signatura Rerum). The early Western Fathers of the Church may without question be called Christian Neoplatonists, to whom creationism was in one way or another foreign. And indeed, what man in his heroic hypostasis will reconcile himself to the position of “creature” or “slave,” and acknowledge “humility” as a virtue? But a still more important point must be taken into account: the absolute refusal of the pagan mind to accept the idea of linear, uniform, progressive time. This was the first and decisive victory of the “reign of quantity.” “Time is the quintessence of things,” wrote Origen in Against Celsus — and in this short phrase very much is expressed. It means that there exists a mass of temporal variations, dependent on the life and situation of bodily and soul matter: a complex counterpoint of durations, sensing the invisible and inaudible circular time-quintessence. The repetition of agrarian and weaving operations under the feminine domination of Demeter and Arachne fixed the uniformity of every process. Events great and small, the tense moments of the duel, the oppressive awaiting of execution — in a word, the qualitative diversity of life — all this dissolved into a uniform one-dimensionality between two nothings: so the land-surveyor does not notice whether under his feet there is a dandelion, a nettle, or bare sand. What troubled the pagans was not the incarnation of the Christian God — they knew any number of such incarnations — but an incarnation chronologically exact, in a geographically precisely indicated place: this was beyond their simple patriarchal understanding.

Hence Western Christianity, on which Christian Neoplatonism exercised the strongest influence, has a character of action not humbly prayerful but expansive and sharp. To the Western sense of the world Byzantium was alien, with its effeminacy and unheard-of luxury — a weak country waging war with the hands of mercenaries, which offered the Turks no serious resistance to speak of. Yet hesychasm did not go unnoticed. After the taking of Constantinople one of the teachers of hesychasm, Gemistos Plethon, emigrated to Italy and taught in the Florentine Academy. Among his listeners was Marsilio Ficino, who left in his letters quite extensive and quite critical descriptions of those lectures.

In conclusion, a few remarks concerning The Metaphysics of the Good News. The author is very skeptical toward the “Latinizing rationalism” of the Catholics, though in the multiplicity of Catholic theology he appears to take account only of the Dominican current, which is indeed distinguished by a rigid logistics. But Aleksandr Dugin writes not a word about the Bernardine and Franciscan wisdom, about the energetic mystical surge of the Chartres school of scholastics, about the great mystics — Eckhart, Tauler, Juan de la Cruz, Ruysbroeck the Admirable. True, Orthodoxy has one advantage: the teaching of Gregory Palamas entered the orthodox dogmatics, which cannot be said of either Eriugena or Eckhart.

In pagan Neoplatonism there is no postulate of the principial transcendence of the “one.” We live in that regime which Proclus and Damascius call the sphere of the eighth hypothesis of the Parmenides. Here, in the absence of the “one,” there abide only material bodies, perceived as a “sum of discrete particularities,” and “knowledge is rather a dreaming than the result of a real perception of the really existing” (Damascius). So they thought at the sunset of the pagan world; such thoughts come to mind now as well. Yes, the scholastic Intellect has degenerated into the pragmatic rationalism of “man-like women” — but then Orthodoxy too has little to boast of as regards its influence on its flock. When the house is in ruin, there is no order around the house either — to recall Reuchlin’s determinations concerning “exo-” and “esoterica.” “This is the Tidings,” writes Aleksandr Dugin, “of the grace-filled deification of the non-divine, of the adoption of the constructed, of the communion of the non-living with the Living.” To be sure, judging by the surrounding evidence, the “constructed” will perish the rusty death of a machine. The author heroically hopes that the pneumatics of the Orthodox ritual is capable of “making the unsalted salt salty.” God grant it!

But in our opinion it is not worth accenting the greatness of Orthodoxy while pointing to the nullity of the other Christian confessions. From a chosen confession it is not far to a chosen people — a thing to which the Russians are much inclined. The Russians have many times “solemnly proclaimed” that it was precisely they who “saved” Europe from the Tatar-Mongols and from fascism. It is not worth “saving” Europe this time from “spiritlessness” — for that there is neither the strength nor the slightest grounds. All of us — in the West and in the East alike — are suffocating under the repressive “reign of quantity.”

This emotional element somewhat lowers the lofty philosophical tone of a most interesting book, one of a kind.

Apparatus · Golovin Review

Apparatus

Who is reviewing whom. Golovin was the effective head of the Yuzhinsky Circle after Mamleev's 1974 emigration and the man who introduced Dugin to Guénon, Evola, and the Western esoteric corpus. This review is therefore the manifestationist teacher assessing the disciple's creationist-Orthodox synthesis — and Golovin's position throughout is the one Dugin's own Chapter VIII assigns to the rebel angels: "what man in his heroic hypostasis will reconcile himself to the position of 'creature' or 'slave'?" The final paragraphs, warning that "from a chosen confession it is not far to a chosen people" and dismissing any Russian salvific mission, read in retrospect as a precise early diagnosis of where Dugin's trajectory led.

Golovin's citations are characteristically loose; the following should not be taken on trust. (1) Bruno's dialogue is La cena de le ceneri — the Ash Wednesday Supper, not a "Friday" supper. (2) No Reuchlin work bears the title Golovin cites; the "definitions" quoted (exoterica as housework, aeon as family time) are not traceable to Reuchlin's De rudimentis hebraicis or his lexica and are best treated as Golovin's invention or embroidery. (3) The terms natura naturans/naturata do not occur in Eriugena; his Periphyseon gives the fourfold division of nature that ancestrally underlies them, but the Latin pair itself is thirteenth-century (Averroist-scholastic), later canonized by Spinoza. Eriugena also could not have "followed" Iamblichus and Proclus, whom he did not know. (4) The "Talmudic school of Mishnat (13th–14th cc.)" does not exist under that name; the claim attached to it is a staple of anti-Talmudic polemical literature, not a documentable school position — this passage, and the reviewer's ethnographic generalizations about "the Jews," are reproduced in translation as evidence of the milieu, not as fact. (5) The Plethon paragraph is wrong on nearly every count: Gemistos Plethon was a pagan Platonist and an opponent of the hesychast establishment, not "one of the teachers of hesychasm"; he visited Italy in 1438–39 for the Council of Ferrara-Florence, well before the fall of Constantinople (1453), and died in Mistra; Ficino (b. 1433) never attended lectures by him — the Florentine Platonic Academy was founded years after Plethon's death, in memory of his visit. (6) Siegfried's Philo von Alexandria was published at Jena, 1875. (7) "Time is the quintessence of things" is not a locatable sentence of Contra Celsum. The Schelling "article" as titled is likewise unidentifiable; the "irrational fall" doctrine belongs to his Freiheitsschrift orbit.

What the review gets right. The two substantive philosophical objections are serious ones: whether "esotericism" is even coherent inside a strict creationist frame (Dugin's whole book is an attempt to answer precisely this), and whether Dugin's triadic analysis of the absolute is Orthodox theology at all rather than Neoplatonism in Orthodox vestments ("the triadic method... is typical of the Neoplatonists, provided one sets aside the pathos of the exposition"). Golovin also correctly notes that Dugin reads natura naturans through Spinoza's usage, and his closing observation — that the book's confessional triumphalism sits uneasily with its philosophical register — anticipates the standard scholarly criticism of Dugin's entire religious corpus.

Text. Translated in full from the Golovin Foundation archive text (golovinfond.ru, posted December 2011; the review is contemporary with the book, mid-1990s). Golovin's ellipses, italics, and unbracketed asides are preserved; obvious typographic slips in the source (e.g. De devisione for De divisione) are corrected silently.
